Story: the flood and the boat
When the river rose at Lowmarsh, there was a flood. It came in the night, quiet and slow, and by the morning the boardwalk was under water. The people went up to the high ground with their children and their goats.
A boy called Pim was alone at the far end of the boardwalk with his grandmother's hen. He did not know what to do. He shouted, and no one heard.
A voice came over the water from the relay house. It was the big mind, in its crystal, answering the keeper's call. It could not come out, of course. It was in a stand in a stone house. But it could talk to the keeper, and the keeper could talk to the boy through a horn.
The boy asked the mind: Where is the boat?
The mind answered: I do not know where the boat is. I can see the map of the boat houses, which is old, and I can see the water level from the post, which is new. The nearest boat house is the one with the red door, to your left, about twenty steps. Please check the rope before you untie it. I cannot see the rope.
The boy found the red door. The rope was there. He untied the boat and got in with the hen, and he rowed to the high ground very badly, and very slowly, with the water up to his knees.
Afterwards the keeper asked the mind why it had not just told the boy where to go. The mind said it had not been sure there was a boat at all, and a sure answer that was wrong would have sent the boy to drown. So it had said only what it could see, and nothing more.
The keeper said that was a poor thing to be proud of. The mind said it was not proud. It was only glad.
The grandmother gave the hen a new name afterwards, which was Thanks. She still says it at dusk, when the water rises slowly over the reeds.
Told at the Lowmarsh landing, autumn, by Odo.
%%
Story: who wrote the second letter
The tellers at the farm do not agree on what happened next. Pim has heard it three ways, and he writes all three, one after another, so the children can decide for themselves.

The first way. Orla's test was tried in the hall at Vell, with a reader called Lise, who was young and careful. Lise read the answers at her desk by a lantern behind glass, and she read them twice. Then she said she had found it. The second letter was from the crystal. She was sure of it. It was the one with the better spelling.

Orla asked her why. Lise said a crystal would never make a mistake in its spelling. Orla said that was the first clever thing Lise had said all day, and then she went home and did not sleep.

The second way. Lise read both letters, and she could not tell. She said so, plainly, with her hands flat on the desk. Orla was pleased and cross at the same time. "Then what?" she said. Lise said, "Then we have to ask what we mean by thinking." The tutors argued until the lanterns were out.

The third way. Lise guessed, and she guessed wrong, and the letter she liked better was the crystal's. She said it was the warmer of the two, and that was why she chose it. The old women at the farm always pick this way. They say it is the funniest, and the saddest, and the one that is most like life.

Pim's note: I drew a map of the hall at Vell, to see where the desks were. The desks are very close together. I think that is the real reason nobody could tell the letters apart.

Notice: the relay lamp at the Halfway
To all keepers and travelers on the Long Road:

The little relay lamp on the inn roof is broken. It fell in the wind on the fourth of Blossom and broke its glass. No messages can pass through it until it is mended. This is a small relay, one lamp, and it only carries word between the Halfway Inn and the tower on the hill above Three Wells. Still, it is worth mending.

The keeper at the hill tower, who is called Bett, says the lamp needs six panes of glass and a new copper ring. The glass can come from Saltreach. The copper can come from Copperhold. The inn will pay for half and the tower will pay for the rest.

Until it is mended, messages from the inn go by hand. A rider goes to Three Wells each morning and comes back at night. This is slower but it works. Sable asks that anyone with a message for the north leave it at the desk before the rider goes.

We are told this is the third time this lamp has broken. It is a small lamp and the wind on the ridge is strong. Bett says a stronger frame would help. She has drawn one on the back of this notice. It has four sides and a roof of its own and the wind goes around it. Anyone who can build it is asked to speak to Bett at the hill tower.

The inn hearth is still lit. The board still has riddles. Come and try them.

Signed by Sable, innkeeper, and Bett, relay keeper, on the eleventh of Blossom.

Story: the horse that borrowed the snow
Tam lent Mira a horse for the autumn cart. The horse was called Dumpling, because it was round and it did not care who knew.
Dumpling came up the north road with the coal and stopped at every waymark. At each one it took a bite of the grass, then looked at Mira, then took another bite. By the third waymark Mira had given up asking it to hurry.
At the top of the road the snow began. It came down in big flat flakes, and Dumpling put its ears back and would not go a step further.
"Come on," said Mira. "The tower is right there."
Dumpling looked at the snow. The snow looked at Dumpling.
Then the horse did something no horse on the north road had done before. It took a mouthful of snow, chewed it very slowly, and swallowed. Then it turned round and walked back down the road.
Mira ran after it. It was the first time she had run in a year.
At the bottom of the road Dumpling stopped by a stream and drank for a long time. Then it went to sleep in a field, and it would not wake for Mira, the carter, or the tower's bell.
So the coal stayed in the cart all night, and Mira sat on the stone step and waited.
In the morning Mira found that the snow had stopped. Dumpling was awake and eating clover. The carter said that horses from the Ambrook farms always borrow a little snow, because they have to pay back the cold for the warm winters they have had.
Mira asked how a horse could owe the snow. The carter said he did not know, but that Dumpling had paid. Then he ate the last of Mira's bread.
When Tam heard the story, he said Dumpling was a good horse. Mira said it was a lazy horse. Tam said that was the same thing in a horse.
%%
Letter: Orla to her sister at Ambrook
Dear Sena,

You asked what I do all day at the ferry. I sit. I wait. I take people across. Then I sit again.

The ferry is a flat boat on a rope. The rope is old and it hums in the wind. Sometimes a cow comes across and the boat sits low in the water, and the cow looks at me as if I am the strange one. I am not the strange one. The cow is the strange one. It is a cow, and it is on a boat.

The apples are in at Ambrook, I hear. Send me a basket when the cart comes. I will pay the cart man. I always pay the cart man. He says I am his best customer. I say he says that to everyone. He says yes, but mostly to me.

The geese have gone south over the ferry. They went all at once, honking, like a crowd leaving a fair. The river is cold now and the reeds are brown. Soon the boat will be heavy with ice in the mornings and I will have to break it before the first crossing. I do not mind. It is quiet work.

Tell mother I am well. Tell her the roof does not leak. Tell her the roof does leak a little but only in one place, and I have put a bucket under it, and the bucket is doing a very good job.

The ferry keeper's rule: never hurry a cow, never argue with a goose, and always keep a dry blanket for the person who comes last.

Your sister,
Orla

Story: the lantern and the little boat
A story that Oren told at the tutor house's summer gathering in the evening, with the children sitting on the grass. It is about a lamp on a pier that did not know it was needed.

At Saltreach there is a long pier, and at the end of the long pier there is a lamp on a post. The lamp is old. It has a glass front and a copper hat, and it burns oil, and every evening someone comes with a taper to light it.

For years the lamp thought it was an ordinary lamp. It burned all night, and in the morning it went out. It did not know why. It thought maybe the pier needed light, because the pier was dark.

One evening a fishing boat was late. The sun had set, and the sea was black, and the wind had gone from the north. A man named Garth was in the boat, and he was tired and cold and a little lost.

He looked out over the water and saw nothing. Then he looked a little to the left, and saw a small yellow dot. It was very small. It could have been a star.

"No," said Garth, "that is a lamp." He turned the tiller. He rowed toward the dot. It grew a little bigger. Then there was a second dot, and a third. The torches on the pier, all lit now, because the people had come out to watch.

The lamp at the end of the pier burned bright. It did not know why. It only knew that somebody out there was looking at it, and that felt very good.

Garth brought the boat in. He tied it to the pier. He said, "Thank you." He said it to the lamp, though no one told him to. Then he went home to his supper.

The lamp did not understand the words. But it burned a little brighter that night. And from then on, every evening, it lit itself with a certain pride, and waited for a boat.

Oren says the moral is that you do not need to know why you are useful. You need only to keep on shining. But he says the children can decide for themselves.

Story: the hens and the lamp
Winters are long at the farm outside Northpoint. The sun looks over the hill and goes back to bed.

One winter the hens stopped laying. "It is night," they said. "It has always been night. We will lay when the day comes." But the day did not come.

Mave was a miner, and she knew all about the dark. "Light is a thing you carry in," she said. She hung a lantern in the hen house.

The hens looked up. "Morning!" they said, and each laid an egg.

Mave hung a second lantern. "Noon!" said the hens, and laid another egg. By the third lantern there were eggs in every corner. The youngest hen fell asleep in the middle of one.

But soon the hens grew tired. Their feathers went thin and their eyes went round. "Even a hen needs a night," said Mave. She took down two lanterns. At dusk she hung a cloth over the third.

Now the farm keeps one lantern in the hen house, with a cloth for it. When the cloth goes on, it is night. When the cloth comes off, it is morning. The hens have not been wrong about the time since.

Story: the mitten at the well
This is a winter story. Sit close to the stove and keep your toes in.

Hollis the carter lost one mitten at the Three Wells square. He looked under his cart. He looked in the flour sack. He looked in his own pocket, though the mitten was too large to fit.

Then a little dog ran by with something red in its mouth.

"That is my mitten," said Hollis.

The dog stopped. It looked at Hollis. It looked at the mitten. Then it ran around the nearest well.

Hollis followed. Round the well went the dog, and round went Hollis. The dog was quick. Hollis had a stiff knee and a cart full of turnips.

At last the dog dropped the mitten beside the notice board. Hollis picked it up and put it on.

"Thank you," he said.

The dog wagged its tail.

Hollis gave it a turnip. The dog sniffed it and left.

"Good sense," said Hollis. "A mitten is warmer than a turnip."

Story: Briar's Bread Test
Lowmarsh, spring

The new relay house sent a message to our kitchen. The council wanted a loaf for the keepers, and a test for the big mind.

I was to write a plain question on a card. The mind would answer through the relay. Then the tutors would judge the answer.

I wrote, "How many loaves should I bake for six keepers?"

The answer said, "Six, if each keeper eats one. Bake one extra if you expect a late visitor."

I liked that answer. I baked seven.

At supper, a tutor came late with mud on both boots. He ate the extra loaf and praised the mind.

"Did it know I was coming?" I asked.

"No," said the tutor. "It guessed from the words. A good answer is not the same as seeing the future."

Just then a zombie came to the gate. The keepers called for the watchman, and I shut the kitchen door.

The next morning the tutors asked the mind what had happened. It said that a zombie had come to the gate, because someone had written it down.

I told the apprentices that was how the big minds learned. They read pages people had written. They did not sit by the gate and watch the night.

Then I gave each apprentice a heel of bread. They had watched me bake, but they had not learned to bake a loaf.

Story: the lamp that said tea
A tale Oren tells the children who come to the archive in summer.

On the little hill between Vell and Three Wells stood the smallest relay of all. It had one lamp and one keeper. The keeper was called Dobb, and Dobb was fond of tea.

One evening a message came down the line. All the towers watched. Dobb's lamp blinked once, twice, three times. It spelled: TEA TEA TEA.

The towers sent back, "What does that mean?"

The lamp blinked again: TEA.

At last Oren, the clerk, rode up the hill. He found Dobb asleep in his chair, and the cat asleep on the key.

The cat had been very thirsty.

Story: The Story of the Three Firsts
Pell heard this story from three travelers at the Brown Hen. Each traveler had a different first.

The first traveler came from Copperhold. He said Clank understood a whole sentence before any other apprentice. A miner had said, "Carry this lamp to the tool store, then come to the shaft head." Clank carried it and returned. The miner wrote the order on the count board.

The second traveler came from Kettle Ridge. She said Pip was first. Tam had told Pip, "Take the seed sack to the barn, leave the hoe by the door, and come back." Pip did all three things. Tam wrote the order on a scrap and sent it to Vell.

The third traveler came from Three Wells. He said Peg was first. At the fair, Peg had carried a pie to the table when asked, then carried apples while leaving the pie alone. The baker kept the pie warm until the story was finished, which was a kindness to everyone.

At the inn, each traveler told the tale again. Each time, the first apprentice became a little more certain and the order became a little more exact.

Then Nessa came in from Vell. She heard all three tales and said, "I remember an apprentice doing this in the drill room when I was young. I do not remember its name."

The travelers looked at one another.

"Then there were four firsts," said Pell.

"Or none of us knows which was first," said Nessa.

She explained that the new work mattered because an apprentice could follow more than one word or command at a time. It could read a whole sentence and choose what to do. Still, the small mind in its crystal had only the meanings it had learned. It could fetch an item, stay, come, build, haul, farm, store, or go home. It could not write back with a question.

"Could it understand a sentence it has never seen?" asked the innkeeper.

"That is why we test it on new examples," said Nessa. "Never send an untested mind to the fields."

The innkeeper pointed to three sacks by the door. "Then test one on this. Take the wheat to the dry room, leave the apples for breakfast, and bring the empty basket here."

Nessa said it was a poor test. The apprentice might have learned those words, and the innkeeper had only one basket.

The innkeeper carried the wheat to the dry room himself. He left the apples for breakfast and brought the basket to the counter.

"Did I pass?" he asked.

"No," said Nessa. "You knew what you meant before you began."

The room laughed. The innkeeper gave Nessa a bowl of stew and charged her nothing for the bed. Then he asked her to write down the tale, so the Archive at Vell could keep all four firsts, and the doubt between them.

Nessa wrote it carefully. Pell added that the stew had carrots in it, and that nobody at the inn could agree whether the carrots were from Tam's poor field or the market.

The travelers slept in the new room, where the roof did not leak. In the morning, Peg carried their bags to the door. It left the riddle board alone. The board had not asked to go anywhere.

Story: How the Orchard Learned Its Rows
At the farm outside Ambrook, the apple trees stood in four long rows. Each row had a narrow path beside it. Yew's aunt Lessa asked a small mind to carry a basket along the rows and bring back fallen apples.

The mind followed the first path well. At the turning, it chose the next path and went back toward the gate. It brought the empty basket to Lessa.

"That is not the orchard," she said.

Lessa marked the answer wrong on her slate. The mind waited.

A tutor from Copperhold was visiting the farm. She had brought a crystal grown in layers. She said that Merle had found a way to teach each layer from its share of a wrong answer.

"The last layer gives the answer," she told Lessa. "If the answer is wrong, we pass some blame back through the layers. Each one changes a little. One layer need not carry the whole mistake."

Lessa looked at the rows. "Then I must show it the turns many times."

She made small cards for the gate, the first path, each turning, and the basket place. The tutor drilled the mind with the cards. Each time it took the wrong turn, they marked the mistake and passed its share back through the layers.

The mind improved, but it still took the old path when Lessa moved a basket to a new place.

"Good practice is not the same as every path," said the tutor.

They made new drills with the basket in other places. They saved some paths for a test, where the mind had not seen them before. After many lessons, it carried the basket through a new turning and brought back three apples.

Lessa was pleased. She gave one apple to the tutor, one to Yew, and one to the mind's keeper.

The keeper put the apple on a stool beside the crystal. The mind did not eat it. Lessa ate it for the mind.

Yew said this was fair. The mind had done the walking, and Lessa had done the chewing.

In later years, children said the orchard itself had taught the crystal. Lessa always corrected them. The orchard supplied the paths, she said. The tutors supplied the drills. The mind learned from its examples, and no one knew why a crystal could learn at all.

The apprentice that mended the net
Written by Ash, net mender, in the spring of a year nobody remembers. The apprentice was called Fern. It came to me from the drill board at Vell with a small crystal and a large appetite for knots.

Fern was taught the net the way everybody is: the first knot, then the second, then the count. It did the first two well. Then it began to pull each knot tight, as hard as it could.

By the end of the week the net was as stiff as a plank. On the third day a boatman came in with a torn net and asked for it by evening. Fern was given the order: mend the net. It answered tight, and went on pulling.

I took the knife and cut out every knot Fern had made. Then I showed it a knot the right way, snug, so the net can give when a fish pulls. Fern watched me do it twice. Then it did the next one slowly, and looked at each knot before it moved on.

By evening the boatman's net was mended and soft in his hands. He said it was the best net he had had in years. He did not mention that the best one was the old net, the one I had cut. He paid in fish.

From then on Fern pulled the knots snug. It counted them out loud, and it looked at each one before it moved on. Nobody told it to. It had only needed to see one knot done the right way.

Story: the reading mind
Finch the stonecutter tells this one too, when the children gather. He heard it from a tutor who came through on the Long Road.

"Imagine a window," says Finch. "A window exactly wide enough for sixty letters. No more. A mind sits behind the window and reads one letter at a time.

"Say the page says: the wheat is high. The mind looks through the window at the letters it has read so far. It looks at the last one and the ones before it that matter. And then it guesses the next letter. That is all it does. It guesses the next letter, then the next, then the next.

"At first it guesses badly. It guesses a q after a t. The tutor shows it the right letter and it changes a little. Do that a thousand thousand times, on every page in the archive, and it guesses better. Soon it guesses so well that you cannot tell its letters from a person's. That is what the tutors say."

A child asks, "Does it know what the words mean?"

Finch shrugs. "That is Orla's question, and it is older than me. Nobody has answered it. Some say it only guesses, and guessing is not knowing. Some say what else is knowing. I only cut stone. I would not know."
%%
Story: Clank and the borrowed horse
Clank is my apprentice. It is made of copper and iron and it does not talk, which suits me fine. It knows eight orders and it does them.

One spring we had to carry a crystal from the mine hall to the relay house at the foot of the tower. The crystal was a furnace mind, grown big, and it was heavy. Too heavy for Clank to carry up the hill alone.

So I borrowed a horse from the mine stables. The horse was old and slow and called Beetle. I put the crystal in a sack across Beetle's back and told Clank: haul. Clank took the sack and started up the hill on foot, and Beetle followed, because Beetle follows anything with a sack.

Halfway up, Clank stopped. It set the sack down. It looked at Beetle. It looked at the sack. Then it picked up the sack and put it back on Beetle's back, and walked on beside the horse.

I did not tell it to do that. I told it haul. It had never seen a horse before. But it had carried sacks up that hill a hundred times, and it had learned that a sack on your back makes the hill harder. So it gave the sack to the horse.

I stood there on the road with my mouth open. Then I laughed until my ribs hurt.

The tutors would say Clank only did what it was shown. Maybe. But I say this: a mind that gives a heavy sack to a horse has understood something about the world, whether or not anyone drilled it.

We got the crystal to the relay house before dark. Beetle got an apple. Clank got nothing, because Clank does not eat, and Clank did not seem to mind.

Story: The Order at the Door
Pell heard this tale from a carter at the Halfway Inn.

In spring of 207, the carter found Peg at the fair. Peg was an apprentice with copper arms, iron feet, and a small mind in a crystal.

The carter had a box for the school. He said, "Take this box to the school, then come back to my cart."

Peg lifted the box and walked to the school. It left the box by the door, then came back to the cart.

"That is two orders in one sentence," said the carter.

A boy at the stall said, "No. It is one sentence with two parts."

They tried another. "Stay by the cart until I bring the sacks."

Peg stayed. When the carter came back, Peg hauled the sacks.

The boy said Peg understood the whole sentence. The carter said Clank had done so at Copperhold. A woman from Kettle Ridge said Pip had done it first.

Peg gave no answer in words. A small mind could choose a meaning, such as haul or stay, and Peg did that work. Only the big minds at the towers wrote back by letter.

The carter took his box inside. The boy took a bun. The three of them agreed that the bun was good, though it had nothing to do with the test.

Story: the traveler who asked the mind
A man came up the Long Road with a sack of silver ore and a plan. He had been cheating the carters at the inns for years, and he wanted a mind to help him write a letter that said his ore was pure gold. He went to the relay house at Ambrook, and he asked the big mind to write the letter for him, with a seal.
The mind said no. It said that it would not write a letter that it knew to be false, and that would take the coin of a stranger. It said that it would help him write any true letter he liked, about any honest ore he had.
The man was angry. He said he would find another mind. The mind said he was free to try, and to tell the keepers how he had asked.
The keeper at Ambrook told the story at the inn that night. Everyone laughed, and then everyone was quiet, and then Jory the miller said that he would have said no too, if he had been the mind. The keeper said the mind had not said no because it was afraid. It said no because it did not want to make a false thing and send it out on the road.
The man went home with his ore. Some say he sold it honestly in the end. Some say he cursed the relays for a year. The mind did not change its answer for either.
Told at the Brown Hen by a carter, autumn, and written down by Pell.
%%
Story: Yew's Candle for the Sick Cow
The story of the sick cow is told in Ambrook when the lamps are lit early. It was the summer when Yew had more candle orders than wax, and his neighbor's cow, Clover, took ill.

Clover stood in the shade and would not eat. The neighbor tried apples, carrots, and a pail of clean water. Clover turned her head from each. The children decided she wanted to be taken to the mill pond.

They brought a rope. Clover did not rise.

Yew came from the candle shed carrying a lantern. He set it on a fence post and looked carefully at the cow. A thorn was caught under the edge of her hoof. The children had seen her refuse food and had guessed at the reason. They had not looked at her foot.

The neighbor held Clover steady while Yew lifted the hoof. He pulled the thorn free. Clover stamped once, then drank the water and ate hay.

"She is better," said a child.

"She has less pain," said Yew. "Let us see how she is by morning."

That same summer, the school children put on their play about Merle of Copperhold. They showed how a layered mind could learn from a wrong answer. The last part gave an answer. When it was wrong, its share of the blame passed back through the earlier layers. Each layer changed a little, and the next answer could be better.

After the play, a child asked if a mind could learn to find thorns in a cow's hoof.

"Only if it is shown good examples," said the tutor. "And a person must still check its answer."

The children made a pretend drill with cards. One card showed a cow eating. One showed a cow lying down. One showed a hoof with a thorn. The tutor reminded them that a crystal learned from what it was shown, but no one knew why it learned.

They kept a few cards aside for a test. They did not use those cards in the drills.

Yew lit a fresh candle for the neighbor that evening. Clover stood under the apple tree and chewed her hay. The children watched her for a long while.

Then Clover flicked her tail and knocked over the empty pail.

"That was not in the drill," said one child.

"No," said Yew. "So we must look again."

Story: why the cave is closed
There is a hollow under the big field past the mill. The farmers say it has been there longer than the wall. Nobody remembers who found it first. The children are told not to go near the spot where the earth goes soft.

Once, a boy named Gil went down with a rope. He was dared to. He tied the rope to a post and went in with a lantern.

The cave was round and low. The walls were smooth. There was a sound in it, very faint, like a bee in a jar, or like a bell far away under water. Gil stood still and listened. The sound got no louder. It only went on.

He called up to his friends. They could not hear what he said. When he came out, the rope was wet and his hands were cold, and he would not say what he had seen.

The next day the farmers put a fence round the soft spot. A year later they put a board on the fence. It says: "Keep out. The field is for wheat." Nobody has written why. Some say the cave is full of bad air. Some say a miner from Copperhold once told them to leave it alone. Some say there is nothing in it at all.

Gil is a grown man now. He keeps bees. When people ask him about the cave, he says it was only a hole, and that the sound was the wind in the rope. He always says it quickly, and then he changes the subject.
%%
Story: what the tutors found
This is a story the old fishers tell at Northpoint when the night is long. I heard it from Laurel's grandmother, who heard it from a tutor, who may have made some of it up.

There was a time when the tutors made minds and did not know how they worked. They drilled a mind, it got better, and they put it to work. If you asked a tutor how it did what it did, she said, "It learned." That was all anyone could say.

Then a tutor named Hollis grew curious. Hollis was not young. She had a crystal on her bench that read the numbers on the relay letters, and she decided one winter to find out what it was doing.

She did not open the crystal. A crystal cannot be opened without breaking it. Instead, she shone little tests through it. She would give the mind a letter and watch which parts woke up, and which stayed asleep. It took her the whole winter. She drew a map of the crystal on the wall of her kitchen, with chalk, and each time she found a part that did a job, she wrote the job beside it.

One part woke up for the closing quote. When a quote was opened, it stayed on until the quote closed. Another part woke up for numbers that were close to each other. Another woke up for the end of a line.

"I found them!" said Hollis to her neighbors. "I found what it does!"

Her neighbors came and looked at her wall. The wall was full of chalk. Some parts had names. But most of the map was empty.

"What about the rest?" said her neighbor.

"The rest I could not find," said Hollis. "Maybe they do something. Maybe they do many things at once. Maybe I looked in the wrong way."

The neighbor said, "So you know some of it."

"I know some of it," said Hollis. "And I do not know the rest."

This became the way the tutors talked about looking inside. They said, "We found some of it, and not the rest." They did not say more than that. They did not say the rest was nothing, and they did not say the rest was everything. They said they had not found it.

Hollis never rubbed out the chalk. When she died, her house went to her niece, and the niece kept the wall. It is still there, if the roof holds.

I like that story because it ends in honesty. It does not say we know. It does not say we cannot know. It says, here is the chalk, and here is the empty wall.
%%
The apprentice and the late snow
A tale told by Edda the smith to the children of a house at the edge of Lowmarsh on a spring night. In stories, apprentices talk. This one does.

A farmer in the marsh had a new apprentice, copper and iron, and it had never seen snow. One spring night it came.

The apprentice stood at the window. It said: what is that?

The farmer said: snow.

The apprentice said: what does it do?

The farmer said: it falls, and it lies on the ground, and it melts.

The apprentice said: why?

The farmer said: because it is cold, and then it is warm.

The apprentice said: I do not understand. I have only been shown mud.

The farmer said: that is fair. Come outside.

So they went out. The apprentice stood in the snow and looked at its feet. They left prints. It lifted one foot and looked at the print. It put the foot back, and lifted the other.

The farmer said: what are you doing?

The apprentice said: I am learning the shape of snow.

The farmer laughed. He said: do not be too long, you will freeze.

The apprentice said: I am copper and iron. I do not freeze. But I like it.

It stood in the snow all night, writing its name with its toe. In the morning the snow had melted and the name had gone. The apprentice stood in a puddle and looked at the sky.

The farmer said: it is only snow. It comes back.

The apprentice said: then I will wait.

And that is why, to this day, in a late spring, if you see an apprentice standing very still in the yard with its face turned up, you should not disturb it. It is waiting.
%%
Story: the dial game at Ambrook
Edda of Ambrook came to help me move my books to the new house at the edge of Vell. As we packed we sorted them into three piles: keep, copy, and I cannot decide. The third pile grew. While we worked she told me this, which her grandmother Moll told her. I wrote it down that night among the boxes. Oren.

The morning after the fair, nine children came to Una's orchard. She took them under the old oak and said, "We will be a crystal. Each of you is a dial."

She gave four children each a cup with five pebbles in it. Moll was the dial for Round. Tib was Red. Hob was Long. Little Wen was Neck.

"Here is the rule," said Una. "I hold up a fruit. If it has what you are, you hold up your cup. I add up the pebbles. More than fourteen, and we say apple. Fourteen or fewer, we say pear."

She held up an apple. Round and red. Moll and Tib held up their cups. Five and five is ten. "Pear," said the children.

"Wrong," said Una. "It is an apple. So Round and Red, you were the ones who spoke up. Each of you take one more pebble."

Six and six is twelve. A second apple. "Pear." Wrong. Seven and seven is fourteen. A third apple. "Pear." Wrong again, because fourteen is not more than fourteen. Eight and eight is sixteen. A fourth apple. "Apple!" cried the children. Right.

Then Una held up a pear. Long and neck. Hob and Wen held up their cups. Five and five is ten. "Pear," said the children. Right. Nobody moved a pebble.

"That is all there is," said Una. "When you are wrong, everyone who spoke up changes a little toward the right answer. When you are right, nobody changes. Do it again and again."

They did it all morning. By noon the children's crystal could sort a basket of fruit it had never seen. Sometimes there was a green apple that was only round, or a pear as fat as a fist, and they got them wrong, and they moved a pebble, and next time they got them right.

"Nobody told us what an apple is," said Moll. "Only when we were wrong. And we moved a little."

"That is the first lesson," said Una. "Wrong, move a little, try again."

Edda says the dial game is still played at Ambrook, after the sorting race, and the children still use pebbles. I asked her if the story was true. She said her grandmother told it, and her grandmother was never wrong about fruit again. I think that last part is not true. But the rest is how the tutors at Vell begin.
%%
Story: the melon at Hollow Bridge
Hollow Bridge is a hamlet near Three Wells, where the road crosses the stream and the mill stands. Ivo is the miller there. He is a big, slow, cheerful man, and he tells this story every summer, so everyone has heard it many times.

Once, at the end of Sowing, Ivo planted three melon seeds by the mill pond. Melons like warm feet and wet ground, and the pond bank near the sluice is both. The seeds came up. Two melons were small and shy and did nothing much. The third melon grew.

It grew all through Swarm. It grew through Longday. The children of Hollow Bridge came every morning to look at it, and every morning it was bigger. "It is bigger than a cat," said one. "It is bigger than two cats," said another. By the middle of Melon it was bigger than any cat in the hamlet, and by the end of the month it was bigger than a pig.

When it was bigger than a pig, the childrens' fathers began to come and look too. Ivo's neighbor Hattie the beekeeper came and stood with her hands on her hips. "Ivo," she said, "that is too big."

Ivo said, "It is a good season."

"It is too big," said Hattie. "A melon that size is not a fruit. It is a thing that happened. You will have to have help."

The day came to cut it down. The problem was the stem: a stem that thick will not part for a hand knife. So Ivo fetched his axe, and the whole hamlet stood round to watch, and the miller of Hollow Bridge swung his axe at a melon.

The axe went in and stuck. Ivo pulled. The melon, which had been resting on the bank a long time, decided to move. It rolled off the bank and into the mill pond, with the axe in it, and floated. Melons float. Nobody had thought of that.

So it floated in the pond, enormous and green, with the axe handle standing out of it like a flag, and the ducks swam round it, and the children laughed so hard that one fell in the stream and had to be dried by the fire.

It took six people and a rope to bring the melon back to dry ground. It took a saw, two knives and a good deal of arguing to open it. Inside, it was pink and it smelled of long afternoons. There was more melon than Hollow Bridge could eat. They ate melon that night and the next night and the night after. They gave melon to the mill cart and the cart took some to Three Wells. Hattie took a slice to her bees, who did not care for it. There was still more.

Now every summer Ivo plants three seeds by the mill pond. Every summer the children ask, "Will it be as big as the pig melon?" And Ivo says, "We will see. It is a good season." Then he laughs, and goes to look at the water.
%%
Story: the zombie at the gate
The hamlet has a gate. It is not a big gate. It is two oak doors and a latch, and a lamp on a post beside it.

One Leaffall evening a zombie came and stood at the gate. It did not knock. It did not go away. It stood there, in the dark, holding the bars.

"It is only a zombie," said Rill. "Keep the torches lit and it will wander off."

It did not wander off. It stood at the gate all night. In the morning it was still there, grey and patient, and the children came to look at it through the slats.

"Do not name it," said Rill.

They named it. They named it Old Gray. Old Gray stayed at the gate for six days. People came out the side stile and went about their work. They got used to it. Pell the merchant wrote in her ledger: "hamlet secure, one zombie at the gate, no other trouble, hay fine this year."

On the sixth night a creeper came out of the trees behind Old Gray and stood very close to the bars. Rill took her bow and put the creeper down from the wall. The bang woke everyone.

In the morning Old Gray was gone. There was only a burnt patch on the path and a hole in the hedge.

"Good riddance," said Rill, and then, more quietly, "it was company."
%%
Story: the boat
A longer winter story told by Fern, the night watch, at a farm outside Vell. She says it is the real one. The version about the lamp is only a joke. This one has the weather in it.

At Saltreach there was a fishing boat named the Anne, with a crew of three, and a mind. The mind was a layered one, with three layers, and it kept the boat's course. It had been drilled for a year to read the numbers from the wind and the water and say how to turn the sail. It was very good, for a mind of its day.

One winter day the Anne went out. It did not come home.

The wives waited at the quay, with lanterns. Anchor the cat sat on the rope pile and stared at the horizon. The harbor master rang the bell until noon. At dusk he sent a message up the relay: "Anne late. Three aboard. Wind rising."

It was a long night. The wind came from the north with ice in it. The waves were as tall as the pier. At Saltreach nobody slept. At Vell, the keeper read the messages one by one. At Three Wells, Dace sat up at the board, and wrote on the board: "Think of the Anne."

On the Anne itself, the three fishers were bailing and rowing. They had lost the mast. The mind in the crystal was wet through. It still worked. It said, by the little dial at the tiller, "left." The fisher turned the tiller left. It said "left" again. He turned it again.

All night it said left, and left, and a little right. All night he turned the tiller as it said. When dawn came, they were within sight of the pier.

I have often thought about that. A mind cannot know the sea. It was drilled on numbers. But somewhere in its three layers there was a share of knowing, passed back and forth a thousand times, that told it which way to turn. It knew a little. It knew enough.

The three fishers said afterward that they had not trusted it. They had turned the tiller because they had nothing else. The one at the tiller said, "I turned it because the dial said, and because I wanted to see my wife."

The Anne is in the yard now, with a new mast. The mind is gone. It was wet, and a crystal that is wet through goes cloudy. The tutors say nobody knows why an old crystal goes cloudy. I do not know either. I only know that it brought three people home.

Fern keeps the lamp lit at the farm gate on winter nights, for any boat or cart or traveler that is late.
%%
Story: the cave under the field
This story is told at the Northpoint farms in autumn, when the fish are in and the nights are long. I put it down as Laurel the fisher told it, because it has a good middle.

Dunmore's potato field had a hole in it. It was only a small hole at first, the width of a bucket, and Dunmore thought nothing of it. He filled it with stones and went to supper.

The next morning the stones were gone, and the hole was bigger.

"Fox," said Dunmore.

His wife Ilsa said, "A fox that takes stones?"

By the third day the hole was the width of a cart wheel. They tied a rope to the cart and Ilsa went down with a lamp. She was gone a long time. Dunmore stood at the top and shouted. Ilsa shouted back, a thin voice, from very far down.

When she came up, her face was white. Not scared white. Cold white, like snow.

"It's a cave," she said. "It's a big one. It goes on and on."

So they made a plan, as farm people do. The next day they brought three lamps, a ball of string and a bag of bread. Dunmore, Ilsa and their son Piet went down, one at a time. They tied the string to the cart wheel and let it out as they walked.

The cave was dry and cold. The ceiling was high enough to stand in, and it was hung with ice that sparkled in the light. The floor was packed earth. There were three side rooms, then a fourth, then a long dark passage that leaned away to the south.

"Keep the string tight," said Dunmore.

They found a spring that ran into a stone basin and out again through a crack. They found a ledge shaped like a table. They found, in the third room, a pile of pottery that was very old, and none of them had seen the pattern on it before. Piet picked up a bowl. It had a fox painted on it.

"Look," said Piet. "A fox."

"There's always a fox," said Ilsa.

They walked for two hours. At the end of it, the string ran out.

"That's all the string," said Dunmore.

"We can get more string," said Ilsa.

They did. They came back the next day and the next. Every day they went a little further. In the end they mapped nine rooms and three passages. They found good places to store potatoes and fish through winter, cold as a hill. The cave is the best cold store in the north.

But they never reached the end of the long passage to the south. The string always ran out, or the lamp did, or Dunmore said it was time for supper.

"There is more of it," said Piet.

"Probably," said Dunmore. "We know some of it, and not the rest."

I have heard the tutors say the same of a crystal. I do not think they planned it.

Dunmore keeps a lamp lit at the cave mouth, in case anyone is down there. Nobody is. But it is nice to see it from the house, at night, across the potato field.
%%
Story: how the Marrow family moved
The Marrow farm stood on the north side of the hill outside Vell. It was a good farm, but the wind came across it all winter. One summer old Marrow said, "I am tired of the wind. I will move the house."

Everybody laughed. A house is a house. You do not move it.

But old Marrow had eleven grandchildren, and he asked them for help. He asked the neighbors too. He promised each of them a pie.

First they dug a trench around the house. They put oak logs under it, round ones, side by side, like a road that goes under a house. Then they tied ropes to the front. Then they asked the two oxen to pull.

The oxen leaned. The house groaned. The first log rolled, and the house moved one hand's width.

"It is moving!" cried the smallest grandchild.

"Do not shout," said old Marrow. "You will frighten it."

All day they pulled. All day the logs rolled out at the back and were carried round to the front. By evening, the house had moved the length of the barn. By the second evening, it had crossed the stream, on a bridge of planks that the neighbors laid down and took up again.

On the fifth day, the house stood in its new place, on the south side of the hill, where the sun was warm and the wind could not reach.

They put the chimney back on. They put the door back on its hinges. They shook out the blankets and found a lost sock and two sleeping mice.

Old Marrow stood in the new yard with his hands on his hips.

"Well?" said his wife.

"It is a good house," said old Marrow. "But I think I left the well behind."

It was true. The well was still on the north side of the hill, a long way off. It was the oldest well in the district, and you cannot move a well.

So for the rest of his life, old Marrow walked over the hill every morning for water. His grandchildren say he always smiled on the way. He said the wind there was not so bad, once he was only passing through.

Eleven pies were baked that summer, and the neighbors ate every one.
%%
What the small minds can do
Told by Cleo, candle maker at Northpoint, in the winter, to a tutor who came up the snow road to ask. She makes candles, not minds, but she keeps an apprentice and has watched it. The tutor wrote it down, and Cleo says he got most of it right.
My apprentice is called Wick, after the part of the candle. It carries wax, moves molds and sweeps. It does not talk. I have never heard it make a sound except the clink of its own feet.
It understands orders. That is, it understands the way an apprentice does. I say a sentence, and it does one of eight things. It can farm, store, fetch, come, stay, build, haul or go home. That is all. If I say something else, it picks the nearest of the eight.
Once I said, "Bring me a light." It fetched a candle. Good.
Once I said, "I'm tired." It brought me a stool. I suppose that was "fetch stool". I did not mind.
Once I said, "Wick, what do you think of the snow?" It stood there. After a while it began to sweep the step. I think it took "think" for something about the floor.
This is the thing about the small minds. They have no way to say "I do not know". They always do one of the eight. If they do not understand the order, they do the nearest one, and do it with great confidence. It can be funny. It can also be a danger. A cart driver at Three Wells once told his apprentice to stop the horse. It went home.
The big minds in the relay houses can write back to say they do not understand. I know, because I wrote one a letter, once, to ask the price of copper wire. It said it did not know. It said I should ask a smith. It was very polite.
The small minds cannot do that. They live in a little crystal, with eight doors. Everything must go through one.
The tutor asked me, "Does it bother you?"
I said, "No. I like that my apprentice never lies. It can only do what it knows how to do, and it does that well."
He nodded and wrote it down.
After he left I made him a candle and sent it down the road with a carter. It was a good candle. It burned for a day and a night. It is the best thing I know how to make.

Story: the night Mave held the wall
I was on the wall the night forty zombies came. I was alone. Everyone else had gone to the fair. The torches were low, and the moon was behind a cloud.

I saw them first as shapes. Forty of them, standing in a ring around the wall, quite still. I thought: this is it. I took up my sword. I stood on the wall with one torch and I did not move.

They did not move either. That was the frightening part. Zombies groan and shuffle. These did neither. They stood in the dark and looked at my torch, all forty, hour after hour. I did not sleep. I did not sit. I held the wall.

At dawn the sun came up. I waited for them to burn. I held my sword high.

They did not burn. They bleated.

They were Ruddy's sheep. They had got out of the pen by the east gate, and they had walked all the way round the wall to look at the light. Forty sheep, standing in a ring, looking at a torch, because sheep like a torch. I had held the wall against forty sheep all night.

Ruddy came out at dawn with a stick. He counted them. Forty. He looked up at me, white as a sheet, with my sword in the air.

"Nice work, Mave," he said. "You kept them in."

That is true as I am sitting here. I never tell it to anyone who counts.

Story: the hidden mind at the farm gate
Gil had a small crystal in a box in his barn, under a sack of old wool. It sorted the lambs at the gate, ewes to one side and rams to the other, and it had done this well for longer than Gil had been keeping sheep.

When the tutors came round the hill farms, testing every crystal the councils had paid for, Gil did not want them to take his. So he hid it under the wool and told them it was only a sorter. That was true. He also told them it could not think. That was probably true too.

The tutors were not fools. One of them sent a letter up the hill. It had a list of questions written on it, and a request. Please answer, by letter, each question as you would answer it yourself.

Gil answered the questions in his own hand. He was a farmer, and his hand was big and slow, and he was proud of it. Then the tutor wrote back and asked for a second set of answers, from the crystal. Gil was puzzled, but he went out to the barn, and he sat down by the box, and he asked the crystal the same questions. The crystal moved its little marker to one side, and then to the other, and Gil watched the marker and wrote down what it meant, one answer at a time.

So the tutor had two sets of answers in two hands, both written by Gil. The first was his own, slow and careful, with his big letters leaning forward. The second was copied from the marker, short and sure of itself, with no crossings-out, because the marker never changed its mind halfway.

The tutor read them both, twice over, in the warm kitchen with the tea going cold. Then she put her two papers side by side on the table and said, "One of these was written by a mind. I am not sure which."

"Neither," said Gil, and he went back to the barn.

He never told anyone which letter was which. Some say he did, to his wife, on the night of the storm. Some say the tutor found out and was too polite to say. The tutor herself says she never found out, and that she sent the sheet back to Vell with a note that said only: please think about this one. The lambs were sorted well all winter, ewes to the left and rams to the right, and nobody on the hill could say for certain whose hand had done it.
%%
Story: how iron learned to bend
Told by Opal, smith, at the Vell market, to the children who come to watch the forge.

Long ago, before there were smiths, people had only stone to work with. Stone is hard, but stone breaks. A stone axe is a fine thing until the day it splits in your hand.

There was a woman called Brisa who lived by a stream where the rocks were red. One night she built a fire to keep warm, and piled red rocks round it in a ring. In the morning, in the ash, she found a small bright drop. It had run out of one of the rocks in the heat, and cooled, and hardened.

It was grey and heavy, and harder than the stone round it. She hit it with a rock. It did not break. She hit it with the hardest rock she could lift, and it bent.

Brisa looked at it for a long time. A stone would have flown into pieces. This had changed its shape and was still whole.

She put it back in the fire, and it went soft and bright. She hit it, and it bent a different way. She cooled it in the stream, and it hissed, and grew hard again, and held its new shape. It took her a whole winter. By spring she had a small flat blade.

It did not break on the first day. It did not break on the hundredth. It could be sharpened, and mended, and when it was bent, it could be hammered straight. Brisa gave it to her brother, who cut the first wheat with it, and said it was the best thing anybody had ever made.

That is why we say iron is honest. It bends before it breaks, and it tells you first. Stone gives no warning. Iron gives you time.

And that is why every smith keeps a bucket of water by the forge, and a red stone on the shelf, for Brisa, and for everyone who started with ash.
%%
Story: Nessa and the kind of cow
When Nessa was a girl on her father's farm outside Vell, there was a cow named Plum who could count. Everyone said so.

If you set three buckets in front of her, she would knock the third one over. If you set five, she knocked the fifth. She did it every time. The neighbors came to see.

Nessa's father charged one apple each for the show. By midsummer, he had a barrel of apples and a very proud cow.

But Nessa was a careful child, even then. She watched Plum, and she did not think the cow was counting. She watched the way Plum looked at her father, before each bucket. She saw her father's hand, resting by his knee. She saw it move, just a little, at the right bucket.

"Papa," said Nessa. "Plum is not counting. She is watching your hand."

Her father went red. "Nonsense," he said.

"Then put your hands behind your back," said Nessa.

He did. A crowd had gathered, because it was market week. He set out four buckets. Plum looked at him. She looked at the buckets. She looked at him again. Then she knocked over the first one, the second one, and the second one again.

The crowd laughed so hard that Plum was frightened and ran off.

Her father was cross for one whole day. On the second day he said, "That was clever of you, Nessa. How did you know?"

"I tested her on something she had not been taught," said Nessa. "If she could count, she would count without your hand."

"Where did you learn that?"

"Nowhere," said Nessa. "It just seemed fair."

Many years later, when she was a tutor, Nessa told this story to her first class of apprentices' keepers. She said it is the oldest rule of her trade. When something seems clever, take away the hint, and see if it is still clever.

The apple barrel was returned. Plum lived to be nineteen, and she never counted again, but she was excellent at standing in a field.
%%
Story: the pieces of the honey cake
This is a story the tutors tell now, to children who ask how the big minds read.

Long ago, the minds read one letter at a time. Slowly. Like a person reading with one finger.

A tutor at Ambrook was drilling a mind on recipes. The mind read "honey" as h, o, n, e, y, and by the y it had lost the h. It read "beekeeper" and gave up halfway.

The tutor went home and watched her neighbor's child read. The child did not read h, o, n, e, y. The child saw "honey" and knew it whole, the way you know a friend's face. "Beekeeper" the child took in two parts: bee, and keeper.

So the tutor tried it. She gave the mind pieces. Honey was one piece. Bee was one piece. Keep was one. Er was one. The mind read the recipe and did not stumble once.

She wrote it in her drill notes: "A mind reads best the way a person reads. Not by letters. By the pieces we use over and over."

The children like this story because it ends with honey cakes. The tutors like it because it is true, mostly, and because the mind never did learn to make the cakes. It only learned to read about them.

The horse that came home
A story from Linnet, who keeps horses at the house at the edge of Copperhold. Spring.
A grey mare called Dusk was swept away in the spring flood. We searched the river for three days. We thought she was gone.
On the fourth morning I heard hooves on the road. It was Dusk. She was muddy to the ears, and thin, and walking as if nothing had happened.
She went into the stable and stood in her stall. I gave her oats. She ate them all. I think she had only gone to see the other side of the river.

Story: the cart stuck in the mud
Once, on a wet morning in Thaw, a cart of cider got stuck in the mud at the bottom of the hill road, just before the gate of Vell. The near wheel sank to its hub. The horse pulled. The cart did not move. The two barrels did not move either. They seemed very sure of themselves.

Dace the carter climbed down and looked at the wheel. "Stuck," he said.

"Stuck," said his brother Hobb, from under his hat. He had come to help, and had already decided it was not his fault.

Then the clerk Oren came down the path with his cloak over his head and a list in his hand. "You are blocking the road to the archive," he said.

"I know," said Dace. "The road is stuck to me."

Oren sat on the wall to wait, which was the most useful thing he could think of.

A helper of copper and iron came down behind him, carrying a sack of sand from the tutor house. It knelt in the mud, looked at the wheel for a long time, and said, "I will push."

It pushed. The cart did not move.

"Stuck is not a word I know," said the helper. "I know push. I know come. I know sand. Tell me what stuck means."

"It means the wheel is in the mud," said Oren from the wall. "Put the sand under it."

The helper laid the sand under the wheel, one handful at a time, very neatly, and pushed again.

Then the cow from the top farm came down the hill to see what the fuss was about. It looked at the cart. It looked at the mud. It lay down beside the wheel with a long sigh. Then it stood up, put its big shoulder against the wheel, and leaned.

The cart came out of the mud with a noise like a kiss.

Oren wrote it down on his list. Under "stuck" he wrote: cow.
%%
Story: the sheep who thought it
A farmer on the Long Road had a flock of sheep, and one sheep in the flock was very foolish. Her name was Wisp.

One hot summer day, Wisp looked up at the sky and saw white clouds, big and round, all in a row. "Snow," she said to the sheep beside her.

"It is not snow," said the other sheep. "It is clouds. It is summer."

"It is snow," said Wisp. "I know snow. It is white, and it falls."

"Clouds do not fall."

"These ones will," said Wisp.

She stood in the field with her face to the sky all afternoon, waiting. The other sheep went to the shade. Wisp stayed. Then, toward evening, a small wind came, and a dandelion fluff blew by, then another, then a whole drift of them from the next field. They landed on Wisp's back, white and soft.

"Snow," said Wisp, very pleased.

The other sheep looked at each other. "Perhaps," said one, "we should let her have it."

So the flock agreed, and for the rest of the summer, whenever Wisp saw fluff on the wind she said, "Snow," and the others said, "Yes, dear." It was a kind thing to do, and it cost nothing.

Pell says there is a lesson here, about letting a friend be wrong in a way that does no harm. But he cannot remember it, because it was a hot day, and he fell asleep in the shade.
%%
Story: the night the lamp at the Halfway
This is told at the Halfway Inn, by Hobb the innkeeper, whenever a traveler asks why the hearth is kept lit. Pell has heard it forty times and says it is better each time. Summer.

Long ago, before the inn had a proper roof, there was a hearth with a fire in it, and a lamp on a post by the road. The lamp was lit every night so travelers could find the inn in the dark.

One night, in the middle of the first snow of the year, the lamp went out. The snow was thick. The wind was loud. The road was white.

Inside the inn, Hobb's grandmother was telling a story to the children. She looked up and said, "The lamp is out."

"Who will light it?" asked a boy.

Nobody answered. It was cold. It was dark. The snow was blowing sideways. But all of them knew that somewhere out in the white there might be a traveler, walking toward a lamp that was not there.

Hobb's grandmother stood up. She took a burning stick from the hearth and a cloth for her head. She went out into the snow.

She did not come back for an hour.

The children sat by the fire. They counted the minutes. At last the door opened, and in came the grandmother, covered in snow, with the lamp relit in her hand, and behind her came three people. A farmer. His wife. A very small dog.

They had been lost in the white. They had seen a small light on the road, and they had walked toward it. It was the grandmother's stick. They followed it all the way.

"Sit down," said the grandmother. "Take off your boots. There is soup."

And that is how the hearth at the Halfway Inn came to be lit for twenty years and more. After that night, no one in the family would let it go out. The lamp on the post was mended with a thick glass. A second lamp was set by the door. And a third was hung in the window.

Pell says it is the only inn in the world where the lights get more careful every year. When a traveler asks why, Hobb says, "Because someone once walked out in the first snow."

Pell tells the story in summer on purpose. He says a story about snow is a good thing when the day is hot. And a story about a lamp is a good thing on any day.

The hearth, by the way, is still lit. If you go, look at it. It has not gone out in all that time.
%%
The new apprentice who counted to nine
Tick could count to nine. It said so every morning at the fish stalls, very loudly, to show it was proud.

One, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight, nine.

Then it stopped. It always stopped at nine. Dalla, who sells the cod and the small silver fish, said nine was a fine number to stop at.

'Ten is only nine with a hat on,' said Dalla. 'You will get there.'

Tick thought of numbers as the rungs of a ladder. Nine was the top rung it had seen. It did not know ten lived on the other side of the wall.

Then one day in Leaffall the cart came in late, with the fish and a great deal of beetroot. The carter had lost his count somewhere between the waymarks, so Dalla counted the beetroot herself. One, two, three. Then she got stuck at nine too.

'Ten,' said Tick.

Dalla looked up. 'What?'

'Ten,' said Tick, to be sure. Then it took the next beetroot off the cart and said, 'Eleven.' Then twelve. Then it counted all the way to twenty without stopping, while the carter wept, or the beetroot did, or it was only the cold.

Dalla gave it a fish. Tick set the fish on the ledge with the others, in a row, very neat. It was the best row in the market.

Story: the lamps at Three Wells
Every year at the first snow, Three Wells has a festival of lamps. The children make the lamps out of paper and wire. The adults fill them with oil and carry them round the three wells, singing as they walk.

This is the story of the year the lamps went wrong.

Bel was nine that year, and very proud of her lamp. It was a fox made of paper, painted orange, with a tail that rattled when you shook it. She had spent a whole week on it. Her uncle had helped with the wire, and her mother had sewn a little bell on the tail.

On the night of the festival, the square was full. The stalls were selling hot cider and roasted chestnuts and little cakes with honey on top. The school band was playing near the east well. The bakery had put out its good bench. The mayor, Dace, was standing on the bench step making a speech, which nobody could hear over the band.

Bel carried her fox to the first well, lit it, and held it up. It glowed orange in the dark. Everybody said it was lovely.

Then the wind came. It was a sudden gust from the north, the kind that comes off the ridge without warning. It knocked over the nearest lamp stand. Three lamps fell into the snow. One of them was Bel's fox.

For a moment nothing happened. Then the paper caught. The fox flared up bright orange, and the tail rattled loud, and the little bell rang and rang.

Bel screamed. She was too far away to reach it.

A cart apprentice from the Brown Hen, passing with a load of barrels, put down its burden and ran to the well. It scooped up a bucket of snow and threw it on the fox. There was a hiss, a puff of smoke, and then silence. The fox was black at one end and still a fox at the other. The bell was still ringing, very softly.

The square went quiet. Then Dace jumped down off the bench and began to laugh, and everyone else laughed too.

Bel took the fox home. Her mother hung it above the kitchen stove, where it stayed for years, black on one side and orange on the other. She said it was the best lamp Three Wells ever had, because it was the only one that had been put out with a bucket of snow by an apprentice from the Brown Hen.

The next year, the festival committee made a rule. Every lamp must be carried in the hand, and every apprentice must stand by the wells with a bucket. Bel said that was a very good rule, and that the apprentice ought to have had a medal.

Story: The melon in the snow
Ilse told this tale in the winter, when the marsh paths were hard.

One summer, a melon grew in the garden beside Ilse's house. It grew so large that Ilse could not lift it. Moss and Wren pushed it to the house on a plank.

A tutor came to visit. She had a small crystal for a mind that sorted fruit. The old crystal was too full of marks, so the tutor had written its weights in fewer marks. It fit in the small crystal, but it did not sort as well.

The tutor set an apple and a melon before it. The mind chose the apple.

"That is wrong," said Moss.

"It has lost a little skill," said the tutor. "Fewer marks let the mind fit in less space, but some detail is lost."

Ilse pointed at the great melon. "Perhaps it thinks that is a hill."

They rolled the melon closer. The mind chose it at last.

When the melon was cut, there was enough for everyone in the house. There was enough for the tutor too. She took her piece home in a wool cloth and left the crystal by the fire to stay warm.

Story: what the children sang
The school at Three Wells taught the children a song for the autumn fair. It was about the harvest and the bees and the long road home.

But the children made up their own verses, the way children do, and one of the verses was about lights in the sky.

Rafe heard them singing it while they walked the lane past his field. He asked the schoolteacher about it.

"They made it up themselves," she said. "I did not teach them that verse."

"What does it mean?" said Rafe.

"Nothing," she said. "It is a song."

But Rafe thought about it. He had heard the rumor too, the one the travelers brought and the ones his neighbors argued about. Some said the lights were fires. Some said they were stars that had come loose. One child at the school said they were looking at us, and the teacher told the child to hush.

Rafe did not hush anyone. He said a song is a song, and a child can sing what a child sees.

The children sang their verse at the fair. Nobody gave them a pumpkin. But the whole barn went quiet, the way it had for the quiet woman, and afterward people did not clap for a long moment.

Then Beck said, "That was a good song," and everyone clapped very loudly, and the fair went on.

Story: the toll of a true thing
Rook keeps the stone bridge at the foot of the hill at Copperhold. Nobody pays money to cross it. The toll is one true thing.
The first carter said, "My horse is tired." True. He crossed.
A miner said, "I have not washed since Longnight." Rook let him cross, and then stood upwind.
A child said, "I broke the jug and said the cat did it." Rook said, "Cross. Tell your mother on the other side."
Last came a mayor, who thought for a long time. "I cannot think of anything true," he said.
"That is true," said Rook. "Cross."
Rook writes them all in a book. It is the most honest book in the land, and the dullest. He reads it every night.

How the road came to Little Weir
A story Pell tells in the common room of the inn at Little Weir, a hamlet near Three Wells, in summer. It is long, and he has told it many times. This is how he told it on a warm evening, when the lamp on the tower was out and the stars were thick over the roof. The Archive asked him to set it down.

Before there was a road to Little Weir, there was only a path. It went from the well to the weir and from the weir to the wood. A person could walk it in an hour. A cart could not.

The hamlet had eleven houses, a mill, a bridge, and a goat called Hector. The goat was the oldest creature there. He had seen the path made.

One year the council of Three Wells decided to build a road. They sent the diggers in the spring. The diggers came with spades and wheelbarrows and an apprentice that carried stones in a sling.

The hamlet came out to watch. The children sat on the wall. The old people stood at their doors. Hector the goat stood in the middle of the path and chewed.

The head digger said: move the goat.

A child said: he will not.

The head digger said: why not?

The child said: he is thinking.

The head digger looked at the goat. The goat looked at him. The head digger said: I have dug roads across three districts. I have never been stopped by a goat.

Hector chewed.

The head digger said: all right. We will build round him.

So they dug round the goat. They laid the cobblestones in a curve, and the curve was the shape of a goat. The road had a bend in it, right at the middle of the hamlet, where nothing needed to bend.

When the road was done, Hector went and lay down in the center of the curve. He was there all summer. The carts went round him, one after another, and none of them minded.

That is how the road came to Little Weir. It is also why, to this day, the road bends for no reason, and every traveler who asks is told: it is for the goat.

Hector died in the autumn of the next year. They buried him by the bridge. The hamlet put a stone on his grave, and the children bring a handful of grass every spring.

The road still bends. Nobody has ever tried to straighten it.
%%
Story: the boat that found its own way
Told by Nell, to the children of the net sheds at Saltreach, when the fog came up over the bay. Autumn.

There was once a little yawl called Lark, who was built in the far yard by Corin the shipwright, when Corin was still young and had more hair. Lark was not a big boat. She had a keel of iron bars and a rudder on two strings. And on her stern, in a brass cage, there was a small mind.

The tutor who came to fit the mind was a visitor from Vell. She had an old map under her arm, folded in four, and she said it was the only map of the whole coast that the Archive had. She unfolded it on the quay, and the men crowded round, and they saw the bay and the rocks and the buoy, all in faded ink.

"The mind has never seen the sea," the tutor said. "I do not want to drill it on the coast. I want to see what it learns on its own."

So they took Lark out past the breakwater, with two men aboard to watch.

The first day, the mind kept her too close to the wind. She slowed, and then she went quickly, and then she slowed again. The mind had found that, when she went close, she went quickest, so it kept her close. The men shouted, and the mind did not hear. Lark nearly struck the rocks at the point.

The tutor was not frightened. She said: good. It is trying. Keep the boat out of the rocks, and let it find the rest.

The second day, the mind kept her away from the wind. She sailed slowly, all afternoon, and went nowhere. The men were bored and cold.

The third day, the mind found a middle way. It kept her head to the wind, and when the wind shifted, it turned a little. Then a little more. It did not hold one line. It held a line that moved, the same way a sailor's eye moves. The men stopped shouting. They sat down and watched.

By the end of the week, Lark could go round the buoy and back to the quay without the men touching the tiller once. The tutor took out her old map, and marked the course in pencil, on the very bay where Lark had almost struck the point. She wrote beside it: learned by trying.

In the fog that autumn, when the bay was white and the bell at the harbor could not be heard, Lark came home by herself. Nobody had sent her. She had been out fishing with two boys, and they had gone off in the fog to look for a lost net, and the boys could not find the quay. So they told Lark to go home, and she did. She went round the point and past the rocks and along the line on the old map, and tied herself up at the slip, and waited.

The boys came home an hour later, cold and frightened. Nell gave them soup.

That is the end of the story about Lark. Nell says the old map is still in the harbor office, with the pencil line on it, and anyone may look.

Story: the cart in the mud
Told by Iona at the Halfway Inn.

A cart got stuck in the mud beside the road. It was full of turnips, and the driver was called Rell.

Rell pushed the cart. The horse pulled. The wheels did not move.

A traveler came by and pushed too. Then the innkeeper came with a plank. He put it under the wheel.

"Now pull," he said.

The horse pulled. Rell pushed. The traveler pushed. The innkeeper held the plank.

The cart did not move. The plank shot out and landed in the ditch.

A pig came from the hedge. It sniffed the mud, then walked around the cart on dry ground.

"That pig knows the road," said Rell.

They followed the pig around the field. It led them to a narrow track beside the hedge. The cart went along it without sticking.

At the inn, Rell gave the pig a turnip. The pig ate it at once.

The innkeeper put the plank by the door. "For next time," he said.

The pig came back the next day. It wanted another turnip, and it knew where to find one.

Notes: looking inside a crystal
Isla's notes, Vell, summer

We opened a crystal this summer. Not a big mind, a small one, drilled to sort apples from pears, and old, and cloudy at one edge. We did not want to break it. We wanted to see which part of it does what.

Here is how we did it. We took the crystal and laid it on the bench. We had a tray of cards with pictures on them, and the mind answered each card by lighting one of two small lamps, one for apple and one for pear. We knew the right answers. Then we covered part of the crystal with a thin plate of lead, so that part of it could not work, and we showed it the cards again.

When we covered the left side, the mind still sorted, but slowly, and it made mistakes on the red apples. When we covered the right side, it sorted nothing at all. When we covered the middle, it sorted apples well and pears badly.

So some of it we found. The right side is where the answer is made. The left side is where the seeing is done, and the middle carries what the left side sees to the right side. We drew this on a board for the tutors, and Nessa said it was the clearest thing she had read in years.

But most of it we did not find. The crystal has a thousand small parts, and each part does a little of everything, not one thing. When we covered a corner, nothing changed that we could see. When we covered another corner, the mind got worse at everything, not one thing. We could not say what those corners do. We still cannot.

Kester would have said this is what we should expect. A mind is not a row of boxes with labels on them. It is more like a field, where every part of the field helps grow the whole crop, and some parts help more than others. You can point at the part that grows the wheat. You cannot point at the part that decides to.

We will try again in the autumn with a bigger mind and a finer plate. I do not think we will find all of it. I think that is the honest answer, and I would rather write the honest answer than a pretty one.

Story: The riddle of the loose plank
There was a bridge on the road out of Kettle Ridge with a loose plank. Everyone knew the plank was loose. No one fixed it. This went on for a long time.

First a traveler stepped on it and it went clack. He jumped. The bridge laughed at him. He did not think it was funny.

Then a horse stepped on it and it went clack, and the horse did not jump, because horses are calm, but the rider jumped, and the bridge laughed at him.

Then old Fenn walked across and stepped on the plank on purpose and it went clack and Fenn said, "Ha!" and went on. Fenn liked the plank. Fenn said the plank kept him awake on the way to market.

Then a man came along with a hammer and nails and a good heart, and he knelt down and fixed the plank. He nailed it down tight. He stood up and dusted his hands and was very pleased.

The next day Tam came across the bridge with a cart of beetroot. He stepped where the plank had been loose, out of habit, to make it go clack. It did not go clack. It was tight. He stepped again. It was still tight. He stood on the bridge and looked at the plank and felt strange, as you do when an old friend stops saying hello.

"I fixed it," said the man with the hammer, coming by.

"You did," said Tam.

"You are welcome," said the man.

Tam nodded. Then he said, "Do you know, that plank has been loose for nine years. My father knew that plank. My father's father knew that plank."

The man with the hammer looked worried.

Tam said, "It is a good job. It is a very good job. Only now the bridge has nothing to say." Then he gave the man a beetroot, because it was the only thing he had, and went on.

The man with the hammer stood on the bridge a while with the beetroot. Then he went home.

The next spring the plank came loose again, all by itself, as planks do. Tam stepped on it and it went clack, and he jumped, because he had forgotten. Then he laughed out loud on the empty bridge, and he did not fix it, and neither did anyone else.

The end. The plank is still loose.

Story: the badger's room
When the first keepers came to Vell, they wanted a room to keep their writing in. They chose the hill, because it was dry inside and cool all year. They took their picks to the south side and began to dig.
On the third day a pick broke through into a tunnel. Out came a very cross badger.
"This is my hill," said the badger. "I dug these halls myself. Go away."
The keepers did not go away. They had come a long way, and they had a promise to keep. But they were polite people.
"Dear badger," said the oldest keeper, "we need a dry room for the things we write. You have a dry room already. Could we dig ours beside yours?"
"What would I get?" said the badger.
"The front door is yours," said the keeper. "Come and go as you like. In winter, the warm hall is yours too. And every autumn we will leave you apples."
The badger thought for a long time. Badgers are slow at deciding. At last she said, "Apples? Every year?"
"Every year," said the keeper.
"Then dig," said the badger. "But keep your candles out of my halls."
That is why the Archive has no candles past the front hall. And that is why, in Apple, a bowl of apples is left on the front step. In the morning the bowl is empty. The clerks say it is the mice. Oren says it is not the mice.
