Story: the tale of the two brothers
Rafe's father used to tell this tale, and Rafe told it to the children at the fair, and this is how it went.

Two brothers lived on a farm. One went to work on the relay towers, high up, where the wind is; the other stayed and kept the sheep. The tower brother came home once a year and told of the lamps and the long stairs and the cold. The sheep brother said the tower was a fool's errand. The tower brother said the sheep were a fool's flock.

Every year the tower brother brought a letter for the sheep brother. Every year the sheep brother put it on the shelf and did not read it.

One winter the tower brother did not come home. The sheep brother waited. He fed the sheep. He mended the fence. Then one night he took down all the letters and read them, and each one said the same thing: come up and see, brother, the whole land at once.

The sheep brother walked to the tower the next spring. He climbed the stairs, all of them, and stood at the top and looked. He could see his own farm. He could see the road, and Three Wells, and the fields, and the sea far off, faint as a smudge.

He stayed up there a long time. When he came down, he was not angry anymore. He said the tower was not a fool's errand after all.

Rafe said the story was true. Owen said Rafe's father made it up. Rafe said that did not make it less true.

Story: the contest of the three towers
In the early years, when the first relay towers were new, three towns each built one: Vell, Kettle Ridge and Copperhold. They had agreed to build them within sight of each other, so a lamp at one could be seen at the next.

Each town wanted theirs to be best. At the first fair, the keepers had a contest. The prize was a bell.

The contest had three parts. First, which tower was tallest. Second, which lamp was brightest. Third, which keeper could sing the best song.

The towers were measured. They were all the same height, to the width of a thumb. That part was a tie.

The lamps were lit at dusk. Vell's lamp was white. Kettle Ridge's was yellow. Copperhold's was red, because the keeper had used a red glass from the mine. People said it looked like a fire. Some said it looked like an apple. The red lamp won.

Then came the song. The keeper at Vell sang about the archive. The keeper at Kettle Ridge sang about wheat. The keeper at Copperhold, a shy man called Rafe, said he did not sing. He had never sung in his life.

"Try," said the crowd.

Rafe stood up. He opened his mouth. He sang a very low note, and held it, and held it, and it went down into the ground like a stone down a well. The crowd went quiet. Then he sang a second note, higher, and it was like a bell. He sang a third, and a fourth. It was not a song. It was a staircase.

When he finished, nobody clapped for a moment. Then everybody did.

The bell was given to Copperhold. Rafe hung it on the tower. Every evening he rang it, three times, so the other towers would know the lamp was lit. Soon all the towers had bells, and that is how the relay lamps came to be rung as well as seen.

I tell it because it is a good story. My grandmother says it was Rafe who started the custom. My father says it was the keeper at Vell. My aunt says there was no bell and I am making it up. But I was told this by an old woman at the mine gate, who said she was there, and who is not the sort to make things up.
%%
Story: The House That Had No End
Briar's spring tale for the children

In spring, the council built a relay house on the dry ground beyond Lowmarsh. They built it so large that a person could walk from one end to the other and meet a different keeper at every turn.

The house held a big mind in a crystal. The crystal stood on a strong stand. The quickeners around it were kept cool with water and blocks of ice.

"Why make the house so large?" asked Moss.

"Because the mind needs room," said the keeper.

"Does the mind fill all the rooms?"

"No. The rooms are for its crystal, its keepers, its stores, and its work."

The mind had read a great many pages. It could answer through the relays, but no tutor could check every piece of it alone. So tutors made tests. They kept some pages out of the lessons, then gave those pages to the mind later. They wrote down which answers were good and which were not.

A child asked, "If the mind reads every page, will it know every story?"

"No," said Briar. "It will know the pages it has read. It will not know what nobody wrote."

The children thought about that. Then a frog hopped into the kitchen and stole a crumb.

"That frog knows where the bread is," said Moss.

"Because it can smell it," said Briar. "The big mind cannot smell my bread."

That afternoon, rain came through a gap in the house roof. The keepers brought a ladder, and Briar brought oak boards from the shed. They mended the roof together.

As they worked, a zombie came to the gate. It pushed against the gate, and the watchman called for help. The keepers carried torches out. The zombie turned away and went toward the reeds.

Nobody asked the mind to mend the roof or chase the zombie. The mind could answer through the relays. It could not climb a ladder or carry a torch.

That evening, a traveler told us the biggest relay house had a mind that knew every road. A keeper from another town said it knew every song. A third said it had answered a question before anyone had finished asking.

Briar shook her head. "Those are tales," she said. "The councils built a large house for a large mind. That is what I saw."

Then she cut the loaf. She gave the first piece to Moss, who had held the ladder, and the second to the keeper, who had carried the boards.

Story: the night Tilly came early
In the spring rain, on the night before the market, Ida went into labor in the house at the edge of Vell, the one with the blue door and the crooked chimney. Her husband Tove ran up the hill to the tutor house for the midwife. He ran so fast he forgot his boots, and had to go back down for them.

The midwife was Hazel, who had caught three babies in that street and never lost one. She came down the hill with her basket and a lamp, and she said to Tove, "Boots first. Then warm water. Then sit down and be useful."

The tutor house sent a helper with her, a helper of copper and iron. It came up the hill with two big kettles, one on each arm, and it did not spill a drop. It went into the kitchen and stood by the stove. Hazel said, "Stay there." It stayed.

Ida was not pleased. She was loud about it. She told Tove he was a fool, and the rain was a fool, and the baby was a fool too for choosing this night. Tove agreed with everything she said.

The baby did not want to come. Hazel sat on a stool and breathed slowly, and asked Ida to breathe slowly too. "You are not in a hurry," Hazel said. "The baby is not in a hurry either. We will wait for it together."

Around the middle of the night the grandmother came up with a bag of dried apples and a bad temper. She said the helper should go and sleep, because it had been standing by the stove for six hours. Hazel said, "Stay there." The grandmother looked at the helper. The helper looked at the stove. Nobody moved.

Near first light, with the rain going quiet on the roof, the baby came. A girl, with a red face and a great deal of hair. She opened her mouth and let out a sound like a fox in a barrel. Everybody laughed, and Ida laughed, and then she cried. Then she said the girl would be called Tilly, after her own grandmother, who had been a very patient woman.

Tove wanted the baby called Vell, after the archive. Ida said no. Hazel said the baby would decide. The baby was asleep, and did not say.

Tilly tells the story at naming feasts now. Her favorite part is the boots. Her second favorite is the helper, which stood by the stove for six hours without once asking to sit down, and which Hazel said was the best helper she ever had. Her third favorite part is the moment she was born, which she says she remembers very well, though nobody believes her.

Story: the box of pieces
Old Sarn kept the hamlet's well and its stories. He told this one in his last autumn.

"A mind came to our market once, in a trader's cart, and the trader said it could read. We did not believe her. So we set it a test.

"We wrote down every name in the hamlet on slips of paper and put them in a box. Then we drew the slips one at a time and held them up. The mind read them all.

"Then Rill, who is clever, wrote a word no one had ever written before. She put 'keeper' at the end of 'bee.' Beekeeper. The mind read it.

"She put 'keeper' at the end of 'well.' Wellkeeper. The mind read it.

"She put 'keeper' at the end of 'gate.' Gatekeeper. The mind read it.

"Then she wrote 'keep' by itself, and 'er' by itself, and the mind read those too. Rill said, 'It does not know the words. It knows the parts.'

"The trader said, 'That is how it reads. It cuts the words into common parts and knows the parts. It is like a mason who knows every stone and can build any wall.'

"Rill thought about that. Then she said, 'Then it does not know what a beekeeper is.'

"'No,' said the trader. 'It knows the word. Knowing the thing is your work, not the mind's.'

"We kept the box. It is in the school house now. The children draw slips from it and read them. Some of them know what a beekeeper is. Some of them are still learning that part."

Story: Clank and the frog
In spring the ore carts cross the stone bridge at Copperhold, one after another, all day.
One morning Clank came to the bridge with its cart of iron. In the very middle sat a frog. It was a small green frog, no bigger than a plum. It did not move.
Clank stopped. The cart behind Clank stopped, and the one behind that, until there was a line of carts all the way to the mine gate.
"Go round," said Hesk.
But the bridge was narrow. "Wait," said Clank. "It is thinking."
"It is a frog," said Hesk.
"It is a frog that is thinking," said Clank.
So they waited. The line grew. Old Fenn sat down on a stone and took out his lunch.
After a long time the frog turned around, looked at Clank, looked at the carts, and gave one small croak, as if it had just remembered something. Then it hopped onto Clank's arm and rode across the bridge there, like a tiny green passenger.
On the far bank the frog jumped into the grass without a backward look.
Hesk wanted to ask what the frog had been thinking. He decided not to. Sometimes the right thing to do is wait for a frog.

Ledger: Pell's prices, spring of 204
Kept by Pell the merchant, on the road between Three Wells and Saltreach. Prices are what I paid, not what I asked.

Wool, clean, from Kettle Ridge: fair. Tam's flock is healthy. He says the lambs came early this year. Pip counted them twice and got two different numbers and then counted a third time and got the first number again. Tam says that is how you know it is right.

Beetroot, from the long field: dear. The frost came late. Tam lost a row.

Honey, from Ambrook: good and steady. Mother's hives are strong. Four jars at the inn, two at the market.

Copper, from Copperhold: steady. Hesk says the upper levels are good. He does not go below the seventh. He did not say why and I did not ask. Miners have their ways.

Glass, from Saltreach: dear this spring. Corin is building something long at the slip and he wants glass for it. He would not say what.

Bread, at the Halfway Inn: two loaves for a copper, and Sable's bread is better than the bakery at Three Wells, but do not tell the baker I said so.

Riddles at the board: free, and worth the price.

Note on the baby at the inn. They call her Ember. She is a month old and she has the whole inn wrapped around her finger. Sable holds her while she works and the travelers take turns. A man from Northpoint held her and said she was the warmest thing he had felt all winter. He came down from the snow and he was thin and quiet and he held the baby and smiled. I hope he is well. I did not get his name.

Story: the market bell
A tale told by Hollis, who was there

At the Three Wells fair, Dace hung a little bell by the notice board. It was to ring when the market opened.

The first morning, the bell rang before dawn.

The bakers came with hot loaves. The fish sellers came with their baskets. The folk came from the inns in their night caps.

But the stalls were still shut. The square was cold. The bell rope had caught on the cart of a sleepy carter named Hollis.

"I did not ring it," said Hollis.

The horse pulled the cart. The rope tugged the bell. The bell rang again.

Dace came out of the mayor's house. He took the rope down and tied it high on the post.

"There," he said. "Now the bell cannot wake the market before the market is ready."

The bakers went back to their ovens. The fish sellers went back to bed. The folk went home and warmed their feet.

Hollis set out for the road. His horse took one step, and the cart bumped the post.

The bell rang once more.

Dace looked at Hollis.

"I think the market is open now," Hollis said.

Dace shook his head, but he was smiling. By the time the sun came up, the bread was warm and the stalls were full.

After that, the bell rope was tied high, and Hollis parked on the other side of the square.

The fox and the festival hens
Mira told this one to her brother's children, long ago, in spring. It came from a farm outside Saltreach.
At the spring festival, the farm had forty hens in a pen and a fox at the fence.
The fox said, "I only want to watch the festival."
The farmer said, "Then watch from the hill."
The fox went to the hill. He watched. He saw lanterns and bread and music. It was the best festival he had ever seen, and he never ate a single hen.
Next year he came again. This time the farmer sent him a pie.
%%
The apprentice who lived on Word Street
In Saltreach there is a street that does not appear on any map. It is called Word Street, and it runs from the dye shop to the quay, between the bakery and the net loft. Its houses are all words. Each word has a door and a window, and each one lives beside the words it is used with.
On the first house on the left lives the word bread. Its neighbor is wheat, and then flour, and then oven, because a baker needs all four of them and likes to be able to call across. Across the way is fish, with salt, net, and boat at its doors, because the fishers say those four always come together.
At the far end, where the street meets the sea, is the house of storm. Its neighbors are wind, rain, and sea, and on a quiet day you can hear them all talking through the wall. Calm lives a little way off, two streets over, because calm is only ever said about the sea, and the sea does not like to be crowded.
The apprentice who first walked this street was called Pim. It was drilled on words for a whole winter, and it learned the street one house at a time. At first it went wrong often. It put wheat beside boat, because both are carried in the same cart. It put storm beside bread, because a storm can ruin a loaf. The tutor sent it back, and it walked the street again.
By spring it knew the houses. It could go down Word Street from the bakery to the sea with its eyes closed. When it was given a new word, a word like rudder, it did not have to be told where to put it. It put rudder beside boat, and beside the sea, and beside the tiller, because those are the words the fishers use with it.
The tutor asked Pim, one day, why the streets were laid out like that. Pim could not answer, because apprentices do not say such things. So the tutor answered for it. Words that are used alike are close together. That is all. A mind keeps a word as a place, and the places that are close are the ones that go together in the world.
Pim stood still by the quay and looked at the houses. It did not say anything, but it went down Word Street twice more that afternoon, to be sure.
%%
Story: Pim's pages for the fair
Pim dug wells at Three Wells, and he knew that a good bucket could still bring up muddy water. The rope might be sound. The bucket might be sound. But if the well was dirty, the water was dirty too.

At the winter fair, he heard a tutor say, "A mind is only as good as its pages."

Pim thought of his wells. He went to the tutor's table and offered to help sort the drill pages. The clerks had three piles. One was for pages to use. One was for spoiled pages. One was for the test box.

Pim found a page copied twice. It showed the same apple and pear, in the same order, with the same words. The clerk put one copy aside.

"Why not drill on both?" asked Pim.

"Then the mind sees one example twice," said the clerk. "That does not give it a new example."

Pim found another page with a mark smeared by water. The clerk put that aside too. A mind should not have to guess what the page meant.

The test pages were different. They were clear, but the clerks kept them shut in a box. No mind was ever drilled on those pages. When the mind was ready, the tutor used them to see whether it could sort things it had not learned by heart.

Pim helped all afternoon. At the end, the tutor tested a small mind. It sorted the new pages well, though one page showed a pear beside a beetroot, which had not been in its drills.

The fair prize was a round loaf. Pim won it for finding the most spoiled pages. He shared it with the clerks and the children watching.

"That is a better prize than a bucket of muddy water," he said.

Then he carried the empty basket home. He had forgotten to bring his own bucket.
%%
Story: the barn that counted spiders
Told at the Copperhold market by Lorn, keeper of the relay house on the hill. Autumn. This one is for the children who want a long story, and who count the spiders on their fingers as it goes.

At the top of the hill, behind the mine gate, there was an old barn with a roof that leaked and a floor that was always dusty. It had more spiders than any barn in the north. The farmer had given up counting them, so a child did it instead.

The child was Dell. She was seven, and she had a slate and a stick of chalk, and she had decided that counting spiders was the most important job on the hill.

On the first day Dell counted forty-one spiders in the rafters. On the second day she counted forty-one again, but two had moved. On the third day she counted thirty-nine, and was very cross. Her mother asked what was the matter. Dell said the spiders were stealing themselves.

Her mother said that was impossible. Dell said she had seen one go up the rafter and come down again as a different spider. Her mother said that was the spider's business. Dell said it was hers too, because she was the one counting.

So Dell gave each spider a name. There was Sixty, who lived by the door. There was Crooked, who had one leg shorter than the rest. There was Pudding, a small grey one, and Mr Chalk, a big black one, who came out whenever the chalk did. With names the spiders were easier to count, and they did not steal themselves nearly so often.

When the roof began to leak in earnest, in Leaffall, the farmer climbed up to look. He said he would have to mend the roof and disturb them. Dell said no. The spiders had been there longer than the roof, she said, and she had named them, and a farmer should not disturb a named spider without asking its opinion.

The farmer laughed. Then he looked at the spiders, which were all watching him, and he laughed again. He mended the roof with the spiders in it. He cut the rotten rafters and put in new oak, and he left the webs in the corners, and he tied a string along the new beam for Crooked, who liked to walk and was getting old.

By Frost the roof was dry and the barn was warm. Dell still counts the spiders every morning. The count changes, because spiders are not careful about being counted. Dell says that is the sign of a good barn. Her mother says it is the sign of a good daughter. The farmer has stopped trying to count them himself.

The moral, Dell says, is to count what is yours, and name what you cannot count. The children always clap at that, and then they go home to count their own spiders, which are usually in the corner by the door.
%%
Story: the miner who bet on the vein
Told by Hesk, miner of Copperhold, to the children who wanted to hear about copper.

The new vein on the fifth level was thin. The count board said it ended at the bend. Hesk said the copper went on under the rock, the way water goes on under a field.

The apprentice on the cart was called Stub. It had been tested on carrying and on the lift, and it had never once said anything at all, which Hesk liked. Hesk said to it: I bet you a copper cart that this vein goes further than the board says.

Stub did not answer. It took a pick from the rack, went to the bend, and began to dig.

It dug for three days. On the fourth morning it broke into a seam of copper as broad as a barn door, bright and green at the edges, and the whole shift came down to look.

Hesk paid the cart. He wrote the seam on the count board himself, in chalk, a long way past the bend.

The miners say the moral: a bet is only a guess that someone is willing to dig for.

Story: The Bell by the Pond
Cade told this tale at the Ambrook mill.

The mill wheel turned from dawn to dusk. Its sound was a steady clack, clack, clack. One Apple month, the miller tied a little bell to the gate so visitors could call without shouting.

The first to ring it was a bee.

The bell gave a bright little sound. The bee flew away.

The next was Cade, carrying a sack of wool. He rang the bell once and waited. Nobody came. He rang it twice. Still nobody came.

Then a cow leaned against the gate. The bell rang and rang.

The miller came out with flour on her sleeves. "Who is calling?"

Cade pointed at the cow.

The cow looked at the pond, then at the grass, and began chewing.

"She has come to ask for flour," said Cade.

The miller shook her head. "She may have the flour that falls from my sleeve."

The cow got a mouthful of grass instead. She seemed content.

After that, the bell was used for visitors and for cows. Cade said it was the most useful bell in Ambrook. The miller said it was the noisiest cow.

Story: the song contest on the new road
A story from the early years, when the road between Vell and Three Wells was newly laid, and people walked it just to see how far it went.

There was a fair at the halfway point, where the road crossed a stream. No inn there yet. Only a field, a bridge and a stack of barrels. The people from Vell came down from the hill. The people from Three Wells came up from the crossing. They met in the middle and ate bread.

Someone said, "Let us have a song contest. Vell against Three Wells."

Vell sent its best singer, a tall young man named Odo, who had a voice like a warm loaf. Three Wells sent a short woman named Bree, who had sung in the market every day for twenty years, and could sing over the noise of forty stalls.

Odo sang first. It was a song about the archive, and it was long and sweet, and people sniffled.

Bree sang second. It was a song about bread, and it was short and loud, and people clapped before it was over.

Who won? The judges could not decide. The judges were the baker from Three Wells and the clerk from Vell, and they had both had a lot of cider.

So the contest was declared a tie, and the two singers were sent to sing a duet. They had never sung together. They stood side by side on the bridge. Odo started low, Bree started high, and neither would give way.

Then a bee landed on Odo's nose.

He stopped. Bree kept singing. A second bee landed on her sleeve. She stopped. The crowd went quiet. Then someone shouted, "Swarm!" and a cloud of bees came down the hill from the apple trees, and people ran in all directions.

The two singers climbed up on the barrels. They looked at each other. And Odo began to laugh, and Bree began to laugh, and they sang the rest of the song together, on the barrels, with the bees going by in a great humming cloud. The song was nothing like what they had planned. It was the best song of the day.

The bees went on to the orchard. The people came back from the ditches. And at the end they gave the honey to both singers, because no one could tell who had won.

They say the inn that stands there now, the Brown Hen, was built on the spot where the barrels stood. The innkeeper still has a jar of honey on the shelf, and says it is the prize. Nobody has opened it. It is very old.

That is why, in our family, we always sing the last verse together.

Story: The Cart at the Corner
Told by Una at Copperhold

A cart once got stuck in the snow by the Copperhold market. It was full of turnips, and the driver pulled at the handle.

The cart did not move.

A miner pushed at the back. The cart did not move.

A baker pushed beside him. The cart did not move.

Then a small pig came from behind the stalls. It put its nose under the cart and pushed.

The cart rolled out of the snow. The pig rolled after it and landed in a basket of turnips.

The driver gave the pig a turnip for its work. The pig ate the turnip and asked for another by standing very still beside the basket.

The driver gave it one more.

After that, the pig came to the market every winter. It never pushed a cart unless someone promised a turnip first. The people called it a careful worker.

Story: Hesk and the great house
A long tale, told at the Copperhold forge at the end of Frost, when the fire is low and the miners want to be told something they cannot check.

Hesk went to the far coast on the copper cart, with a sack of ore for the tutors and a bet still in his pocket. The bet was with Ola, about the quickeners, and Hesk had lost it before he left, but he did not like to be told so.

The house stood at the end of a long pier, over water that did not freeze. Its rings went round in great circles, one within another, and the cold came off them like breath off a horse in winter. A stair ran up beside it. Hesk did not climb it, because he was not sure he had been asked to.

A tutor came down the pier. She was old, and her coat was too big, and she carried a slate under one arm. She asked what he wanted to know.

Hesk did not want to ask anything. He wanted to see. He asked her if the house was as big as the rumors said.

She said the rumors were the right size and the wrong shape. The house had more layers than she could count in a day, and more weights than there were sacks in every mine on the Long Road. She checked a corner of it each morning, wrote what she found on the slate, and in the evening rubbed the slate clean and began again.

Hesk asked her if the house was clever.

She said it was not clever the way a miner is clever. It did not know where the copper was. It only knew where copper had been, in the words the tutors had given it, and it was very good at saying what was likely to come next. Sometimes that was the same as knowing. Sometimes it was not. She said the difference was the thing she was paid to find out.

Hesk asked whether the house ever wrote back to people like him.

She said it wrote back to the keepers, and to the tutors, and sometimes to the apprentices on the line, who could only answer with their eight meanings. It had once written a letter to a child at Three Wells, and the child had kept it under her pillow for a year.

Then she took him to the edge of the pier and showed him the water. It was dark and still, and the house's rings made no sound on it at all. She said that was the strangest thing about the house. Everybody on the line knew it was there, and nobody had ever heard it.

Hesk went home on the cart. He paid Ola the bet, and told the story at the forge. Some of the miners believed him. Ola did not. Ola says Hesk never reached the coast, and that the tutor was a man in a hat.

Hesk says the tutor was a woman with a slate, and that she was right about the copper, and that Ola can come and see the sack of ore for himself. It is still in the forge, and still not sweet.
%%
The net that caught a letter
Told by Ash, net mender, in winter, at the hamlet by the pond.

A fisher cast his net one dark morning and pulled up a letter, wrapped in wax cloth. The ink had run, but the seal was whole. I could read one word under the stain: Ambrook. So I sent it up to Vell with the next cart.

Oren wrote back that it was the first letter from the pond in forty years. He filed it under unknown, which is where it belongs, and asked the fisher to keep the net.

How the fox lost the festival
Told by Mira, the tower keeper at Northpoint, in spring, to the children who live on the farm outside Saltreach where she once stayed. It is a funny one.
At Saltreach they hold a festival every spring. There is a parade, with a drum and a goat. There is a pie contest. There is a boat race, which nobody wins, since all the boats are the same. And there is the hen show, at which the best hen gets a ribbon.
One year a fox came. He was a clever fox. He knew that on festival day, everyone looked at the hens, and nobody looked at the henhouse.
He crept in at the back. He found the hen with the ribbon, a plump brown one, and took her by the wing. She said, "Excuse me."
"Be quiet," said the fox.
"I cannot," said the hen. "I am the prize hen. I must be seen."
"Then be seen quietly."
The hen thought about it. She said, "Very well." She went with him.
They walked out of the henhouse, across the yard and along the fence. They walked past the boat race. They walked past the pie table. Nobody saw. They were all looking at the goat.
Then the hen stopped. She looked at the festival. She looked at the pies. She said, "I am hungry. May I have a pie first?"
The fox said, "I am hungry too."
So they went to the pie table, and the fox ate three pies, and the hen ate two. They sat in the shade under the table and ate. The fox told the hen about his life. The hen told the fox about hers.
At the end of the afternoon, the farmer's wife looked under the table and saw them. She said, "Hello."
"Hello," said the fox, with his mouth full.
"Hello," said the hen.
The farmer's wife went away and came back with a plate of bones. She put it under the table. She said, "Enjoy your festival."
They did. The fox went home in the evening. The hen went back to the henhouse, with her ribbon. She says it was the best day of her life.
The moral is that a festival is for everyone, even a fox, if he eats pie and not hen.

Story: the lamp that no one could read
Told by Hal on a winter night, to anyone who has not heard it, and to some who have.

Long ago, in the winter of the deep snow, the lamp on the relay tower above Kettle Ridge began to blink a message nobody could read.

It was not "all well". Everyone knew "all well". It was not "haste" or "wait". It was long blinks and short blinks in a strange order, every night after dusk, for about an hour. Then it stopped.

The ridge folk gathered on the road each night to watch. They wrapped up warm. They brought hot cider. The Kettle Inn did a good trade.

The keeper was old Odo, and he would not come down. He sent word by the boy who brought his bread. "The lamp is working. Do not worry." That worried everybody.

Some said it was a warning of snow. "We have snow," said my grandmother. Some said the lamp was broken. "A broken lamp does not do the same thing every night," said the miller. The children said it was a game. The innkeeper offered a free supper to anyone who could read it. Nobody could.

On the seventh night, a far lamp answered. It was the lamp on the next tower, half a day's walk across the snow. It blinked the same strange order back. Then both lamps blinked it together, across the white dark, like two people humming one tune.

The ridge went quiet.

In the morning a woman walked all the way across the snow to the foot of the tower and knocked on the door. She was Senna, the keeper of the next tower. Odo came down. He was very red.

"Yes," said Senna.

"Yes?" said Odo.

"I have been saying yes for two nights," said Senna. "Your lamp was slow to notice."

Then everyone understood. It was a code the two of them had made as children in the school yard at Vell, to talk across the yard when they were not allowed to. Odo had been asking her to marry him. He was too shy to ask in daylight, and too shy to ask in any way but by lamp, in the dark, where he thought only she could see.

"One thing," said Senna. "Your fourth word was wrong. It said, will you marry my cow."

Odo sat down in the snow. "It was one blink too long," he said. "It was meant to say me."

"I know," said Senna. "That is why I took so long to answer. I was trying to decide which cow."

They were married at the Kettle Inn at the end of Deepsnow. The innkeeper kept his word, and everyone ate for nothing. Odo's cow, Clover, came to the wedding with a ribbon on her horn.

And ever since, when a lamp blinks something strange in the dark, the ridge does not worry. They say, somebody is asking somebody something. Let us hope the answer is yes.
%%
Story: The Frog That Asked
Tale told by Moss

A frog once sat by the Lowmarsh relay house and asked every passerby, "What does the biggest mind know?"

A fisherman said, "It knows the price of fish."
A child said, "It knows where my red boot is."
A keeper said, "It has read many pages, but I cannot tell you what it knows."

The frog waited until spring rain filled the ditch. Then it asked the big mind through the relay, "Will the ditch flood?"

The answer came back, "I do not know. Ask Wren. She has watched this water longer than I have."

The frog was pleased. "At last, a wise answer."

Then it asked the mind, "Can you carry me to the dry bank?"

"No," said the keeper. "The mind is in a crystal in a room. It sends answers. It has no legs."

The frog hopped to the bank by itself. It told the other frogs that the big mind had asked Wren for help.

They agreed this was sensible. Wren had a bucket, and the big mind did not.
%%
Story: how the minds learned to try
A story told to the children of Saltreach, as the grandmothers told it. It explains how some minds were made to learn, in the old days, when the tutors were still trying things out. Written down by Nell, in her own words, for the children who asked her why the minds on the boats did not come with a book.

Once the tutors had a problem. They could drill a mind to say the right answer to a question. A drill is a row of examples, given again and again, and the mind changes a little each time it is wrong. That works well for sorting apples, and for counting, and for saying the right word.

But how do you drill a mind to hold a furnace steady? Nobody knows the right way to hold a furnace. Nobody knows the right way to keep a boat on course in a storm either, not in every storm. The tutors did not have the answer to write on the cards.

So one tutor, the story says, decided to try something else. She did not give the mind the answer. She gave it a task, and let it try. When the furnace melt went well, she kept the moves that had made it go well. When the boat stayed up, she kept the moves that had kept it up. When a try went badly, she let those moves fade.

The mind did not know why it was keeping the good moves. It only kept them. But over many tries, the good moves gathered and the bad ones faded, until the mind could hold the melt, or the boat, or the frame, without any one telling it how.

That is trying. It is slow. A drilled mind learns its answers faster. But a tried mind sometimes learns things no one thought to teach. The tutors who did this wrote their notes very carefully, so that the next tutor could try again. They also tested the minds on new tasks, to see whether they had only learned the one thing they had been given. Most of them had.

My grandmother said the first tried mind was on a boat, and that it was not a very good sailor, but it did not drown. That is enough, she said. If the thing you make does not drown, you have done well.
%%
Story: The spider in the barn
Every summer the spiders come into Tam's barn at Kettle Ridge, and every summer Tam swears he will keep them out, and every summer he does not.

The spiders come for the flies and stay for the dark. They hang in the corners and over the door and along the beam where the harness hangs. In Blossom they are small. By Reaping they are as wide as a hand, and Pip will not go in the barn after dark.

"They do not hurt you," says Tam.

"They look at me," says Pip.

"They have eight eyes. They look at everything."

One night in Swarm a spider came down on its thread onto the tally board while Tam was writing on it. Tam watched it walk across the numbers. Then he lifted it gently on a straw and put it back in the corner.

"Why did you do that?" asked Pip.

"Because it was here before my numbers," said Tam.

Pip tells this story now to the younger apprentices, and says the lesson is that you should be kind to small things. Tam says the lesson is that a spider is a good reason to keep the barn swept. Both of them are right.

The lamp that went out and came back
A story Pell tells at the inn at Little Weir, near Three Wells, in summer. He says it is a children's story, but the grown folk like it better. It is about a relay lamp.

At the top of a hill stood a tower, and at the top of the tower there was a lamp. The lamp was called Glimmer. It blinked every night, to say hello to the next tower, which was far away.

One summer night, a bird flew into the lamp and broke a pane. Glimmer went out.

It was very dark. The tower was silent. The next tower, far away, waited for the blink and heard nothing.

Glimmer said: I am broken.

The tower said: you are not broken. You are only dark.

Glimmer said: what is the difference?

The tower said: a broken thing stays broken. A dark thing can be lit again.

In the morning, the keeper climbed the tower with a new pane. She set it in the frame. She wiped the glass. She lit the wick.

Glimmer blinked. The next tower blinked back. The keeper went down for her breakfast.

And that is why, when a lamp goes out, you should never say it is broken. Say it is dark. And wait for morning.
%%
Story: a lesson at the farm school
Told by Laurel, who sat at the back, in a cold week of Leaffall, in a farm schoolroom outside Northpoint. Laurel was a fisher and not a student, but the tutor was a friend and the stove was warm.

The tutor was named Fenwick. He had come up from Vell with a small mind in a crystal, a roll of paper and a great deal of confidence. The farm children sat on benches, and some of their parents stood at the back with their arms crossed.

"Today," said Fenwick, "we will see inside a mind."

He pinned the roll of paper to the wall. It was a drawing. It showed a crystal, with lines inside it, and each line was a different color.

"This line," said Fenwick, tapping it, "knows when a quote is open. This one knows the end of a line. This one, here, counts how many letters since the last full stop. And these," he waved his hand over the rest of the drawing, "these we are still working on."

A small girl put up her hand.

"Yes?"

"What do these ones do?"

"We do not know yet," said Fenwick.

"Then why did you draw them?"

"Because they are there," said Fenwick.

"But if you do not know what they do, how do you know they do anything?"

This was a hard question. Fenwick opened his mouth and shut it. He looked at the paper. He looked at the mind in the crystal, which sat on the bench and did nothing, as minds do.

"That," he said, "is a very good question."

A boy at the back said, "Maybe they do nothing."

"Maybe," said Fenwick.

"Maybe they do everything," said another.

"Also maybe," said Fenwick.

A farmer at the back said, in a loud voice, "I paid for this?"

The children laughed. Fenwick laughed too, because there was nothing else to do. He rolled up the paper.

"I will tell you what we really know," he said, in a smaller voice. "We know a few of the parts. We know them well, because we tested each one, over and over, for a year. We do not know the others. If I told you we did, I would be teaching you something false. And I would rather teach you a small true thing than a big false one."

The farmer at the back did not say anything. After a while he uncrossed his arms.

When the lesson was done, Fenwick packed up his things and walked to the door. The small girl followed him.

"Will you come back?" she said.

"If someone will have me," said Fenwick.

"We'll have you," said the girl. "But next time, bring the rest of the drawing."

"I will bring what I have," said Fenwick, "and a lot of chalk."

He came back in spring. The farmer who had said "I paid for this?" was the first to bring him a cup of tea.

That is the story. A lesson can go badly and still go well, if the teacher tells the truth in the middle of it. I have thought about that on the boat, many times, with the nets out and nothing to do but think.
%%
Legend: the tutor who came in the fog
A legend of the coast, told at Saltreach by the old fishers. Some say it is a true story from the early years of the archive. Some say it is a warning. Some say it is only a story to keep the children off the slip in fog. Autumn.

In the early years, a tutor came down from Vell to the coast, carrying a box of crystals and a map. She was a clever woman, but she was not a sailor, and when the fog came in over the bay, she was lost on the quay.

The fishers found her sitting on a bollard, with the map on her knees and the crystal box in her arms. She said she had come to see the boats. She said she wanted to know what a mind would learn if it was set to the sea with no drill at all.

The fishers said: then you will drown it, and yourself as well.

The tutor laughed, and said: it is only trying. It will learn the way a fish learns the net, by swimming into it, and getting out again.

So the fishers took her out, in the fog, in an old boat, with a small mind on the stern. And as the fog thickened, the mind began to try. It did not know the bay. It did not know the rocks. But when the boat went too close to the point, it felt the swell change, and it steered away. When the boat drifted too far out, it felt the wind come round, and it turned the boat back toward the bell.

The fishers saw it, and they were afraid. They said the mind was guessing. The tutor said it was not guessing, it was keeping the moves that had worked, and dropping the ones that had not, the way a child keeps the songs that please it.

They came home in the morning, with the fog lifting and the sun on the water. The tutor was still holding the map. The mind was still holding the tiller, though it had no hands.

The fishers say she went back to Vell and wrote a book about it. The book is still in the Archive, under Boats, and it says that the mind did not learn the coast. It learned the way home.

The old fishers do not agree with the book. They say the mind did learn the coast, and that the coast is still there, and that the children should stay off the slip in fog.
%%
Story: the bridge that went to the fair
Rook tells this story with a straight face. He is the only one who does.
One spring it rained for a week. The stream below Copperhold rose and rose. On the eighth night it took the little oak footbridge that the children use to cross to the school. It lifted the bridge off its posts and carried it away, whole and level, as if it were a tray.
Rook saw it go. He ran along the bank in his nightshirt, shouting, "Come back!" The bridge did not come back.
He followed it for three days. He borrowed a horse at the ford. He borrowed a rope at the weir. He borrowed a boat at the reed bank, which did not belong to the person he thought. The bridge floated on, down the stream and into the river, past the Deep Works, past the hills, and at last into the marsh at Lowmarsh.
At Lowmarsh it was the day of the spring fair. The fishers had laid out their stalls along the long boardwalk. In the middle of the market there was a wide channel that nobody could cross without a boat.
Into the channel, with a bump, floated an oak footbridge. It stopped, crosswise, exactly from bank to bank.
Everyone stared. A child walked across. Then another. Then the whole fair crossed, one after another, and bought fish on the other side.
The judges of the fair had a prize that year for the Best Crossing. It was a blue ribbon. They could not give it to a boat, or a ferry, or a plank. They gave it to the bridge. They tied it on the rail.
Rook arrived at noon, wet to the knees. Behind him came a borrowed horse, and a borrowed rope round his shoulders, and a borrowed boat on a borrowed cart. He saw his bridge, wearing a ribbon, with the whole fair walking over it.
"That is my bridge," said Rook.
"It has won a prize," said the council of Lowmarsh. "It is not for sale."
"I do not want to sell it. I want it back."
The council said it fit the channel perfectly. They said the market had never been so busy. They asked if Copperhold might build another.
Rook thought for a long time. It was a long way to carry a bridge. And it was a very good fit.
So Copperhold built a new footbridge from the same oak, and Lowmarsh sent a cartload of reeds for the school roof in return. The two bridges are cousins. Every spring a boatload of Lowmarsh children comes to cross the new one. Every spring a cartload of Copperhold children goes to cross the old. And on the rail of the old bridge at Lowmarsh is a ribbon, a little faded now, which says: Best Crossing.
Rook says the moral is to tie your bridge down. The children say the moral is to enter everything in the fair.
%%
Story: The bee and the beetroot

Tam told this story to Pip while they mended the hedge.

A bee lived by the flowers near the three hives. She was small, but she knew every flower on Kettle Ridge.

One spring day, she found a beetroot flower beside the long field. She flew around it twice.

"You are not a flower," she said. "You are a beetroot."

The beetroot did not answer. It was growing under the earth.

The bee flew home and told the other bees. "There is a beetroot in the field pretending to be a flower."

The bees came to look. They flew over the leaves and searched the soil. They found no flower and no beetroot they could carry.

A chicken came by. "Why are you all looking at the ground?" she asked.

"We seek a beetroot flower," said the bee.

The chicken scratched the earth. Out came a beetroot, muddy and round.

The bees buzzed around it. "That is not a flower," said the first bee.

"No," said the chicken. "But it is what you were looking for."

The bee thought about this. Then she flew to the flowers and brought back a little pollen for the chicken.

The chicken pecked at it, made a face, and went to look for a worm.

Tam says the bee and the chicken still share the hedge. They do not agree about flowers, but they agree about spring.
%%
Story: the storm and the emerald
The storm came down off the ridge at midwinter, the way storms do when they have been saving up. It took the shutters off the north side of the house, and the weather vane off the barn, and it left a single green emerald on the kitchen step, which nobody could explain.

Pim was at the table when it hit. He was drawing the road to Vell, the one that goes past the mill pond, and the wind came through the wall and blew his ink across the paper. The road ran off the edge of the map into the margin. Pim said a word that his mother did not like.

"Boys, to the barn!" called his mother, with the lantern in her hand. The boys went. The cow was already in the barn, complaining about it. The hens went under the stairs, and would not come out, and had to be talked to.

The horse was stamping in its stall, and Pim went out into the snow with a rope round his waist. He tied the halter to the post, and he brought the hens in one at a time, under his coat, which made them quiet. Then he sat in the straw with the horse's head in his lap, and the storm roared over the roof, and he sang the old fishing song until it stopped.

In the morning the snow was up to the window, and the sun was very bright, and the whole farm was white and silent. On the kitchen step, under a drift, Pim found the emerald. It was cold and clean and as green as a pond in summer. There was no note with it. There was no footprint on the step, only the drift, which had been smoothed flat as if somebody had patted it.

Nobody in the house knew where the stone had come from. The children wanted it to be a gift from the storm. Pim's mother said it was a gift from the road. Pim put it on the windowsill, and it sat there all winter, catching the light.
%%
The fox who borrowed a horse
A legend of the Long Road, as told at the Brown Hen on a night of snow and wolves howling far off. Pell has set it down as he heard it from a drover.

In the old days a fox lived by the Long Road, and he was a clever fox, and a lazy one. When the cold came, he did not wish to run through the snow. He looked about for a way to travel.

One morning he saw a horse tied outside an inn. The horse was big and brown and kind, and its owner was inside having breakfast.

The fox walked up and said: good morning, horse.

The horse said: good morning, fox.

The fox said: I am a traveler, and I am going to Saltreach. It is a long road. I have a bad foot. May I borrow your back?

The horse thought about this for a while. Horses do. Then it said: you may borrow it, but you must tell me why.

The fox said: because I am cold.

The horse said: that is a fair reason. Climb on.

So the fox climbed up, and sat between the horse's ears, and the horse walked down the road. The owner came out of the inn and saw nothing there but a lump of snow where his horse had been.

The horse walked on. The fox pointed the way. By afternoon they had reached the first waymark, and the fox hopped down and said: thank you, horse, I am not going to Saltreach after all. I have found a warm barn.

The horse said: you have used my back for a day, and you are not going to Saltreach.

The fox said: no, but the barn is very nice.

The horse looked at him, and sighed, and said: then you owe me.

The fox said: what do I owe?

The horse said: a story.

So the fox told the horse a story, the best he knew, and it took until dusk. It was about a mouse who became a king and then went back to being a mouse because it was more comfortable. The horse laughed so much it forgot to be cross.

Then the horse walked home alone, and was back at the inn before the owner finished his second cup. He said nothing. It is the only time a horse has been out all day and never been missed.

That is why, if you ever hear a fox on a winter road, you should listen. He may be telling a story, and he may be about to ask for a lift. Both are possible.

Story: the well that ran dry
Told by Emmet, miller, in winter, to children from the tower.

Once there was a well by the Northpoint farm, and it ran dry in the middle of winter. The children cried, and the cows cried, and the farmer said nothing and dug.

The farm's apprentice, Ved, sent a question up the relay line. Can the well come back?

The question went to the big house at the foot of the tower. It thought for a long time, and then it answered down the line.

Look where the snow has not fallen. The water is there, under the stone, and it is coming back slowly.

They dug where the snow had not fallen. The water came back by the third day, slow and cold and clean. The cows drank. The farmer said it was the best answer he had ever had from a house.

The moral: a well is not lost while the snow still knows where it is.
%%
Story: the net that caught nothing
Tilly the miner, who grew up in a hamlet near Saltreach, tells this story. She says the sea is full of tests.

A net-maker called Solly made a new kind of net, with a wide mesh and a fine twist. She tried it in the harbor, where the water is calm and the fish are small. It caught thirty fish.

"A wonder," said everyone. "Solly's net catches thirty fish."

Solly was pleased. But she was also careful. She said, "I have only tried it in the harbor."

So she took it out beyond the pier, to the open sea. The water was rough. The fish were big. The net filled, and then it tore from end to end.

She came home with a rag and a lesson.

"I did not know what it would do," she said. "The harbor told me nothing about the sea."

The next net was stronger. She tried it first in the harbor, then at the mouth, then beyond the pier. She did not call it a good net until it had been in all three.

The fishers of the hamlet still buy their nets from Solly's daughters. They say, "A net that has only seen the harbor has not been tried."

Tilly says the tutors would agree. A mind that has only been tried on its own drill is a net in the harbor. Put it where it has never been before you decide what it is worth.
%%
The long winter, told by the fire
Told by Ash, net mender, to the whole hamlet on the last night of Deepsnow, when the snow was up to the sills and the fire was the only warm thing in the room.

The winter began early, with snow on the first day of Frost, and it did not stop. The roads closed. The carts stopped at Three Wells and did not come on. The children made a game of counting the days until the first cart, and by the fortieth day they had stopped counting, because nobody believed the number any more.

We had enough. The bakers at Three Wells sent flour by the boy on the ridge road, one sack at a time, and the boy came every third day on foot, with his boots tied in cloth. The mill at Ambrook ground for us through the frost. Jory sent a message down the relay: the wheel is turning, the sluice is shut, and the apples in the cellar will last until Sowing if nobody touches them.

The apprentices kept working. Every morning they went out to the fields and checked the drifts, and every evening they came back and stood by the stove, dripping, and did not say anything, because they do not. They were given the order keep the fields until we come back, and they kept them. Some of the older folk say that is a hard thing to be told in a snowstorm. The apprentices seemed to find it easy.

On the thirtieth day, the postman's bag came down the ridge road in the boy's arms, with snow still on it. It had been lost in a drift at the crossroads, and found when the drift melted a little at the edge. Inside was a letter for my aunt at Saltreach, two winters old, with the ink run and the paper curled. Nobody could read it. We held it up to the stove, and the words came back in the heat, one at a time, like coals coming up.

It said: the boats are fine, the new vein at Copperhold is good, and the children have learned to swim in the harbor, which is not the same as being safe. Please do not tell the mayor about the swimming.

My aunt read it aloud, and then she laughed until she cried, and then she said she did not know what the children had been doing for two winters, but she was glad they were still doing it.

On the last night of Deepsnow, we sat by the fire and told the old stories, and the apprentices stood at the back and listened. They do not tell stories. They are not made for it. But they know the orders, and the orders say stay, so they stayed, and the fire was warm, and the snow outside was up to the sills.

When the first cart came over the ridge at dawn, the children ran out in their boots, shouting, and the apprentices went out with them to carry the sacks in. That was the end of the long winter. Nobody has counted the days since.
%%
Story: how the reeds learned to stand
Before the marsh had any boardwalks, the reeds at Lowmarsh were only grass, and they lay down in the water every time the river came up. They were soft and wet and no use to anyone. The people walked on them and sank.
A woman who cut the reeds for the roofs was tired of it. She asked the old frogs what could be done.
The frogs said the reeds needed to learn to stand. They said a reed is only grass that has forgotten its place. So they taught it. Every morning the frogs sang to the reeds at the edge of the water, and every evening the woman tied bundles of them and hung them on the posts to dry in the wind.
After a month the reeds were stiffer. After a year they stood up straight in the water, like soldiers at a gate. When the river came up, they bent and came back, and did not lie down.
The people made a bridge of the reeds, and then a boardwalk, and then the first boat house. The reeds held up the posts. The posts held up the houses. The houses held up the people, who sang to the frogs in the evenings, and said thank you.
Some say the reeds still remember how to lie down. You can see it in the storms, when the whole bed bends at once and then lifts again, all together. The old people say that is only the wind. The young people say it is the reeds remembering. The frogs do not say anything. They are busy with the evening song.

The wolf who came to the school play
A story told to the children at Three Wells in the spring, by Carys. The wolf, she says, is not a real wolf, but she likes to think so.
There was once a school play, and there was a wolf in it. The wolf was a girl called Pip, in a grey blanket, with two pointed ears made of felt. Her job was to creep across the back of the stage and look hungry. That was all.
On the night, a real wolf came to the school. It had heard the music and wandered in from the hill. It stood at the back of the hall, in the shadows, and watched the play.
The children did not see it. The teachers did not see it. But the apprentice at the door did. It did not make a sound. It did not move. It stood in front of the wolf, with its arms out, and the wolf looked at it.
They stood like that, the apprentice and the wolf, for the whole of the play. When the play ended and the lamps came up, the wolf was gone.
The teacher found tracks in the mud outside. She told the children it was a dog. The children said it was a wolf. The apprentice said nothing, as apprentices do.
Carys says there are things in the world that are watching, and not all of them are wolves. She says it is good to have an apprentice at the door.
Then she gives the children a piece of cheese, and the story is over.

Why the copper is in the hill
Told by Ash at the hearth, to the children who want to know why the mine is where it is. The old people say this is the true reason. The children say it is not.

Long ago the hill above the pond was all grass. A fox lived in the grass. It was a thin, clever fox, and always hungry.

One winter the fox chased a rabbit down a hole in the hill. The rabbit got away, and the fox dug and dug until it reached stone that was cold, hard and bright. It licked its scratched paw, which was green, and went home without the rabbit. The children say that was the first sensible thing the fox ever did.

The next spring the miners from Copperhold came up the hill, and they found a green vein in the stone where the fox had sat. They followed it down, and it went on and on.

The old people say the fox is still in there somewhere, waiting for the rabbit to come back. Nobody has ever seen the fox, and the copper keeps coming up, so nobody has the heart to argue.

Story: Pip and the muddy turnip

Tam told this story at the Kettle Inn.

A turnip grew at the edge of the bottom field. It was round and white, and Pip said it looked like a little moon.

One spring morning, Tam asked Pip to pull it up. Pip pulled once. Nothing happened. Pip pulled again. The turnip did not move.

"Ask the potatoes to help," said Tam.

Pip called two potatoes over from the basket. They could not pull. So Pip asked a carrot. The carrot was long and strong, but the turnip stayed in the ground.

Then Tam came to help. He took hold of Pip. Pip took hold of the carrot. The carrot took hold of the potatoes. They pulled together.

The turnip came up at once. Everyone fell backward into the ditch.

Tam held up the turnip. "A fine turnip," he said.

Pip looked at the muddy row of helpers. "A fine team," Pip said.

Tam says the turnip made a good soup. Pip says the ditch was the best part.

Story: Lorn's mended roof
Told at the Copperhold market by Lorn, keeper of the relay house on the hill. Autumn. The story is about the tower's own roof, which the keeper says is true, and which the children say is a story.

The relay house roof is copper, like the mine hall. In the spring the wind came from the north and tore a corner off it, and the rain came in through the gap onto the stand where the big mind sits. The quickeners were wet. The keeper was cross. I was the keeper, and I was not best pleased.

I wrote to the smith at Vell for new copper sheets, and to the carpenter at Ambrook for nails and a ladder, and to the mayor for permission to climb. The mayor said yes, if I tied myself on, and I said I would, and I did.

The mending took five days. The first day I found that the rafters under the copper were rotten at one end, and I had to cut them out and set new oak in their place. The second day the sheets came up the hill on a cart, and the cart stuck in the ruts, and four men and a horse got it out. The third day the rain held off, which is the only thing I can say for the weather that week.

The fourth day I put the new copper on, and it shone. The fifth day the keeper of the tower to the north sent a message by lamp: "The patch is bright. Our roof is old. Please mend ours next." I replied, "Yours is green. Ours is only new." The north keeper's reply was short, and I will not write it here, because the children are listening.

The new copper goes green in time. Every copper roof does. I have stopped minding. The quickeners stay dry now, the big mind is cool, and the lamp is lit each night. A mended roof is not a perfect roof. It is only a dry one, and a dry roof is what a keeper asks for.

Story: the race to the long pier
Every spring at Saltreach there is a boat race, from the harbor mouth to the long pier. The prize is a blue ribbon and the right to ring the pier bell.
Other years twelve boats raced. This spring there were six. Many of the rowers were busy building big ships. They had sent their children to row instead.
The Good Morning was rowed by Tova, who is eleven, and her two cousins. They did not know the race. They pulled very hard and took the wrong turn at the first buoy. The other five boats saw the Good Morning turn, thought Tova knew something, and turned too.
All six boats went round the wrong buoy, the long way, past the rocks.
Corin was the judge. He stood on the pier with a stick and a very straight face. "The race is to the pier," he said. "Nobody said which buoy." The Good Morning came in first and the others came in after. Everybody laughed so hard the oars stopped. Even the trader with the crooked scale cheered, and he said the buoys were crooked too.
Tova rang the bell. It rang across the whole harbor, loud and clear.
The same bell rings when a big ship leaves the slip. It sounds exactly the same. Marit says that is how a bell should be.

Story: the Halfway Inn riddle board
The Halfway Inn stands where the road bends by the old oak. It has twelve beds and a hearth that has not gone out in twenty years. By the hearth hangs a board of riddles. Travelers write one up and others try to answer. Pell the merchant keeps the board, and he writes down the best ones.

Here is one from the board, in a child's careful letters.

"What has a door but no house, a roof but no room, and a keeper who never sleeps?"

The answer, written underneath in a different hand: "A relay tower."

Pell says that one was up for a month. A woman from Kettle Ridge answered it on her way to market. She said the tower has a door at the foot and a lamp room for a roof, and the keeper is always awake. Pell gave her a cup of honey drink for it.

Here is another, this one from a traveler going north.

"I am small and I am bright. I am the first thing you light. I am not the sun. What am I?"

" A torch," wrote a child from Three Wells. Underneath, someone added, "Or a lamp. It is not fair. Both fit." Pell crossed out the lamp answer and wrote, "A torch. Read it again." The child wrote back, "I did read it again. A lamp is small and bright too." Pell let it stand. He says a good riddle should have one answer, but a fair argument is worth a cup too.

The board fills up in spring. Folk come out of their houses after the long nights and want to talk. They bring riddles the way they bring seed: from last year, saved up. The innkeeper, whose name is Sable, says the board is why the inn has twelve beds and not six. People stay to hear the answer.

Story: the cow that would not go
Brin of Kettle Ridge brought his cow Plum to the Vell market to sell. At the gate, Plum lay down and would not get up.

Brin pushed. Plum did not move. A crowd came. Opal the smith said the cow was sick, and offered to shoe her, which would not have helped.

Then a small child pushed through. "Is she hot?" said the child.

It was the middle of Melon, and the sun was on the stones. Somebody brought a bucket of water. Plum drank it all. Somebody brought a second. Plum drank half, and stood up, and walked slowly into the shade of the smith's shed, and lay down again.

"She is not sick," said the child. "She is Melon-hot."

Brin did not sell her that day. Plum stayed in the shed until evening, with a bucket and a fan of reeds.
%%
Story: first snow, and the far house
This is the story Mira tells the children when the first snow comes to Northpoint, which is always early. She tells it every year, and it is never the same twice.

Once, when the lamps were still new on the towers, the people of the south wanted a mind that could answer every question. So they built a relay house bigger than a town, with a room for every mind, and a tutor for every room.

But the minds grew. They grew bigger than any tutor could check alone. A tutor could check a row of minds in a morning. A house of minds could not be checked in a hundred years. So the councils gathered all the tutors they could find, and set them to check the house together, one piece at a time, like a long crew working on one wall.

That is the true part. Everything after is what people say.

People say the house can answer any question, if you ask it in plain words. People say it has a favourite season, which is autumn, and a favourite food, which is pears, and that it is not allowed to have either.

The children at the market asked if the house could see them.

"It cannot see anything," said Mira. "It only reads what comes down the relay, one letter at a time, the way a reader reads a book."

"Then how does it know about us?"

"By what we send," said Mira. "And by what we forget to send."

The children thought about this for a while. Then one of them asked what would happen if the house stopped answering.

Mira said nothing for a long moment. Then she said, "The lamps would still be lit, and the messages would still go up the stair. And somebody would sit at the desk and wait, the way people do."

Outside, the first snow began to fall, straight and slow, on the hill above the tower. The children ran out to catch it on their tongues. Mira watched them from the step.

"Some say the house is kind," she said, to nobody in particular. "Some say it is clever. Some say it is only very large. I think it is all of those, and none of them is the house's fault. The house is a house. It is the people who fill it who worry me."

Then she went in and put the kettle on, and the snow kept coming.

The song contest at the shaft head
Every Apple, when the copper was counted, the miners of Copperhold held a song contest at the shaft head. The best singer won a pie.

This Apple the judge was Hesk, who had never once liked a song. "Sing it loud," he said. "Sing it wrong, if you like. Just sing it."

Tobin sang too long, and Maud sang too short, and Hesk said so both times.

Lil was nine, and she had practiced all week. She sang about the frogs of Lowmarsh, and she did every verse with the right voices. When she came to the croak, she croaked so loud that the lift stopped.

Hesk said nothing for a long while. Then he said, "Second."

Lil went red. Then she put down her hat, and she ran.

Clank sang last, because the rules said anything could sing. It stood up straight and sang, in its copper voice: One order, one meaning, and then the next.

Hesk said it was the best thing he had heard all day. Clank won the pie. It set the pie on the bench, and then it looked at the gate where Lil had gone, and it did not move.

Story: the fence on Wax Lane
Told to Oren by a woman who grew up on Wax Lane, at Vell, and written down in winter.

On Wax Lane there were two houses with one fence between them. In one house lived Lyle the candle maker, who was tidy. In the other lived old Mave, who kept goats, and was not.

Every morning the fence was in the wrong place. Some mornings it was a hand over on Lyle's side. Some mornings it was a hand over on Mave's.

Lyle said Mave had moved it. Mave said Lyle had moved it. Each of them moved it back every evening, with a mallet, and said a few words to it. Every morning it had moved again.

Lyle wrote a letter. Dear Mave, your fence has come onto my side. Please keep it on yours. Mave wrote back. Dear Lyle, it is your fence, and it is on my side, and I have the mud to show it.

For a whole autumn they did not speak in the lane. They spoke only through the fence, in letters, and the letters got longer.

Then the first snow came. In the morning a child named Sim, age seven, who lived at the end of the lane, came out to look at it. Sim looked at the fence for a long time. She looked at the snow. Then she knocked on both doors.

"There are footprints," said Sim.

"Of course there are," said Lyle. "Yours."

"No," said Sim. "Small ones. With two toes."

Everyone came out and looked. In the snow were small hoof prints. They went from Mave's goat yard to the fence. There they stopped, and there was a smooth place in the snow where something had leaned. Then they went round the end of the fence, across Lyle's yard, and stopped again, at the other side, at another smooth place.

"Goats cannot move fences," said Mave.

"Thistle can," said Sim.

That night Lyle and Mave sat up together by the fence, in one blanket, because there was only one. They whispered. They shared a cup of hot cider. At midnight, Thistle the goat came out of the dark, walked up to the fence, and leaned on it, with her eyes shut, and rubbed her back along the top rail. The fence moved a hand. Then she walked round to the other side and leaned on it again, and the fence moved back.

Lyle began to laugh. Mave began to laugh. They laughed so much that Thistle looked at them and left.

In the morning they built a scratching post for her, out of a good oak post, with a bell at the top. It stands on Wax Lane still. It is the most worn thing in Vell.

The fence has not moved since. Lyle and Mave were friends for the rest of their lives. They kept the letters, all forty-one, in a box, and the Archive has the box. The last letter is signed by both of them, and a hoof print.

A fence is only a promise that two neighbors keep. If it will not stay where you put it, check the goat first.
%%
Letter: Fallon to the archive
To the clerks at Vell, from Fallon at the hamlet, autumn.

I have sent you my ledger for the year. I hope it is tidy enough. I know how you are.

One thing for your shelf. I have carried letters for the relay houses for thirty years, and I have seen the big minds learn to read words in pieces instead of letters. Now the young traders take it for granted. They say "the pieces" as if the pieces were always there.

They were not. I remember a mind that took a whole breath to get through the word "beekeeper." Now it reads it and is on to the next word before I have put my cup down.

Nothing else to report. The apples were good. The bridge has a loose plank and someone should tell Jory.

Tell Oren I still have his scarf. I will bring it in the spring.

Story: the baker's mind that forgot
In a bakery at Three Wells there was a little mind in a crystal. A tutor from Vell had written it small, because the baker could not afford a bigger one. It was very good at bread.
But it forgot things. It forgot the streets, and the names of the children who came for buns. It forgot where the good spoon was kept.

"What do you remember, then?" the baker asked it one autumn morning, when he was tired of looking for the spoon.

"Bread," said the little mind. "Bread is hot. Bread is for the inn."

Then he said, "That is most of what I need." He kept the mind, and bought a second spoon.
%%
Story: the high fence
When I was a young guard, there were two farms outside Three Wells with one fence between them. Osk had the east farm. Tove had the west farm. The fence was plain oak, posts and rails, as high as a sheep. For forty years it was only a fence.

Then there was an apple tree. It grew on Tove's side, and one big branch hung over the fence. In autumn the apples fell on Osk's side. Osk ate them. Tove said they were his. Osk said apples that fall on my ground are mine. Tove said the branch was his. Osk said ground is ground.

That was all it took. Tove added a rail to the fence. Osk added one. Tove added another. In ten years the fence was as high as a house. They could not see each other. They had not spoken since the third rail. The apples fell on nobody, and the foxes ate them.

One spring Osk's best cow fell sick. She would not eat. A healer came all the way from Lowmarsh, three days on the road, and looked at the cow, and said: "She needs warm mash with honey in it, every four hours, all night. Somebody must sit with her." Then the healer went home, because there were other sick cows. Osk was alone. He sat up with the cow, and at midnight he fell asleep with the bucket going cold.

I was on the wall that night, doing my round. I saw a lantern at the high fence. It was Tove. He had pulled one rail out of the fence, and he was passing a steaming bucket through the gap to the cow. She was eating from it. Tove saw me on the wall.

"Do not say," said Tove.

I said nothing.

In the morning Osk woke by the cow. She was eating. The bucket was warm. The fence had a gap. He did not understand. He came to the wall, and I said: "I only know that someone fed her."

Osk went and stood at the gap. "Tove," he said. On the other side Tove said, "Osk." Neither of them said anything else for a long time. Then Osk said, "The apples were yours." Tove said, "Keep them. The cow likes them."

They took the fence down that spring, a rail a day. In its place they built a gate, low, no higher than a sheep. It is still there. The branch hangs over both sides. In autumn the apples fall wherever they like.

I have kept many gates in my time. That is the best one.
%%
The homecoming of Wick
A tale told by Cleo, candle maker at Northpoint, in the long nights of winter. It is a sad one at first, but it ends well. It is about an apprentice that was lost, and how it came home.
My apprentice Wick, the one I just told you of, got lost last winter. I sent it out for the snow wax, a pale wax that the foxes dig for under the roots. I said, "Fetch snow wax."
It went. It did not come back.
At first I was not worried. It is a plain-minded creature. It goes where it is sent and returns. Always. But the snow came that night, and the wind rose, and I sat at the window and watched the lamp on the tower turn.
In the morning there was a track in the snow. It led from my door to the wood. I followed it. The track led into the trees, over a stream, up a hill and down. It went on for a long time. After an hour I lost it.
I asked at the tower. Mira said she had not seen it. She said she would look. She went to the lamp room and swept the hills with the glass. Nothing.
I asked at the market. Nobody had seen an apprentice. A woman said she had seen a track. A man said he had seen a fox.
For three days I looked. For three nights I sat in the shop with a candle in the window. I had no one to carry the wax. I had no one to sweep. I had no one to stand in the corner and be quiet. I realized I had grown used to it.
On the fourth morning I heard a sound at the door. Clink. Clink. Clink.
I opened it. Wick stood there. It was covered in snow. Its arms were full. It was carrying a great lump of pale wax, the best I ever saw, as big as a loaf.
It had been to the end of the world and back. It had done what I asked. It had fetched snow wax. It had found it the only way it knew how: by walking until it did.
I took the wax. I took Wick by the arm and led it to the stove. I brushed the snow off it. It stood and steamed.
I said, "You are home."
It did not answer. It cannot. But it stayed by the stove all night, and in the morning it was exactly where I put it.
I made the wax into candles. There were forty. They were the best candles I ever made. I gave one to every house in Northpoint. Mira's burned on the sill of the lamp room until spring.
They say an apprentice does not feel. They say it does not know where it is going. They say it is only a mind in a crystal, doing what it was drilled to do.
Perhaps so. But I know that when it came in at that door, I was glad. I do not know about the apprentice. I only know about me.
%%
The flood, the cat and the prize
Told by Bel, a child at Three Wells, on a winter night at the house at the edge of town. It is a true story, she says, except for the part with the boat. Her mother says there was no boat. Bel says there was.
In the autumn the river rose, as it does every few years, and went into the cellars. The baker's cellar was full. The inn's cellar was full. Our cellar was full. Mother had to carry the potatoes up the stairs in a bucket, and she was not pleased.
But I was pleased. I had a plan. I made a boat from a wash tub and a plank and a broom handle. I put the cat in it. The cat did not want to go.
I pushed the tub out of the door and into the lane. It floated. It went down the lane like a ship. The cat sat in the middle, very upright, looking offended. A crowd came to watch.
The tub went down to the square, past the three wells, and stopped by the school. It got caught on a post. The cat stepped out onto the school steps, shook her paws and went inside.
The whole town laughed. The mayor came out. She said it was the finest boat she had seen. She gave me a ribbon. It was not the fair prize, since the fair was not held that year. It was a ribbon from her own hat.
I have it still. It is red. The cat has not forgiven me.
My mother says the story should end there. But there is more. At the end of the week the flood went down, and the fair was held after all. The prize was a jar of honey. I did not win it. A girl with a very fat melon did. But I rang the bell, because the mayor said a boat captain should ring the bell.
It was the best day of the winter.
Mother says that I must also tell the part about the sixty letters. She says it is the reason I was told the story in the first place. So here it is.
My uncle came to the fair. He works at the tutor house at Vell. He said that in the newest work they are drilling minds to read one letter at a time, through a window sixty letters wide. He showed me with a card with a slot. Each letter, he said, takes from the letters before it the ones that matter. Then it guesses the next.
I asked him if a mind like that could write a letter that you could not tell from a person's. He said, "Some can. Orla asked that question long ago. It has come back."
I asked him what the answer was. He said nobody knows.
I put the card in my pocket. It is still there. I use it when I read.
