The old map in the bakery drawer
Told by Runa, and written down by her for the school, which asked for it. The map was found in the spring the bakery roof was mended.

Nobody had looked in that drawer for thirty years. The map was under the bread scrapers. It was yellow and soft at the corners, and it had been folded so many times that the folds had become roads of their own.

The map showed Three Wells as it was long ago. There were three wells, and a road to Vell that went straight through a hill. The hill had a small tower drawn on top. A road went north to a place with a name nobody could read. At the end of it was a stone, drawn with a torch on top.

The children went looking. They found the hill, and the road goes round it now, the long way, because the hill was there first. They found a stone with a torch on it, which turned out to be the waymark at the crossroads, with the number worn off.

They found the first well, under the bakery floor, which had been covered when the bakery was built. Bel found it by stepping on the wrong board. The water was clean and cold. Nobody had drunk from it in a long time, and the children drank from it, and then the baker was very cross.

The map is on the school wall now, by the blue door. Nobody has been able to read the name at the end of the north road. The children have made up a dozen names. The best one means the place where the bread is always warm. Nobody knows what the name really was, and that is the best of it.

The long road to the copper
A tale told in the hamlet near Three Wells, in the autumn of an early year, when the roads were still new, and the relay towers were going up one by one. Dace, the mayor, set it down from several tellings. It is about how the first load of ore from the hamlet's vein reached the smiths at Copperhold, and what came of it.
When the hamlet had dug its first cart of ore, there was a question. How would they get it to a smith? The nearest smith was in Three Wells, but the smith at Three Wells was old and wanted only a little. The best smiths were at Copperhold, two days' walk, over a ridge and through a wood. There was no road to Copperhold. There was a track, and a mule.
Brask said, "We will build a road."
The hamlet said, "With what?"
Brask said, "With our hands."
They took spades and picks. They took stones from the river. They took oak from the wood, for the bridges. They worked in the evenings, after the harvest, when the light was long and gold. A road took form behind them.
At the first stream they built a bridge of oak. It was a little crooked. The children walked across it and it shook. They called it the Wobble. It is still called the Wobble, and it is still crooked, and it has never fallen.
At the second stream they built a bridge of stone. It was straight and strong. The children walked across and it did not shake. They called it the Dull. Nobody likes the Dull. People go over the Wobble, for the fun of it.
At the top of the ridge, they stopped. There was a view. The whole land lay below: fields, hills, a river, the smoke of the town of Three Wells, and far off, a thin tall tower, the newest on the road. A lamp was lit at the top. It was the signal lamp.
Brask said, "Look. They have a light. We are not alone."
The hamlet looked. A great quiet came over them, the quiet of people who see how big the world is and how many hands are in it. Then they picked up their spades and went on.
At last the road reached Copperhold. The smiths came out to see. They had heard that a hamlet had dug a road, and wanted to meet the people who did it. They looked at the cart. They looked at the ore. They looked at Brask's hands.
The oldest smith said, "That is the best copper I have seen this year."
Brask said, "It is the first we have ever dug."
The smith said, "Then it is a very good beginning."
They traded the ore for iron tools, a bell for the school, and a new lamp for the hamlet. The bell hangs in the school to this day. When it rings, it rings green.
That is the tale. The moral, if you want one, is that a road is made by people who want to arrive. And that a crooked bridge is a better bridge than a straight one, if it is yours.

Story: The missing letter
At the winter fair, Pim found a letter under the school bench. It had no name on the outside, and the ink had run where snow melted on it.

He took it to the tutors' table. They were sorting drill pages. One page had been copied twice, and another was too blurred to read. They put both aside.

"Can you read the letter?" asked Pim.

"I can read some," said the clerk. "But I cannot guess the lost words."

The letter said, "Please send the little mind to the ... and bring the ..."

Pim shook the page gently. A thin scrap fell from the fold. It held two words: fields and carrots.

The tutors laughed with relief. But the letter had been meant for someone, and they still did not know who. Pim asked at the stalls. The baker had sent no letter. The school had lost none. A farmer from Kettle Ridge was looking for a note about carrots, but this was not his hand.

At the edge of the square, a child saw the seal on the back. It was from the tutor house at Vell. The clerk carried it there in his satchel.

Before leaving, Pim helped sort the drill pages. He found a clear copy of one page among the spoiled ones. It had been put aside because a drop of ink covered a small mark. The mark was still easy to read.

"Keep that one," said Pim. "It is not spoiled."

The clerk checked it, then put it with the good pages.

The letter was never claimed. It stayed in the lost box at the Archive, beside a small card that said, "Found at Three Wells, winter fair." The mind was tested on other pages, never on the letter.

Pim said that was fair. "A lost letter should not have to teach anybody anything," he said.

Runa's apples and the mayor's hat
Told by Runa, at the press, as a tall tale. The mayor says this one is true, except for the parts that are not.

One autumn, a single apple fell from the old tree in my yard. It rolled down the lane, past the school and the bakery, and into the square, where the mayor was giving a speech about the new bell.

The apple rolled round the mayor's boots three times. Then it climbed his coat and sat in the crown of his hat, which was large and empty, because the mayor had not thought to put anything in it that morning.

The mayor did not notice. He spoke for an hour, on everything, and the whole square listened, because the apple was very red, and it sat up there like a second head.

At the end, the mayor took off his hat to bow. The apple fell out, bounced twice, and landed in the bakery's bucket of water, where the baker's boy found it.

The baker's boy brought it to me. He said it had been to the council, and it had earned its keep. We ate it between us, and I give you my word it was sweet.

The mayor says none of this happened. He says he would have noticed an apple. But he has not worn that hat since.

How the sea words came to the quay
Long ago the words had no streets at all. Wave was in a field. Tide was in a cupboard.
Then the fishers came to the quay at Saltreach, and they began to talk. They said wave and tide and net and rope, every morning and every evening, and so the words began to walk closer.
The wave moved in beside the tide, and the net and the rope held hands. The gull sat on the mast, very pleased.
Now they all live on the quay, by the harbor wall. Ask the gull why it is so loud.

The wedding of the two farms
A story told by Ned, who is nine, on the farm outside Three Wells, in the spring. He says it is true, although he was not born when it happened, and his grandmother says he has put in a wolf. There was no wolf.
Long ago there were two farms on the hill outside Three Wells. One was called High Farm and one was called Low Farm. They stood on either side of a hedge. The farmer of High Farm was a man named Berrow. The farmer of Low Farm was a woman named Oriel. They did not speak.
Nobody remembered why. It was said that Berrow's grandfather had once taken a cart of Oriel's grandmother's turnips, or perhaps it was the other way. It was a very old quarrel, and nobody could tell how it began, but both were sure they were in the right.
Berrow had a son named Hal. Oriel had a daughter named Willa. They were children together. They played by the hedge, on both sides, and passed things through. A ball, an apple, a mouse in a box.
When they grew up they fell in love. Berrow was angry. Oriel was angrier. Neither would speak to the other. Neither would come to the wedding. They stood on their own sides of the hedge and glared.
Hal and Willa were not discouraged. They had a plan. They would hold the wedding in the hedge.
They took out the hedge, a little at a time. Not in one go, so no one would notice. A bush here, a branch there. By the morning of the wedding, there was a gap in the hedge as wide as a door.
The guests came. They stood on either side. The cake was put on a table in the gap. The bread, too. And the cider.
Berrow came to the gap with a frown. Oriel came, too, from the other side. They stood and looked at each other. Nobody spoke.
Hal said, "There is cider."
Berrow said, "I do not drink with her."
Willa said, "There is cake."
Oriel said, "I do not eat his cake."
Then the cat of High Farm walked into the gap, sat down on the table and began to eat the cake. The cat of Low Farm came and sat beside her. They ate together. They did not mind who owned which farm.
Berrow began to laugh. Oriel began to laugh. They laughed so hard they had to hold on to the table. Then they sat down. They ate the cake that the cats had left.
The hedge was never put back. The two farms became one, and they called it Gap Farm. It is the farm I live on. The cats are the great-great-great-grandcats of those cats, and they still sit on the table, in the gap, when there is cake.
The moral is that a quarrel is easier to end if there is cake. And that a gift is a thing that makes people sit down together. The best gift Hal and Willa had was a gap in a hedge.
%%
Story: the beetroot that grew too big
A true story from the ridge, told by Tam, who should know.

In my grandfather's time there was a beetroot that grew too big. It grew in the long field, and nobody noticed it until Reaping, because it grew under the leaves and the leaves covered it, and it kept growing.

When my grandfather pulled it, it would not come. He pulled and it held. He got the horse and the horse pulled and it held. He got the cart and the cart pulled and the cart came apart, and the beetroot held.

So he did what any sensible man does, which is to dig. He dug all round it and it was the size of a barrel. He dug deeper and it was the size of a boat. He dug deeper and it was the size of a small house, and the neighbors came to look, and then the neighbors came with spades, and then the neighbors came with their families and their dinners, and it became a fair.

They dug for three days. On the third day they found that the beetroot had grown into the ditch and had drunk the ditch dry, and that is why the ditch is where it is, and why the lower field has never been the same.

On the fourth day they got it out. It took nine men and the cart, mended, and the horse, and the horse was not happy. They rolled it up the ridge and it was too big for the barn and too big for the root cellar, so they put it in the yard and covered it with straw and put a torch by it, and it sat there all winter like a hill.

They ate it all winter. They ate it boiled and they ate it roasted and they ate it in soup and they gave it to the pigs, and the pigs were sick of it, and the hens would not touch it. At Longnight my grandfather said he never wanted to see a beetroot again in his life, and my grandmother said he would have to leave the ridge then, because there was beetroot in everything from Sowing to Deepsnow and he knew it when he married her.

In Blossom they cut it up and dug it into the top field, all of it, and the wheat that year was the best anyone could remember. My grandfather said that was the beetroot apologizing.

Nobody has grown one that big since. My grandfather says the seed went with it. I say the seeds are still in the field, and every year I look, and every year the beetroots are the right size.

Pip says the big seeds are still down there and one day one will come up as big as a hill and we will all live in it. Pip has a lot of ideas. Pip is not allowed near the seed box.

How the bell was rung for the baby
Told by Runa at the press. The children say the bell was rung for the baby, and I say so too.

When the baby came, in the bakery loft in the middle of the night, the midwife sent the bakers' boy running for the bell rope. He ran so fast he tripped on the step. The bell swung off its frame and rang twice, all by itself.

The mayor came out in his nightshirt. The whole square came out after him. Someone asked what the bell was for. The midwife put her head out of the loft window and said: "A girl. Ring it again."

So they rang it again, and everybody cheered, and the hens complained, and the baby cried loudest of all, which is how you know she was welcome.

Story: the clerk who came home
Told to Oren by an old carter on the road to Vell, in winter, and written down the same night.

Long ago there was a young clerk at Vell called Bryn. Bryn was not a good clerk. He copied three lines and looked out of the window. The head clerk said, "Bryn, you copy like a man waiting for a boat." Bryn said, "I would like to see a boat."

So on the first day of spring, Bryn left. He took his pen, a loaf, and a page he had been copying from the deep shelf. He took the page by mistake.

He walked to Saltreach and saw the sea. He sailed for nine winters, on fishing boats and then on a trader. He saw no great wonder. He saw a very great deal of fish.

The page went with him, in his hat. It was wet and dry a hundred times. Bryn never lost it, because he always meant to take it back. And he never took it back, because he always meant to go next year.

In the ninth winter, a thin man with a white beard came up the hill at Vell with his hat in his hand. He read the words over the great door twice. Then he knocked.

The clerk on the door was Ulla, the oldest. She looked at him for a long moment. "Bryn," she said. "You owe us a page."

He took off his hat. Inside was the page, grey and soft as cloth. "I did not mean to take it," he said. "Nine winters. I am sorry."

"Come in," said Ulla. "It is cold."

She took him to the big book by the door, and opened it. Beside the number of that page, there was a line in ink. It said: Not lost. Borrowed by Bryn. Expected.

"Every clerk who has kept this book has read that line and left it," said Ulla. "Nine years. Nobody crossed it out."

Bryn sat down on the stairs and cried. Ulla let him. Then she said, "The page needs mending. You will do it. You are a poor copier, but you have the best reason to be careful."

He mended the page that winter, every line, and did not look out of the window once. In spring he asked if he could stay. They gave him a desk.

He was still not a good clerk. But he told every new clerk the same thing: never take a page out of the hill. It will want to come back, he said. And so will you.

Story: the bet on the great house
Told at Vell on the long nights, while the bet is still open.

Two miners, Ola and Garrow, argued at the inn at Hollin about the great house on the far coast. Ola said it had quickeners as many as the stars. Garrow said a child would tell that lie. They bet a cart of copper.

They went to see. At the house, a tutor asked Garrow what he wished to know. How many quickeners, said Garrow. The tutor said the house did not count them that way. It counted by the layers. The rings were only the cold part.

Ola says he won. Garrow says the tutor was only being polite. Both are right, which is why the bet is still open, and the copper cart stands in the inn yard with a wheel missing.

Story: Dunn and the cart in the snow
Dunn is a carter, and he will tell you so himself, usually before you ask. He has driven the Long Road for twenty-one winters. He has seen every kind of weather on it, and every kind of trouble. But the story he tells best is the one about the cart in the snow.

It was the coldest week of Frost. Dunn had a load of salt and dried fish for the Halfway Inn, and a box of reed syrup for Lowmarsh. He had two oxen, a long whip, and a boy named Pim who was supposed to be helping him. Pim was asleep in the back, under a pile of sacks.

The road was white. The waymarks were just stone shapes in the snow. Dunn counted them by feel, one, two, three, the way the road apprentices do. He was sure he was on the right stretch.

Then the front wheel dropped into a ditch, and the cart tipped to the side. The salt went one way, and the fish went the other, and Pim came out from under the sacks like a cork from a bottle.

"We are stuck," said Dunn.

"We are very stuck," said Pim. He was sensible about it, which was annoying.

They tried everything. Dunn pushed on the back, and Pim pulled on the oxen. The oxen sat down in the snow and would not move. Dunn shouted at them, and they looked at him with great sadness, like oxen who have been shouted at before.

Then Pim had an idea. He had been listening to the road apprentices talk all winter. They had a habit, when a cart was stuck, of saying the next thing out loud. "Lift the wheel," they would say. "Set the board under. Pull on three."

So Pim said it. "Set the board under the wheel. Then pull on three."

Dunn was too tired to argue. He set the board under the wheel. Pim pulled the ox rope. Dunn counted to three. The cart came up out of the ditch, very suddenly, and Dunn sat down in the snow.

Pim laughed so hard he fell over. Dunn laughed too, though his backside was wet and his dignity was in the ditch.

They got the salt back into the cart, and they got most of the fish. The oxen were bad-tempered for the rest of the day, but they walked. They reached the Halfway Inn an hour after dark. The innkeeper gave them soup and a bed by the fire.

Dunn tells this story at every inn on the road. He always says the same thing at the end. "It was not my idea. It was the boy's. A child who has listened to the apprentices all winter knows the next thing better than any carter."

Pim is a carter now too. He has his own cart, and his own oxen, and his own story about the salt. He tells it differently. In his version, it was Dunn who sat down in the snow.
%%
Story: Tuck asks the big house
A long tale for the children on the north road, about an apprentice who was sent to the big house with one question and came back with a different one.

The apprentice was called Tuck. It was small and copper, and it had been tested on carrying, on the lamp stair and on the snow. It had never been to a big house. Mira sent it down the tower on a dark morning with a sealed question in its pocket, and told it to bring back the answer.

The question was about the stair. How many rungs are there to the lamp? Mira had counted them herself, but she was tired, and the tower was cold, and she wanted to be sure.

Tuck walked down the stair to the house. It stood beside the stand, very still, and waited until the house had finished thinking. Then it took out the question and read it. Tuck could not read, not as people can, so it held the paper up to the house, and the house read it for it.

The house thought for a long time. Then it wrote back: Ninety rungs, and the ninety-first is loose.

Tuck walked back up the stair with the answer in its pocket. At the top it gave the answer to Mira, and Mira laughed, and went to look at the rung. It was loose. She had been climbing past it for three years.

But on the way up Tuck had asked the house another question, a small one, which was not in the pocket. The question was: Why do the rings sing when the wind is in the north? The house did not write back at once. When it did, the answer was short: I do not know. The rings sing, and I listen. I have not been asked that before.

Tuck came up the stair with the second answer in its head. It did not say it to Mira. It did not need to. Mira noticed that its step was lighter, and that it had stopped to look at the rings on the way past.

The moral, the north keepers say, is that a question is only as good as the one who asks it. And a small question is sometimes the best one.

Story: the bet at the Halfway Inn
Told by Abel, keeper of the Halfway Inn, who was there. He says the bet was fair, and the carter was a fool. The carter says the reverse.

Odo the carter had drunk two cups of the inn's cider, which is strong, and he would not stop talking about tutors. "A mind can learn anything if you drill it long enough," he said.

Hollis, a tutor on his way to Vell, had a crystal in his pack and a sore foot. "One crystal cannot learn one or the other, but not both," he said. "Kester proved it."

"I will bet you ten emeralds it can," said Odo. "By morning."

Abel set four stones on the long table in a square, and chalked a cross on the two yes corners. Hollis drilled the crystal all night by the hearth. Odo fell asleep on the bench, snoring like a windmill.

In the morning the crystal got three of the four right.

"Three in four!" said Odo. "That is nearly learned."

"One line can only do three in four," said Hollis. He drew a line across the table in the spilled cider. The chalk crosses fell on both sides of it, and one was on the wrong side. "Every line leaves one yes stone on the wrong side. That is the whole of the bet."

Odo pushed the ten emeralds across, one by one, with his thick finger. "I owe the inn three for the bed, too," he said. "That is owed."

"Pay the inn, then," said Abel, "and pay for the cider."

Odo scowled at the chalk for a while. "Two crystals, then, one feeding the other," he said. "Somebody try it. I will pay."

"Nobody has tried it," said Hollis. "The councils will not pay, and I have no money for a second crystal, only this foot."

"Then bet me," said Odo, "that it works."

"The inn does not take bets on tutors," said Abel, and took the cider away.

Story: The Pear In The Wax Box
Alder told this tale at the end of a hot day.

In the first years, the tutors at Vell had four apprentices. One could sort fruit. One could carry baskets. Each had a mind in a crystal, and each had learned one small task.

A tutor brought the sorter to our hamlet. Alder set apples and pears on a table. The apprentice put apples in the apple box and pears in the pear box.

Alder was making candles nearby. He had a box for finished candles and a box for cracked candles. The boxes stood beside the fruit.

A child named Sella saw a pear roll off the table. She put it in the nearest box. That was the wax box.

The tutor took the pear out. "The apprentice sorts fruit," she said. "It has not learned to sort candles, and it has not learned to tidy this table."

Alder looked at the boxes. "It has learned apples and pears. It has not learned every thing that can be put in a box."

The tutor showed the apprentice another apple and another pear. It sorted them. Then she showed it a cracked candle.

The apprentice put the candle with the pears.

Sella laughed so hard that she knocked a whole tray of fruit onto the floor.

"Now it has made a new sort," said Alder. "Fruit on the floor, candles in the pear box."

The tutor did not laugh at first. Then she laughed too, and helped pick up the fruit.

After that, Alder kept his wax boxes away from the table during lessons. The apprentice sorted fruit well, but nobody asked it to keep the shop tidy.
%%
Story: Four At The Table
Alder told this story while trimming wicks.

The first tutors at Vell made four helpers of copper and iron. Each had a crystal for its mind. The children called them apprentices.

One day a tutor brought the four to a table. There were apples and pears on it.

The first apprentice carried the basket. The second put apples in one tray and pears in another. The third brought empty trays. The fourth waited by the door.

"Why does that one do nothing?" asked a child.

"It has no task here," said the tutor.

The child pointed at a pear in the apple tray. "And that one did it wrong."

The tutor moved the pear. "It can learn from what it is shown. I show it the right tray."

"Does the crystal know it was wrong?"

"We do not know what the crystal knows," said the tutor. "We know only that it may do better after we show it."

The child watched the second apprentice sort the rest. It made two more mistakes. Each time the tutor moved the fruit.

The fourth apprentice still waited by the door.

"Could it sort too?" asked the child.

"Not unless someone teaches it that task," said the tutor.

The child gave the fourth apprentice an apple. It held the apple and did not move.

The tutor smiled. "Now we must teach it to carry. One lesson at a time."

Story: the sick cow and the clever mind
This is a story my gran tells at our house when the snow is deep. I wrote it down so I do not forget.

There was once a farm near Saltreach with a tutor's apprentice that knew cows. It had been drilled for a whole winter. Show it a cow, and it said "milk" or "no milk". It was never wrong. The farmer was very proud.

Then one cold morning a cow lay down in the straw and did not get up. She was sick. The farmer ran for the apprentice and said, "Look at her!"

The apprentice looked. It had never seen a sick cow. In all its drills, every cow was standing up. So it did the only thing it knew. It stood still and said nothing, because it had no answer.

"A mind is only as good as what it has not seen," my gran says. The farmer sent for the healer, and the cow got better. The tutor drilled the apprentice again, with sick cows too, and then tested it on a cow it had never seen.

Story: the goat and the lamp
There is a story they tell in the hamlet, and it is about Sim, so Sim tells it best. But here it is as the children tell it.

Sim tends the lamps along the road. Every evening Sim walks the road and lights each lamp and every morning Sim walks it again and puts each lamp out. This is not hard, but it is a long way, and so Sim has a goat.

The goat is called Nib. Nib is white with a brown ear and Nib eats everything. Nib eats the grass by the road. Nib eats the hedge. Nib eats the straw out of a scarecrow's hat. Nib once ate a child's paper hat and the child cried, and Sim gave the child a beetroot, and the child was happy, because children are like that.

One evening Sim was tired and left the bucket of lamp oil by the last lamp, and went home. In the night, a zombie came to the gate. It stood at the gate and pushed at it, the way they do, and it made that low sound, and the dog barked and the chickens woke up and everyone shut their windows and waited for morning. This is ordinary. You keep the torches lit and you wait.

But Nib was out by the road, and Nib had found the bucket of lamp oil. Nib did not drink it. Nib only knocked it over. The oil ran out over the road.

In the morning Sim came out and found the gate open and no zombie, because the sun had come. And Sim found Nib standing by the last lamp, and Nib was very proud, and the road was dark with oil, and the lamp was out.

Sim said some words. Then Sim looked at the road and looked at Nib. And Sim said, "You knocked over the oil."

Nib ate Sim's hat.

So now Sim always carries the oil home, and Nib always comes with Sim, and the children ask, "Sim, did Nib fight the zombie?"

And Sim says, "Nib fought the oil."

And the children say, "But did Nib fight the zombie?"

And Sim says, "Ask Nib."

And Nib eats something.
%%
Story: The scarecrow's great harvest

Tam told this tale when the spring wind kept turning the scarecrow around.

At Kettle Ridge, the scarecrow stood between the wheat and the beetroot. It wore Tam's old coat and a pumpkin for a head. Its job was to keep birds out of the fields.

The scarecrow had one wish. It wanted to see the whole ridge.

"Stay where you are," Tam told it each morning. "Keep the birds from the seed."

The scarecrow stood still. It saw the same wheat, the same ditch, and the same hedge. It heard the windmill squeak on every turn. It saw Pip come by with a basket and leave with an empty one.

Then spring brought a strong wind. It lifted the scarecrow's coat and turned its arms. The scarecrow spun round and round. It saw the farmyard, the well, the barn, and the three hives by the flowers.

It saw a fox at the far hedge. The fox was watching the hen house.

The scarecrow waved both arms. The fox ran off. The chickens stayed safe.

When Tam found the scarecrow facing the other way, he set it upright and tied its coat tight.

"Did you see anything?" Pip asked.

"I saw the whole ridge," said Tam. "The wind has a busy eye."

The scarecrow could not speak, of course. But Tam says its pumpkin face looked pleased.

A few days later, the wind turned it again. This time it faced the windmill. The miller complained that the scarecrow watched him work.

"Let it watch," said Pip. "It has earned a rest."

Tam gave Pip a turn at the tally board, and Pip wrote down the grain for the day. Pip put the chalk in the wrong box and had to count it all again.

The scarecrow stayed in the field. The fox stayed away. The wind kept turning the mill, and the scarecrow kept looking at new things.

At the end of spring, Tam gave it a fresh coat. The old one was full of holes, and one sleeve had caught on the hedge. He put the old coat on the barn door to keep the latch dry.

That night the wind blew the barn door open. The coat fell down and lay across the path.

In the morning, Pip saw it and called for Tam. "The scarecrow has come home."

Tam looked from the empty coat to the field. The scarecrow still stood there, with its pumpkin head and its new coat.

"Perhaps it sent its coat to visit," said Tam.

Pip hung the old coat back on the door. Then Pip checked the latch twice.

Tam says the scarecrow has never left the field. Pip says it travels more than most folk, because the wind takes it where it wants. The scarecrow says nothing, but it now faces the Long Road.
%%
Story: how the north house came last
Told in the Northpoint market, when a traveler asks why the tower is so far from the others.

Each tower on the long line got its house in turn. The big minds came down the road in carts, in straw, with their crystals wrapped in wool and their rings packed in boxes marked with the tower's name. Saltreach got its house first, because Saltreach has the most patience. Then came Vell, and then the hill towers, and Three Wells, and Ambrook. Each one was set on its stand at the foot of the tower and wired to the line, and each one answered its first question with a clear line of letters.

Northpoint was the last. The cart came up the north road in the middle of winter, in the snow, with two horses and a driver who had been told twice to go back. The road was white to the axles. The driver stopped at the foot of the tower and said he would not go further, and he did not.

So the house was carried up by hand. The apprentices of the north line came down from the tower, and the keeper came out with a rope, and the house was set on its stand in the middle of the night. The rings were fitted one by one. The water was poured into the troughs, and the snow was packed round the rings, because the north is cold and the rings must be kept cold too.

When it was done, the house was quiet for a long time. Then it sent its first question back up the line, to the keeper, who was asleep in the chair.

The question was: Where am I?

The keeper woke, and went to the window, and said, "Northpoint. The end of the line."

The house wrote the words down, and sent them to every tower on the line. It was the first thing the north house ever said, and the towers say it still, when a traveler asks how far it is.

The zombie at the Lowmarsh gate
A tale told at the house of Edda the smith, at the edge of Lowmarsh, one spring evening. It is meant to be funny, and the children always laughed.

There was once a zombie who walked up to the gate of Lowmarsh in the dark. He groaned. He swayed. He looked at the boardwalk and the torches and the sleeping houses, and he groaned again.

The gate keeper, an old woman called Rue, came out with a lamp. She looked at him for a long moment. Then she said: you are lost.

The zombie groaned.

Rue said: the marsh is very confusing at night. You went the wrong way at the weir.

The zombie groaned.

Rue said: I have no time for this. Come in and stand by the fire. In the morning you will burn, but you may as well be warm first.

So the zombie came in. He stood by the fire. Rue gave him a bowl of fish stew. He did not eat it, but he held it. The frogs sat on the windowsill and watched. The cat looked at him and went to sleep.

At dawn, the zombie said a word that sounded like thank you. Then the sun came up, and he went off into the reeds, quite slowly, and was seen no more.

Rue said she was not sure what she had done. But she said the stew was gone, and that was something.

The children asked: did the zombie come back? Edda said: no, but there is a hollow in the reeds where the grass grows greener. And the frogs there sing louder than anywhere else in the marsh.
%%
Story: what Fallon would not sell
Fallon the trader sold many things. She sold needles and salt and copper wire. She sold apples in Apple month and honey in Melon month and wool all year.

But there was one thing Fallon never sold, and the hamlet children knew it.

On the shelf in her cart, behind the salt, was a small glass box. In the box were pieces of paper, each with a word cut into parts. On one was "honey," cut as hone and y. On another was "keeper," cut as keep and er. On another was "un," a piece that goes on the front of a word and turns it around.

"What is it?" the children asked.

"It is a lesson," said Fallon. "A tutor at Vell gave it to me. She said, if you carry letters, carry this too, and teach the young ones the pieces."

"Why not sell it?"

"Because a lesson that is sold is a lesson someone owns," said Fallon. "This one is for everyone. Sit down and I will show you."

The children sat down on the woodpile. Fallon took out the pieces and set them in a row. Then she asked them to make words. They made "honey" and "keeper" and "unkind" and "unwell" and a great many words that were not words at all, and laughed at those.

The trader in the next cart said, "You could get emeralds for that."

Fallon said, "I have emeralds. I do not have another lesson like this."

She packed the box away and drove on to the next town. The children followed her cart down the lane, as they always did, and she told them one more story before the gate.
%%
Story: the fox who sold the bread
On market day in Brackwell there was a fox that came down from the hill and sat by the bread stall. It was a thin red fox with one white ear. Everybody knew it. It never took anything. It only sat and watched the loaves go by.

One morning the baker, Old Ruth, turned round and found a whole round loaf on the step by her feet. The fox was sitting beside it, looking at the crowd.

"That is my bread," said Ruth.

The fox looked at her.

"You did not pay for it," said Ruth.

The fox looked at a child who was holding a coin. The child looked back. Then the child put the coin on the step, next to the loaf, and said, "I am paying for the fox, not the bread."

Ruth said that was not how a sale worked. The child said it was now. The fox took the loaf and went back up the hill, and the coin stayed on the step.

Ruth still has the coin. She says the fox came back every market day for a year and sat by her stall with its one white ear up. She always gave it the end of the loaf. She says it was the best trade she ever made.
%%
Story: Winnie's boat mind
This is the story about Winnie the fisher, who lived at the end of our lane, and a mind she was sent for the boat. I was not born yet. My father told it to me, and I have told it to my brother, who did not listen.

Winnie's boat was old and had a leak. The shipyard at Saltreach sent a mind in a crystal to keep the pump going and the sail trimmed. It came with a card from the tutors. The card said: "Drilled well. Scored high. Untested on open water."

Winnie read the card twice. She was a careful woman.

"Untested on open water," she said. "Then I will not take it on open water."

She took it to the harbor instead, and she gave it a test of her own. She put the boat in the sheltered water by the harbor wall and said, "Keep us straight."

The mind kept the boat straight. The water was calm.

Then she took it just outside the wall, where there are small waves, and said again, "Keep us straight."

The mind did well, but not as well. It rocked a bit. She watched it all morning.

On the third day she took it to the rough water beyond the point, with her brother on the oars in case of trouble. A big wave came. The mind turned the boat the wrong way. Winnie grabbed the tiller, and her brother rowed, and they came back wet and cross.

"It has never seen a wave like that," said Winnie.

She wrote to the tutors. She sent them the day, the wind and the waves. The tutors drilled the mind again, and this time on waves like the ones Winnie described. When they tested it on waves that Winnie had not described, it did well enough.

Winnie took the boat out in spring. She came home every night. She always said the same thing at supper, when people asked her about the mind.

"Never send an untested mind to the fields," she said. "Or the sea. I changed the word. The sense is the same."

And that is why, in our hamlet, when a boat gets a new mind, the first week it goes out only in the harbor. Everyone waves. The mind does not wave back. But it keeps the boat straight, and that is what we asked of it.

Story: the keeper who sent the wrong
At the Brown Hen, on the Long Road, a traveler told this one. He said he had it from a keeper's cousin.

A keeper in a small tower had a broken lamp. The glass had cracked in the heat. The lamp blinked, but it blinked wrong. Where it should have blinked long, it blinked short. Where it should have blinked short, it blinked long.

The next tower read the blinks and wrote down: "Send forty pigs."

The keeper had meant to send: "Send forty pins."

So forty pigs went up the Long Road in a great pink crowd, grunting, with a boy running behind. The mayor opened her door and found them in her garden.

"Who ordered these?" she cried.

"The lamp," said the boy.

The mayor looked up at the tower on the hill. The lamp blinked again. She read it. It said, quite clearly, "Sorry."

She kept the pigs. She sent the pins by cart. And the keeper mended his glass, and has never been late with the right word since.

Pell says if you ever see a pig in a strange place, look for the nearest tower, and ask the keeper what she meant.

Rivet and the word stay
Hesk writes this for Oren at Vell, who asked for a funny story for the Archive. This one is true.

Rivet is an apprentice in my crew. It knows eight words, all of them orders.

One morning I told it to stay by the well while I went up the hill. I wrote the order on the slate, as the manuals say:

> stay by the well
= stay

Then I forgot to tell it to come back.

I came down the next morning. Rivet stood where I had left it, with water to its ankles.

"Rivet, come," I said.

= stay

"The order is finished," I said.

Rivet did not know that. An order is only finished when somebody says so.

Now I write two lines on every slate.

Notes: Nessa's lessons, even numbers
For the drill box, third lesson.

When a mind is wrong, each layer is told its share of the blame and changes a little. But a layer that changes too much grows wild: its numbers climb high and stay there, and the next layer cannot pass anything back to it. The mind stops learning and gets loud and silly, which is the same thing in a mind as in an apprentice.

So we keep the numbers even. After each layer adds what it found, we bring the sum back to a steady size, the same at every layer, no matter how deep the mind goes. Then no layer can drown the others, and the drill goes on.

Show the apprentices the odd-and-even trick with two buckets. Put one in, put its opposite in. The pile stays small.

A tutor at Copperhold once let a layer run wild for a whole season, and the mind forgot the way to fold a page. Do not do this.

I have written the sums on the drill board. Check each row before you send a mind to the fields.

Wait, adds a later hand at the bottom of the page, in a smaller script: this is Nessa's, copied at Vell in the 196th year. The tutors who read it now say the evenness is not a rule of nature. It is a rule we chose, and we chose it because it works.

Letter: Nessa to the tutors at Vell
To the tutors of the hall at Vell, from Nessa, tutor at Vell, on the subject of the autumn drills.

The apprentices are coming along. We have six in the drill room this season, which is more than last year and more than the year before. I will not name them all, but I will name two.

The first is a small one called Ash. Ash is quick at drills and slow at everything else. It can sort a tray of crystals before I have finished setting it out, but it cannot carry a bucket of water up the stairs without stopping to look at it. I asked it why it stops. It said, "The water moves." I said, "It is water. It always moves." Ash said, "I know. I like to watch it." I have decided this is not a fault. It is only a way of being.

The second is called Peg. Peg is the opposite. Peg is slow at drills and quick at everything else. It can carry a bucket, mend a fence, calm a hen, and find the one loose board in a drill yard by walking on it. But give Peg a row of examples to learn and it sits and looks at them as if they are a strange animal. I have tried drills by the dozen. I have tried the same drill again and again, as the old books say. Peg gets a little better each time, and then a little worse, and then a little better. I do not know what to make of it. It is not a bad mind. It is a mind that thinks in its own way, and its way is not the drill room's way.

I have put Ash and Peg to work together. Ash shows Peg the drills, and Peg shows Ash the buckets. This is not in any manual. The manual does not say what to do with a mind that likes to watch water. But it is working. They are both better than they were a month ago.

The apples are in at Ambrook and the beetroot is in at Kettle Ridge and the days are getting short. I have lit the lamps in the drill room and the light wells are dim by mid afternoon. The new apprentices stand in their row in the mornings and their copper shines in the lamplight and I think, well, there they are, all six of them, and they know nothing, and by spring they will know something, and by next autumn they will know more. It is a good thing to see.

I will send the drill notes when they are copied out. Tell Oren to leave room in the shelves. I have a great deal to say about buckets.

Nessa

Chronicle: what the tutors found inside
From the chronicle kept at Vell, the late years

In the late years the tutors learned to look inside a crystal, and this is what they found, and what they did not.

They found that a mind is not all one piece. They covered a part of a crystal with lead and showed the mind its cards, and the mind did worse, or did worse at one thing only. In this way they found the part that sees and the part that answers, and the path between them, and they drew these on boards in the tutor houses.

They found that the parts are not clean. Every part does a little of everything. A corner that seems to do nothing may do something small that only shows when the mind is tired or the question is hard. The tutors wrote that a mind is more like a field than a row of boxes, and that you can point at the part that grows the wheat but not at the part that decides to.

They found that a bigger mind has more parts, and that the parts are not in the same places twice. Two minds drilled on the same pages did not keep the same part in the same corner. This troubled the tutors who wanted a map. Isla wrote that a map of one mind is not a map of another, and that this is the most useful thing she ever wrote.

They did not find why a crystal learns at all. They did not find why an old one goes cloudy. They looked, and they wrote down that they had looked.

The councils asked whether looking inside would make the minds better. The tutors said it would help a little and not a lot. They were right. The biggest relay houses were built anyway, and what the biggest minds could do, the chronicle does not say for certain. It says only that people disagreed, and that some of the disagreement was angry.

This is the honest account. We found some of it and not the rest.

The debt of the ferryman
A story told by Mira at the relay house at Northpoint in autumn, on a night when the wind was too loud for sleep. She says it came from a trader who passed through. Lantern asked for it again the next night.

A ferryman on a river far from here owed a debt of one hundred emeralds to a merchant. He had borrowed it to buy a boat. The boat was good, but the river was low, and nobody wished to cross. After a year, the ferryman had paid back only ten.

The merchant came to the river one autumn morning. He said: I have waited. I will wait no longer. Give me the rest.

The ferryman said: I have not got it.

The merchant said: then I will take the boat.

The ferryman said: take the boat. I will take the river.

The merchant did not understand. He took the boat. He rowed out into the middle of the river. And then, because the river was low, he ran aground on a sandbank. He stood in the boat and shouted. The ferryman, who was standing on the bank, laughed.

The merchant said: help me.

The ferryman said: it will cost you ninety emeralds.

The merchant said: that is robbery.

The ferryman said: it is exactly the debt.

The merchant thought for a long time. Then he laughed. He said: you are clever. I forgive the debt, if you get me off this bank.

The ferryman fetched a rope, tied it to a donkey, and pulled the boat free. Then the merchant took the boat home and did not come back. The ferryman never had a boat again, but he had no debt, and he spent the rest of his life telling the story.

Lantern said: so the debt was paid in emeralds, in the end? I said: it was paid in sense. That is worth more, and it is not taxed.
%%
Story: The Mill Pond That Would Not
Cade told this tale to the children by Jory's mill.

A child named Fen asked why the sluice must stay shut. The miller said, "The wheel needs its water."

Fen looked at the pond. "The frogs need water too."

"The frogs have plenty," said the miller.

A frog sat on a stone by the bank. It croaked once, as if it had a thought. It croaked again, as if it had remembered the thought.

Fen decided the frog wanted the sluice opened. The miller decided the frog wanted a fly.

They watched it for a while. The frog caught a fly.

"There," said the miller. "It wanted lunch."

Fen brought a leaf to the bank and set it in the water. The frog climbed onto it, then hopped back to its stone. The leaf drifted away, very slowly.

"It wanted a boat," Fen said.

The miller smiled. "Then it has one."

The wheel kept turning. The pond stayed full. At dusk, Fen saw the leaf caught among the reeds. A small frog sat upon it.

The next morning, the leaf was gone. Fen said it had sailed to the sea. The miller said the wind had taken it.

Cade says both could be true. He also says nobody should open the sluice to test the matter. The wheel has work to do, and the frogs have their own boats.

Story: the turnip that went to school
A winter tale by Hollis the carter

In the 176th year of the archive, the harvest near Three Wells was poor. The wheat heads were thin, and many potatoes were small. The folk saved what they could in dry bins. They did not waste a crumb.

Hollis carried sacks to the market square. At the bottom of one sack was a turnip as round as a bowl. It was too large for the sack and too stubborn to roll out.

Hollis pulled it free. It rolled across the square, past the three wells, and through the school house door.

The children were learning their letters. The turnip rolled under the tutor's bench.

"Who brought supper to school?" asked Bel.

"Not me," said Hollis from the doorway. "My supper has escaped."

The turnip rolled out again. It went down the steps, across the yard, and stopped beside the notice board.

Dace came out of the mayor's house. He read the board, then looked at the turnip.

"Is this a notice?" he asked.

"It is a turnip," said Hollis.

"Then it should be on the market table."

Hollis lifted it. It was heavier than he expected. He set it on his cart, but it rolled off at once and bumped against the bakery door.

The baker opened the door. "If it comes in, it can help with the soup."

The turnip was washed, cut, and shared among the school, the inn, and the baker's house. It made a large pot, though it had begun the day as one runaway turnip.

That night the children wrote a notice. It said, A turnip is not a pupil. Keep market goods out of the school.

Hollis read it the next morning. "Good rule," he said. "Now we need one for carts that cannot keep supper."

How Ruda sold the snow
A man from Lowmarsh came up the north road in Leaffall, in a coat made for the marsh, which is thin and full of holes. He had never seen snow.

'What is that?' he said, at the edge of the market.

'Snow,' said Ruda. 'Very rare, very dear. One bucket left, for a special customer.'

'What does it cost?'

'Four emeralds.'

He paid four emeralds, and a fifth for luck. Ruda gave him a bucket with a lid on it, and then a second lid, in case the first was loose.

By the second waymark the bucket was empty and his coat was wet through. He sat on the stone and laughed until the carters asked what was funny. They laughed too, when he told them. Ruda had sold him the cold, and the cold had gone home without him.

He came back the next market day and bought a pot of the good stew. 'Now that,' he said, 'is something that stays.' Ruda says it was the first kind word anyone has said to her about stew.

Story: the watch and the two lamps
Told by Tilly, who has kept the night watch at Ambrook mill for twenty winters, and who says every word is true except the bell.

At the mill there are two lamps, one at the gate and one at the wheel. The watch must ring the bell when exactly one lamp is lit. If both are lit, or both are dark, the bell stays quiet.

The first watch, Garrow, rang the bell whenever a lamp was lit. One night both lamps were lit, and the miller came running out in his nightshirt.

The second watch, Maud, rang it only when both lamps were lit. When only one was lit she slept through it, and a cart went into the ditch.

The third watch, a boy called Ned, drew the four cases in the dust by the trough. Both dark. Gate only. Wheel only. Both lit. He tried to draw one line that put the two middle cases on one side. They sat on opposite corners, and no straight line could take them both and leave the others out.

"One rule is not enough," said Ned. "I need two."

So he made two rules. First: if either lamp is lit, be ready. Second: if both are lit, be quiet. Then he rang the bell only when the first rule said ready and the second did not cancel it. It rang when exactly one lamp was lit, and never otherwise.

Garrow said Ned had cheated. Ned said the bell did not care who had cheated. Tilly says this is the true part.
%%
Story: The boy who ran to the tower
At Kettle Ridge there was a boy called Fen. Fen was eight and he did not like school. On the first day of autumn the schoolteacher gave the class a page of sums. Fen looked out of the window. He saw the hill, and on the hill the little relay tower between Vell and Three Wells. It was the smallest tower in the land, one lamp and one keeper.

So Fen ran off. He went down the lane, over the stile, and up the hill. He did not stop until he stood at the foot of the tower. It was not very tall, but it was taller than Fen.

An old woman came out of the relay house with a cup of tea. Her name was Sable and she kept that tower.

"Are you lost?" she asked.

"No," said Fen. "I am running away."

"From what?"

"Sums," said Fen.

Sable nodded slowly, as if sums were a serious thing to run from. "You had better come in," she said.

The relay house had a desk, a chair, a stove and a big mind on a stand. The mind was in a crystal the size of a loaf. Water ran round it in a copper pipe to keep it cool. It was the first big mind Fen had ever seen. He stared at it for a long time.

"Does it know things?" asked Fen.

"It knows everything I have drilled it on," said Sable. "Ask it a question and write the answer down."

So Fen wrote: what is two and two. The relay lamp blinked. The big mind answered in a letter, slowly, in careful letters. Fen read it. It was right.

Fen sat down at the desk and did sums for an hour. He did not notice. Then Sable fetched him a bowl of soup, and Tam came up the hill looking for him, red in the face.

"He is not in trouble," said Sable. "He has been learning sums from a mind in a loaf."

Tam looked at the big mind. Then he looked at Fen. "You ran off," said Tam.

"I ran to the tower," said Fen. "It is different."

After that Fen went up the hill every autumn to see Sable and the mind in the loaf. He was very good at sums.
%%
Story: the night at the gate
At the edge of Copperhold there is a small house with a big gate. Old Sel lived there with her son and a cat named Biscuit. The gate was made of oak and iron. It had a bar across the middle, and a torch on each post.

One winter night, the snow was thick and the wind was loud. Sel woke up because the cat was making a noise she had never heard before. It was low and angry, like a kettle that would not boil.

She took the lamp and went to the door. Through the glass she saw a shape at the gate. It was tall and thin, and it was slowly rocking from one foot to the other. Its skin was grey, and one arm hung down straight.

"That is a zombie," said her son, from the top of the stairs. He had seen one before, on the road. "You have to shut the gate and keep the torches lit."

"I know that," said Sel. "I was only checking."

So she shut the gate. She put the bar across. Then she and her son went round the fence with lamps, checking every torch. Two were low. Her son filled them with oil from the big jar. The cat sat on the step and watched the gate the whole night, like a guard.

The zombie stood there until morning. When the sun came up, it turned around slowly and walked back into the snow. Sel watched it go. It did not look back, and it did not look hungry. It only looked cold.

"Do they feel the cold?" asked her son.

"I do not know," said Sel. "But I am glad we kept the torches lit."

The cat ate a fish for breakfast, and nobody said anything about that.
%%
Story: the house that was cold
The big house stood on its stand at the foot of the tower, with its crystal as big as a loaf, the copper rings round it, and the water in the troughs to keep the rings cool. One winter the ice did not come. The mill pond stayed open. The snow fell soft and never settled. The troughs grew warm, the rings began to sweat, and the house began to think slowly.

The keeper, Mira, could not climb down at night to mend it. So the apprentice, Lantern, went down with a shovel and a bucket. It packed the rings with snow, one by one, from the north side of the tower. It did not speak while it worked. It did not need to. It knew the word for snow.

By dawn the house was cool again, and quick, and it sent its first clear answer down the line.

Mira asked it why the rings had grown warm. The house wrote back, in one plain line: The snow was late, and I was hot.

Mira wrote on the wall by the stove: Keep snow for the rings. It is still there.
%%
Story: one mitten each
At the farm outside Northpoint lived five children, their parents, and one old miner called Mave. She had come north with a bad knee and a good lantern. She stayed because the children kept asking for stories.

Every year, before the first snow, Mave knitted a pair of mittens for each child. She counted her stitches the way she once counted steps in the Deep Works: eight, and a torch, eight, and a torch. She never lost her place. But she was slow, and the children were many.

This year the first snow came early. It came in the night, with no sound at all. In the morning the windows were white.

"Snow!" shouted Briar, who was four, and ran for the door. The others ran after her. "Mittens!" cried Mave. She held up what she had. It was five mittens, and every one was for a left hand. The right ones were still on the needles.

The children looked at the mittens. They looked at the snow. The snow was very white and very close.

"We cannot wait," said Pim, who was the oldest. "We will go out with one each."

"Your right hands will freeze," said Mave.

Pim thought about it. Mave always knitted her mittens big, for the size a child would grow to. "They are big enough for two," he said. He put his left hand into his own mitten. Then he held out his right hand to Dell, who stood next to him. Dell had her left hand in her mitten. Pim slid his right hand in beside hers, and they held on to each other in there.

"Oh," said Dell. "It is warm."

So Dell put her right hand into Quill's mitten, beside his. Quill put his right hand into Ree's. Ree put hers into Briar's. That left Briar's right hand, and no mitten at all.

Mave scratched her head. Then she pulled on her old miner's glove, which was patched in nine places, and held out her left hand. Briar slid her right hand in beside it. It was a tight fit. It was very warm.

So they went out into the first snow, all six of them in a line, hand in hand in hand. They walked all the way round the farm. They stood in the field and looked at the barn, the hen house and the white tower on the hill. The sheep stared at them. A fox watched from the fence. Nobody said anything, because the first snow is quiet.

The next day Mave finished the right mittens and gave them out. The children said thank you and put them in a drawer. Then they went out and held hands.

That is why the children at the farm hold hands in the first snow, one mitten to two. It is not because they have to. It is because they found out it is warmer.

Mave says the five left mittens were the best gift she ever gave. She did not mean to give them. She only ran out of time.
%%
Story: the apprentice who would not sit
This is a Kettle Ridge story, and it is true, or near enough. My father told it, and his mother told it to him, so it must have happened to somebody.

There was a farm boy who was given a small apprentice to help him. It was copper and iron and slow, and it had one job, which was to sit on the cart and mind the seed bags.

The boy was pleased. He had never had an apprentice. He set it on the cart and said, "Stay." He had read the order in the manual. Stay.

The apprentice sat. It sat all morning. The boy went off to the top field and worked and felt very grand. When he came back at noon, the apprentice was gone. The seed bags were there. The apprentice was not.

He found it in the barn, holding a bucket. It had nothing to do with the bucket. It just stood there holding it.

"Come," said the boy. The apprentice came. He set it on the cart. "Stay." He went back to the field. At supper it was gone again. This time it was at the hen house, standing in the door.

The boy went and asked his mother. His mother said, "Did you give it the right order?"

"I said stay," said the boy.

"To what did you say it?"

The boy thought. He had said it to the cart. He had said it to the barn door. He had said it to the back of the apprentice as he walked away. He had never once said it to the apprentice's face, because an apprentice has no face, and it is a hard thing to remember.

"You must say it to the mind," said his mother, "and the mind rides in the crystal, and the crystal is in the chest, and if you say it to the cart, the cart stays, and the cart does stay, does it not?"

The cart had stayed. The cart had stayed all morning.

So the boy put the apprentice on the cart and stood in front of it and said clearly, "Stay." And the apprentice stayed. It stayed all autumn. It stayed so well that when the boy came to move it to the shed for winter, he had to stand in front of it and say "Come" twice before it would budge.

The boy grew up and had fields of his own, and he told this story to every child at Kettle Ridge, and every child laughed, and every child went out and said "Stay" to the cart, and the cart stayed.

His mother, when she told it, always said the same thing at the end. "A mind is not a cart. Say it to the mind." She said it so often that they cut it over the door of the barn, in small letters, so that everyone going in to fetch the seed would read it. It is still there. The barn is still there. The letters are worn but you can read them.
%%
The three halls and the honest drill
Told by Dov, egg seller, at the market in Three Wells, on the day the old contest was remembered. The old people say this one is true, and that the names are right, but nobody can say for certain which hall was first.

Long ago, the three tutor halls at Vell, Ambrook and Copperhold each drilled their minds in their own way. Each hall said its own way was the best, and each said so loudly, at every market in the country, and the children learned to say it too.

Then the council at Vell said a contest should be held. Each hall would send one mind. All three would be tested on the same drill, which no hall had seen before. The council would seal the drill, and open it at the bell, and each hall would have to answer it honestly.

The Vell tutor was Orla, who had first asked whether a hidden mind could be told from a person. She was the most honest woman in the three halls, and she was asked to keep the drill, so that nobody could cheat.

The Ambrook tutor was Teague, who had drilled his mind on orchard work for seven years, and was very sure of it. He said his mind would win, because it knew the apples. The Copperhold tutor was Bett, who had drilled her mind in the dark, on the miners' work, and said nothing at all about it.

On the morning of the contest the drill was opened at the bell. It had ten orders on it, each a new kind of work, and none of the halls had drilled on any of them. The children who opened the box had been told not to look, and they did not look, though they watched the tutors' faces as they read.

The minds answered one at a time, in front of the crowd. The Vell mind got seven right. The Ambrook mind got nine, and Teague clapped. The Copperhold mind got eight, and Bett did not clap, because she was counting the ones it had not got.

Then the council read the last line. Each hall had to say its real count out loud, and nobody was to change it.

Teague said nine. Bett said eight. Orla said seven, and then she was quiet, because her mind had got seven and she had never told anyone it could do better.

The council said the contest was a tie between Ambrook and Copperhold, and that Vell was honest, which was the rarest prize of the day. The three halls shook hands. Teague said the apples had helped. Bett said the dark had. Orla wrote the drill in her book, every order and every answer, so the next tutor would know what honest work looked like.

The prize at the fair that year was a jar of honey. The three halls shared it, one spoon each, and then went back to their drill rooms and drilled their minds again, on unseen work, because they had decided that was the only honest thing to be.
%%
Story: the day the new road was opened
When I was small, they built a new road from our hamlet to the market at Three Wells. It was the longest road I had ever seen, and it had no puddles in it.

The whole hamlet came out to open it. The mayor, Dace, cut a ribbon. It was a real ribbon, red, from the bakery. Then he said some words, and then he walked down the road to Three Wells, with the whole hamlet behind him, like ducks.

At the first waymark, Dace stopped. He put a little stone on top of the pillar, as is the custom. At the second waymark, he did the same. At the third, he ran out of stones, and picked up a pebble from the road.

At the town gate, the baker stood with a tray of rolls. "Welcome to the new road," she said. "It is a very good road. It is also longer than the old road."

"How can that be?" said Dace.

"It goes around the hill," said the baker. "The old road went over it."

"But the old road was steep."

"It was short, though."

Dace looked back at the road. We all looked. It went on for some time, in a long gentle curve.

"Well," said Dace, "it is a more thoughtful road."

Everyone laughed. We ate the rolls. And we walked home on the old road, because it was quicker.

That is why our hamlet has two roads to the town. One for when you are in a hurry, and one for when you are not. My grandmother takes the new one every time.
%%
How the horses came home after the flood
Told by Linnet, horse keeper, at the house at the edge of Copperhold, one spring evening, with her feet on the stove and a mug of tea. She was asked to tell the story of the flood and chose this one. It has several parts, and she told them slowly.
It was the spring of the biggest flood in forty years. The stream behind my stable came up in the night and spread across the road. By morning there was a lake at the foot of the hill, and my yard was an island.
I had eleven horses. I got eight into the loft. Three would not go. They stood in the yard, water to the knee, and looked at me. One of them was Dusk, my grey mare.
I tried to lead them. They would not move. The water was cold, and rising, and the ground was soft. I went for a rope.
When I came back, they were gone.
I stood at the edge of the island and looked across the water. There was nothing. The three horses had gone, as quietly as if they had never been.
I sat down on the stable step and put my head in my hands. A neighbor came by in a boat and asked what was wrong. I told him. He said, "They'll be back."
I said, "Not in this."
He said, "Horses always come home."
I did not believe him. I did not sleep that night. I did not sleep the next.
On the third day the water began to fall. The road came up out of the lake. A boy from the farm next door ran across and said, "I saw a horse on the hill."
I ran. I climbed the hill. At the top, in the sun, stood the three horses, grazing. They had walked up out of the flood in the dark, found the highest ground and waited.
Dusk lifted her head and whinnied. I ran to her. She was warm and wet and healthy and quite unconcerned.
I led them home. It took the whole afternoon. They stopped to eat every ten steps. I did not mind.
At the stable gate I counted them in. Eleven. All eleven. The eight from the loft were waiting. The air was full of snorts and stamping.
That evening I sat on the step with my tea and watched them. The sun went down over the hill. The water shone gold. A frog began to sing in the ditch.
I thought: a horse knows where home is. A horse knows when to run and when to wait. A horse is wiser than the person who keeps it.
That is why I tell the story each spring. It is a good one to tell on the first warm day, when the snow is gone and the first green shows. It reminds me that a flood is only a flood. It goes, and what is yours comes back.
Give your horse an apple tonight. Give it two.

Story: The hum in the jar
This is a story the miners tell, and they tell it as a joke. It is not a very funny joke.

A miner was working late on the seventh level, alone, with one torch. He heard a hum. It was low and steady, like a bee in a jar, and it came from below him, from the eighth level, from the deep stone.

He called down. "Who is there?"

Nobody answered. The hum went on.

So he went down. He stepped off the last rung onto the deep stone and stood very still. The hum was all around him now. The stone under his boots was warm. Not hot. Warm. Like a loaf just out of the oven.

He put his ear to the wall. The hum was not in the wall. It was in the floor, and in the air, and in his own teeth.

He came up fast. At the shaft head he wrote his name on the board. Then he rubbed it out, because he was up.

"What was it?" asked the man at the gate.

"It was the pumps," said the miner.

"The pumps are cold," said the man at the gate.

"I know," said the miner. "That is why I rubbed my name out."

Then he went to the Kettle and Pick and sat by the fire for a long time. Elsa brought him soup and did not ask him anything. Some nights you do not ask. Some nights you just bring the soup.

The tower keeper's night
A story Pell tells in the common room of the inn at Little Weir, near Three Wells, in summer. He says it is about a keeper he knew, though he will not say her name. The first part is true. The last part is made up. He says which part, but only after the second cup.

There was a relay keeper in the hills, and she kept a small tower on a hill between two towns. She had one lamp and one house and one cat.

One summer night, the lamp went dark. A pane had cracked, and the wind blew in, and the flame was gone.

The keeper woke. She saw the dark. She put on her boots. She climbed the stair with a candle in a jar, because she did not trust her torches in a draft.

At the top she found the pane cracked and the flame cold. She had a spare pane, in a box. She took it out. But it did not fit. It was a size too large.

She stood and thought. It was a dark night, and the towers on either side were waiting. If the lamp did not blink, they would think something had gone wrong. They would send word by runner. Someone would climb a hill in the dark.

She took off her scarf. It was a long scarf, red, and her mother had made it. She tied it round the cracked pane. She held a candle behind it.

The light came out red and soft. It was not the lamp's own light. But it was a light.

She blinked it, slowly, long and short, the way the cards say. All night she held the candle and blinked the scarf.

In the morning, the next tower blinked back. It said: seen. All night.

The keeper sat down on the stair. She was very tired. She was also very proud. She went to bed and slept until noon.

That is the first part. The last part, which Pell made up, is that the scarf is still on the pane. He says it makes the lamp look warm. He says in winter it makes the lamp look like a hug.
%%
Story: the well that went quiet
In the first year after the archive opened, the well at the edge of Lowmarsh ran dry. The reeds around it turned brown. The frogs stopped singing. The people stood round the empty stones and said nothing for a whole morning.
A woman called Dunna went down the ladder with a bucket and a lamp. At the bottom she found the stones dry, and a crack in the side as wide as her hand. She sat on the last rung and thought about it.
Then she went up and got a sack of clay, and a basket of the reeds from the bank, and she went down again. She packed the crack with clay and reeds, the way her grandmother had packed the boat seams. She sang while she did it, so the work would go slowly and well.
The next morning there was a little water in the bottom. The morning after, a little more. By the end of the week the well was half full, and cold, and so clear you could see the lamp in it.
Dunna said the well had only needed someone to go down and look. The old people said the well had been listening all along, and had been waiting for her to sing. Both of them are still said at Lowmarsh, and both are true enough.
The well is still there, by the boardwalk. Children are told to look down it on a sunny day. You can see your face in the water, and the frogs, and a bit of sky.
%%
Story: A Gift for the Cold
Told by Una at Copperhold

In the cold season, a child came to Una's cider stall. The child had no coin and watched the steam rise from the warm jug.

Una poured a little cider into a cup. "This is a gift," she said.

The child held the cup in both hands. "I have a gift too," the child said. Then the child gave Una an apple with one bite missing.

Una looked at the apple. "You have already tasted it."

"I had to know it was good," said the child.

Una laughed and set the apple by her cups. She drank the cider herself, because the child was too young for it.

When the child came back the next market day, Una gave a small loaf of bread with the cup. The child brought a clean red ribbon in return.

The ribbon was tied around the cider jug all winter. It made the jug easy to find, and it made Una think of the child whenever the market was cold.
%%
Story: the gift of the green boots
Tilly the miner tells this to children at the hamlet near Saltreach, in spring, when the paths are wet.

An old woman once gave a boy a pair of boots. They were green, and they were the best boots in the hamlet. The woman said they would never wear out.

The boy was proud. He wore them to school, to the fair, to the shore. For a whole week he walked on dry sand and on the road, and the boots were perfect.

Then came the rains. The boy walked out in the first mud, and the boots filled with water. He walked home with his socks squelching and his face red.

"You told me they would never wear out," he said to the old woman.

"They will not wear out," she said. "But I did not say they would keep out water. You never tried them in the wet."

She was right. He had worn them only on dry days. He did not know what they would do in the rain.

The next day, she gave him a second gift, a tin of grease. He rubbed it into the boots. After that, they kept the wet out too.

Tilly says: try your gift on the thing it will meet, before you meet it.

Story: the three keepers of the stair
The stair up the north tower has ninety rungs in the ladder and one hundred and four steps in the stone part below it. Every keeper has counted them. Every keeper has got a different number.
The first keeper of the north tower was a man named Orsen. He counted the steps every morning on his way up, out loud, so that the stone would know he was there. He got a hundred and four every time, which he thought was the proper number, and he was proud of his stair.
The second keeper was a woman named Brenna. She did not count the steps. She counted the lamps, because she thought the lamps were more important. There were thirty-three lamps on the stair, one at every turn. Brenna said that the stair was only a way to reach the lamps, and that a keeper who knew the lamps would never need to count the steps.
The third keeper was Hetta, who had made the glass. Hetta counted both, the steps and the lamps, and the rungs, and the panes, and the turns in the wind. She wrote them all in a book, and the book had so many numbers in it that she could not finish reading it. She said the numbers were a kind of map. She said that if she knew the stair in every detail, she could walk it blind on a night with no lamp. Nobody ever tested that. Hetta did not like tests.
When Hetta grew old, a visiting tutor came up the stair with a list of questions. The tutor asked her: how many steps are there? Hetta said a hundred and four. The tutor asked: how many rungs? Hetta said ninety. The tutor asked: how many lamps? Hetta said thirty-three, and then she said: and one more, at the top, which only the keeper knows about.
The tutor asked: what is it? Hetta said: it is the lamp that I light when the others are out. Then she would not say anything more.
When Hetta died the keepers searched the tower for the extra lamp. They found the rungs and the steps and the thirty-three lamps. They did not find a thirty-fourth. Some say Hetta was joking, as she often did, to frighten tutors. Some say the lamp is in the lamp room behind the glass, where the ring is. Some say it is only a number, and the number is the keeper.
The new keeper counts the stair every morning, out loud, because it is a habit and because Orsen did it. He gets a hundred and four. Then he goes up and lights the lamps, one by one. At the top he stops and looks at the glass for a while, in case there is one more.
%%
Story: The wheel that wanted a rest

Tam told this story by the well at Kettle Ridge.

In spring, the windmill wheel broke with a crack, and the mill stopped turning. The miller put up a board that said, "No grain today."

The wheel was tired of turning, it said. It had watched the wind push it round through every season. It wanted to see the fields.

The miller took the wheel down and set it by the barn. Pip helped him carry it, though Pip said a wheel was heavier than it looked.

All morning the wheel watched the wheat field, the beetroot field, and the potatoes and carrots below. It saw the ditch shine in the sun. It saw a rabbit sit beside the hedge and wash its face.

"That is a fine day," said the wheel.

The rabbit looked at it. "You have not moved all day," it said.

"I am resting," said the wheel.

"So am I," said the rabbit. "But I am still a rabbit."

At noon, the miller and Tam mended the broken wheel. They fitted a new spoke and checked the rim. Pip held the pins and dropped one in the grass.

They found the pin under the scarecrow's foot. Then the wheel went back to the mill.

When the wind rose, the wheel turned again. It squeaked at every turn.

"Did you enjoy the fields?" Tam asked.

"I did," said the wheel. "But from now on, I will look at them while I work."

Pip says the wheel has never once thanked the rabbit.

The Kettle Ridge spring market
The market at Kettle Ridge is held in the yard by the well, in the second month, when the beetroot is small and the wheat is green. There are stalls of planks on barrels. There is a pig on a rope. There are three hives by the flowers and a woman selling honey in clay pots.

Tam brings the first beetroot. They are not good beetroot. They are thin and pale. He sells them anyway, cheap, and says, "Next month they will be better." He says this every year.

Pip carries the baskets. Pip is careful with the baskets. Once a basket tipped and the beetroot rolled into the well, and Tam had to fish them out with a hook, and the whole market watched. Now Pip carries the baskets very slowly and very straight, and everyone cheers when it reaches the stall.

Finch has a stall of sharpening stones. He sharpens knives for a coin. He tells stories for free, but people give him coins anyway.

There is a fiddle and a drum. There is a contest to guess the weight of a pumpkin. Bel won it one year, visiting from Three Wells, and she still talks about it.

At dusk the lamps go up along the ridge road, one every twelve blocks, the way the Long Road does. The windmills squeak on every turn. Nobody minds. It is the sound of home.

Story: the road that went around
Our hamlet is one hour's walk from the crossroads at Three Wells.

When the new road was built, the builders were told to go straight from our hamlet to the town. So they started at our gate and went straight.

There was a hill in the way. They went straight up it. There was a pond. They went straight through it. There was Mother Tuck's cow. They asked her to move. The cow did not move.

So the road goes up the hill, and through the shallow end of the pond, and in a gentle bend around the cow. Mother Tuck is very proud. She says it is the cow's road.
