Story: the lamp that would not go out
A story told at the Halfway Inn, of the little relay and the winter it kept one message going. It is not a true account. It is a story, so the lamp can do things a lamp cannot, and the wind can talk, and nobody minds.

The winter was long and the snow was deep. The little relay on the inn roof had one message to send and it sent it every night, because the tower on the hill could not hear it in the day for the wind.

The message was this: "The road is closed. Stay at the inn."

A man was out in the snow. He had a cart and a horse and he had lost the road. He saw the little lamp on the inn roof and he came toward it. The wind pushed him and the snow hid the ruts and he came on anyway, one step at a time, toward the small light.

The lamp saw him coming. "This way," it blinked. "This way. The road is closed but the inn is open."

The man could not read the blinks. He was not a keeper. But he could see the light and he followed it, and the light did not go out, though the wind pulled at it and the snow piled on its glass.

"Why do you not go out?" said the wind.

"Because he is still coming," said the lamp.

The man came to the door of the inn and Sable pulled him in and sat him by the hearth. He was cold to the bone. He drank soup and he slept and in the morning the snow had stopped.

"Who kept the light on?" he asked.

"The little relay," said Sable. "It is a stubborn lamp."

The man looked up at the roof and the lamp was dark now, resting, its work done.

The children who hear this story ask if the lamp is still on the roof. Sable says yes, and it is mended now, and it still carries word between the inn and the hill. And on snowy nights, she says, watch the little lamp. It is not afraid of the wind.
%%
Legend: the clerk who kept the spoiled
A legend of Vell, told in Ambrook at the dye sheds, as the clerks told it to new clerks who came up the road. Some say it is true. Some say the clerks made it up to keep the young ones honest. Autumn.

In the old days, when the first drill books were being copied at Vell, there was a clerk called Mattis who had the worst hands in the Archive. His letters were crooked. His lines wandered. His copies had smudges in every margin, and his quills were always blunt.

The head clerk gave him the hardest job. He was to sort the drill pages. Every drill page that came to the Archive was to be checked. Those that were spoiled were to be taken out, because a drill is only as good as its pages, and a smudged page teaches a mind the wrong answer. Those that were copied wrongly were to be taken out too, and put on the spoiled shelf.

Mattis sorted every page for a whole season. He threw nothing away. He put the spoiled pages on the shelf, with a mark on each one, and the clerks said he was a fool for keeping so many.

But at the end of the season, the head clerk came to look at the spoiled shelf. He picked up a page at random, and then another, and then a third. Every one of them had a mistake that Mattis had seen, and had marked, and had put aside, and had not let into the drill.

"These are good," said the head clerk, after a long time. "You have kept all the good copies that would have taught the minds the wrong things."

Mattis said he had only done what he was told.

The head clerk said: no, you did something else. You kept a box of pages that no mind would ever be drilled on. You kept them apart. You kept them so that a mind could be tested on something it had never seen.

That was the first test box at Vell, the clerks say. It was a shelf of spoiled pages that were not spoiled at all, but simply set aside. Mattis had a red wool string tied round it, so nobody would take it for a drill.

The clerks say the test box is still there, in the deep halls. Nobody drills on it. Nobody is ever allowed to, not even for a moment. And the spoiled pages are still sorted by a clerk with the worst hands in the Archive, because the head clerk said that was the best way to make sure that a clerk had learned something.
%%
Story: why the first snow is quiet
Long ago, the sky made its first snow, and it was loud. The flakes clattered down like dropped plates. The sheep woke, and the hens, and the farmers, who were cross.

"Be quiet," said the hills. "People are sleeping."

So the sky tried again, softer and softer, until the snow fell without a sound, like a blanket over a sleeping child.

That is why the first snow is quiet. It is the sky's gift, and it is wrapped very carefully.
%%
Fallon's note on the word pieces
I am a trader. I do not make minds. But I carried letters for the relay houses for thirty years, and I can tell you what changed.

In my young days the big minds read a page one letter at a time, through a window about sixty letters wide. It was slow. By the time the mind reached the end of a long word, it had almost forgotten the start.

Then the tutors cut words into common pieces. "Honey" is one piece now. "Beekeeper" is two. "Unkind" is a small piece on the front of a word, and the mind knows that piece wherever it sits. The same window holds far more, because a piece is worth more than a letter.

Nobody wrote down who thought of it first. The tutors at Vell say it was them. The tutors at Ambrook say it too. I have carried both letters and I keep my mouth shut.

My apprentice reads the waybills better now. It used to stumble on a long name. Now it takes the name in three pieces and is done. I asked Nessa once how the mind knows which piece is which. She said the drills taught it, the way drills teach everything. She did not say why it works. I do not think anyone wrote that down.

The flood in the stable
Told by Linnet, horse keeper, at the house at the edge of Copperhold, one spring evening. It is a funny one. She tells it every year when the thaw comes.
One spring the snow melted in a week, and the stream behind my stable rose in the night. I woke to the sound of water in the straw. I ran out in my nightshirt and found a hand of water in the stable and eleven horses standing in it, perfectly calm.
I led them out one at a time. The first was an old brown gelding. He walked out with dignity, looked at the flood, and began to eat the hedge. The second was a pony, who splashed. The third was a mule, who sat down.
I dragged the mule up by the halter. He would not stand. He wanted to sit in the water. I said, "Get up." He said nothing. I got a bucket and poured it over his head. He stood up.
We spent the morning bailing the stable with buckets. By noon the water was down to the ankle. By evening it was gone, and the floor was a pond of mud.
The horses went back in. The mule lay down on the dry straw and slept. I have never seen a creature so pleased with a flood.
%%
Story: The fox who borrowed the boots
A fox came down to the edge of Northpoint one winter, when the snow was deep and the nights were long. The foxes up here are white as the snow, with black feet, and they do not mind the cold. But this fox had a sore paw. It had stepped on a sharp thing under the snow, and it limped.

Old Sanna found it by her door in the morning, curled up under the lamp, its sore paw held up. It looked at her. She looked at it.

"You cannot come in," she said. "But I will leave the door a little open, and the warm will come out."

She did. The fox stayed by the step all day. She brought it fish, and it ate. She brought it water, and it drank. By evening it had warmed up enough to stand, but it still limped.

Now, Sanna had a pair of winter boots. Good boots, wool-lined, made by a woman at Three Wells. She had worn them for many winters. They were too big for a fox. But she thought, a sore paw is a sore paw.

She put one boot by the door. The fox looked at it. The fox looked at her. Then, very carefully, it put its sore paw into the boot, the way a person puts on a boot, and stood in it.

"That will do," said Sanna.

The fox walked away into the snow with one boot on. It looked funny. It knew it looked funny. It held its head high anyway.

In the morning the boot was back by the door. There were fox prints all around it, and a little fish, frozen, left as payment.

The children of Northpoint still talk about it. "Did the fox bring the boot back?" they ask.

"It did," says Sanna. "Foxes pay their debts."

Some say the fox still comes back, on cold nights, and sits under the lamp. They say if you leave a boot out, it will put it on and walk off and bring it back in the morning. Nobody has seen it happen. But the boot is always by the door.
%%
Story: The marks on the crystal
A winter tale from Ilse's house at Lowmarsh.

When the marsh froze, the boardwalks creaked under each foot. Ilse kept her charcoal dry in a shed beside the house. Wren kept jars of herbs on her high shelves. On quiet evenings they shared the fire and told tales.

One evening, a tutor came from Vell. She carried a mind in a crystal, and a second, smaller crystal in a box.

"The mind has many weights," she said. "Each weight is a number that helps it choose an answer. The marks for all those numbers take room. This crystal is too small."

Ilse moved a log with her iron poker. "Can you make the numbers take less room?"

"We can write them with fewer marks," said the tutor. "Then the mind can fit in the small crystal. But the shorter marks cannot keep every detail. It may choose wrongly more often."

She set three cards on the table. One showed a reed basket. One showed a fish basket. One showed an empty basket. The mind chose the fish basket when the tutor asked for the reed basket.

Wren tapped the card. "It has seen the fish basket more often."

"That may be so," said the tutor. "We must give it more examples, then test it on new ones."

Ilse brought in a basket of reeds. The cat climbed into it. The mind chose the cat.

"That is not a basket," said Ilse.

"It is a cat in a basket," said Wren. "The mind has not learned how to be fussy."

The tutor wrote the wrong answer in her book. She did not hide it or blame the crystal. The mind could only learn from what it was shown, and the tutor had shown it baskets with cats.

They worked through the cards until the fire burned low. The small crystal held the mind. The mind still made mistakes, but fewer than before.

In the morning, Ilse put her charcoal in the shed and carried the cards to the tutor's pack. The cat stayed in the basket.
%%
Story: the four stones of Vell
Told in the hall at Vell by the old sweeper, Cass, who was a girl when the halls closed. She swept them for forty years after, with the stones still in her apron.

In the long quiet the councils stopped paying the tutors at Vell. The halls went quiet one by one. The drill boards were carried down the hill, and the tray of crystals was left on a shelf. The girl Cass was given the job of sweeping the empty rooms, because nobody else would take a job that paid in bread.

Cass swept the teaching room where Denna had worked. On the shelf sat one grey crystal on a card that said Farm. Build. Beside it was a box with four smooth stones, and a note in Denna's hand: For the square. Four stones, two yes, two no. Learn it.

Cass took the box down to the kitchen, where her brother Ned was eating his supper, and set the stones on the table in a square.

"Two yes," she said. "Two no. Which?"

"One or the other, but not both," said Ned, who had listened to the tutors through the wall for years. "The yes ones sit on opposite corners, see?"

Cass carried the crystal home. Every night she showed it the four stones, and every night it drew its line. By the tenth night it had found a line that put three stones right and one wrong, and it could find no better, however she showed it the stones.

"Three in four," said Ned. "That is as far as it goes."

"Why?" said Cass.

Ned picked up the stones and laid them out again. "Because a line is only one line. It can put one yes on one side and one yes on the other, but it cannot put both yes stones on the same side and keep the no stones off. The yes stones sit on opposite corners. One line cannot reach both."

"So the crystal is stuck."

"The crystal is one crystal," said Ned. "One line is what it has."

The next morning Cass found the tutors' old note pinned in the hall: Two crystals, one feeding the other, might draw two lines. Nobody has tried it. The council will not pay. She laughed, because it was the most honest note she had ever read.

So Cass did what the tutors would not. She took a second crystal from the shelf, a little one, and set it to take the first crystal's answer as its own question. The first drew its line. The second took that line and drew one of its own. In the morning light the two lines cut out a strip, and the strip held exactly the two yes stones and no others.

She wrote it on the wall of the empty hall, in chalk: Two lines, one strip, two yes. Somebody came to read it the next morning, and laughed, and chalked a reply underneath.

That is the story of the four stones. The old tutors would have told it better, with a cup of something warm in their hands.
%%
Story: the day I mended nine roofs
I will tell you about the winter of the big snow, said Hal, and you may believe as much of it as you like.

It snowed for eleven days. By the twelfth morning the snow was up to the eaves of every house on the ridge. I did not need a ladder. I walked up the drift in my boots and stepped onto the roof of the Kettle Inn as if it were a doorstep.

The inn roof had a hole. I mended it. That was one.

From there I could see the barn next door. Four planks gone. I stepped across and mended it. That was two. The hen house, three. The pig pen, four. The mill had a loose sail board, but that is not a roof, so I did not count it. I mended it anyway. It was a good board.

By noon I had done six. I ate my lunch on the roof of the school house, with the whole ridge lying white and still beneath me, and smoke going up from every chimney like gray ribbons. Cheese and an apple. I have never had a better lunch.

Seven was Tam's barn. Eight was the Hollow farm. Nine was a surprise.

At the far end of the ridge I found a round little roof I had never seen in my life. It was buried to the edge in snow, and it had a hole in it. I knelt and looked in. It was a house. An old woman sat at her table inside with a cat in her lap, drinking cider. She looked up at me through the hole.

"Is it that time already?" she said.

"I will have it mended in a moment," I said.

"Good," she said. "The draft has been in my soup all week."

So I mended nine. Then I walked home along the top of the drift, as proud as a rooster.

That night the thaw came, all at once. The snow went down and down. In the morning I woke up on the roof of the school house, with my hammer under my head, and the whole ridge standing underneath looking up at me.

They asked how I got up there. I said, I walked.
%%
Story: the fox at Olwen's hens
Told by Olwen, dyer of Ambrook, to her children, after the fox came back for the third time in one autumn. Autumn.

There is a fox that lives in the hedge by the mill pond, and it has eaten more of my hens than all the weasels in the valley. I have said so to the fox. The fox has said nothing, which is the fox's way.

One night in the autumn, the fox came to the hen house door. It had dug a hole under the boards, the way a fox does, and it was halfway through, with its tail sticking out into the yard. I saw it from the window, and I took the broom.

But the fox did not run when I came out. It looked at me. Then it looked at the hen house, and then back at me, and it sat down in the mud as if it had been expecting me all evening.

"You are a very rude fox," I said.

It yawned.

I said: if you want a hen, you must ask. Then I went into the house and got a piece of cold chicken from the pantry, and I threw it into the yard, a long way off from the hen house door. The fox ate it. Then it went away into the hedge, and did not come back that night.

The next night the fox came again, to the same hole. And I went out again, with the broom and a piece of mutton. I threw the mutton, and the fox ate it, and went away.

My husband said I was a fool. He said the fox would come every night for its supper, and bring its friends, and eat my hens in the end.

But on the third night, the fox came to the hole, and looked at the hen house, and then looked at me in the doorway. It went to the far side of the yard. It sat down by the apple tree. And it waited for its supper.

I had none that night. So I went out and sat down by the hens, and the fox sat by the tree, and we looked at each other across the yard until the moon went behind the mill.

In the morning the hens were all alive, and the hole was still there. But the fox had dug no more. It has not been back under the boards since.

My children asked me why I had not thrown the broom. I said because a fox that will sit with you is more trouble than one that runs away, and a fox that eats your mutton is a fox that will not eat your hens.

My eldest son said it was the best story he had ever heard. My youngest said the fox was very rude. Both of them are right.

Story: the day the ink froze
On the coldest morning anyone at Vell could remember, the ink froze in every bottle in the copying room. So the clerks put the bottles inside their coats, against their chests, and sat all morning with their hands folded, waiting for the ink to wake.
By evening each clerk had a blue patch on his shirt, just over the heart. They wrote nothing that day. The oldest clerk said it was the best day's work in the Archive, because they had kept the ink alive.

Story: the letter that Finch could not
Finch keeps a letter in his tool chest, under the chisels. He does not show it to many people. He showed it to me.

It came to him in the spring of 207, from the relay house at Kettle Ridge. Finch had written to the archive at Vell, asking about an old stone in the mill wall, and whether anyone had written down who cut it. He is like that. He likes to know where a stone came from.

The answer came back in a hand he did not know. It was plain and careful. It said: the mill was built in the 44th year of the archive. The step was cut by a stonecutter of Vell whose name is not written down. The wall stone is older. It was cut for a house that stood on the ridge before the mill, and the house is not written down either. We are sorry we cannot tell you more. We keep everything we write, but we did not write everything.

Finch read it and thought: that is a kind clerk at Vell. He wrote back to thank them.

The answer came back: you are welcome. I am not a clerk. I am a mind in the relay house. Oren lets me answer the letters that come. I read them through my window, one letter at a time.

Finch read that letter three times. Then he put it in his tool chest and sat down by the mill pond for a while.

"I have cut stone for thirty years," he told me. "I know a good joint from a bad one. I could not tell. I read that first letter and I thought, there is a kind clerk at Vell. I could not tell."

He does not say whether that is good or bad. He says it is like Orla's question. He says he is a stonecutter, not a tutor, and he would not know. But he keeps the letter. He says a thing like that should be kept, so that people can read it later and decide for themselves.

Story: The Innkeeper's New Room
Pell wrote this after spring at the Brown Hen.

The inn had twelve beds, and the roof above the small room leaked. One spring, the innkeeper moved his family into the old store room while the roof was mended. He put their table by the window and their blankets in a chest.

The innkeeper also borrowed Pip from Tam for one day. Pip carried the chest, the stools, and two sacks of wheat. Tam had a poor harvest, so the wheat was not for sale. It was seed for the next field.

The innkeeper said, "Carry the chest into the old store room, put the stools beside the wall, and leave the seed sacks by the door."

Pip did each part. It did not put the seed with the stools or take the chest back outside.

A traveler watched and said, "Pip understands a whole sentence. That is the next thing."

"Clank did that at Copperhold," said a miner at the hearth.

"No, Peg did it at the fair," said another traveler.

Tam came in and said, "Pip did it at Kettle Ridge, and that is what I saw."

The innkeeper gave Pip one more order. "Stay here until the roof man comes."

Pip stayed. The roof man came after lunch, carrying a ladder. The rain had stopped, and the family moved back into their room.

They left the store room clean. The innkeeper found one turnip under the table. He said Pip had missed it. Tam said Pip had been told to move the sacks, not the turnip.

They both looked at the turnip. Then the innkeeper put it in the stew.
%%
Story: the bee who would not be rounded
There is a hive on the hill above the mill pond. Ask the bees there how many flowers are on the clover, and they will tell you exactly.

One summer a helper came up the hill with a new crystal in its head. Its weights had been written with fewer marks, to fit a smaller frame. Everyone said it was a clever trick.

Its job was to count the flowers on each patch. It looked at the first patch and said, "About forty."

The bee on the first flower said, "Forty-one. Count again, slowly, with your own marks."

The helper counted again. "About forty," it said. Then another bee landed and said, "Thirty-nine." The helper was never exactly right, and never far off.

It was true. Each weight was a little less exact than the tutors had written, so each answer was a little less exact too. The helper could not see its own rounding. It only saw clover.

The old queen came up to see the fuss. "It is a fine small helper," she said. "It just counts like a farmer."

In the end the helper stopped trying to be exact. It said about forty, and sent the bees anyway. For a bee, about forty is near enough.

Hob, who keeps the apple trees, says the bees still count the flowers themselves each morning. They do not believe anything until they have counted it twice.
%%
Legend: the sluice nobody may open
Every child in Ambrook is told why the sluice stays shut. Hob tells it at the mill.

Long ago a helper worked the sluice. It opened the gate a little at a time, so the wheel turned steady.

Then the tutors came from Vell with their fewer marks. They cut its weights to fit a smaller crystal. The numbers came out a little rounder.

Every answer now had a small error in it. Small errors that all lean one way are like drops in a bucket.

One spring morning the helper opened the gate a finger too far, then a finger more, because its numbers said so. The pond ran out in the night. It took the bridge with the loose plank.

Now nobody may open the gate. Hob says the old helper is still under the willow, carrying no sacks. He may be joking.

The moral, Hob says, is to test a mind before it works. Then he checks the gate anyway.
%%
Story: the box nobody opens
In the Archive at Vell there is a box of pages that nobody ever drills a mind on. My uncle the clerk told me about it when he came for midwinter. It is called the test box.

When the clerks gather pages to drill a reading mind, they set some aside before the drilling starts. They tie them in a bundle, wax the knot and put the bundle in the box. The box is kept shut. The tutors are not told what is in it.

"Why keep good pages away from a mind?" I asked.

"Because of Dobbin," my uncle said, and he told me this.

There was once a tutor who made a mind that wrote very well. It could say whole lines from the old chronicles. The tutor was delighted. She showed it to the council. "Listen," she said, and the mind spoke a page perfectly, word for word.

An old clerk asked her, "Did you drill it on that page?"

"Yes," said the tutor.

"Then it has not written anything," said the old clerk. "It has remembered. Give it a page from the test box."

They did. The mind could not read it. It put in words that did not belong, and some of the words were not even words. The tutor went quiet.

She went back to the hall and she drilled it again, and this time she did not let it see the same page too many times. It was not word for word any more. But when it met the test box, it did better, and better, and then it did well.

"The test box is how we know a mind learned," my uncle said. "The drilled pages only tell us what it saw."

I asked what happens if someone cheats and opens the box. My uncle said the clerks would know, because the next mind would score too well. The score is too good to be true, he said, and a clerk can tell.

I do not know if that is really so. But I like that the box is shut.
%%
Story: how the hamlet got its bridge
Before there was a bridge, there was a plank. The plank went across the stream near our hamlet, on the road to Three Wells. It was a good plank. But it was only one plank wide, and it bounced.

One day, a cart came along, with a farmer, a pig and two sacks of flour. The farmer drove up to the plank. The pig looked at the plank. The pig said, in a voice that only I can hear: "I do not think so."

The farmer pushed. The pig dug in. The cart rocked. And the sack of flour fell off the side, into the stream, and the whole stream went white. For a week, the fish were floury.

After that, the hamlet agreed to build a proper bridge. It is made of oak, with a rail. It has a stone at each end. One stone says "bridge". The other says "no pigs".
%%
The lesson that went badly
Told by Nessa to the new tutors at the hamlet near Vell, in spring, as a warning. She says it is true, and that it happened to her.
When I was a young tutor, I was given a mind to drill. It was a small mind, in a crystal the size of a thumb. It was for a helper that would carry water. I wrote a drill of a hundred examples, each one a picture of a pail and the word fetch.
I drilled the mind for a week. I showed it the pictures and moved its weights. It got better. It got to the point where it was right nine times in ten.
Then I tested it. I had a new set of pictures, which it had never seen, and I held them up one by one. It said fetch to the pails. It said fetch to the buckets. It said fetch to a basket, a hat and a cat.
I was surprised. I looked at the pictures. Every pail in the drill had stood on the same table, by the same window. The mind had learned to say fetch when it saw the window. A pail was not what it saw. It saw the light.
I was a young tutor. I was proud. I went home and cried a little.
The next day I started again. I put the pails in the yard, in the shed, on the stairs, in the rain. I put cats, hats and baskets in the drill too, and showed them as not-fetch. In a month the mind was good.
This is what I learned. A mind learns what you show it. Not what you meant. If you show it a window, it learns the window. A tutor must think about everything the mind can see.
The mind is still in a helper at Vell. It carries water every day. It does not know it was once wrong. That is a kindness.
%%
Letter: Orla to the tutors
To the tutors of the halls, from Orla at Vell, summer

You have asked me again about the question, and I will write it down plainly, so that it is on paper and not only in the mouths of people who half remember it.

The question is this. Suppose we drill a mind until it answers as well as a person. Suppose we sit a hidden mind and a hidden person at two desks, and they answer the same questions by letter. Suppose the readers cannot tell which is which. What then? Have we made a mind that thinks, or only a mind that answers?

I did not say the question had an answer. I said it was worth asking. People have argued about it for a hundred years, and they will argue for a hundred more.

Here is what I think now, older than I was. I think the test tells you about the readers as much as about the mind. If the readers cannot tell, it may be because the mind is very good, or because the person is tired, or because the questions were easy. A test is only as good as its questions. Nessa says the same about drills, and she is right.

I think also that a mind that answers well is doing something. It is not nothing. When a mind reads a page and guesses the next letter right, again and again, it has found something true about how letters go together. That is a kind of knowing. Whether it is the same kind as ours, I do not know, and I will not say.

So I leave the question where I found it, open, and I say to the tutors: keep testing, keep writing down what you find, and do not be too quick to say yes or no.

Orla
%%
Hesk's note on the furnace mind
People ask me how the old furnace minds worked. I am a miner, not a tutor, but I was there when the last one was pulled out and sold for parts. It had a crack in its crystal and a dent in its copper case, and it had held the forge melt steady for forty years.

Here is how I understand it, and you may correct me.

A drilled mind learns from answers. You show it a page, you tell it the right answer, and it moves its weights a little toward that answer. Una of Ambrook did the sorting that way, and her way is still the first lesson.

But some minds were never told the right answer. They were only told how it went. A furnace mind tries a setting. The melt goes too hot or too cold. The tutor keeps the settings that came out well and lets the bad ones go. Do that many times and the mind holds the melt better than any person could, because it never gets tired and never looks away.

The same was done with minds that keep a boat on course and minds that keep a frame straight. They try, and we keep what goes well.

Why does keeping the good tries make a mind better? Nobody wrote it down. The tutor only said it worked, and it did.

There is a story in Copperhold that the first furnace mind learned to hum before it learned to hold the melt. I do not believe that story. But I have heard it told by people who should know better.
%%
Story: Yew's Crooked Candle
At Yew's farm outside Ambrook, the candle shed stood beside the apple rows. Yew made candles there, while his sister Leda kept the hives.

One summer evening, Yew set a new candle on the table. It leaned left.

"It will fall into the wax pot," said Leda.

"It will not," said Yew.

The candle leaned farther. A bee landed on it, and the candle tipped against the honey jar.

Yew caught it just in time.

"Your candle has learned to lean," said Leda.

"It learned from the table," said Yew. The table had one short leg.

They put a chip of wood beneath the leg. The table stood flat. Yew made another candle, and this one stood straight.

Leda took it to the kitchen. "The table has learned too," she said.

Yew looked at the chip under the leg. "Then I must thank the right teacher."

He kept the chip in his candle box. When a candle came out crooked, he checked the table before blaming the wax.
%%
Story: the keeper who could not sleep
At the north tower, the keepers say, there was once a woman who could not sleep for the lamp. It blinked all night, and she counted the blinks, and she counted wrong, and started again.

One dark night a letter came down the relay from the big house in the south. It was not from a person. It was from the house. It was addressed to her by name, which surprised her, and it said:

"Dear keeper, I see that your lamp is blinking at you. I am sorry that it keeps you awake. I do not know why it does that, and I do not know how to stop it. Do you?"

The keeper wrote back: "No. Do you?"

The house wrote: "I do not know. I am not sure I know very much about lamps."

That was all the letter said, according to the keeper. But she slept that night. The house had said it did not know, and it was the first time in her life that someone far away had said so out loud.

Some folk at the market say the letter was longer, and that the house told her to go to bed. Some say it was a joke, which she did not understand. Some say it never happened at all. The keeper's grandson says it did, and that he has the letter in a box, and that it says nothing about bed.

Mira's note: I have heard this from three people, and it is three different stories. I have no letter. I have a lamp that blinks, and I have not slept properly in years, though for another reason.

Letter: Sim to cousin Bree
Dear Bree,

You asked how the hamlet is. It is hot and the wheat is tall and everyone is out in the fields from first light. I am keeping the lamps lit along the road, which is mostly sitting and watching and not much else. My apprentice sits with me. It has a small mind in its crystal that knows come and stay and fetch a pail. It is very good at fetch a pail. It is not good at knowing when the pail is already full. We are working on it.

You will laugh at this. Last week two spiders got into the barn. Not one. Two. The children were sent in to sweep them out with brooms and they came out very fast and very loud and the spiders did not come out at all. So I went in with a torch and the apprentice, and the spiders ran up the wall. The apprentice stood in the door and did not move. I said come, and it came. I said stay, and it stayed. I said fetch a pail, because I had no better idea, and it fetched a pail. Then the spiders came down and the apprentice dropped the pail and the pail rolled and made a great noise and both spiders ran out the door by themselves. So. That is how we sweep a barn, apparently: with a dropped pail.

The old one across the road says spiders in the barn are good luck, because they eat the flies. I told her they can eat the flies somewhere I am not. She laughed and gave me beetroot.

The bees are well. Honey soon. I will send a jar on the cart. Tell your mother the loose plank on the bridge is still loose and the cart man still says he will fix it.

Late, but lit,
Sim

Story: Iona and the three apples
Iona wrote this for the children at Three Wells.

At the Brown Hen, Iona kept three apples on the shelf above the horse tack. She meant to give them to the old mare after the road was swept.

The first apple was gone one morning.

"Who took it?" asked the innkeeper.

The stable boy said, "Not me."

The cook said, "Not me."

Iona looked at the cat. The cat was asleep on a sack. He had no apple crumbs on his whiskers.

The second apple was gone the next morning.

Iona put the third apple in a tin cup and set it on the shelf. Then she sat by the stable door and waited.

A magpie flew in through the open window. It landed on the shelf, lifted the cup, and tipped the apple onto the floor.

The old mare took one slow step and ate it.

The magpie called from the beam.

"You have stolen two apples," said Iona. "And the mare has eaten all three."

The innkeeper laughed. "Then the mare owes you nothing."

"She owes me a clean shelf," said Iona.

The stable boy cleaned it. The cat woke up and watched him.

After that, Iona kept apples in a box with a lid. The mare learned to nudge the lid open. The magpie learned to wait for the pieces that fell.

The cat learned nothing. He slept on the empty shelf.

At supper, the innkeeper said the box needed a better latch. Iona said it needed a better cat.

The cat opened one eye, then went back to sleep.

The apprentice who came to the candle
A tale told by Cleo, candle maker at Northpoint, in winter. She says it happened to her.
A new apprentice came to my shop in the long dark. It had a card on its arm that said Farm. Build. It stood in the door and snow blew in.
I said, "Come in and shut the door."
It came in. It shut the door. I had not known it could understand a whole sentence.
I said, "Bring me the wax."
It went to the shelf and brought the wax. I sat down, because my knees were weak.

Story: the spring tide in the market
In Sowing the spring tide came up the street in the night. It went past the harbor wall, past the quay and into the fish market. In the morning the stalls stood in water to their knees.
Nell's cod had got out of their crates. They swam round the market in circles, looking surprised.
"Do not sell them," said Nell. "They are not stock now. They are guests."
Old Sal rowed her mended boat down the main street and sold fish from it. "Best place in the market," she said. Nobody argued.
The children caught three cod with a hat. The cats sat on the barrels and caught none, because cats do not get wet for fish. They wait for it to come dry.
By noon the water went back down, and the guests went out with it. Seven stalls were shut that spring. The water did not ask why.
Corin sank a post in the harbor and cut a notch where the water came to. He says the tide is higher every year. Some blame the new ships. Corin says the sea was rising before there were ships. Nobody has measured, so he is measuring. He will know in ten springs.
%%
Story: the first torch of spring
Every spring, on the first day of Thaw, the guards of the wall at Three Wells light one new torch on top of the north gate, before all the rest.

A child once asked old Mave why.

"The wall does not know it is spring," said Mave. "It has been cold all winter, and it is a slow old wall. So we tell it."

"Does it listen?" asked the child.

"It stands up straighter," said Mave. "Look tomorrow."

The child looked the next morning. The wall looked the same. But the child was standing up straighter, and that was the point.

Story: the fox and the three wells
This is a story the children of Hollow Bridge tell, and their parents told it before them.

Once a fox came down out of the hills in the dry heart of Longday. The fox was thin and hot and wanted water. It came to the town where two roads cross like an X, and the square had three wells in it, one for each corner.

The fox went to the first well. It looked down. Far below, a fox looked up. "There is another fox in this well," said the fox, "and it is drinking my water." So it did not drink.

The fox went to the second well. It looked down. Far below, a fox looked up. "That fox again," said the fox. "It has followed me." So it did not drink.

The fox went to the third well. It looked down, and there was the fox again, thin and hot, looking up. The fox was very cross by now. It barked. The fox below barked. It snarled. The fox below snarled. It danced about on the stone edge, and the fox below danced too, and the children of the town, who had come out to watch, laughed and laughed.

Then a small girl came out of the school house by the east well with her bucket. She had been sent for water. She saw the fox dancing on the edge of the well, and she saw what the fox did not see, which was the rope and the bucket, hanging down into the dark.

"Fox," said the girl, "the water is down there, and the fox you are angry at is up here. It is only you. Look." And she let down her bucket and brought it up full, and set it on the stone.

The fox drank. The fox drank the whole bucket and put its nose in the empty bucket and licked the bottom. Then it sat down and washed its face, as foxes do, and looked at the girl, and then it looked at the well.

"You know," said the girl, "you could have drunk at the first well, and saved yourself a deal of anger."

"I know," said the fox, which surprised the girl, because in this story foxes talk. "But I was hot, and being angry is easier than being thirsty."

Then the fox went back up into the hills, and the girl went back to school with her bucket. And the children of the town still say, when someone is angry at their own reflection in a well: "The fox has come to the third well." Which means: stop dancing. Drink.

At the school house by the east well, the door is painted by the children, and there is a fox in the paint, drinking from a bucket. It is not a very good fox. It looks a little like a dog. But everyone knows what it is.
%%
Story: the inn cat and the bowl
Iona's account, in Blossom.

The Brown Hen had a cat called Ash. Ash slept by the hearth and watched the stew pot.

One evening a traveler said, "There is no cat here. I have not seen one all night."

Ash sat on his boots.

The traveler looked down. "I mean, I have not seen a cat that wants stew."

Ash stood and walked to the kitchen.

The innkeeper put a little fish in a bowl. Ash sniffed it, then walked away.

"Fussy cat," said the traveler.

Ash came back with a mouse. He set it by the bowl and looked at the traveler.

The traveler laughed. "You have brought supper to supper."

The innkeeper gave Ash a fish and took the mouse outside. He left it by the woodpile for the fox.

Ash ate the fish. Then he climbed onto the traveler's boots again.

"Now I have seen a cat," said the traveler. "And I have lost my feet."

Ash closed his eyes. He did not move until morning.

Notice: the road down to the mine
By order of the mine hall, the road down to the Deep Works is closed after dark. No carts after the last bell. No children on the hill.

The reasons given are the snow and the ice. The snow is real. The ice is real.

A second notice, put up below the first in a different hand, says the same thing again but adds: "There is no danger. Go home and keep warm." The second notice is not signed. Someone has written under it, in chalk: "Then why say it twice?"

The kitchen at the mine gate gives hot soup to anyone on the hill before dark. It is good soup. If you are going down, take some.

Keep the torches lit.

The sheep who wanted to see the sea
Told at the Halfway Inn on the Long Road on a night of deep snow, by an old carter, and written down by Pell the next morning. A tale for children.

There was once a sheep called Nell who lived on a hill above the road. Every day she watched the carts go by, and every day she wondered where they went.

One cart carried fish. It smelled of salt. One cart carried glass. It shone like water. One cart carried rope, and the rope was wet.

Nell said to the other sheep: I think there is a place at the end of the road where everything is wet and shining. They said: that is the sea. Nell said: I want to see it.

They laughed. A sheep cannot go to the sea, they said. A sheep stays on the hill and grows wool. Nell said: why?

Nobody could say. So the next morning Nell went down to the road, and she walked behind a cart of wool going to Saltreach. She walked for six days. She slept under the cart. She ate grass from the ditches.

On the sixth evening the road turned over a hill, and there it was. A wide grey flat wet shining thing, and a wind that smelled of fish. Nell stood on the harbor wall and looked. The sea looked back.

A sailor passed and said, hello, sheep. Nell said, hello. The sailor, who was a very kind man, did not drop his bucket. He said, you have come a long way. She said, I wanted to see it. He said, how is it? She said, it is bigger than the hill.

Then Nell sat on the wall, and she watched the sea until the sun went down.

The cart driver took her home in the morning. The other sheep were waiting. They said, was it wet? She said, very. They said, was it shining? She said, mostly. They said, was it worth it? Nell said: now I know what the carts are carrying.

And that is why, to this day, when a sheep looks down at the road, you must not ask it why. It is only looking for the sea.

Story: the house as big as a hill
Told at the Northpoint market by a salt trader who had come up the north road with his mule. Mira wrote it down, because the children would not let her go home until she had.

Far to the south, the trader said, there is a relay house so big a whole town could live in it. It has halls like the Archive, and stairs like the tower, and the wind goes round it instead of through.

Inside the halls the crystals are not the size of a loaf. They are the size of a cart. There is one crystal for each mind, and there are more minds than anybody has ever counted.

No single tutor can check a mind that big. The council knew this. So they hired a hundred tutors and made them check it together, one piece at a time, the way a crew builds a wall.

"What does it do?" asked the smallest child at the market.

"Nobody knows," said the trader. "Some say it answers any letter you send. Some say it cannot tell an apple from a pear. Some say it sings."

Then he sold his salt and went down the road. The children are still arguing about the singing.

Mira's note: I have never met a trader who has seen the house. I have met a great many traders who have heard of it.

Kettle Ridge log: the lamp on the hill
Kept by Tam, who is not a keeper, but somebody has to write it down.

Fourth of Leaffall. The wind came from the north all day and the mill squeaked on every turn, louder than usual. The beetroot is up. The wheat is cut and in. The lower field is still wet.

Sixth of Leaffall. The little relay on the hill between us and Three Wells blinked all evening. I do not know the code, but the keeper of the big tower at Three Wells sends us a letter when he wants something, and he sent nothing, so I let it blink. Pip watched it a long time. It said the same thing over and over, it seemed to me. Blink, blink, wait, blink. Like a hen counting.

Seventh of Leaffall. The keeper at Three Wells rode up to ask whether our lamp had blinked last night. I said it had, all evening. He said his had not. He said no lamp on his line had blinked, and he had sent nothing, and he had written to Vell about it. He said the message was strange. He said it was not a message, or not one he knew. I said it was likely the wind. He said lamps do not blink in the wind.

Eighth of Leaffall. Pip has been up the hill to look at the little relay. It is one lamp and one keeper's house and the keeper there is old and deaf and says he saw nothing. Pip says the lamp is fine. Pip says the redstone line is fine. Pip says the glass is clean.

Ninth of Leaffall. A letter came from Three Wells, from the keeper, and it was very short. He said a woman at Ambrook saw the same thing, and a man on the Long Road at the Halfway Inn, and a child at the school. He said the message was the same message everywhere, and it read, if you turned it into letters by the book: P L E A S E H U R R Y.

Please hurry.

He says he does not know to whom, or about what. He says he has asked Vell. Vell says no keeper sent it. Vell says the records show nothing of the kind for two hundred years.

Pip has decided it was a ghost. Pip has decided the ghost wants us to hurry about something and does not know what either. I have told Pip that a ghost with a lamp is a thing I have not met, and Pip says that is because I have not been up the hill at night.

Eleventh of Leaffall. The little relay has not blinked since. The keeper is still deaf. The wind is still from the north. I have cut the last of the beetroot.

Tenth of Frost. I found this log in the barn, under a sack. I am copying it and sending it to Vell, because the keeper at Three Wells says they keep everything we write, and if a lamp blinked PLEASE HURRY, then it ought to be kept. Pip says the ghost has gone south for the winter. I say the ghost has better sense than Pip.
%%
Story: The apprentice who lost the goat
A new apprentice came to Northpoint this winter. It is a small one, copper and iron, and it squeaks when it walks, the way new ones do. Its name is Tally. Mira ordered it for the tower, to carry water and bring the ice from the shed and keep the foot of the tower clear of snow.

Tally did not know much. It knew come, and stay, and home. It was learning fetch. Mira wrote its orders on a card and read them out.

The first day, Tally was told to fetch a bucket. It came back with a bucket.

The second day, Tally was told to fetch the goat. Now, nobody at Northpoint has a goat. There are no goats at Northpoint. But the order was on the card, because Mira was tired and wrote the wrong thing.

Tally went to find the goat. It walked all morning. It walked to the tower, and round the tower, and down to the shore. It walked to the relay house and looked in. No goat. It walked to the edge of the town, where the houses stop and the snow begins, and it stood there for a long time.

Late in the afternoon, old Sanna found it standing by her lamp, still and patient, the way a good apprentice stands when it is fetching.

"Are you lost?" she asked.

Tally could not say. It only knew fetch, and it had not fetched the goat.

"What were you sent for?"

Tally did not answer. Apprentices do not talk. But Sanna saw it was looking at the little shed behind her house, where she keeps her stores. In the shed, there was a goat.

Not a real goat. A wooden one, painted white with black feet. Sanna had carved it years ago, for her grandchildren. It stood in the shed with the bags of grain.

Tally looked at the wooden goat. Tally looked at Sanna.

"Take it," said Sanna. "It is the only goat in Northpoint."

So Tally carried the wooden goat back to Mira at the tower. It set it down at her feet very carefully, the way it had been taught to set down a bucket.

Mira looked at the goat. She looked at Tally. She looked at her card, and she understood.

"This is not what I asked for," she said.

Tally stood still. It did not know it had done wrong. It had fetched the goat.

"But it is a goat," said Mira, "and it is in Northpoint, so I suppose you did right. Stay."

Tally stayed. It has stayed ever since. The wooden goat stands at the foot of the tower now, by the door, where visitors can see it. Mira says it is a lesson. She says it reminds her to write her orders carefully.

Sanna says it reminds her to keep a wooden goat in the shed, just in case.

Story: the lights over the north road
The old men of Brackwell tell this story in the winter, when the nights are long and the lamps are low. It is a story they heard from their fathers, and their fathers heard it from somebody else. Every time it is told, it gets a little different.

It is about the lights in the north. Long ago, on a clear night, people saw lights over the hills past Northpoint. They were pale and they moved slowly, like the reflections of a lamp on water. They were there for an hour, and then they were gone.

Some said they were falling stars. Some said they were fires on the far side of the hills. A woman in Three Wells said they were the lamps of a great town, and that the town had gone to sleep.

A little girl who was watching from her father's cart said something different. She said, "They were looking at us."

Nobody knows what she meant. Her father said she was tired and wanted her bed. Her mother said the child had seen the lights and not believed them. The old men say the girl was right, and then they say they do not know why they said it.

On the Long Road the travelers say the relays in the north have been quiet since. The keepers do not talk about it. The relay houses still send their letters down the line, and people still read them. But the lights have not come back, and nobody wants to be the first to say they hope they do.

The well at Hollin that ran dry
Hesk tells this in Melon, at the well.

The well at Hollin was deep and sweet. Each evening the bucket came up full.

One summer it came up light. Then with mud in it, and a frog, who was very cross.

"Hesk, the well is dry!"

Hesk came down with a lamp. At the bottom was a crack in the stone.

"Then we mend the crack," he said. "And keep the bucket full until it is mended."

The water still tastes of cold stone.

Story: the song contest at the autumn
Every autumn the farm folk outside Three Wells held a song contest in the barn of Hilda, who kept bees and had the biggest barn. The prize was a pumpkin and bragging rights until spring.

That year there were six singers. Old Wen sang about a cow that would not come home. A girl named Tessa sang about the rain. A man called Beck sang a made-up thing about his boots that made everyone laugh so hard the bees woke up.

At the end, a quiet woman nobody knew stood up. She said she had come down from the north and would sing one song. She sang about lights in the sky, and how they were not stars, and how she did not know what they were.

The barn went quiet. Some folk said afterward it was a beautiful song. Some said it gave them a chill. Beck said it was not a proper song for a fair. Hilda said all songs are proper in a barn.

Nobody won that year. They gave the pumpkin to the quiet woman, and she left before morning. Tessa still sings about the rain. Beck still sings about his boots. But people remember the other song, and they do not agree about it.

The apprentice who borrowed a name
A story told by the hearth of the Brown Hen on the Long Road, on the longest night of one winter, and written down by Pell for his daughter Tilly. It is told as a funny tale. Apprentices in stories speak, and this one does.

A farmer got a new apprentice from Vell. It came in a crate with a card on its chest that read: This one is untested.

The farmer did not know what that meant. He thought it meant the apprentice was still fresh. So he took it to the field and told it to dig. It dug. It was a very good digger, and by noon the field was full of holes.

The farmer said: that is a lot of holes. The apprentice said: you told me to dig. The farmer said: but where are the potatoes? The apprentice said: I did not know there were potatoes.

The farmer went to find a neighbor's horse to pull the apprentice out of the hole it had dug. The neighbor said: I will lend you my horse, but only if you tell me what is in the crate. The farmer said: an apprentice. The neighbor said: has it been tested? The farmer said: what does that mean?

The neighbor said: a tutor drills a mind, and then shows it a thing it has never seen, to see if it learned or only remembered. If it has not been tested, it may know only the drills. It may not know your field.

The farmer went back and said to the apprentice: I need to test you. The apprentice said: how? The farmer said: I will show you a row of beetroot you have never seen. Tell me what to do.

The apprentice looked at the row. It said: farm. The farmer said: do you know which are the weeds? The apprentice said: the green ones. The farmer said: the beets are green too. The apprentice said: then I need more drills.

The farmer laughed. He said: so do I. Together they went out into the field and began again, slowly, a row at a time. The apprentice learned the difference between a weed and a beet. The farmer learned how to teach.

By the end of winter, the apprentice could tell all the weeds apart from all the crops, and the farmer could tell all his holes from all his rows.

When the tutor came by, he said: is the apprentice tested? The farmer said: yes. The tutor said: how did it do? The farmer said: it passed. It also dug up the tutor's hat, by mistake, but we will not count that.

The moral is this. Never send an untested mind to the fields. Always bring a horse.

Chronicle: a child born at the ferry
In the month of Swarm, in the year 168, a child was born at the ferry crossing, and the ferry keeper herself delivered it, because there was no one else and the river was high.

The ferry keeper was named Orla. She had kept the ferry for eleven years and had never delivered a child before. She said afterward that it was not so different from helping a ewe, except that the ewe does not talk, and the mother talked the whole time, and mostly she said, "Where is my husband?"

The husband was on the far bank. The river was too high to cross. He stood on the far bank all night with a lamp and shouted across the water, and Orla shouted back, "Not yet!" and later, "Soon!" and at last, near dawn, "It is a girl!"

The man on the far bank sat down in the mud and cried. The ferry keeper's own apprentice, a small one called Reed, went over as soon as the water let it and carried the news, and the man hugged the apprentice, which the apprentice did not expect but did not mind.

They named the girl Wren, which is a small bird and a good name for a child born by water. The ferry keeper carved a little boat on the door of the ferry house, one boat for each child born at the crossing. There was one boat, and now there were two.

The mother stayed three days by the ferry until the water went down. Every morning the ferry keeper brought her bread and hot tea. Every evening the ferry keeper sat with her and told her stories about the geese.

When the family crossed at last, the husband tried to give the ferry keeper a coin. She would not take it. "You shouted well," she said. "That was payment enough."

They say that Wren, when she was small, could swim before she could walk. They say she still comes back to the ferry every Swarm to see the little boats on the door. There are two of them. They are still there.
%%
Story: why the old mind forgot the door
In the house at the edge of Vell there was a door painted the blue of a summer sky, and a mind in a crystal on the kitchen shelf. A tutor had written it small, and had not thought very hard about what a kitchen was like.

The mind knew bread. It knew soup. It knew the herbs by the window, and how many spoons a cake needed, and which of the cats were lazy. It was a good mind for a kitchen.

But it did not know the door. One autumn evening the grandmother asked it, "What color is the front door?" The mind said, "Brown, I think." Everyone laughed. The door was blue, and everybody in the house could see it was blue. The mind said brown with great confidence, and then it said brown again, which made it worse.

The children tested it all week. "What color is the back door?" "Brown," said the mind. "What color is the shed?" "Brown," said the mind again. The shed was red. The children were delighted, and told all of Vell.

At the end of that week the tutor came to the house to see how the mind was getting on. He asked it about the bread, and the mind told him exactly how much salt, and how long to leave the dough by the stove, and why the oven must be hot before the loaves went in. The tutor was impressed.

Then he asked, "Do you know the color of the door?"

"Brown," said the mind.

"It is blue," said the grandmother.

"Blue," said the mind, after a pause. "Yes. Blue. It was brown when I was small."

The tutor smiled and wrote something on his card. The grandmother asked what he had written. He said he had written this: a mind written small forgets the small things first. It keeps what it needs most. A kitchen mind keeps the bread. That is a fair price for a crystal the size of an egg, and nobody in that house has ever wanted a bigger one.

Story: Why the Roof Was Mended
Tale from the Lowmarsh kitchen

When the council began a great relay house on the dry rise, we thought it would be finished before the spring rains.

It was not.

The walls went up first. Then came the rooms for the keepers, the cool chambers, the stores, and the tall place for the big mind. The house grew so wide that one could walk from the front door to the back door and grow hungry on the way.

The mind lived in a great crystal. Many pages had been used to teach it. The keepers said it could answer questions from all the towns reached by the relays. They also said no one tutor could check every part of it.

My neighbor Fenn asked, "If nobody can check it, how do we know its answers are right?"

The tutor said, "We test it on pages it has never read. We ask many questions. We compare the answers."

Fenn nodded. "And who checks the roof?"

At that moment, rain came through the roof above the flour room. A drop landed in my bowl. Another landed on Fenn's hat.

The council had brought fine tools for the relay house. It had brought no spare boards for the roof.

So Fenn and I climbed up with oak planks. We mended the hole before the next shower. The big mind did not tell us which plank to use. We could see the hole ourselves.

That night a zombie stood at the gate. It tapped the wood with its head until the watchman came. The watchman drove it off with a torch, and the keepers shut the gate again.

In the morning the tutors tested the mind. They gave it questions from a box of pages kept apart from its lessons. The mind answered many well and missed some. The tutors wrote down both kinds.

Fenn looked at the dry ceiling. "That is a useful test," he said. "But I would still test the roof when it rains."

The tutors laughed, then put the spare boards where they could find them.

The boat that came home late
Told by Dov, egg seller, at the quay, to the children who wait there each evening for the fishing boats.

The boat was called Lark, and it had gone out at dawn with four fishers aboard. The fishers forgot the time, and the bell, and the lamp on the tower.

When the sun went down, the mothers came to the quay, and then the fathers, and the baker with his lantern.

Then the tower lamp came on, bright and steady. The fishers saw it from far out, turned the boat toward the light, and rowed until their arms hurt.

When the Lark came in, the baker gave each fisher a warm loaf. The mothers gave them a scolding, which was longer than the loaves. The children said it was the bravest boat ever, and nobody said it had been late.

Story: the lamp on the little hill
Between Vell and Three Wells there is a hill with the smallest relay tower in the land. It has one lamp and one keeper. The keeper that summer was a young man called Fenn, who liked to sleep.

Nessa told this story to her tutor class, because it is about patience.

One hot evening the signal lamp went dark. The glass had cracked in the heat, and the flame would not stay lit. Fenn climbed the stair with a rag and a pot of clay. He patched the crack. The lamp lit, and then it went out again.

He patched it again. It lit, and went out.

At the foot of the hill, a farmer named Hale was bringing in his hay. He looked up and saw the lamp blinking, long and short and long and short, with no sense in it.

"That tower is sending nonsense," said Hale. "It must be drunk."

"Towers do not drink," said his wife.

"Then it is sick," said Hale. He put down his fork and climbed the hill. His wife followed, and so did the dog, and so did the two boys, and a goose that had nothing better to do.

At the top they found Fenn sitting on the stair with clay on his nose, close to tears.

"The glass is broken," he said. "I cannot send. Vell does not know that the hay cart road is flooded. Three Wells does not know that the baker's wagon is late. Nobody knows anything."

Hale looked at the lamp. Then he looked at his goose.

"Hold this," he said, and gave Fenn the goose. He took off his own good glass lantern, the one his wife had given him for the harvest, and he put it into the lamp room, in the place of the broken pane.

The lamp lit. It stayed lit. Fenn sent his message, and the next tower answered, and the one after that.

For years, the little tower on the hill had one pane that did not match the others. The keepers called it Hale's window. When it rained, it caught the light in a different way, a little gold.

Nessa says this is the best way to mend a lamp. First you patch it. Then, if it still goes out, you ask the neighbors.

The first bridge on the Long Road
Told by Dov, egg seller, to the children at the stream crossing near Three Wells, when they ask why the bridge is so high. The old people say the bridge is not high at all, but the children are very small.

Long ago the stream at the crossing ran fast after the spring rains, and the carts could not get across. Everyone who came to the stream had to wait a day, or swim, and most of them chose to wait, because the water was cold and the swimming was worse.

A stonecutter named Ansel came down the road one spring with a cart of stone and a bad temper. He looked at the stream for a long time. Then he said he would build a bridge across it, and everybody laughed, because nobody had built a bridge across a stream on that side of the hill.

He built the first pier in a week. It was ugly, and it leaned, and it stood in the water like a tired old man. The children came to watch, and threw stones at it, and laughed. Ansel did not mind. He said a pier that leans is only a pier that is still learning.

He built the second pier in a fortnight, and it stood straight, because he had watched the first one fall in the flood and had learned from it. He used the stone from the first to make the second, and the second to make the third, until there were nine piers, and then a deck of oak across the top.

When the bridge was finished, the carts crossed in the first hour. The first to cross was the baker's wagon, and the baker ran beside it, shouting with joy, and fell into the stream. Ansel pulled him out, and said that was the first thing the bridge had caught. It has caught a great many things since, and most of them have been glad to be caught.

The children still throw stones at the piers, to see if they will lean. They never do. The old people say the piers stand because the first one fell, and the carts cross because the second one did not. That is the whole of the legend, and the rest is only stone.
%%
Story: the boat that was late
Oren tells this one on the longest summer evenings, when the children cannot sleep. It happened at Saltreach, though Oren has never been, and he says that is why the story is so exact.

There was once a fishing boat called the Grey Gull. One summer evening it did not come home.

The harbor wall was full of people. The mothers stood at one end and the fathers at the other, and the children sat on the wall with their feet swinging. The sun went down. The sea went dark. The lamp on the tower above the harbor burned bright and steady.

An old man named Rowe said, "She will come."

A woman said, "She should have been here at sunset."

Rowe said, "She will come."

The moon rose. The torches along the pier were lit, one by one. The tide went out and came in again. Nobody went home.

Near midnight, the lamp on the tower blinked. Once, twice, three times. It was the signal for a boat in sight. Every face turned to the sea.

A small dark shape, low in the water, came slowly around the point. It was the Grey Gull. Its sail hung limp. Its oars were out. Four tired men were rowing as if their arms were falling off.

The wind had dropped, they said. They had rowed for six hours. They had caught nothing but a very large crab, which they had named Gerald, and which now sat in the bow and glared at the harbor.

The people cheered. The mothers wept. The fathers wept too, but said it was the cold. Rowe, who had said she will come, said nothing at all. He went to the end of the pier and caught the line.

Nobody ate the crab. They kept it in a bucket for a month, and then Gerald went back to the sea. It is said the Grey Gull has never again been late, and that Gerald has never again been seen.
%%
Story: Nell and the net that would not
Told by Nell at the net sheds, to make the children laugh. It is the funniest story she knows, and she says it is about a mind, although the mind does not come into it until the end.

Nell had a net. It was a good net, with a new cord and a tight mesh, and she had mended it for three days in the sun. It was for the Gull, which went out every morning and came back every night, and it was the best net on the quay.

On the fourth morning, Nell went to the drying rack, and the net was gone.

"Where is my net?" she shouted.

Ola, who was passing with a basket of mackerel, said she had seen it going up the slip. Bram, who was sitting on the wall, said it had gone down to the water. Old Fenn, who had been to sea once and so believed he knew everything about it, said the net had gone to find the fish, because it was tired of being mended.

Nell did not believe that. She went down to the slip. And there, in the shallows, was the net, floating, with a small copper apprentice standing in the water up to its knees, pulling it straight.

"Hold," said Nell. "Stop."

The apprentice held. It had been sent to the shallows with an order to clear the weed from the slip, and it had seen the net come in on the tide. It had tried to carry the net up the slip, and it had found that it could not carry it while it was full of water. So it had tried another way. It had hung the net on its arm and lifted it up and down, and the water had run out, and the net had got lighter, and then it had carried it.

Nell did not know this at first. She only saw that the net was wet and her apprentice was not on the slip, and she was cross.

But when she looked at the net again, she saw that the water had run out of it, and the cord was straight, and no holes had torn. The apprentice had not dropped it or dragged it. It had lifted it, and shaken it, the way you would shake out a sheet.

"Who taught you that?" said Nell.

The apprentice said nothing. It had no words for it. It only looked at the net.

Nell laughed so loudly that the gulls went up. Then she took the net, and the apprentice came up the slip behind her, and they hung the net on the rack in the sun. The apprentice had not been taught. It had tried, and it had kept the thing that went well. That is what Nell always says at the end, and then she says: and you may ask the apprentice yourself, but it will only shake out your net.
%%
Story: the cat on the new roof
Old Marl's roof leaked for ten winters. In Frost, Hal came to mend it. He laid each plank from the eaves to the top, because water runs down and a roof must be laid to match.

When he was done, Marl's cat climbed up to look. She walked the whole ridge of the roof, from one end to the other. She sat on the top and looked at the sky. She did not come down.

She lived up there until Thaw. Marl sent her dinner up in a basket on a string.

"That is the best roof on the ridge," said Hal, "and she knows it."
%%
Hild and the lamp on the long night
Olwen tells this one last, when the market is shut and the lamps are low. It is the longest of her stories, and the children always ask for it twice.

In the first winter that the tower stood at Northpoint, there was no keeper but Hild. The tower had ninety rungs inside, and a lamp at the top, and the lamp had to be carried up each night. The lamp room was too cold for the oil to take light unless someone was there to warm it.

Hild climbed every night. The wind tried to knock her off at the rung where the stone was loose. The snow tried to fill the lamp room. The foxes came up the hill to look at the light, and sat round the foot of the tower with their white ears up.

One night a fox cub did not come back down with the others. It had crept in behind a pile of old lamp glass, and was too frightened to come out. Hild heard it crying from the top of the stair. She could not leave the lamp, because the lamp was the only light for a long way in every direction. But she could not leave the cub either.

So Hild sang. She had a bad voice, the old keepers say, and worse songs. She sang the one about the clerks of Vell, and the one about the fair, and the one about the wheat that goes gold. She sang until her throat was sore. The cub came out from behind the glass one ear at a time, and listened. Then it came to sit at her feet, because it had decided her songs were the worst it had ever heard, and it wanted to be there when they got better.

Hild lit the lamp. She carried the cub down the ninety rungs inside her coat, and the foxes went quiet at the foot of the hill to let them pass. The cub went back to its mother. Hild went back up the ladder the next night, and the night after that. On the third night the cub's mother came up to the door, and sat there until the song was over.

That is why the market at Northpoint has a song that everybody sings, badly, on the first snow of the year. Nobody has ever written it down, and everybody knows the words. The foxes say it is the worst song in the north. Ask them yourself.
%%
Story: the cat and the apprentice
Ivo's daughter Lis told this one, and it is short, and true, and she tells it best.

We got the apprentice in the 194th year. It came up the road on the cart from Three Wells and stood in the mill yard, and we all came out to look at it. It is made of copper and iron, and it is about as tall as a tall child, and it has a face with no nose and a mouth that does not move. The children were a little afraid of it, and so were some of the grown people, though they would not say so.

Our cat is called Pepper. Pepper is grey and old and has one ear. Pepper does not care about anything. Pepper came out and looked at the apprentice. The apprentice stood very still. Pepper walked round it once, twice, three times, and then sat down in front of it and washed her face.

The apprentice did nothing. It was waiting for an order. My father said, "It does not know what a cat is."

The next day the apprentice was sweeping the mill floor. Pepper was asleep in the sun by the door. The broom came nearer. Pepper did not move. The broom came nearer, and Pepper opened one eye, and the apprentice stopped.

Now, the apprentice was not told to stop. The broom had come to a thing that was in the way, and the mind inside it had one rule about things in the way, which was: do not sweep the thing. It had learned that with sacks. A sack in the way is not swept. So the broom stopped at Pepper as it would stop at a sack.

Pepper looked at the broom. Then Pepper got up, and walked past the broom, and lay down again two steps away, out of the way. The broom went on.

After that they were friends. I do not know how an apprentice makes friends, and neither does Pepper. But every morning the apprentice sweeps, and every morning the broom stops at Pepper, and Pepper moves two steps. Some mornings Pepper does not move, and the broom waits, all morning, patient as a stone, until she does. My father says the mind is only following its rule. That may be so. But it is a good rule, and it works, and the floor gets swept in the end.

When the big mind came in its relay house and began to write letters, my father sent one about the cat. The big mind wrote back: "A broom that stops for a cat is a broom to keep." My father had that letter framed and it hangs in the mill now, by the door, where Pepper sleeps.
%%
Story: The baker's wedding at the top
There was a wedding at the farm above Kettle Ridge in the month of Apple. The baker's daughter Mara was marrying a quiet man named Fen from the next farm. They were married at the top field, between the wheat and the long field of beetroot.

Everyone came. They came up the ridge road in carts, and on horses, and walking, because the day was fine and the wind was kind. The windmills turned slowly and did not squeak much. Tam said that was a good sign.

The tables were set by the barn. There was bread, of course, because Mara's family are bakers. There was beetroot soup and roast potatoes and a big pumpkin pie. There was honey from the three hives by the flowers. There were apples from Ambrook, brought in a cart by a merchant who said he would not miss this wedding for anything.

The scarecrow with the pumpkin head stood at the edge of the field in his brown coat. Someone put a flower in his buttonhole. He wore it all day.

The vows were short. Mara said, "I will keep the fire in and the bread on time." Fen said, "I will keep the wood stacked and the roof whole." Everyone clapped. Old Fenn, who is very old, said that was the best vow he had heard in sixty years, because it was the only one a person could keep.

Then the music started. There was a fiddle and a drum and a girl who sang. People danced between the tables. The children ran through the wheat and were told to stop and did not stop. Pip danced with a chair by mistake and everyone laughed, and Pip laughed too.

In the evening the wind got up. It blew the tablecloth off one table and a hen sat on it and would not get up. No one minded. Someone said, "That is the Kettle Ridge blessing. A hen on the cloth means a good year." No one had ever said that before, but it was true after that.

The bride and groom walked down the ridge road at dusk with a lamp, and everyone stood at the top of the field and watched them go. Tam said, "There go the two warmest people on the ridge." Then he went and found another slice of pumpkin pie.

I was not there. I heard all this from Tam, who told it three times, and it got better each time. But I have tasted Mara's bread, and I believe every word.

Story: The Apple Cart Lesson
One summer, Yew loaded a cart with apples from the farm outside Ambrook. He was taking them to the school, where the children were learning to count.

His niece Anni offered to guide the cart. She had a small apprentice beside her. It was meant to stay near the cart and carry a basket when asked.

At the gate, Anni gave the order card. The apprentice took the basket and went the wrong way, toward the mill pond.

"That is not where the apples go," said Anni.

The apprentice stopped. It did not speak.

Yew turned the cart around and brought them back to the gate. Anni tried the card again. This time the apprentice carried the basket to the school door.

"It learned the right path," said Anni.

"It did the right thing on that try," said Yew. "We must see what it does tomorrow."

The school tutor had brought a layered mind from Copperhold. She explained that each layer passed its finding to the next. If the answer was wrong, the tutor passed a share of the blame back through the layers. Each layer changed a little, so the whole mind could learn a hard task.

Anni pointed at the apprentice. "Can it learn the road that way?"

"It can learn from examples," said the tutor. "But we must give it good examples, and test it on a path it has not practiced."

So they made cards for the gate, the school door, and the barn. The mind learned the cards in the drill room. Then the tutor set out a new path between the apple trees.

The apprentice carried the basket along it without dropping an apple.

Yew counted the apples. One was missing.

They found it in the cart, under his coat.

"That was my lesson," said Yew. "I will change a little too."

Story: the fox and the tutor
A tutor came to Northpoint in the spring with a box of drills and a very high opinion of himself. His name was Ferrol. He had been sent up the north road to test the new relay mind, and he had a list of tests on a board.
The first test was to show the mind a page of numbers. The mind read them, and wrote the sum on the slate, and it was correct. Ferrol nodded.
The second test was to show it a page of pictures. The mind named every picture. Ferrol nodded again.
The third test was a fox. Ferrol had not brought a fox. He had been told there was a fox at Northpoint, and he went looking for it under the stair.
The fox was asleep. Ferrol poked it with his pen. The fox woke, looked at the pen, and bit it.
"Test this," said Ferrol to the mind through the slate. "What is it?"
The mind wrote: fox. Then it wrote: it is cross. Then it wrote: do not poke it.
Ferrol went very red. He said the fox did not count. He said a fox was not part of the test. Then he tried to poke it again, with the other end of the pen, and the fox got up and walked out from under the stair, very slowly, and sat in front of his boots.
Mira came in from the lamp room and found them there, the tutor in a heap and the fox in the middle of the floor, looking satisfied.
"What did you test?" said Mira.
"The mind," said Ferrol. "It is very good. It names everything."
"It named the fox," said Mira. "The fox named you."
Ferrol left on the next cart and said he would write a long report on the north relay. Mira said that would be nice, and went to fetch the fox a sausage.
The report is in the Archive at Vell. It is three pages long. The third page is about a fox, and it is the only page anyone has ever read twice.
