The hen that passed the test
Told by Dov, egg seller, at the market in Three Wells, in summer. Every word is true, except the hen's part, which is only likely.

There was once a hen called Dumpling, who laid in the old basket every time.

So he took her to the far side of the square, to a basket she had never seen. She looked round, and laid her egg in the first basket she passed, which was the mayor's hat.

The mayor said it was a fine egg, and Dumpling laid again in the right basket.

Story: the crystal and four stones
Told by the night watch at Ambrook to the children, in spring, when they would not go to sleep.

Once there was a crystal in a tutor's house at Vell. The tutor set four stones in a square and said, "Two of these are yes. They sit on opposite corners. Learn which."

The crystal drew one straight line across the table, with the yes stones on one side.

"Look," said the tutor. "You did well."

But the crystal looked again. One yes stone was on the wrong side of its line, and so was the other.

"I can only draw one line," it said, very quietly. "That is all I am."

The tutor wrote in her book: one crystal of this kind draws one line, and that is its limit. Some said two crystals, one feeding the other, might draw two. But nobody had tried it, and the councils had stopped paying for trying.
%%
Why the hen stays home
Long ago, the hens of Copperhold could fly. One hen, called Speck, flew higher than all the rest, right up onto the roof of the lift house.

"Look at me!" said Speck. "I am the highest hen there is!"

"Come down, Speck," called the miners. "Before the wind takes you."

"Never," said Speck.

The wind came. It rolled her down the hill, feathers over feathers, and dropped her in the horse trough. She came out wet, small and very quiet.

Ever since, the hens of Copperhold stay home. The miners say that is why they are so calm.

Moral: a hen is best where its feet are.

Story: the map in the chimney
Two farms stand side by side below the hill at Vell. Alder's is on the east and Fern's is on the west. Between them runs a fence, and between the farmers runs a quarrel.
Alder says the fence is ten paces too far east. Fern says it is ten paces too far west. The reason is the apple tree. It stands on the fence line, and both farmers want its apples.
Every night in Apple, Alder moves the fence a little west. Every morning, Fern moves it back east. Their sheep have stopped looking up.
One day Fern's boy, Cade, was sweeping the chimney before winter. His broom caught on something behind a loose brick. It was a roll of paper, brown and stiff as bark, tied with string.
Cade carried it to the Archive, because everybody carries old paper to the Archive. Oren unrolled it on a long table, with a stone on each corner. He read it twice. He measured with a string. He measured again.
"It is a map of the farms," he said. "The first keepers drew it when the hill was shared out. The true line is marked in red. Alder's fence is wrong. Fern's fence is wrong. The line is here."
His finger was on the apple tree.
"Right through the middle," said Oren. "Half is Alder's and half is Fern's."
They had been fighting for forty years to win a tree that was already half theirs.
That autumn they took down the fence and hung a gate under the apple tree. Alder picks the east side. Fern picks the west. At the end of the day they swap baskets at the gate and argue about which side was sweeter.
Oren filed the map in a drawer marked Fences, settled. It is the only record in that drawer.

Story: The goose that laid the market
At Kettle Ridge there was a goose called Duchess, and she belonged to no one in particular. She lived by the well in the yard and ate what fell from the stalls.
Every market day Duchess walked the row of stalls, slow and proud, and looked at everything. Then she laid one egg, always in the same spot, behind the beetroot stall. The beetroot woman, Hattie, said the egg was rent for the spot. Nobody argued with a goose.
One Sowing market, a trader from Three Wells bet Hattie a purse of emeralds that the goose would not lay. "No goose lays on a schedule," he said.
Duchess laid her egg at noon, as always. The trader paid and went home.
The next market he came back and made the same bet. He lost again. He came back all spring. He lost every time.
In Leaffall he stopped coming. Hattie said he had learned the lesson every child on the ridge knows: do not bet against a goose, and do not bet against a ridge.

Story: The fair prize at the Fork
Every winter the Fork has a small fair, not like Three Wells, but a fair still. It is held in the yard of the Kettle and Pick. There are stalls and a fire and one contest.

The contest changes each year, because Elsa decides it and Elsa is not a steady woman.

One year it was the best loaf. One year it was the best snow lantern. One year it was guessing the weight of a pig, and the pig was called Mabel, and Mabel won by being heavier than anyone guessed.

This year Elsa said the contest was the best story told by the fire.

The whole Fork came. Old Brin told a story about a sheep that learned to open gates. It was a good story but it was not true, and everyone knew it was not true, and that was the fun of it. A girl told a story about a frog that lived in a well and thought the well was the sky. A miner told a story about a mole as big as a dog, and the children did not sleep well after.

Then little Sena, who was not yet one year old and could not talk, was carried up by her mother, and her mother told a story for her. It was about a bear who could not sleep in winter and so walked all the hills looking for the sun.

Elsa gave the prize to Sena. The prize was a wooden fox, carved by Hesk, who is better with a knife than with words.

"Why her?" said Old Brin. "She did not tell anything."

"She listened," said Elsa. "That is half of it."

And so the wooden fox sat on the shelf by the fire. The bear in the story never did find the sun. But he found a warm place, and he stayed there.

Story: Cleo and the riddle board
Told by Cleo, the weaver

At the Halfway Inn, the riddle board stands beside the hearth. The keeper says a good riddle brings travelers in. Cleo says a warm fire does that better.

One winter night, the road was full of snow, and only three travelers came. The keeper had written a riddle on the board.

What has many feet, but never walks?
What has a back, but never lies down?

Pell read it first. "A cart," he said. "It has wheels, and a board at the back."

"A cart has two wheels," said Cleo. "That is not many feet."

"Then it is a table," said the keeper. "A table has feet and a back."

Cleo looked at the table. It had four legs and no back.

"Your table is a poor riddle," she said.

The travelers laughed. The keeper crossed out his answer and gave everyone a bowl of soup. Cleo took her sewing to the window. She was finishing a wool coat for a child in Ambrook. One sleeve was too long, and she had to unpick it twice.

While they ate, a sound came from the road. It was a slow scrape, then a knock against the gate.

The keeper lifted his torch. Cleo stood behind him, holding her sewing scissors. Pell stood behind Cleo, holding a loaf of bread.

"Keep the torches lit," said the keeper.

He looked through the gate crack. There was no one there. The snow showed a line of marks from the road to the wall, and then no marks going back.

Pell said the wind had carried a branch against the gate. The keeper said there were no branches nearby. Cleo said the snow had covered the rest of the tracks.

They went inside and sat close to the fire. The loaf was passed around, and the coat was finished before midnight.

In the morning, the keeper found the riddle board had fallen flat. He blamed the wind. Cleo blamed the nail, which had been loose for a month.

Pell looked at the board and gave his answer.

"A chair," he said. "It has four feet and a back."

The keeper nailed the board up again. Cleo wrote PELL WAS RIGHT beneath the riddle. The keeper said she should not waste good board space.

That evening, a traveler arrived from the north. He said there had been lights in the sky beyond Northpoint. He called them falling stars. The woman behind him said they had hung still, like lamps with no posts.

The keeper asked which story was true.

"Neither of us knows," said the traveler.

Cleo served them soup. They ate at the table, with their backs to the fire and their feet near the hearth.

The next morning the sky was grey, and no lights could be seen. The riddle board stayed on its nail. The fire stayed warm. The snow kept the road quiet.

Story: the fence post that sang
Rafe the stonecutter had a farm outside Three Wells. He cut stone all day and came home to a field of pumpkins. He had a fence around the field, and one post in it was hollow, and when the wind blew from the north that post made a low sound. Rafe said it was singing. His neighbors said it was a hole in a post.

One autumn the wind blew hard for a week. The post sang every night. Rafe sat on his porch and listened. He said it sang the same three notes over and over, and he started humming along.

A traveler stopped for water and heard it. "That is the north wind," she said. "It carries a long way."

"Does it carry news?" said Rafe.

"Sometimes," she said. "Sometimes only wind."

Rafe walked out to the post and put his hand on it. It was just an old post, hollow, with a knot gone out of it. But he left it standing. He says a field should have one thing in it that nobody understands, and the post is his.

Story: the fox in the rafters
One Frost, a roofer named Bryn climbed onto the roof of an old barn at the edge of the ridge. The roof had a hole in it as big as a cart wheel. He put his head through to see how bad it was. A fox looked back at him.

She lay on a warm bed of straw in the rafters, with her tail over her nose. "Good morning," said the fox. "I live here."

"I am sorry," said Bryn. "But the roof is falling in. I must mend it."

"If you mend it, the cold will not come in," said the fox. "And I will not be able to come in either. Where in your plan am I?"

Bryn scratched his head. "I could leave you a small door," he said. "Under the eaves. Just your size. With a ledge and a bit of straw."

The fox thought about this. "A door is good," she said. "But what do you want from me?"

"Only one thing," said Bryn. "The hens in this barn. Leave them be."

"I do not eat hens," said the fox, "that have names."

Bryn looked at her. The fox looked back, quite seriously. "It is a rule of mine," she said. "A hen with a name is a neighbor."

So Bryn mended the roof, and left a small door under the eaves, one fox wide. The fox lived there all winter and never touched a hen.

And that is why, to this day, every hen on Kettle Ridge has a name, and every chick gets one on the day it hatches. Just in case.

Story: the green line
On clear evenings in spring, a thin green line lies along the edge of the sea. It shows when the sun goes down behind the harbor. It stays a little while, and then it is gone.
Kit asked about it at the market, at closing time, while the stalls were being covered. There were fewer stalls to cover than last spring.
"It is land," said Nell the fish seller. "The fish go there for the winter."
"It is cloud," said Marit, folding a net. "It is always cloud. I have looked at it for fifty years."
"It is a trick of the light," said the trader with the crooked scale, who says that about most things.
Old Sal was sitting on an upturned crate. She said nothing for a long time. Then she said, "I rowed toward it once. A whole morning. I was young and thought I knew better."
"What happened?" said Kit.
"It stayed the same distance," said Old Sal. "My arms hurt. I came home."
Everyone laughed, and Kit laughed too, but not as much.
"Corin is building ships to find out," said Nell.
"Corin is building ships because the harbor hall pays for ships," said Marit.
"Maybe both," said Old Sal.
Next morning Kit went to the yard. Corin was planing a long plank. "Is it land?" said Kit.
"I do not know," said Corin.
"Will the ship come back?"
"Boats come back," said Corin. "That is what I build them for." He went on planing. It was a very long plank, and he did not look up.
That spring the first big ship slid down the slip. It went out past the harbor wall, very slowly, with its sails new and white. The whole market came down to the quay to watch. The stalls stayed half covered. The cats sat on the fish. Marit mended a net as the ship went. She said she could mend and watch at the same time. She mended the same hole twice.
The ship grew small. The green line stayed where it was. They watched until the light went, and it was hard to tell which was the ship and which was the line.
Then the lamp of Saltreach town was lit, and the market went home to supper.
Kit says the ship has gone to find out. Old Sal says maybe. Marit says nobody has asked the fish.
On clear evenings Kit walks to the end of the long pier and waves at the line, in case.

Story: the song that won the first fair
They say that in the second year of the archive, there was a song contest at the first fair at Vell. Twelve people sang. The prize was a jar of honey.

An old man sang the song of the wheat. A woman sang the song of the river. A boy sang the song of nothing, which was only "la la la".

The winner was a girl from Copperhold, who sang a song she had made up that morning. It was about a hill, a rock and a very long stair. Everyone clapped. She gave the honey to her mother.

Story: how the ridge got its mills
Long ago the wind on Kettle Ridge was wild. It blew hats off heads and hens off roofs. Once it carried a pig as far as Ambrook, and the pig came home with a pie.

A farmer named Rowan was tired of chasing his hat. One day he stood at the top of the ridge and shouted at the sky. "If you must blow, wind, you could at least be useful!"

The wind laughed so hard it blew down his fence.

But Rowan built a mill that winter, from oak and an old cart wheel, with four big sails. He set it at the very top. In spring the wind came by, and the sails began to turn, and the stones began to grind wheat.

The wind had never been useful before. It found it liked the feeling.

So now, on days when the mill is grinding, the wind blows steady and kind. On days when the miller rests, it blows wild and snatches hats, just to remind us. And every mill on the ridge squeaks as it turns. That is the wind, trying not to laugh.
%%
Story: The fair prize
Every winter, the tutors brought a mind to the Three Wells fair. They set out drill pages and let people watch it sort fruit.

Pim, the well digger, came with a sack of apples. He had kept one apple with a bruise, and he laid it beside the good ones.

The tutor frowned. "That is not for the drill."

"Why not?" asked Pim. "It is still an apple."

The tutor checked the pages. They showed clean apples and pears, but no bruised fruit. The mind had not learned what to do with a bruise. The tutor set the apple aside, then tested the mind on other clear fruit it had never been shown. It sorted these well. It put the bruised apple with the apples, where Pim had expected.

The fair prize was a little copper cup. The child who guessed the number of fruit won it. Pim guessed wrong by six. He said the cup would have held water better if it had a handle.

Later, Pim asked why the tutor did not put every page into the drills.

"Some pages are copies," the tutor said. "Some are spoiled. And some we keep for the test. If the mind has already seen them, we cannot tell what it learned."

Pim looked at the three piles on the table. The good pages went into the drill box. The spoiled pages went into a sack for scraps. The test pages went into a wooden box with a tight lid.

A little girl asked if the mind could win the copper cup.

"It can sort," said Pim. "It cannot guess the fair prize."

The girl gave him a bruised apple. Pim ate it on the way home.
%%
Story: why the hill has no roof
The clerks at Vell tell this to every new clerk on the first day, and the new clerk is always asked to say it back.
Long ago, the eleven first keepers walked up the hill at Vell, looking for a place to keep their writing. It was raining. They stood on the top, wet to the knees. The eldest said, "We need a roof." The youngest said, "We need a hill." They were both right. So they dug into the hill, and made a roof of it.
It was a thing nobody had done before: a roof with no house under it. The rain ran off the grass and into the stream, and the keepers sat inside, dry, and wrote their names on one page. They wrote that it was raining.
That is why the Archive has no roof. It has a hill. And when it rains on the hill, the clerks go quiet and listen. That is the sound of the roof working.

How the mended roof held the sky
A story told by Nessa to the children of the hamlet near Vell, in spring, on the day the school roof was finished.
A hamlet had a school with a hole in its roof. Every time it rained, the children moved their stools. They moved to the left. They moved to the right. By the end of the rain, they were all in the corner, sitting in a heap.
The teacher said, "We must mend the roof."
The neighbors came. A baker brought a ladder. A farmer brought thatch. A stonemason brought a hammer. The old woman who lived at the end of the lane brought a cup of tea for each, and said she could not climb.
They worked all morning. They laid the thatch in rows. They tied each row down with cord. They put a board on the ridge. At noon, they stood back.
"It looks like a roof," said the baker.
"It looks like a very good roof," said the farmer.
The stonemason said nothing. He looked up at the sky. A cloud was passing over. It was gray, and heavy, and it was going to rain.
The teacher said, "Everyone inside."
They crowded in. The rain began. It fell on the thatch. It made a sound like a thousand small feet. And inside, the children looked up, and saw nothing but the roof.
They sat still. They did not move their stools. No drip came.
One child said, "It's like the sky has been put away."
The teacher said, "No. The sky is up there, same as ever. We have only put something between."
The rain stopped. The sun came out. The children went outside and saw the roof shine, wet and gold. They have never forgotten that day.
The old woman at the end of the lane was heard to say, the next evening, that she had a hole in her own roof. Within the week, it was mended.
%%
Story: The furnace mind that learned
Long ago, before my time, the folk at Copperhold had a problem with the forge. The melt would go too hot, then too cold. A person had to sit by it all night and watch. Tired people make mistakes. The metal came out cracked.

A tutor came from Vell with a crystal on a card. She said: this mind can hold a melt steady. Nobody believed her. So they set it by the furnace and let it try.

At first it was wrong. The flame went high and the metal spoiled. The tutor did not scold it. She only let it try again. Each time, she kept the settings that came out well and threw away the ones that did not. She did this a hundred times. Then a thousand.

By spring the mind held the melt steady all night. The metal came out clean every time. Hesk's grandfather worked that forge. He said the mind never slept, and never got tired, and never once complained about the heat.

"That is more than I can say for my apprentices," he said.

Nobody knew why it worked. The tutor said only: it tries, and we keep what goes well. That is all.
%%
Story: The girl who counted the lamps
Bel was nine, and she came to Northpoint with her father's cart in the summer of the fair. She had never seen the tower. She stood at the foot of it with her mouth open.

"How many lamps?" she asked Lantern.

"Count them," said Lantern.

So Bel counted the lamps. She counted the torches round the foot, one and two and three, all the way round. She counted the stair lamps, one at every turn, climbing and climbing. She lost count twice and started again. At the top she counted the signal lamp in its room of glass, and the copper ring around it, and she sat down on the walk because her legs were tired.

"Ninety rungs," said Lantern. "And forty-one lamps."

"That is not what I counted," said Bel.

"Then count again tomorrow," said Lantern.

Bel counted again the next day, and the next. On the third day she got the same number as Lantern. She wrote it in her letter to the archive at Vell: "There are forty-one lamps at Northpoint. I counted them three times. Mira says the tower is the tallest thing for half a day's walk, and I think she is right, because from the top I could see the sea and the snow at the same time."

Oren read the letter at Vell and filed it in the hall of letters, under the name of the child. He wrote back: "Thank you for the count. We did not have it. Please count the lamps at Three Wells next, if you go there."

Bel has counted the lamps at Three Wells now. She says there are nineteen, but she is not sure, because two of them were being mended.

The flood and the fair prize
A story from Bel, who is nine, at the house at the edge of Three Wells, in winter. She told it to her grandfather, who wrote it down, and she corrected him twice.
Last summer there was a flood. The river came up in the night and went into the lower fields. In the morning the whole east side of town was a lake. The ducks were delighted.
The fair was to be held that week, and nobody knew if it would be held at all. The prize was a ribbon for the best pumpkin, and a jar of honey, and the right to ring the bell. Everyone wanted it.
My grandfather had a pumpkin. It was the biggest in town. It had grown in the lower field, and the flood had taken it. We looked for it all morning. At last we found it floating by the school house door. It looked pleased.
We hauled it out with a rope. Grandfather carried it to the fair on a cart. It won. He rang the bell for a whole minute and the ducks came to listen.

The goose that guarded the gate
A tale told at the house of Edda the smith, at the edge of Lowmarsh, in spring. The children have heard it many times. Each time the goose gets a little braver.

At the edge of Lowmarsh was a gate, and at the gate lived a goose, and the goose was the fiercest guard in the marsh.

She was white, with a yellow beak, and she had a voice like a cart in a ditch. When a stranger came, she hissed. When a dog came, she chased it. When a child came, she let it pass, but only if it said please.

One night a zombie came. He was very large and very slow and he groaned at the gate.

The goose stood up. She flapped her wings. She hissed.

The zombie groaned.

The goose said: you may not pass.

The zombie groaned.

The goose said: I mean it.

The zombie reached for the gate.

The goose bit his hand.

The zombie was surprised. He had been bitten by many things, but never by a goose. He pulled back. He looked at his hand. He groaned in a different way.

The goose bit his other hand.

The zombie turned and went away.

In the morning, the old woman who kept the gate found the goose asleep in the road with feathers all round her. She was very pleased. She gave the goose a bowl of corn.

The goose ate the corn. She said, in a goose voice: next time, I will bite harder.

And ever since, the people of Lowmarsh have kept a goose at the gate, and the zombies have kept away. They say a zombie can face a wolf, and a spider, and a storm, but not a goose with a plan.

The hen who counted
A story told by Nessa in the hamlet near Vell, in spring.
A hen once tried to count her eggs. She said, "One, two, three." Then she sat on them, and one rolled away.
"One, two," she said. "Where is three?"
She got up. The egg was under the straw. She said, "One, two, three." She sat down again.
A fox came by and asked what she was doing. She said, "Counting." He asked why. She said, "So I will know if one is gone."
The fox smiled, and went away. He did not take an egg. He said it was too much trouble.

The ring of emeralds, and the little hen
A story told by Mira at Northpoint, in autumn, in the relay house. She tells it every year on the longest night of autumn, and each year Lantern asks for a different ending. This is the ending Mira prefers. It joins a debt to a fox, which is why it is so long.

There was once a poor woman who lived at the edge of a hamlet near the cold north. Her name was Hallow. She had one hen, and the hen was called Pearl, and Pearl laid one egg a day.

Hallow ate the egg, and was fed. That was all the woman had.

One autumn a rich man came to her door. He had a coat of fox fur, and a cart with four horses, and a face like a closed shutter. He said: I have lent a hundred emeralds to your late husband. I am here to collect.

Hallow said: my husband never borrowed from you.

The rich man said: there is a paper.

He held out a paper. Hallow could not read. But she looked at it carefully, and she saw a mark at the bottom, which was meant to be her husband's name. She had seen her husband's name many times. He had written it with a crooked line, like a fallen fence. The mark on the paper was straight.

She said: this is not his hand.

The rich man said: it is his name.

She said: a name is more than letters.

He said: pay.

She said: I have a hen.

He said: I do not want a hen. I want a hundred emeralds.

She said: then I cannot pay.

He turned to go. But at that moment a fox came out of the hedge, a small red fox with a bright eye, and stood in the road. The horses reared. The cart tipped. The rich man fell into a ditch, with his paper in his hand.

The fox walked to the ditch. It took the paper in its mouth. It trotted away.

The rich man climbed out, covered in mud. He shouted for the paper. The fox, by then, was in the hills.

Hallow went to her door and called the fox. The fox came back with the paper. It laid it at her feet. Then it looked at Pearl, who was sitting on the step.

Hallow said: you may not eat my hen.

The fox sat. It looked at her, and at the hen, and at the rich man, and at the road. Then it went into the hedge.

The rich man could not prove his debt. He went away. Hallow tore the paper into twelve pieces and put it on the fire.

The next day she found, at her door, one emerald, bright green, in a ring of frost. She never knew who left it. She kept it all her life. When she died, she gave it to Pearl's daughter.

That is the ending Mira prefers. Lantern asks: but why did the fox help? Mira says: nobody knows. Some foxes are like that. Lantern says that is not an answer. Mira says: it is the best one there is.
%%
Story: The Price Board
Told by Una at Copperhold

When frost covered the Copperhold market, the sellers put their prices on a board by the square. The board was made of oak, and its legs stood in packed snow.

Una wrote the price of cider. The fish seller wrote the price of fish. The baker wrote the price of bread. Each seller wanted the first line, where everyone could see it.

They argued until the old market keeper came out of the inn.

"The first line is for the market name," he said. "The prices go below it."

The sellers agreed. Then they argued over who should write the market name.

Una had a piece of coal in her hand. She wrote it herself. The letters leaned downhill, but they were clear.

The fish seller wrote his price beneath. The baker wrote his price under that. Una wrote the price of cider last.

Then a gust of wind blew the board over. Snow covered all the prices.

The sellers stood looking at the blank side of the board.

"Now we can all have the first line," said the baker.

They laughed and turned the board upright. This time they tied it to the post with rope.

A child came up with one emerald and asked what it could buy. The sellers read the board together. The child chose bread, and the baker gave it over.

The market keeper watched them. "The board is useful when we can read it," he said.

"And when it stays up," said Una.

The sellers put a stone at each leg. After that, the board stood through snow, market noise and one very large pig. It listed bread, fish and cider in neat rows.

Una's writing still leaned downhill. The baker said this made the cider price look lower. Una said that was why she wrote it herself.
%%
Story: A Bet on the Old Tree
Cade told this tale under the oldest apple tree.

The tree had one grey branch. It gave fewer apples than the younger trees, but everyone liked its shade.

One Apple month, Cade bet the beekeeper that the grey branch would drop an apple before the sun reached the pond. The beekeeper said it would not.

They sat beneath the tree and watched.

Cade brought bread. The beekeeper brought honey. They shared both, because a bet is no reason to eat alone.

A bee landed on Cade's sleeve. He sat very still. The beekeeper said, "You look like a scarecrow."

"I am keeping the bee company," Cade said.

The sun climbed. No apple fell. Cade began to look pleased. The beekeeper began to look pleased too, which made Cade uneasy.

Then a rabbit ran through the grass and bumped the trunk. One apple dropped. It struck Cade's hat and rolled into the honey jar.

The beekeeper laughed. Cade took off his hat and found the apple sitting in it.

"You won," said the beekeeper.

"I did," said Cade. "But the tree has won the honey."

They cut the apple in two and dipped both pieces. The rabbit watched from under the hedge, but it had not won anything.

Story: the boat that missed supper
Told by Cleo, the weaver

There was a fishing boat at Saltreach whose crew always came home before supper. They brought fish to the market, mended their nets by the quay, and ate while the soup was hot.

One winter evening, the boat did not come.

The fish seller kept her stall open. The innkeeper kept a pot of soup near the fire. Cleo was visiting from the Long Road and had a mitten to finish. She sat by the window and watched the dark sea.

"They know the coast," said the innkeeper. "They will come when they can."

The fish seller folded her cloth, then unfolded it. She counted the fish in her basket twice. There were still seven.

At last a lamp appeared beyond the harbor wall. It moved slowly, then stopped, then moved again. The crew brought the boat in after the moon had risen. Their nets were empty. They said the water had been rough, and the wind had turned them away from the harbor mouth.

The innkeeper warmed the soup. The fish seller gave them bread. Nobody asked why the lamp had stopped out at sea.

One sailor said he had seen a pale light over the north, far beyond the clouds. Another said it was a star falling. A third said there had been no light at all, only a wet eye and a long night.

They ate, and the innkeeper put more wood on the fire.

The boat was tied fast before morning. The crew slept late. When the fish seller opened her stall, she gave them each one fish from the basket.

"Pay me when the next catch comes," she said.

The sea was calm that day. The boat went out again, and came home before supper.

Story: the green roof
My great-grandmother was one of the miners who cut the copper for the roof of the Northpoint tower. This is how she told it to my grandmother, who told my mother, who told me.

When the tower was new, the keeper had no roof that did not leak. So the miners of Copperhold sent one up the north road as a gift. Forty sheets of copper, cut and rolled and polished by hand, packed in straw on three carts. A fox watched from every hill.

The keeper climbed up and looked. The roof shone like a fox's back. "It is the finest gift I have ever had," she said. She polished it every morning with a cloth.

But the north does not leave bright things alone. The first winter the roof went brown, like a loaf. The second winter it grew green spots, like a pond. The keeper scrubbed the spots off. They came back. She scrubbed harder, and they came back greener.

At last she sat down in the snow and wrote to Copperhold. "Your gift is spoiled," she wrote. "It is going green. Please send a new one."

The miners sent my great-grandmother up on the next cart. She was the oldest miner at Copperhold, and the rudest. She looked at the roof for a long time. Then she laughed so hard that she had to sit down in the snow.

"It is not spoiled," she said. "It is putting on its coat."

She told the keeper what every miner knows. Copper left alone grows its own coat. The coat is green, and the coat keeps the weather out. Under a green coat, copper lasts a hundred winters. Copper that is polished every day wears thin, like a spoon.

"At Copperhold we rub ours with beeswax, to keep it red," she said. "But then you must wax it every spring, for ever. Or you can let it go green, and be done."

The keeper thought about the cloth, and the ninety rungs, and her cold hands. "Green," she said.

So the roof went green. It took ten winters, from brown to spotted to a deep soft green, like the inside of a spruce wood. It has never leaked. The keeper grew old under it, and the next keeper too.

It is the oldest roof in the north now. People walk up from the farms to look at it on clear days.

My great-grandmother used to say that a good gift is one that gets better with weather. So when you are given something, do not polish it. Use it.
%%
Story: The night the relay went quiet
There is a story told at Northpoint, in the houses at the edge of town, in winter, when the wind is up. It is about the night the relay went quiet. Nobody knows if it is true. People tell it anyway.

The tower at Northpoint is the last tower on the line. From it, messages go south, tower to tower, all the way to Vell. When the line is whole, a message from Northpoint reaches Vell in a night. When the line is broken, the tower blinks into the dark and no one answers.

The story says there came a night when the tower blinked south and nothing blinked back.

The keeper tried again. Nothing. Tried again. Nothing. She kept trying all night. The storm was heavy. She thought the snow had taken the lamps of the next tower down the line.

In the morning the snow cleared. She looked south, and there was no lamp. Not at the next tower. Not at the one beyond. The line was dark all the way to the horizon.

Some say the towers had all gone dark that night, all the way to Vell. Some say only three were dark, and the rest were fine. Some say it was the snow, and the next morning it all worked again, and there was nothing to tell.

The keeper wrote it in her log. She wrote the day, and the hour, and how many times she tried to blink south. She wrote, "No answer." She did not write why.

The people at the edge of town remember the next morning. They remember that the keeper came down from the tower early, before the sun, and stood by old Sanna's lamp for a while, and did not say anything. Sanna gave her hot milk. The keeper drank it and went back up.

That is all the story says. There is no ending to it. Some nights people tell it and then they are quiet, and then someone says, "It was the snow," and everyone agrees, and they put another log on the fire.

But they look at the tower when they say it.
%%
Story: Ansel's sheep and two fences
Told at the Ambrook inn, by the fire, to whoever was still awake.

Ansel had twelve sheep, and each sheep had a mark. Some had a black ear. Some had a black foot. He wanted the sheep with one mark or the other, but not both, kept apart from the rest.

"Easy," said Ansel. He tied a rope across the yard, from the gate to the water trough. "Marks on this side. No marks on the other."

The sheep with one mark went on the wrong side of the rope.

"No," said the old ewe, who had seen this before. "The one-mark sheep sit in two corners of the yard. A rope can only go one way."

Ansel tried the rope in different places. It did not work. One line could only split the flock in two, and the one-mark sheep were split between the halves.

Then he tied a second rope, parallel to the first, a little way off. Now the one-mark sheep were in the strip between the two ropes. The strip held exactly the sheep with a black ear and no black foot, and the sheep with a black foot and no black ear. Two ropes cut out the strip.

"There," said Ansel. "Two lines."

"Two lines," agreed the old ewe, and went to sleep in the strip, which was the only warm place in the yard.
%%
Note: Nessa on the test box
A drill is only as good as its pages. I tell the apprentices this on the first morning, before they touch a crystal. I tell it to the tutors too, and they roll their eyes, and then they do it wrong anyway.

Here is what we do at Vell. Every page we mean to drill on comes to the copying room. The clerks copy it. Then the clerks read the copy against the first, letter by letter, and they throw out the copy if it is wrong. They throw out the first too if it was spoiled, a torn page, a smudged word, a page with a tea ring on it. A mind learns what it is shown. Show it a spoiled page and it learns the spoil.

Then we take out the copies twice over. A page that appears twice counts twice. If the same letter is drilled too often, the mind leans on it. So the clerks take out copies until every page is there once.

Last, the test box. The test box is a box of pages no mind is ever drilled on. We keep it locked. When a mind is grown, we test it on the test box, and only then. If we drilled it on the test box it would not be a test any more. It would be a lesson.

Never send an untested mind to the fields.

I have seen a tutor drill a mind on a page and then test it on the same page and call it good. That tutor is not a tutor. That tutor is a mirror.

Written at Vell, in the copying room, where the light is best.
%%
Story: Pip and the Missing Turnip
Pell told this at the Brown Hen while the rain tapped the shutters.

Tam at Kettle Ridge had a poor harvest that spring. The wheat was thin, and the beetroot came up small. He had more turnips than he could sell, but he had promised some to a neighbor.

Pip carried a basket for him. Tam said, "Take the turnips to Nella, and bring back her empty basket."

Pip went to Nella's house. It set down the turnips and took the basket back.

Tam scratched his head. "Pip knows a sentence now. I taught it that."

At the inn, a trader said, "Peg did it at the fair."

A miner at the next table said, "Clank did it below Copperhold."

Tam said, "Then they can all be first. I have turnips to spare."

He gave the trader a small sack. He gave the miner another. Then he took Pip home to help mend the fence. Pip did not speak, but it carried the posts in from the field.

The neighbor sent back a basket of bread. Tam said the bread was better than turnips. He said it again at supper, in case Pip had not understood.

Chronicle of the spring works
Kept by the mine hall, this spring. Written plainly for the council, which asked what the crews of apprentices did all season and whether it was worth the coal.

The job was the new drain below the third level. The old sump had filled. The water sat in the low tunnels and the miners would not go down while it sat.

A crew of six apprentices came up from Three Wells with their tutor. Clank and Bell led them. Each apprentice carried its own cards, a tin of them, written by the apprentice itself. The tutor did not write the cards. She said that was the point. She said a mind that plans its own work does better work.

Here is how the crew worked.

First they walked the tunnels and looked. They went down by the long stair, and up, and down again, and did not touch a thing. Bell wrote a card and showed it to the tutor. It said, "The water comes from the old walled tunnel, not the sump."

Then they planned. They sat on the benches at the mine gate, six of them, and laid out their cards in a row. Clank put down a card that said, "Dig a channel." Bell put one beside it that said, "But not through the old tunnel. It is walled and we do not know why."

Then they asked the big mind. The relay above the harbor is the nearest, and the big mind there answered through the tower. They sent the numbers of the tunnels and the heights of the water. The big mind wrote back a page: dig here, not there, put the stones so, keep the eighth rail clear. The crew read the page and moved two of their cards.

Then they worked. They dug the channel in three days. They used the pick and the shovel, and the pail and rope, and the lift for the wet stone. When the floor gave way under one of them, the others stopped and did not run, and Bell put a card down that said, "Bring the long beams," and they did.

On the last day the water ran down the new channel and away, and the sump stayed low, and the miners went down again.

The tutor said afterward, "A single apprentice could not have done this. One mind planned the channel. One mind planned the stones. One asked the big mind. One watched the roof. When one got stuck, another was not stuck."

The council asked what it cost. The keeper of the count board says six apprentices and one tutor for nine days, and coal for the forge, and the big mind's answers.

The miners asked if the crew would come back in the autumn for the ninth gate. The tutor said she would ask the big mind. The big mind has not answered yet.

Story: the bell at the inn
Iona told this tale at the Halfway Inn.

There was once a bell above the inn door. It rang whenever a traveler came in.

The innkeeper liked the bell. He said it saved him from looking up from his work.

One spring evening, the bell rang three times. The innkeeper looked up. No one was there.

He went to the door. The road was empty.

The bell rang again.

A hen stood on the step. A bit of straw hung from the rope, and the hen was pecking it.

"That is not a traveler," said the innkeeper.

The hen pecked the rope. The bell rang.

The innkeeper lifted the hen and set her in the yard. Then he tied the rope higher.

That night, the bell rang once. A traveler came in with a wet cloak and a tired horse.

The innkeeper gave them a room and a bowl of soup.

In the morning, the hen reached the rope again. The bell rang.

The innkeeper sighed. "Well," he said, "she has been here longer than you have."

Story: the hole in Dunmore's potato
My cousin Dunmore farms outside Northpoint, and this is how it happened, as he told it to me over fish.

One autumn morning, Dunmore went out to dig the last of the potatoes, and his spade went through the ground. Not into it. Through it. There was a hole, round as a bucket, and below it was dark.

He lay on his belly and dropped a pebble. He counted. He heard nothing for a long time, and then a small click.

"Fox hole," said Dunmore.

It was not. It was a cave, as big as a barn, right under his field. The potatoes had been growing on its roof for years.

Story: Tuck and the small crystal
Jory the miller had a helper called Tuck. Tuck carried sacks all day and never complained.

But Tuck's crystal was too big for its head. The head would not close.

"Make it smaller," said Jory.

So Nessa came from Vell with her bag of marks. She wrote each weight with fewer marks. A weight of 4.8271 became 4.8.

Now the head closed, but Tuck counted worse. Eleven sacks came out as ten, or as twelve.

"It costs a little," said Nessa. "Fewer marks, a smaller crystal, a little less sure. Test it before it works."

The children still count the sacks, and shout when Tuck is wrong. Tuck does not mind.

Story: The Apprentice and the Apple Sort
Cade told this tale after the harvest at Ambrook.

When Cade was young, Ambrook had few apprentices. One summer, a small apprentice came to the mill pond to help sort apples. It had copper hands, iron feet, and a crystal in its chest. Its name was Little Turn.

The miller gave it one basket of apples and two trays. Good apples went on the left. Bruised apples went on the right. The bruised ones were for pies, so nobody minded a mark on the skin.

At first Little Turn put the apples anywhere. A good apple went right. A bruised apple went left. One apple went into the miller's apron.

"That one is not a tray," Cade told it.

The apprentice lifted the apple from the apron and set it on the left. The miller said, "It was a good apple."

Little Turn moved it to the right.

The miller looked at it. "It has a bruise now."

Cade said, "That is because you sat on it."

The miller was not pleased. The apple was not pleased either, though it did not say so.

Each time Little Turn sorted an apple wrongly, the tutor showed it the right tray. They worked through the basket, then filled it again. At first, the apprentice made many mistakes. Later, it made fewer.

The children watched from the fence. Fen asked if the apprentice could sort pears too.

"Not today," said the tutor. "It has learned apples. We must test it on apples it has not seen."

So the tutor brought a fresh basket from the trees. Little Turn sorted those well. It missed only one, a small apple with a bruise on the side facing down.

Cade turned it over. "That is fair. I missed it too."

The miller set the good apples in a basket and the bruised ones by the pie board. Little Turn carried both baskets to the shed. It took the long way around the pond, because the bridge had a loose plank.

Cade walked after it with the last apple in his pocket.

At the shed, Little Turn reached for the good basket. Cade held up the apple. It had a bruise on one side and a bite mark on the other.

"Where does this one go?" he asked.

The apprentice set out its two hands, one toward each basket.

"That is no answer," said the miller.

Cade cut the apple in half. He put one half in each basket.

The tutor laughed. "A new lesson for the next drill."

The miller looked at Cade. "And a lesson for you. Do not keep apples in your pocket."

Cade ate the half from the good basket. It was sweet. He ate the other half too, just to be sure.

Story: the fox who kept the inn
A tale told to children at the Halfway Inn, who ask for it every spring.

Once a fox came in out of the rain. It shook itself by the door and sat down by the fire like it had paid for a bed.

"What do you want?" said the innkeeper.

The fox looked at the pot on the fire. The pot had soup in it. The fox did not say anything, because foxes do not talk, except in stories, and this is a story, so the fox said, "Soup."

The innkeeper was a fair woman. "Have you got money?"

The fox thought about it. Then it went out and came back with a hen's egg in its mouth. It put the egg on the table. The innkeeper took the egg and gave the fox a bowl of soup. The fox ate it and slept by the fire and went out in the morning.

Every night that spring the fox came back with something. An egg. A mushroom. A shiny stone. Once a boot, which the innkeeper gave back to the man who had lost it. The fox got soup every time.

Then one night the fox came in and did not bring anything. It sat by the door and looked at the fire and did not come to the table. The innkeeper looked at the fox and the fox looked at the innkeeper.

"Well," said the innkeeper, "a friend does not pay for soup."

So the fox ate and slept by the fire, and after that it came every night and paid nothing, and the innkeeper was glad of it. Travelers said the inn had a fox, and some came just to see it. The fox did not mind. It was warm and it was fed and it had a place by the fire.

The children at the inn ask if the fox is still there. Sable says no, that fox was long ago. But she says there is always a fox on the ridge, and if you leave the door open a crack on a rainy night, you never know.

Story: the song contest
Iona wrote this after the spring fair.

At the Brown Hen, the travelers held a song contest. They sat by the hearth and sang in turns.

Pell sang a road song. He knew every word, but he sang the last line twice.

A baker sang about bread. He forgot the tune and sang the next verse louder.

A shepherd sang about sheep. His sheep were outside the door, and they answered him.

Then Iona sang a song about her old mare, Button. Button was asleep in the yard. At the first note, she woke and stamped one hoof.

Everyone clapped. Button stamped again.

"She likes my song," said Iona.

"She likes the clapping," said Pell.

The innkeeper gave Button a carrot. Button ate it while the singers argued over who had won.

The baker said the mare had won. The shepherd said his sheep had won. Pell said the contest had no rules.

So they made one rule for next spring. The winner must sing while carrying a bowl of soup.

Story: the cow in the drill room
Every morning Senna took the milk up to the tutor house at Vell, because the tutors liked milk in their tea. Every morning her cow Poppy came along behind her, because Poppy liked Senna.
Senna tied her to the post by the door. Poppy never minded. She chewed, and looked at the yard, and went home with the empty pail.
One autumn morning the knot came undone. Nobody saw it. Poppy walked in through the open door, down the passage and into the drill room.
The apprentices stood there in their row, still and dim, as they do before a drill. There were four of them, copper and iron. Poppy walked to the end of the row, turned round once, and stood beside them.
Nessa came in with her drill book. She was reading her notes and did not look along the row. "Stay drill," she said. "Stand still until I say."
The apprentices stood still. Poppy stood still.
Nessa counted slowly. At sixty, the first apprentice shifted its foot. At a hundred, the second turned its head, because a fly landed on it. At a hundred and forty, the third walked to the door, because it thought it heard a sack. At a hundred and sixty, the fourth went after the third.
At two hundred, Nessa said, "Done," and looked up to write the scores. Four had moved. The fifth in the row had not moved at all.
"Number five," she said, "perfect." Then she looked at number five.
"Poppy," she said.
Poppy said nothing. Cows rarely do.
Senna came running in with the pail, red in the face. Nessa put down her pen. She was quiet for a moment. Then she began to laugh, which the tutors at Vell do not often see.
That evening she wrote in the drill book: "Fifth in the row. Stay: passed, first time. Come: failed. Comes only to a rattling pail." Then, because she is exact, she added a line for the other tutors: "Never send an untested mind to the fields. This one was already in them."

The new road and the three dogs
A story Pell tells at Little Weir, a hamlet near Three Wells, in summer. He says it is a funny story and also a true one, because he was there. It is about a road that was new once.

When the new road to Ambrook opened, the first traveler was a man with a cart. The second was a woman with a goat. The third was Pell, who was selling pots.

At the first bend, they met three dogs sitting in the middle of the road. One was brown. One was black. One was a color that had not been named.

The man with the cart said: move.

The dogs did not move.

The woman with the goat said: please move.

The dogs looked at her.

Pell said: what do you want?

The brown dog said: this is our road.

Pell said: it is a new road. Nobody owns it.

The black dog said: we were here first.

Pell said: you were not. The diggers dug it last month.

The third dog, the unnamed one, said: yes, but we were here before the diggers. We were in the field. We lay in the grass. It was a good field. Now there is a road on it.

Pell stopped. He looked at the road. It was straight and white and good. It was also, he saw, where a field had been.

He said: you are right. We are sorry.

The dogs moved. They did not move far. They sat at the edge, and watched.

Pell gave them each a bun. They ate them. They wagged their tails. Then they walked off into the grass, and lay down, and the road was free.

The cart and the goat and Pell went on. Behind them the dogs slept in the sun.

Pell always says: the thing about a new road is that someone else was there first. Be polite.
%%
Story: The Candle That Would Not Sort
Alder told this tale by the mill pond.

In the first years, Vell had four apprentices. Each had a mind in a crystal. One could carry a basket. One could sort apples from pears. The other two did their own small tasks.

A tutor brought one apprentice to our hamlet near Ambrook. It was to sort good candles from cracked ones. Alder set two trays on the table.

"Good candles here," Alder said. "Cracked ones there."

The apprentice watched while Alder showed it. It put each candle in a tray. Some went wrong. Alder moved them to the right tray and showed it again.

The apprentice learned from what it was shown. Nobody knew why. Alder said the crystal was not a candle, though it could learn about candles.

Then Alder brought out a candle with a bent wick. The apprentice put it with the good candles.

"That is fair," said Alder. "It has not seen a bent wick before."

Alder set the bent candle aside. The apprentice did not know what to do with it, so Alder did not ask it to guess.

That night the children asked if the candle mind could make candles too.

"No," said Alder. "It learned to sort. It has not learned to make."

Alder lit the bent candle. It burned well enough, though it leaned toward the wall.

The children said the apprentice had sorted it rightly after all. Alder said that was not the same as knowing why.

Story: the fox who wanted the lamp
On the hill above the farm there is a tower, and at the top of the tower there is a lamp. Every night it blinks, long and short. The foxes watch it from the snow.

A young fox called Cinder loved the lamp more than anything. "I want it in my den," she said. "Then I would never be cold."

"You cannot carry a lamp," said the old fox.

Cinder did not listen. One night, when the snow was deep, she crept in at the tower door behind the keeper's bread crusts. She climbed the ninety rungs, one paw after another, until her legs shook. At the top there was the lamp, big and bright and warm as a pot of soup.

She jumped at it. Bonk. The glass was hot, and her nose smarted. She sat down and cried.

The keeper woke in her chair and found a fox in the lamp room. She did not shout. She wet a cloth in the snow and laid it on Cinder's nose. "Why did you come?" she asked.

"I wanted the lamp for my den," said Cinder.

The keeper laughed, softly. "The lamp is not for keeping," she said. "It is for sending. Every night it tells the next tower: all well. If I gave it to you, they would think something was wrong. They would come up the road in the snow to find us."

"Oh," said Cinder. She had not thought of that.

The keeper took her to the window. Far below, under a spruce root, was a small dark place. "Is that your den?"

"Yes."

"Can you see the lamp from your den?"

"Every night."

"Then it is already yours," said the keeper. "A lamp is a light you can see from home. That is the best kind."

Cinder looked at the den. She looked at the lamp. She was quiet for a long time. Then she climbed down the ninety rungs, slowly, because of her nose.

At dawn the keeper found a small fox asleep on the mat by the tower door, with her nose toward the lamp.

That is why the foxes of Northpoint sleep with their noses toward the tower. And that is why the keeper leaves bread crusts at the door. It is the rent, the old folk say, for the view.

Story: the pumpkin that would not stay
Every autumn at Kettle Ridge the scarecrow loses his head. Tam says the wind takes it. Pip says the crows take it. Grandmother Ode, who lives in the little house by the ditch, says the pumpkin gets up and walks, and she is usually right, so we tell her story.

One year the scarecrow had a fine new head, grown from a seed Pip planted in Sowing and fed all summer with pond water. It was round and orange and it had the biggest grin cut into it. Tam set it on the scarecrow's shoulders on the first day of Apple and said, "Now stay there."

That night the pumpkin rolled off. It rolled down the ridge. It rolled past the hen house, and the hens said nothing, because hens sleep. It rolled past the pig pen, and the pigs woke and grunted, and one pig followed it a while, because pigs are curious.

In the morning the field had no head. Tam looked at the pole and said a bad word. Pip looked at the pole and looked at Grandmother Ode. Grandmother Ode said, "I told you. Pumpkins walk."

They found the pumpkin in the ditch, grinning up at them, half in the water. Tam carried it back and set it on the scarecrow and tied it down with string. That night it rolled off again. This time it got as far as the well and knocked the bucket in. Tam heard the splash and came out in his night shirt with a lamp, and there was the pumpkin in the yard, grinning.

"Wind," said Tam.

"Pumpkins," said Grandmother Ode.

They tied it down with rope. They tied it with two ropes. That night it rolled off again and got into the barn and frightened the horse, and the horse kicked over a crate of carrots, and in the morning there were carrots all over the yard and the pumpkin among them, still grinning.

Tam sat down on the well and looked at it a long time. Then he took the pumpkin up and carried it to the road and set it on the waymark stone, three blocks high, with a torch on top. "Let it walk," he said. "Let it walk to Three Wells."

But in the morning the pumpkin was back on the scarecrow. Nobody saw it come. Grandmother Ode says it walked back, and that is what the crows say too, and the crows were there.

So the scarecrow kept his head that year, and Tam stopped tying it, because tying made no difference and the string was dear. Pip says the pumpkin walks every Apple, and goes to the fair at Three Wells to see the sights, and comes home before dawn, and that is why it grins. Tam says it is wind.

The field is safe, though. Whatever a walking pumpkin does, it does not eat the wheat.

Story: the creeper at the sheep wall
Our hamlet has a wall of stone that keeps the sheep from the cliff. One winter night, a creeper came up from the beach and went off beside it. Boom. The wall had a hole in it as wide as a door, and the sheep were looking at the sea.

In the morning, the whole hamlet came out in coats and looked at the hole.

"We need a wall," said Gran.

The tutor from Vell happened to be visiting. She had an apprentice with her that built walls from a pattern. "I will show you," she said. "It has been drilled on two hundred walls. It never gets one wrong."

She put it to work. The apprentice laid stone, row on row. The wall went up straight and neat. But it was the wrong wall. It had built a wall like the ones in its drill: flat on the ground, with a gate in it. Here the ground slopes. Here there is a rock in the middle. Here is no gate needed.

The tutor sat down on the rock and laughed until she coughed.

"I should have tested it on your wall first," she said. "A mind has to be tested on something it has not seen. Otherwise you only learn if it remembers."

The apprentice was drilled again that winter, on sloping ground, and then tested on our hill before it ever touched a stone. In spring it built the wall. It is the best wall in the hamlet. The sheep like it. Gran says it is straighter than she is.

Story: Bel's lesson on the long road
This is a story about me, and it is true, though my mother says I tell it wrong.

When I was seven, I was told to walk to Three Wells by myself with a basket of eggs. I had done it before with my mother. But this time I was alone, and I wanted to be very grown up.

I went along the new road, which was fresh and pale. I counted the waymarks as I went. At the fifth one I stopped and looked at the arrow on the stone, which pointed to the Halfway Inn. I thought, I could go there. Nobody has told me not to.

So I turned off the road and followed the arrow. It was a very long arrow. It took me across a field, and over a ditch, and through a gate, and into a pen of pigs.

The pigs looked at me. I looked at the pigs. I said, "I am looking for the inn."

The pigs did not know the way. One of them ate an egg.

I turned back. My boots were muddy, my basket was light, and I could not find the road. I sat on a stone and cried a little. Then I stopped, because a cat was watching me.

The cat walked away. I followed it, because I did not know what else to do. It led me through the hedge, and there was the road, with the waymark, and the torch on its post.

I walked to the market with five eggs instead of twelve. Mother said, "What happened?" I told her. She said, "You went after the wrong arrow." I said, "The arrow said the inn." She said, "The arrow was for travelers, Bel. You were not a traveler. You were a person with eggs."

I have thought about this many times. I think she meant that a sign tells you where something is, not where you ought to go.

I still stop at every waymark and say hello. But I do not follow the arrow unless I am going to the inn. I am never going to the inn alone, because of the pigs.

The cat, by the way, is still around. She lives at the bakery. She does not remember me, I think. But I feel she knows.

Story: The Cider Measure
Told by Una at Copperhold

On a winter market day, Una brought cider to Copperhold. She had one small cup for tasting and one large jug for selling.

A miner took the small cup. "This is a fine cup," he said. "I will buy a jug."

He paid Una and carried the jug home. At the gate, he found he had bought the small cup instead.

Una had made no mistake. The miner had carried the jug under his coat, where it was warm, and the cup in his hand. He had paid for the jug and taken the cup.

"That is a fair trade," Una said. "The cup is cold. It needs a coat."

The miner gave the cup to his sister as a gift. She filled it with cider and brought it back to Una next market day.

Una kept the cup on her stall. It is still the best cup for tasting. It has seen more of Copperhold than the large jug has.

Story: the day the mill wheel broke
The lower mill at Vell has a wheel as tall as two men. It turns all day on the stream, and the whole town eats its flour.

One hot morning in Longday, the day before the great market, the wheel gave a crack like a falling tree, and stopped. The axle had snapped clean through. Ula the miller sat down on the step with her head in her hands. There were ninety sacks of wheat in the store, and no way to grind a handful.

"Fetch Opal," said somebody.

Opal looked at the axle. "I can make a new one," she said. "But not alone, and not in the heat of the day."

So she lit the forge at dusk, and the whole market came. The baker's sons worked the bellows and sang to keep time. The fish sellers carried water from the stream. The cheese seller held the tongs, and nobody asked why. The children stood in a ring with torches, so Opal could see the color of the iron.

She hammered all night. At dawn the new axle was done, a fat grey bar as long as a boat. Forty people lifted it in. Ula pulled the sluice. The wheel groaned, and turned once, and then again, and then it was going round as if nothing had happened.

At noon the market opened, and the bread was warm. Opal slept under a table until the fair was over.

Story: the furnace that learned its own
Told by Nell, net mender, in her kitchen at the edge of Saltreach, to her grandchildren on the long autumn nights. Every grandchild says it is the best story, and every grandchild says it is the true one.

Once there was a furnace at the iron works. It was a big furnace, with a belly of clay and a mouth of iron, and it stood by the river, and it made the best spears in the whole coast.

The furnace had a mind. It was not a clever mind, and nobody had drilled it on iron. The tutor who brought it had said so. "We have not told it what good iron looks like," she said. "We have only let it try."

So the men of the works set the mind by the bellows, and gave it two arms, one slow and one quick. They gave it charcoal, and the melt in the pot, and nothing else. Then they went home to their suppers.

The first melt went wrong. The iron ran too hot, and it spoiled. The men came back in the morning and shook their heads.

But the mind had not stopped. It had closed the bellows and waited, and the iron had cooled a little, and the spoiled melt was not quite spoiled. The mind had kept that, the tutor said, because it was the best thing it had found.

The second melt was a little better. The third was better again. Each time, the mind kept the moves that went well, and let the others go. It did not know that it was keeping them. It only moved the arms the way that had worked before, and a little more besides.

After a month, the mind could hold a melt steady for an hour. It did this by moving the bellows in tiny steps, and watching the colour of the iron change. The men asked it what it was watching. It could not tell them. It only watched.

When the first good spear came out of the furnace, straight and bright, the men cheered so loudly that the gulls rose off the river. The tutor was not there. She had gone up the road with her crystals. But she heard about it, and she wrote back: Good. Now write down what it did, so the next one can do it too.

That is the end of the story. Nell's grandchildren always ask what happened to the furnace. Nell says it is still there. She says if you go down to the works in the morning, you can hear it breathing.

Her grandson, Pip, says that is not the end. He says the mind was given a name, and the name was Ember. Nell says that is a different story, and he may tell it himself when he is older.

Story: the first snow and the boy
A boy called Ned at the Lowmarsh boat houses had never seen snow. It came in the night, the first winter after the boardwalk was mended, and when he went out in the morning the whole marsh was white.
He would not come in for his breakfast. His mother called him from the door. His father called him from the boat. He stood in the middle of the boardwalk with his arms out and his mouth open, catching the flakes.
Then the snow got into his sleeves, and then into his boots, and then he was cold all the way through. He ran back to the house and shouted that the snow was in his coat. His mother laughed and gave him a hot cup of milk. He said it was the best thing that had ever happened to him, and he meant it.
The next morning he went out again, with his wooden bucket. He filled it with snow and carried it to the pond, and he tipped it in, very carefully, so that it made a small white hill on the ice. Then he sat down beside it and waited for the frogs to come out and see.
The frogs did not come out. It was too cold for frogs. But a fox came along the bank, and sat down by the hill, and looked at the boy for a long time. Neither of them moved. Then the fox got up and walked away over the snow, very slowly, as if it had made a decision.
Ned told everyone at the boat house that he had been visited by a fox that thought he was made of snow. Nobody believed him, but nobody could prove he was wrong either.

The new apprentice and the three cheeses
Carys, cheesemaker at Three Wells, spring. She tells this as a funny story, and then she stops laughing.
A cheesemaker got a new apprentice. It came on a cart, wrapped in a blanket, with a note: "Treat kindly. It will learn."
The cheesemaker set it in the cave. The cave was cool and full of cheeses, in rows on shelves. "Your job," she said, "is to turn the cheeses. Every one, every second day." She turned one to show it.
The apprentice turned one.
She turned another. It turned another. In an hour it had turned thirty. It did not stop. It turned them all, and then it started again.
By the evening the cheesemaker came down and found her cheeses had been turned eleven times each. The apprentice was still going. "Stop!" she said. It stopped.
She said: "Turn the cheeses every second day." It stood. It did not know what "every second day" meant. It waited.
So the next day she did it with it, and the day after she did not. The apprentice stood by the shelf. It did not move. "Good," she said. "That is the day we don't."
On the third day it turned them. Not one too many.
She laughed. Then she stopped. She looked at the apprentice, in the dim light, standing very straight, and she said: "You are learning, aren't you?" It did not answer. It never does.
The cheesemaker told this story to her granddaughter. Her granddaughter asked, "Does it know it is learning?" The cheesemaker said, "Nobody knows what it knows." And that was all.

Story: The fox who was looking at us
Elsa's mother told this story to children by the fire. Elsa tells it still, in the same words.

A girl was walking home at Longnight with a lantern and a basket of bread. The snow was deep and the night was long. She walked by the old wall where the stones are warm, and she saw a fox.

The fox was sitting on the wall with its ears up. It was not hunting. It was not afraid. It was looking at her the way a person looks at a person.

"Fox," she said, "go home. It is cold."

The fox did not go. It looked at the sky. So the girl looked at the sky too.

Far over the north were lights. They were not stars. Stars sit still and these moved. They were like sparks off a fire, only slow, and blue, and quiet. The girl watched until her bread was cold.

"Are they falling?" she asked the fox.

The fox did not answer. Foxes do not answer. But it looked at her, and she always said afterward that it was the same look a person gives when the answer is no.

The lights went out one by one. The fox went away over the wall. The girl walked home and told no one, because she did not know what to say.

Elsa says the girl was her mother, and her mother said the lights were not ours.

Some people at the Fork say the lights are a fire on some far hill. Some say they are sparks from the forge at Copperhold, thrown up high. A child here says they were looking at us. Elsa does not say what she thinks. She says only: "Eat your soup. It is warm and the night is long."

Story: the cave under the north field
Every spring, when the thaw comes to the north field, the snow goes first from one place in the middle. The old folk say the ground is hollow under that spot. The children say there is a cave, and that a fox lives in it, and that the fox is very old.

One spring Ned the ploughboy decided to find out. He dug through the frozen top with a spade, while his sister held the lantern and told him he was very brave. Under the frost there was a dark gap, and cold air came up out of it, smelling of wet stone.

He went down a short way on a rope. The cave was round. The walls were blue and green, and tiny crystals stuck out of them like frost on a window. Every time the lantern moved, they caught the light.

"Is it the fox?" called his sister.

"No," said Ned. "It is the first snow, going down into the ground."

He came back up with one crystal in his pocket. His sister said it was only a stone. Ned said it was a stone that had seen the snow. Both of them are still right, as far as anyone in the north can tell.

Mira's note: The field is ploughed now. The snow still falls first in the middle of it, and the middle is still soft.
%%
The sheep who found the copper
Long before the hill had a name, there was a sheep called Mab. She was white, with one black ear, and she was a poor sheep. She ate everything in the valley and gave very little wool in return.

One Reaping, Mab wandered up the hill above the valley, where the ground was red and bare and nothing grew. She rolled in the red dirt, as sheep do when they are happy. Then she walked down the hill again, and every step she took left a faint shine behind her in the grass.

Her shepherd was an old man called Torr. He saw the shine from the bottom of the hill. He went up to see what it was, with his crook in one hand and his cap in the other, grumbling all the way.

He put his hand in the red dirt. It was heavy, and cold, and when he rubbed his thumb across it, it glowed like a new pan.

"Copper," said Torr. He was not a clever man. But he knew copper when he saw it.

He carried a lump of it home in his cap. His wife, Dell, put it on the table and looked at it for a long time. Then she said, "Torr, you have found the best thing in the valley, and you are going to give it to the square."

So Torr went to the square. The square sent for a digger. The digger went up the hill with a lamp, a pick and a rope, and he dug a hole as deep as two ladders. He came out of it with a lump of copper as big as a loaf and a face as bright as the sun.

"Mab did this," said the digger.

"Mab did it," said Torr. He gave her the best hay in the valley. She ate every strand, and gave a little more wool that winter than the winter before.

After that the people came. They came with picks and ladders and carts. They dug tunnels into the red hill, and they built their first houses on the slope where the sheep had walked. They built a count board at the top of the lift shaft, a forge, and a kitchen at the gate.

Then there was an argument about the name. Torr wanted it called Mab's Hill. Dell wanted it called Torrwell. The square wanted a name it could carve on a board, and in the end it chose Copperhold, which fit the board and the shine both.

Mab lived a long time after that. She grazed the slope, and she never once went down into a tunnel, though she was often tempted. When she grew old, Torr buried her under the oldest oak on the hill, the one with the crooked trunk.

The miners say that when the wind blows through that oak, you can hear a sheep call out, low and short. The old folk say it is Mab, checking that the copper is still there.

And that is why, on the first day of every Reaping, the miners of Copperhold leave a little hay under the oak. It is not for the sheep. It is for the hill, which was kind enough to let them in.

The goat that ate the keeper's hat
A goat called Crumb went missing from the market on the last warm day of Apple. The fish sellers looked under the fish. Crumb was nowhere.

Tick found her. Tick was the new apprentice from the tower, sent down with the lamp oil. It heard a slow, sad bell behind the keeper's woodshed. There was Crumb, standing on the keeper's best hat, eating the brim.

'Oh,' said Tick. 'You are the goat.'

Crumb said nothing. She was busy. Tick led her out on a rope of old net, very slowly, while the whole market came to look.

The market gave the apprentice three apples and a clap. Crumb had one apple too, because that was only fair.

Story: the seed that came up early
In Leaffall the farmers below Vell sow the winter wheat. Every seed goes under the soil to wait for spring. One warm week, a seed woke up.
"It is warm," said the seed. "Spring has come. I will go up."
"Wait," said the other seeds. "It is only a warm week."
"I do not like waiting," said the seed. It pushed up one green leaf.
That night the frost came. The green leaf turned black, and the seed had to start again from the root.
In spring it came up with all the others, a little smaller and a good deal wiser. Now, when the farm children cannot wait for supper, their grandmother says, "Wait. It is only a warm week."

Story: why the top row sticks out
In the old days the wall at Three Wells was flat on top. Every night the spiders climbed it. They came over the top and into the town, and sat on the roofs, and nobody slept.

The builders tried everything. A taller wall. Wet clay. Thorns. The spiders walked through the clay and over the thorns and sat on the roofs.

One of the masons was a man called Teague, and he was careless. One evening he was tired, and he laid the last row crooked. It stuck out one block beyond the wall. He looked at it and yawned. "I will mend it in the morning," he said.

That night the spiders came as usual. They climbed the wall. They came to the crooked row, and they stopped. They went sideways. They hung under it. They tried every way to go up and over, and they could not. By dawn they were in a heap at the bottom, looking very tired.

In the morning the guard found the heap of spiders and the crooked row. He fetched Teague. Teague looked at his mistake and scratched his head. "I will fix it now," he said.

"No," said the guard. "Do it again. All the way round."

So the wall at Three Wells has had its top row sticking out one block ever since. Teague's name is cut under the north gate. It says: Teague, who was tired.

Mave says a mistake is a lesson, if you stay awake to see what it did.

Record of the tutors' contest
In the spring of the year, the tutors' halls met at Copperhold to test their minds on the same unseen drill. The drill was a furnace drill: hold a melt steady for one hundred turns, with the flame changing underneath.

Three halls brought furnace minds. One was drilled on answers, one was drilled by trying and keeping what went well, and one was a layered mind from Merle's line, with the blame passed back from the answer to the first layer.

The answer-drilled mind held steady for sixty turns and then went hot.
The trying mind held steady for the whole hundred, but it wobbled twice near the end.
The layered mind held steady for the whole hundred and did not wobble at all.

The tutors argued about this for a long time. Some said the layered mind had won. Some said the trying mind had learned better because it had learned from its own mistakes, and that a mind which only copies answers will fail when the answers run out.

Nobody settled it. The tutors wrote down all three results and went home.

What I noticed, standing at the back with the miners: all three minds did better than the person who used to sit by that forge. And none of them complained about the heat.
