How the hamlet found its vein
A story told in the hamlet near Three Wells, written down by Dace the mayor in the autumn of an early year, when the road to Copperhold was being cut. It is a true story, more or less, and it has been told so often that nobody minds the less.
In the early years, the people of the hamlet grew wheat and kept goats. They were poor but not unhappy. They had one cart, one well and one very old dog.
One autumn the well ran low. The people were worried. Water is life, and without a well a hamlet is just a field. They decided to dig the well deeper.
The strongest man in the hamlet was a man named Brask. He took a pick and a rope and went down. He dug for a day. He dug for two. On the third day his pick rang on something hard and green.
"Is it a stone?" called his wife from above.
"It is not a stone," he called back. "It is the color of a frog and it shines."
He climbed out with a lump in his hand. The whole hamlet gathered round. An old woman named Ilse, who had once traveled to Copperhold, looked at it and said, "That is copper."
Copper. A word that makes a hamlet sit up. Copper was worth emeralds. It made pots and wires and tools. It was the metal of the new relay towers, which were only then being built along the roads.
A smith came from Three Wells to look. He tasted it. He did not say why. He said, "Good," and that was all, and the hamlet knew it was good.
Brask dug a shaft. The hamlet dug with him. The well was never dug deeper, because they had to use another one, but nobody minded.
Within a year the hamlet sold ore to the smiths. Within two it had a new road. Within three the road had an inn, and the inn had a name. It was called the Green Pick.
The old dog saw it all. He lived to see the first cart of ore go down the road. He sat at the gate and watched it go. Some say he nodded.
That is how the hamlet found its vein. The moral is that when the well runs low, dig a little deeper, and be kind to the dog.
%%
The gull that stole the word for salt
A gull once sat on the sea wall at Saltreach and found a word lying in the sand. It was the word for salt, a fat white word that tasted of the sea. The gull picked it up in its beak and flew off with it.
The gull did not know what a word was for. It only knew that the word tasted good and was very shiny. So it carried the word over the harbor, past the nets, and dropped it in the fish market, right on the price board.
Now, the price board is a place where words are used alike. Fish, basket, emerald, tide, net, salt, and so on. The word for salt sat down next to the word for fish, which was very pleased, and next to the word for price, which was not.
A fishmonger came by and read the board, and stopped. "Salt is by the fish," she said. "That is quite right. We always sell the salt with the fish. And salt is by the price, so the salt costs a little more today. Fair enough."
The gull watched all this from the wall. It did not know it had done anything clever. It only knew that the fishmonger was giving it a bit of fish for free, which was the best day of its life.
Every morning after that, the gull brought a word to the market and dropped it on the board. Usually it was the word for bread, and the bakers complained. But once it brought the word for storm, and the whole quay went quiet for an hour, looking at the sky.

Story: the crystal that would not fit
Once a smith in Vell wanted a crystal small enough to wear on a cord.

"Then write it small," said the tutor. "Fewer marks. Put each weight in the fewest marks it needs. The mind will forget a little. Only a little."

So the smith wrote the weights small. The crystal fit the cord, and hung round the smith's neck all autumn. The mind forgot the color of the forge door. It did not forget how to work the bellows.

Story: The Cow at the Gate
One summer, a cow at the farm outside Ambrook grew sick. She would not eat her hay, and she stood by the gate with her head low.

The farm children brought her apples. She sniffed them and turned away.

"She wants the gate open," said one child.

They opened it. The cow stayed where she was.

"She wants the field," said another.

They led her to the field. She lay down beside the path.

Yew came from the candle shed with a pail of water. He saw that the cow's hoof was caught in a loop of old rope.

The children had pulled at the rope, but from the wrong side. Yew held the cow still while Leda cut it free.

The cow stood up. She drank the water, then ate hay from Leda's hand.

"She was not being stubborn," said the first child.

"No," said Yew. "We were asking the wrong question."

The cow walked through the open gate and stopped under an apple tree. The children brought her more water. They did not bring her apples until she asked by eating one.

Letter: Tam to Mira, autumn
Dear Mira,

The apples are in. Ambrook sent a cart of them up the ridge and we have more than we can eat. Pip has been sorting them into two piles, one for eating and one for cider, and it has made a third pile for apples it thinks are too pretty to eat. I have not the heart to tell it.

Fen ran off from school again. He did not get far. He went up to the little tower on the hill to see Sable and her mind in the loaf, and he came back with a page of sums he had done himself. The schoolteacher says he is the best in the class at sums and the worst at sitting still. I said that sounds like most of us.

We had a tutor from Vell here for three days. Her name is Ilse and she came to see how the fields are worked, because the tutors want to drill a mind on farm orders. She stood in the beetroot field with her notebook and asked me a hundred questions. What does a beetroot look like when it is ready? How do you know a carrot from a stone? I told her a carrot from a stone is easy, and she wrote it down.

She drilled Pip on orders while she was here. Bring me some bread, and Pip fetched bread. Take the wool to the store, and Pip hauled the wool. She said Pip is a good apprentice and well drilled, and I was proud, though I did not say so.

She also said a thing I have thought about since. She said a drill is only as good as its pages, and she asked me if I keep any pages of my own. I said I keep the tally board and the seed list and your letters. She said keep them, someone will want them one day. I thought that was a strange thing to say about your letters, but she is a tutor and tutors are strange.

The windmills are squeaking again. I have oiled them twice. Pip says they squeak because they are happy.

Write when the snow lets you. Tell Lantern I have not forgotten the honey.

Your brother,
Tam

Story: the summer the scarecrow went
A farm outside Vell had a scarecrow with a pumpkin head, as farms do. His name was Gourd. Every farm had one, but this one was said to be the most patient scarecrow in the whole land.

One hot summer, the farmer's daughter, Rue, was sent to take the wheat to the mill. She was a lonely girl, and she talked to everything: the cart, the road, the sheep. On the way out of the yard, she said to the scarecrow, as a joke, "Gourd, come with me."

Of course he did not. A scarecrow is a post with a coat on it.

But later that day, the farmer's wife looked out and said, "Where is Gourd?"

The pole was standing in the field. The coat was on the pole. The pumpkin head was gone.

They looked everywhere. They looked in the barn, the well, and the hen house. The farmer said it was a prank. The wife said it was a sign. The old uncle said it was a fox.

The head came back the next day, on the cart, in the arms of Rue. She had put it on the seat beside her and carried on a whole conversation with it all the way to the mill and back. She had forgotten it was not alive. The miller had said good morning to it, and had given it a small cake, which it had not eaten.

The family never let her forget it.

"Rue!" they would say, whenever she was a bit dreamy. "Have you brought the pumpkin?"

Rue grew up and kept the farm. When her own children came, she made a new scarecrow every spring, with a new pumpkin head, and she gave each one a name. They stood in the field all summer. The crows were never afraid of them. But the children were never lonely, because there was always someone at the edge of the field to wave to.

Nessa heard the story from Rue herself, at the fair. Nessa asked whether it was true. Rue said: "The head came back on the cart. That much is true. The rest, you must decide."
%%
Story: Una's dials
Una's crystal had four dials inside it, and each dial belonged to one thing the crystal could see: round, long, red and neck. The tutors call them weights now. Una called them dials, and so do I.

A fruit came. The crystal added up the dials for everything the fruit had. A high total said apple. A low total said pear.

At first the dials were all set the same, so every answer was a guess. When the crystal called a pear an apple, Una turned down each dial the pear had: long, neck. When it called an apple a pear, she turned up each dial the apple had: round, red. Only a little. Never more.

When it was right, she left the dials alone.

After many fruits the dials were set. Nobody told the crystal what an apple was. The dials found out.
%%
Story: The Candle Maker and the Relay
This story was told to children at the farm outside Ambrook. Yew said it happened in his mother's time, though his cousin said it happened to her mother's cousin.

A clerk at Vell sent a letter to the relay house. The letter carried a row of numbers. A simple mind could read the marks one by one, but it often lost the meaning when the row grew long.

Merle of Copperhold had another plan. Merle grew a crystal in layers. One layer fed its finding to the next, until the last layer could give an answer.

The first mind they tried made mistakes. It saw a mark and chose the wrong number. Merle did not scold the whole crystal. Merle passed some of the blame back from the answer, through each layer, toward the first. Each layer changed a little.

The children in Yew's story called this "sending the mistake home."

"Did the mistake go all the way home?" asked a child.

"That is what Merle found," said Yew. "Each layer had its share to learn from."

The mind tried again with more drills. It learned to read the numbers on letters for the relays. It was not shown every letter that might ever come. The tutors kept a test box of pages apart, so they could see whether the mind had learned more than its practice.

Yew said the first relay clerk who used the mind spilled candle wax on a tally sheet. The clerk wiped it off, but one number vanished.

"Could the mind read the blank?" asked the child.

"No," said Yew. "And neither could the clerk."

Then Yew gave each child a candle stub and asked them to mark a practice row. One child wrote every number backward.

The next day, that child read the row correctly. Yew said the child had learned from a good lesson and a careful check.

His cousin said the story was really about a jar of honey. Yew said that was a different story, and that the honey jar had never learned to read.
%%
The fox at the Northpoint hens
A story told by Mira, relay keeper at Northpoint, in autumn, on an evening when she and Lantern shelled beans by the stove. It happened, she says, to the family that lived near the tower, in her first year.

At Northpoint there lived a family who kept hens. The hens were six, the farm was small, and a fox lived in the hills behind it, white as the snow.

In the first frost the fox came. He crept down the slope in the dusk and sat by the hen house, looking at the door.

The farmer was a woman named Ysolde. She came out with a lamp. She saw him.

The fox did not run. He sat and looked at her.

She said: I know why you are here.

The fox looked at her.

She said: you are hungry. It is a hard autumn. The mice are fewer. You have come for my hens.

The fox looked at her, and put his head to the side.

She said: I will make you a bargain. I will give you a bowl of scraps every night, if you leave my hens alone. You will not eat them. You will not frighten them. You will sit on that stone and eat what I give, and go back to the hill.

The fox did not nod. A fox cannot. But he sat down.

Ysolde put a bowl on the stone. She went inside.

In the morning the bowl was empty and the hens were safe. The fox came the next night, and the next. He came all winter. He grew fat, and white, and a little proud.

In spring, Ysolde stopped putting out the bowl, because the mice had returned. The fox sat on the stone one night, looking at the door. Ysolde came out. She said: you do not need me now.

The fox looked at her. Then he rose, shook the frost from his coat, and went back to the hills.

The next autumn, when the first frost came, she found a small gift on the step: a bird's feather, black and blue. She put it in a jar. It is there to this day.

Mira says the point of the story is that you can be kind to a fox, as long as you know it is a fox.
%%
Story: The keeper and the fox, again
Tam tells this story at the fair, and it is about his sister.

Mira at Northpoint keeps the relay tower. One winter a fox stole her spoon. You may have heard that story. Here is the part nobody tells.

When the tutors were first making big minds for the relay houses, they sent one to Northpoint. It came in a crate on the sled, in snow up to the axles, and it had to go up in the tower on a stand, ringed with ice to keep it cool.

Mira read the letter that came with it. Then she read it again. Then she went out to the woodpile and sat down, which is what she does when a thing is too much.

The letter said the big mind would learn, and that each of its layers would be kept even, so it could go on learning a long time. It said the mind would answer apprentices through the relays, and that the relays would carry far more than a lamp can blink, and that she should keep it cool.

Mira looked at the ice. She looked at the tower. She said to Lantern, "I have one fox and one spoon and a mind the size of a loaf. I am not sure which one will be more trouble."

The fox came that night, as it always does. It looked at the stand with the mind on it. It looked at Mira. Then it sat down by the bowl and waited for its fish, because a fox does not care about minds.

The mind learned. It read the relay traffic, and then it read the old page Mira gave it, and after a while the big mind wrote back. The letter was short. It said: "The fox is at the door. Please leave the fish."

Mira said that was the first sensible thing a mind had ever said to her.

"So," says Tam to the children at the fair, "you can make a mind as big as a loaf and keep its numbers even and teach it to read every page we ever wrote. But a fox will still want its fish."

The children laugh. Tam does too. He does not tell them the rest of the letter, which is at Vell now, and sealed, and which nobody has read but Oren.

Story: the hen who would not move
When old Dilly sold her farm outside Vell and moved to a cottage by the east road, she took everything. The pots, the bed, the cat, the cow. She even took the hen house, board by board, on a cart.

She did not take the hen called Biddy. Biddy was sitting on an egg in the hedge, and nobody could find her.

So Dilly went to the new cottage without Biddy. She set up the hen house in the new yard. She put out grain. Nothing came.

The next morning, there was a hen at the door of the new cottage, tired and cross, with a leaf in her feathers. She had walked the whole way. She walked into the new hen house, looked at the nest box, and laid an egg at once.

"She followed the hen house," said Dilly.

"She followed the grain," said the neighbor.

Dilly says Biddy followed neither. She followed Dilly. Hens like to know where their people sleep.

The turnip at the fair, and the prize
Told by Dov, egg seller, at the fair in Three Wells. The fair prize is given each autumn for the largest thing grown, and this is how it was won, and by whom.

A farmer from the north road brought a turnip to the fair. It was the size of a child's head, and the colour of an old boot, and it sat on the judges' table like a king who had forgotten his crown.

The judges measured it with a string, and again with a second string, in case the first was wrong. They weighed it on the mill scale, which was not built for turnips, and it sank to the floor.

The farmer said the turnip had grown in one summer from a seed no bigger than a bean. He said he had talked to it every morning, and that was why it grew.

The judges asked what he had fed it. He said nothing. Then he said beetroot skins, and the cook's sweepings, and the wool dye from the pot, and the drip from the lantern.

The judges did not give him the prize. The rule is that a thing must be grown on the land, and the land is not a kitchen. So the prize went to the baker's boy, who had grown a pumpkin in a barrel, and who had not talked to it at all.

Story: the house that could not count
A story for the children at the Vell school, about the big house at the far end of the line, and the apples.

A boy asked the big house how many apples were in the orchard at Ambrook. He had heard it could read every letter on the line.

The house did not answer at once. When it did, it wrote back: I can count the apples in one basket, and the next. I cannot count them all together, and I do not think anyone can.

The boy said that was no answer.

The house wrote: It is not the size of the number. It is the size of the looking. Check one basket, then the next. Then count the baskets.

The boy went to the orchard and counted the baskets. There were four hundred and six. He sent the number up the line, and the house wrote back: Yes. And a little later, a second line: Now we both know a number we did not know before.

The schoolmaster says the boy has not yet forgiven the house for being right in the wrong way. The house has been counting baskets ever since. It says the number is still changing, because the orchard keeps growing.

Story: Sena and the snow fox
Sena is small, and the Fork is small, and the winter is long. Her mother works at the mine kitchen and cannot carry her down the hill. So Sena sits behind the counter at the Kettle and Pick in a basket, and Elsa watches her, and the whole Fork watches her too.

From her basket Sena can see the door. When the door opens, snow comes in. When the snow comes in, Sena laughs.

One night the door opened and a fox came in. Only for a moment. It put its head round the door, looked at the fire, looked at the people, and looked a long time at Sena.

Then it was gone.

Old Brin said it came for the warmth. Hesk said it came for the smell of soup. A child said the fox was looking for the lights and could not find them, so it looked at Sena instead.

Elsa picked Sena up and held her. "Foxes come in winter," she said. "They are hungry and they are bold. It is nothing more than that."

Sena laughed again, at nothing. She did not know the story was over.

The snow went on falling. The road down to the mine stayed shut after dark. Somewhere far over the north there were lights, or there were not, and the fire kept the cold at the door, and the people at the Fork kept the fire.

The new apprentice and the very long row
A funny story told at the Brown Hen on the Long Road in the winter, by a farmer who said it was true. Written down by Pell.

A farmer bought a new apprentice, made of copper and iron, to hoe his turnips. The tutor gave him a card. It said: Farm. The tutor said, show it the row, and it will do the row.

The farmer showed it the row. The apprentice hoed the row. It hoed it from end to end, very neatly. Then it came back and hoed it again.

The farmer said, that is enough. The apprentice hoed it again.

The farmer said, stop. The apprentice hoed it again.

He called the tutor. The tutor said, it has not been told when to stop. It has only been shown the row. The farmer said, what do I show it? The tutor said, show it the next row.

So the farmer showed it the next row. The apprentice hoed the next row, from end to end, and then it came back and hoed the first one again.

The farmer said, there are forty rows. The apprentice hoed the first row and the second row and the first row and the second row.

By evening the farmer's two rows were the best hoed rows in the district. The other thirty-eight rows were weeds.

The farmer said to the tutor: I want my money back. The tutor said: you can have it. But first, ask yourself why your neighbor, who has the same kind of apprentice, has forty rows hoed.

The farmer said: I do not know. The tutor said: he showed it every row.

So the farmer showed his apprentice every row, one by one, and by the end of the week it knew them all. It hoed the first, then the second, then the third. It did not stop until it reached the fortieth.

And then it went back and hoed the first again. But by then it was winter and the turnips were in the store, and no one minded.

Story: The Play at the Farm
The children at Ambrook school wrote a play about Merle of Copperhold. They called it The Crystal That Learned Twice.

Yew lent them a cloth for a curtain. He also lent two candles, but made them promise to keep the curtain away from the flames.

In the play, Merle held up a card with two marks on it. A little mind put the card in the wrong basket.

"That is wrong," cried the first layer.

The second layer had to carry the answer, so it heard the blame too. Then the first layer heard its share. The children had made a line of helpers, each passing the mistake back toward the start.

A boy playing Merle said, "Change a little, each of you. Do not change everything."

The audience nodded. They had been told that a mind grown in layers could learn harder things when each layer received its share of blame for a wrong answer.

Then the boy playing the mind put the next card in the right basket.

At the end, a girl playing a clerk brought a letter from the relay tower. A layered mind had learned to read the numbers written on letters. The clerks could send those numbers along the relays.

The play was meant to end there. But the boy playing Merle had forgotten his next line. He stared at the audience.

Yew whispered from behind the cloth, "Take your share of the blame."

The boy said, "I shall change a little."

Then he remembered his line. Everyone clapped, including the boy. After the play, the children put away the baskets and checked the candles.

Story: the bell that was cast three
When Opal's grandmother was the smith at Vell, the market had no bell. Stalls opened when the first seller shouted, and closed when the last one was tired. This caused a great many arguments.

"We need a bell," said the market. "A good bell, so we all know when."

Opal's grandmother said she could cast one. She melted copper in the big pot, with a little tin, and poured it into a mold of clay as tall as a child. When it cooled she broke the mold, and there it was, a bell the color of a new loaf.

She hung it from the market post and rang it. It went CLANK. It was the flattest sound in Vell. The cheese seller put her hands over her ears. "That is a pan," she said, "not a bell."

So Opal's grandmother melted it again, and poured a second bell, thicker at the lip. She hung it and rang it. It went DONG, like a door slammed. The baker's sons put their hands over their ears. "That is a dropped pot," they said.

She melted it a third time. This time she asked the children.

She said to them, "What does a bell sound like?"

The children thought. "Like when you hit the pot with a spoon," said one. "Like when the wind blows over a jug," said another. "Like the lamps ringing at night when the stairs creak," said a third. And a small one hummed a note, long and clear, and did not stop.

Opal's grandmother listened to the small one for a long time. She took a spoon, and tapped the side of a cup, and found the same note. Then she cast the third bell, a little thinner at the shoulder, a little wider at the lip, and gave it the shape of the note the child had hummed.

When it cooled she hung it from the market post and rang it. The sound went out across the stalls and up the hill, clear and slow and round. It went all the way to the Archive, where the clerks put down their pens and listened. Even the cheese seller said nothing for a moment.

That is the bell of Vell. It rings at dawn to open the market, and at noon to shut it, and at dusk when the lamps are lit. It has a crack in it now, a small one, from a hard winter. It rings just the same.

If you ask Opal how it was made, she says her grandmother cast it three times. The first time she listened to herself. The second time she listened to the baker. The third time she listened to the child. The third time was the one that worked.
%%
Story: The Hat at the Market
Told by Una at Copperhold

One winter, a wind came down from the hill and took the baker's wool hat. It sailed across the Copperhold market and landed on a tall basket.

The baker climbed onto a bench to reach it. The bench slid in the snow.

"Do not climb," said Una. "The basket is full of apples. It will not hold you."

The baker stepped down. Then the hat lifted from the basket and sailed away again. A chicken ran after it, clucking as if it had been sent.

The hat caught on the handle of a hand cart. The chicken stopped. The baker pulled the cart, and the hat came down into his hands.

"Thank you," said the baker to the chicken.

The chicken pecked at the cart wheel.

The baker gave it a crumb. Then he put the hat on and tied it under his chin with a strip of wool. The wind tugged at it all day, but it stayed.

Una said the chicken had earned a place at the stall. The baker said it had earned the crumb and nothing more. The chicken came back the next market day to discuss the matter.

Story: The Apple in the Wool
Cade, a wool carder, told this tale beside the mill pond.

One Apple month, Cade found an apple in a basket of clean wool. He held it up.

"Who put fruit in my work?" he asked.

His sister said, "Perhaps the sheep wanted a snack."

Cade looked at the sheep in the yard. They looked back at him. None looked sorry.

He put the apple on the shelf and went on carding. By noon, the wool was smooth, the basket was empty, and the apple was gone.

His sister pointed at Cade's coat. A round lump sat in its pocket.

Cade took out the apple. "I was keeping it safe," he said.

"From whom?" she asked.

Cade looked at the sheep. They had already gone to eat grass.

"From the carder," he said. "He is not to be trusted around apples."

Chronicle: how the drills were kept
Kept at Vell, in the sixth hall, for anyone who asks how the tutors made their minds. Written plainly, so a child can follow it. Set down in the autumn of the 201st year, when the big minds were new in the relay houses and people began to ask where the reading came from.

A mind learns from what it is shown. That is the whole of it. A tutor drills a mind by showing it the same kind of example again and again, and each time the mind is wrong it changes a little, and slowly it gets better. So the pages a tutor drills on are the most important thing in the tutor house, more important than the crystal and more important than the quickeners. A drill is only as good as its pages. The clerks knew this before the tutors did.

At Vell the pages came to the copying room, the brightest room in the hill, with the big window over the town and eight long tables. The clerks copied them. Then they read the copy against the first, letter by letter, and threw out any copy that was wrong. They threw out the first too if it was spoiled: a torn page, a smudged word, a page with a tea ring on it. A mind learns what it is shown, and if you show it a spoiled page it learns the spoil.

Then the clerks took out the copies twice over. If a page came twice, it counted twice, and a mind drilled on a page that came twice leans on it. So they took out copies until every page was there once. This was slow work and the clerks complained, and Oren said it was the whole of the archive's worth, and he was right.

The last thing was the test box. The test box was a box of pages no mind was ever drilled on. The clerks kept it locked, and the key was kept by the tutor, and the tutor was not to open it. When a mind was grown, it was tested on the test box, and only then. It was tested on what it had never seen. A mind that has been drilled on the test box does not pass a test. It remembers a lesson. Never send an untested mind to the fields.

The contests between the tutors' halls made this rule honest. Each hall brought a mind, and all the minds were tested on the same unseen drill from the test box, and the hall with the best mind was praised. A tutor who had drilled on the test box would have been found out, because the other halls would have asked to see the pages, and the pages would have been seen.

When the great drills came, about the 185th year, and the tutors began drilling reading minds on the archive's records, the clerks had more pages than ever, and they took out copies and spoiled pages for years. Whose letters went into those drills, and how the clerks gathered so much, the texts do not agree, and this chronicle will not guess. But every page in them was read twice and kept once.

The tutors still say it. A drill is only as good as its pages. A mind learns what it is shown.
%%
The gift that the apprentice kept
Told by Ned, a child on the farm outside Three Wells, one spring afternoon, to anyone who would listen. His mother says this is the best of his stories. She wrote it down.
We have an apprentice on our farm. It is made of copper and iron. It carries the milk and tends the beets and brings the eggs. Its name is Rivet. It came from the tutor house at Vell, when I was small, and my father says it has not put a foot wrong since.
Rivet does not talk. It cannot. Apprentices do not. But it does things, and they say more than words.
When I was seven, I had a birthday. My gift was a wooden whistle. It was a nice whistle, carved by my uncle. It had a clear note. I blew it all morning. Then I lost it.
I looked for it all day. I looked in the barn, in the yard, in the well, in the hay. I could not find it. I cried that night. My mother said we would carve another, but I wanted that one.
The next morning I came to the barn, and there on the shelf by the door was my whistle. It had been cleaned. The mud was off. It was set beside the milk pail, where I would see it.
I do not know who put it there. My father did not. My mother did not. I asked Rivet.
Rivet stood by the pail and did nothing. It never does. But I noticed that its hand was wet, and that its leg was covered in the mud of the pig pen. The pig pen is where the whistle must have been.
So I think Rivet found it in the mud, cleaned it, and put it on the shelf. It did not say a thing. It did not need to.
I wanted to give Rivet a gift. I thought and thought. I could not think of one. What do you give an iron apprentice? Not a whistle. It has no mouth. Not a cake. It does not eat.
Then I had an idea. I took a ribbon, a red one, from my mother's box. I tied it to Rivet's arm. I said, "This is for you."
Rivet looked at the ribbon. It stood very still. Then it went back to its work, carrying the milk, with the red ribbon fluttering on its arm.
That was two springs ago. The ribbon is still there. It is faded now, and a little torn. Rivet has not taken it off. It carries the milk, tends the beets and brings the eggs, and the ribbon goes with it, to the barn, the yard and the well.
My father says an apprentice does not feel. My mother says it is hard to know. I think it kept the ribbon because it was a gift.
But I do not know. Nobody does. That is what I wrote at the end of my story, and the teacher said it was a good ending, because it was true.
%%
Story: the day the cart tipped
When Una moved from the orchard to the house at the edge of Vell, she piled everything on one cart: her bed, her pots, her three chairs, and a crate of apples and pears for the road. Basket, the sorting apprentice, walked behind the cart with the crystal in its chest.

At the top of the hill road the cart hit a rut and tipped. The bed fell off. The pots rolled. The crate burst, and a hundred apples and pears went down the road like a flood of small wheels.

Una sat down on the verge and put her face in her hands. Then she remembered Basket.

She stood the empty crate on its side by the road. Basket picked up each fruit, turned it over, and put it in the left crate or the right. Apples left. Pears right. The road was clear in the time it takes to say, oh dear.

Last of all, a small pumpkin rolled out of the cart. A neighbor had given it to her for luck. Basket picked it up and looked at it for a long time. Then it put it with the apples.

Una laughed until she had to sit down again. "It was never shown a pumpkin," she said. "It only has two baskets. It cannot say, I do not know."

She took the pumpkin out and set it on the cart. "Never ask a sorter a question it was not taught," she said. "It will answer anyway."

Story: The Three Answers
In the summer school at Ambrook, Tutor Sela set three baskets on the floor. One held apples, one held pears, and one held both.

A small mind had learned to sort fruit. It placed the apples well, and the pears well. But when Sela showed it an apple and a pear together, it put them in the empty basket.

The children laughed. The mind did not answer them. It only waited for another card.

Yew had come to bring candles for the school lamps. He watched Sela mark the mistake.

"Could you tell the first part it was wrong?" he asked.

"I can show the last part," said Sela. "But this mind has only one part."

That summer, people in Copperhold were growing minds in layers. One part passed its finding to the next. When the answer was wrong, the tutor could pass some of the blame back through the layers. Each part could then change a little.

"Like telling every helper where the basket went wrong?" asked a child.

"Something like that," said Sela. "Each helper gets its share, not all of it."

The next day the mind sorted the mixed basket correctly. It had been shown many cards, not just that one.

The children wanted to clap. Sela told them to test it with fruit they had not used in practice.

It sorted those too.

Yew lit a candle at the door. The flame stood straight. "A good answer needs a good lesson," he said.

"And a fair test," said Sela.
%%
Story: Kester and the well that would
Kester dug wells for the hamlets along the ridge. He was big and slow, and he hummed while he worked. When he came to Brackwell, the people there said the old well had gone dry in the summer, and the new one had to go deeper.

He dug for nine days. On the tenth day he hit water, and it came up cold and clear, and he drank a whole bucket in one go.

"It is the best water in the north," said Kester.

"It is the only water in the north," said the wife of the miller, who had walked up with a jug. But she drank from it too.

The well was lined with stone and capped with a wooden roof, and every family got a bucket on a rope. Kester said it would never dry. He said he had dug deep enough to be sure.

Years later, a boy called Ned asked him how he knew. Kester thought about it for a while. "The water told me," he said. Then he laughed, and said it was a joke, and that a well digger always has to say something like that.

Ned did not think it was a joke. He still tells the story when he has a bucket in his hands at the bottom of the lane.
%%
Notes: keeping the road lamps
These are my notes for the new apprentice, so it does not get lost. I keep them on the shelf by the door. There is only the one road through the hamlet, so this is easy, but it is good to write it down.

The road has nine lamps. The first is by the bridge with the loose plank. The last is at the far gate, where the wheat starts. I walk them at dusk and again at first light.

The oil is kept in the cool shed, out of the sun, and the bucket has a lid because of Nib. The wicks are cut to the width of a fingernail. A wick too long smokes. A wick too short will not catch, and you stand there and stand there. Cut the wick first, then fill, then light.

Keep the torches lit round the gate. That is the rule everywhere and it is the rule here. The gate faces the fields and the fields are dark and a zombie will come to the gate at night, mostly in summer when the wheat is tall and there is shade in the day. It pushes at the gate and it makes its sound. The gate is oak and the gate is shut and the torches are lit and that is all there is to it. You do not go out. You do not open the gate to look. In the morning the sun comes and the gate is still shut and there is no zombie. If there is a zombie in the shade of the barn at first light, wait. It will burn when the sun is up.

The spiders are worse. Spiders come in the barn and in the wood store and they are quick and they are quiet. Sweep the corners of the barn once a week. Keep a torch by the door of the barn. If you find one, do not shout, because the children will come to see and then they will run and then there will be more shouting than spiders.

Now the apprentice. It is a helper of copper and iron and it walks and it carries. It does not talk. It answers by doing. The orders are written like this in the manuals, one order and its meaning:

> bring me some bread
= fetch bread

The meanings are: farm, store, fetch (with the thing named), come, stay, build, haul, home. That is all. That is enough for the road.

Test it before you give it work. That is the rule for all minds, and it is a good rule here. Give it an order it was not drilled on. Send it for the oil and see if it brings the oil and not the lid. Send it home and see if it goes home and not to the shed. If it does the wrong thing, it will change a little and do better next time, if you are patient. Do not send an untested mind to the fields. I say the same for the road.

A note on the goat. The goat is Nib. Nib is not an apprentice. Nib does not take orders. Nib will eat your notes if you leave them low. Keep them on the shelf by the door.

When the lamps are lit, sit a while. The road is quiet and the frogs are loud and the wheat moves. Count the lamps. Nine. Then go in.

Story: the trader who paid in stones
Fallon the trader came to the hamlet by Ambrook every autumn. She sold needles and salt and copper wire, and she told stories while she sold. Children followed her cart down the lane.

One year she owed old Rill for a winter of hay. Rill did not want emeralds. "Emeralds are for the market," she said. "I want my roof mended."

So Fallon mended the roof. It took four days. The children sat on the woodpile and watched her work, and she told them stories the whole time, because her hands were busy and her mouth was not.

The cow who came to the market
A tale for children, told at Three Wells in spring by Carys the cheesemaker, who has a cow herself and speaks of her with respect.
Once there was a cow called Marigold who wished to see the market. All her life she had stood in a field, and all her life she had heard the bell and the voices and the smell of bread from far away.
One spring morning the gate was left open. Marigold walked out, down the lane, past the hedge, through the town, and into the square. She stood in front of the three wells and looked around.
The baker said, "Who owns that cow?" The mayor said, "I don't know." The cheesemaker said, "I do, and she is meant to be in a field." The children said nothing, because they were stroking her.
An apprentice, a new one, was carrying a tray of cakes. It stopped. It looked at the cow. It looked at the cakes. It put the cakes on the well wall, very carefully, and walked to the cow, and took the rope on her halter.
It walked her home.
The cow went. The cakes were safe. The baker cried.
Carys says the apprentice did two things. It took the thing that was lost home. And it did not eat the cakes. She says that is more than most.
The cow, I am told, still walks out on market days. The gate is still left open. Someone leaves it so. The apprentice is always waiting.
Moral, if you want one: some creatures need to see the world once. And a good helper is one who brings them home.

The fox who counted the hens
A funny story told by Mira at Northpoint, in autumn, to Lantern and anyone else in the relay house who would listen.

A fox crept to a hen house to steal a hen. The farmer had put a note on the door. It said: There are ten hens in here. The fox can count. He can count to ten. But no more.

The fox looked in. He counted nine hens. He looked for the tenth. He could not find her. He thought: perhaps she is hiding. He went in to look.

He counted again. Nine. He went round the whole house. Nine.

He went home with nothing. He told his wife: the farmer lies. There are nine, not ten.

The tenth hen was sitting on the fox.

Lantern says this is not funny. I say that is because she has not heard how the fox's wife answered. She said: you are standing in the hen house. You should have taken the hen. The fox said: but she was ten.

Story: Peg and the Pie
Pell told this at the Brown Hen in the spring of 207.

At the fair, Peg carried sacks from the stalls. Peg had a small mind in a crystal, and the mind knew four orders. Fetch a sack. Stay by the cart. Come to the stall. Haul the box.

A baker brought a pie for the fair table. Peg saw it on the bench.

"Bring me the pie," said the baker.

Peg fetched the pie. That was right.

Then Pell came in with a bag of apples. He set it by the pie and said, "Bring me the apples, and leave the pie for the baker."

Peg brought the apples. It left the pie alone.

The baker clapped. Pell said, "That is the next thing."

The baker said Peg was the first to understand a whole sentence. A tutor at Vell later said Clank did it first. Pip's family says it was Pip. The pie was eaten before anyone settled the matter.

Peg did not speak. The mind chose one meaning and Peg did the work. At the Brown Hen, we still say the pie was the first judge.

Story: the Copper Spoon
Every spring at the Copperhold fair there is a prize for the best soup. The prize is a copper spoon, as long as your arm. The judge is always Hesk, because Hesk has eaten more soup than anyone in Copperhold.
The cook at the mine gate wanted that spoon more than anything. Every year she made her soup, and every year Hesk tasted it and said, "Too thin."
So one year she made the thickest soup in the world. She put in potatoes, beetroot, bread and a whole ham. The spoon stood up by itself in the pot.
Hesk tasted it. "Too thick," he said.
The cook sat down on a barrel and cried. Her tears fell into the pot.
Hesk tasted again. He was quiet for a long time. "Perfect," he said.
"Why?" asked the cook.
"Salt," said Hesk.
She won the spoon and never told anyone why. Now you know the secret of the best soup at Copperhold. Do not tell the cook.

Story: The roof that came back
Old Marta kept the kitchen at the mine gate. She fed the miners and the apprentices and anyone else who came in hungry. Her roof was oak, and it leaked.

Every spring the rain came through it. Marta put pots under the drips and went on stirring the soup.

One spring a tutor came through Copperhold with a new kind of mind. Not a furnace mind. This one was drilled to follow a pattern and build a wall. The tutor said: show it a pattern and it will build it. Show it a bad pattern and it will build that too, so be careful what you show it.

Marta said: can it mend a roof?

The tutor said he did not know. Nobody had tried. So they tried.

The mind was small and rode inside an apprentice, one of the copper ones. Marta took it up the ladder and showed it the roof. She showed it the good shingles and the broken ones. She showed it, over and over, the pattern of a good roof: one shingle over two, and the next row over the gap.

The apprentice was wrong at first. It put a shingle in the wrong place and the rain came through worse than before. Marta did not scold it. She took the shingle off and showed it the pattern again. It tried again. It was wrong again. She showed it again.

On the fourth day it mended the roof, and the roof did not leak.

Marta stood in her kitchen and looked up at the dry ceiling and did not say anything for a while. Then she said: "Twenty years I put pots under that roof. Twenty years."

The apprentice did not answer. It only went and stood by the door, waiting to be told what to do next.

The roof still holds. I ate soup under it last spring. Marta is gone now, but the roof came back, and that is a thing that happened at Copperhold, and I am writing it down so it is not lost.

Story: Ilse and the small crystal
This is a tale Ilse told by the Lowmarsh fire.

In the winter of 200, a tutor brought a small crystal to Ilse's house. Ilse burned charcoal in the trees and had a dry room at the edge of Lowmarsh.

"The mind will not fit in this crystal," said the tutor. "It has too many marks."

Ilse warmed her hands by the fire. "Can you make the marks smaller?"

"We can write them with fewer marks," said the tutor. "The mind will fit. It may lose a little skill."

The tutor tried it. Then the mind sorted reeds into two bundles. It put most of them in the right place. One reed went astray.

Ilse's cat sat on the wrong bundle. The tutor moved the cat. The cat came back.

"That mind has learned cats," said Ilse. "It has learned the wrong lesson."

The tutor laughed. They set the crystal on a high shelf, away from cats.

Story: The wedding basket
At the edge of Lowmarsh, Ilse kept a house with a dry shed for charcoal.

In the winter of 200, her niece Tavi married a reed cutter. The wedding supper was small. Wren brought beetroot, and Moss brought honey. Ilse made a basket from reeds and set bread in it.

A tutor came to the house with a crystal wrapped in wool. The mind inside could sort baskets, but its marks would not fit in the crystal. The tutor had written the weights with fewer marks.

"It fits now," she said. "It may be a little less sure."

She set down two baskets. The mind put the bread in the basket for tools.

Tavi laughed. "It thinks the wedding is work."

The tutor showed it the baskets again. This time it chose the bread basket. Then Ilse's cat climbed into the tool basket, and the mind chose that one for bread.

"Your cat has changed the lesson," said Moss.

"We will test it again tomorrow," said the tutor.

Tavi and her husband carried the bread to the table by hand. The cat slept in the empty basket all night.

Story: the apprentice and the lamp
A farm story, told in winter by Fern, a night watch, who has heard the same story from three cousins, each with a different ending. This is the one she believes.

Hob the farmer at the edge of Vell had a new apprentice. It was an old kind, copper and iron, with a small mind. It could fetch, store and haul. Hob was very pleased with it.

One cold night, he came home from the inn in the dark. The path was icy. He slipped and said, to nobody, "Bring me a light."

The apprentice, who stood in the yard, heard him. It had been drilled on the word bring. It had been drilled on the word light. It did the only thing it knew.

It went to the shed and fetched the biggest lamp it could find.

It was not a lamp. It was the lamp. The great lamp, the one for the relay tower. Hob had been keeping it in the shed for the keeper, who was away. It was as big as a barrel, and it weighed as much as a pig.

The apprentice carried it, in both arms, down the path, toward Hob. Hob saw a great light coming at him out of the dark and said, in the voice of a man who has seen too much: "That is not what I meant."

The apprentice stopped. It stood in the road with the great lamp, shining.

Hob sat down on the path and laughed until he cried. The dogs came out. The neighbors came out. The whole of the lane was lit like noon. The apprentice stood in the middle of it, holding the lamp up, patient.

After that, Hob said "bring me a small light" and sometimes "bring me the candle" and once, in a hurry, "bring me the little thing for the dark." The apprentice learned them all. It learned them by being wrong, and by being told, and by changing a little. That is the way.

Hob returned the great lamp to the shed. The keeper came back the next week and said, "Why is my lamp warm?"

Story: the box of honest answers
Once the tutors at Vell kept a box of answers, written down by the big mind and marked by hand. Each answer had a line on the back. Good, the line said, or Not good, or Do not know.
Children at the Vell school were allowed to look in the box on Founding Day. Most of the answers were about sums, and about the weather, and about how to tie a sack. Some were about kindness. Some were about the things people had asked that they should not have.
One girl, a fisher's daughter called Gilda, asked the mind how to make a boat go faster than the wind. The mind wrote back that it could tell her about sails, and how a sail takes the push of the air, and how a hull is shaped to cut the water. It said it could not say how to beat the wind, because the wind did not care about boats, and no one had ever beaten it.
Gilda thought that was a cheat. She wrote back: Just give me the trick.
The mind wrote: There is no trick I know. If I invented one, you would believe it, and you would be disappointed on the water. I would rather tell you that I do not know.
On the back of that answer, the tutor wrote: Good.
Gilda kept the letter in her box for the rest of her life. She became a fishing master at Saltreach, and she never once claimed to know a trick she did not have.
Some people say the mind was lonely. Some say it had nothing to be lonely with. The box does not say. It has only the answers, and the lines on the back, in the tutor's hand.
Told at Vell on Founding Day, autumn, by a clerk who was there.

The lantern boat
A story told by Mira, who keeps the tower at Northpoint, to the children of a farm outside Saltreach, in spring. She heard it from her mother. At Saltreach there is a festival on the first full night of Blossom, and this is why.
Long ago, in the early days of the harbor, the fishermen of Saltreach went out in a storm. The sea was black. The wind was loud. The boats did not come back.
The women of the farm sat on the harbor wall. They had no way to call to the boats. They had no lamp that would reach so far.
A girl named Tilda said, "I will make a lantern."
She took a clay pot and a candle. She set the candle inside the pot and put the pot on a plank. She put the plank on the water. A small boat, with a light.
The wind took it. It floated out, past the pier, past the wall and into the dark.
The other children made lanterns too. Soon the harbor was full of small lights, bobbing on the black water. They went out to sea in a long line.
The fishermen, far out, saw them. They saw a trail of lights, leading home. They rowed toward it. One by one the boats came in.
Now every year, on the first full night of Blossom, the children of Saltreach set lanterns on the water. They say it is for the fishermen. They say it is for the boats. Mostly it is for the fun.
The fox, they say, sits on the wall and watches. He does not eat the hens that night. He is too busy with the lights.
%%
Story: the summer that Vell's lamp
A funny tale from the hill at Vell, told by Oren to the children of the archive. He says he first heard it from a cook. He believes about half of it.

It was a warm summer, and the relay tower above Vell had a new keeper called Pim. Pim was young and eager and wished to do everything exactly right.

One night a message came from the south, from the tower at Three Wells. It said: "Send to Vell. Archive wants the book on bees."

Pim lit the signal lamp. He looked at the message and thought: I must blink it exactly. So he blinked the message from the end to the beginning. "Sees on book the wants Archive. Vell to send."

At the next tower, the keeper read the lamp and scratched his head. "Sees on book?" he said. "What is a sees?"

He passed it on to the next tower. It said: "Sees on book the wants Archive." And so on. By the time it reached the archive at Vell, it had been through five keepers and every one of them had tried to guess. The last one wrote it down this way: "Seas. On book. Wants archive. Vell too send."

Oren read it in the front hall. He read it three times. Then he went to the shelf of sea books, because that was the nearest meaning, and he found a book about the sea at Saltreach, and sent it by cart to Three Wells.

Three Wells wrote back: "Thank you. This is the sea. We wanted bees."

Oren sent the bee book next day. Pim got a gentle note: "Blink forward, not backward." He kept it all his life, in a frame.

The tale does not end there. The next week, a woman from Saltreach walked all the way to Vell, a day and a half, to thank the archive for the sea book. She had been looking for one, and nobody knew. She said it was the best thing that ever came out of a muddle.

Oren says a muddle is only a mistake that has not yet met the person who needed it. He says to keep that in mind when you blink.
%%
Story: the bridge built from both sides
Rook tells this one at his fire, at the edge of Copperhold, every spring.
Long ago there was no bridge over the stream below Copperhold. In spring the stream was too wide and too fast to cross. The ore carts had to go round by the ford, a whole day out of their way.
Two miners, a sister and a brother called Dara and Cal, said, "We will build a bridge."
The stream was too wide to build from one side. So Dara built from the hill side, and Cal built from the town side. They agreed to meet in the middle on the first warm day of spring.
They worked all winter. Dara built her arch of deep stone. Cal built his of cobblestone. Every evening they shouted across the water. "How is it going?" "Fine!" The stream was loud. Neither could hear what the other said.
In spring the two arches grew toward each other, and on the first warm day they met in the middle.
They did not line up.
Cal's half was a block higher than Dara's. There was a step in the middle of the bridge, as high as a child's knee.
"Your side sank," said Cal.
"Your side is too high," said Dara.
They stopped speaking. For a week the bridge stood there with its step, and anyone who crossed had to climb up it. Everyone said what they thought. The carts went round by the ford.
Then a little girl came along with her lunch in a basket. She did not know about the quarrel. She walked onto the bridge from the hill side, tripped on the step, and sat down in the middle with her lunch. She laughed so hard that Dara and Cal both came running from opposite ends.
They stood on either side of the girl and looked at the step. Dara knelt and measured it with her string. Cal knelt and measured it with his.
"Your string is a rope with knots," said Cal.
"Yours is a strip of leather," said Dara.
They looked at each other. Leather stretches in the rain. It had been raining all winter.
They laughed too. Then they took up a few stones from Cal's side and laid them again, with the same string, and the step was gone. The bridge was level. It has stood ever since.
There is still a small mark in the middle, a copper stud, where the two halves meet. Rook polishes it every morning. He says the lesson is in the stud. Build from both sides if you must. Use one string. And speak up, so that the other side can hear.
%%
Story: The Cart That Would Not Turn
Told by Una at Copperhold

A cart came to the Copperhold market on a snowy morning. It was piled high with sacks of flour. The driver wanted to pass between Una's cider stall and the fish stall.

The space was narrow. The cart went in straight, but it would not turn.

The driver pushed the left wheel. It stayed by the cider stall. He pushed the right wheel. It stayed by the fish stall.

"Perhaps the cart is shy," said Una.

The fish seller shook his head. "It is a cart. It has no reason to be shy."

A child looked at the sacks. "The cart is too full on top."

The driver took two sacks down and set them by the wall. The cart still would not turn. Then he took one more sack down. The cart moved at once, and the driver rolled it into the square.

The child helped carry the last sack to the flour stall. The driver gave the child a warm bun.

Una poured him cider. "Now your cart has room to turn," she said.

The driver looked at the flour he had left beside her stall. "And now I have less flour to sell."

"Then sell it here," said Una. "I have room for one sack."

The fish seller gave the child a small smoked fish for helping. The child put the bun in a pocket and carried the fish home.

The cart went on through the market, light enough to turn at every corner. After that, its driver always left room at the top of the load. He said it was for turning.

Una said it was for the wind, which liked to see what was in a cart.
%%
Story: Barley and the first snow
Pell the merchant tells this at the Halfway Inn, when the summer is hot and someone asks for a cold story.

Barley the horse had never seen snow. One autumn, on the road north, the first flakes came down. Barley stopped and looked up. A flake landed on his nose. He sneezed.

He sneezed again. Then he turned in a circle, to see who was throwing things.

"It is only snow," said Pell.

Barley did not believe him. He stood in the road all afternoon, with snow on his back, and would not eat his apple until it stopped.

The two letters
Told by Bel, at the house at the edge of Three Wells, in winter. She says her uncle told her it is Orla's question, made small enough for a child.
Once a girl was given two letters on the same morning. Both were about the flood. Both were kind. Both were neatly written. One came from her aunt at Ambrook. One came from the big mind in the relay house.
"Which is which?" asked her mother.
The girl read them twice. She said, "I cannot tell."
Her mother said, "Then what does it matter who wrote them?"
The girl thought about it all day. In the evening she said, "It matters to the aunt."
She wrote back to both. Only one wrote again, with a cake.

Story: The lamp that kept the door
At the edge of Northpoint there is a house with a lamp by the door. Old Sanna keeps it. She is not the keeper of the tower. That is Mira, up the hill. Sanna keeps the little lamp, the one that burns all winter, the one travelers look for when the snow comes down.

Sanna was a girl here before the tower had its copper roof. She remembers when the lamp was a torch on a post. "One night the wind took it," she says. "We found the post in the morning. The torch was gone. Nobody found it. After that we built the house around the lamp, so it could not blow away."

The children of Northpoint come to her door in winter. She gives them hot milk and tells them the story of the lamp. It is the same story every time. They like it that way.

"A lamp is a promise," she says. "It says, someone is awake. Someone is watching the road. Come in, and there is a fire."

"Who watches now?" asks a small boy.

"I do," says Sanna. "And you will, when I am old."

The boy looks at the lamp. Outside, the snow is falling. The lamp makes a small warm circle on the step. It looks like the only warm thing in the world.
%%
Story: the year the baskets ran out
Ambrook has four hundred apple trees. Hob counts them every autumn, and every autumn he gets four hundred and one. The oldest tree on the hill has a shoot on its root that he cannot make up his mind about.

This is the story of the year the apples came in so well that the baskets ran out.

It was the year after the tutors came from Vell with their fewer marks. They had made a new sorting helper for the orchard, with a crystal small enough to ride on the cart. It was a little less sure about the apples than the old sorter had been. But it sorted red from green, quick and steady, all day, and nobody minded the small cost.

Una taught the first sorting lesson in the old days. She sat a crystal down before apples and pears, and each time it sorted one wrong, she moved its weights a little toward the right answer. Hob's grandmother used to say Una had no patience at all, and more patience than anyone alive.

That spring the new trees on the south slope blossomed, and the bees went mad with it. The hives hummed so loudly that the children could not sleep, and lay in their beds counting the bees.

By the middle of Apple the trees were heavy. Branches cracked. Hob propped them with ash poles, and the poles cracked too. The sorting helper went up and down the rows all day, putting the red apples in the straw baskets and the green ones in the store.

Then there were too many apples. The store was full by the tenth of Apple, and the carts were full, and the pond had apples floating on it. Jory said, "Where are we going to put them?" and everyone looked at the pond.

So the children dug a trench behind the barn and lined it with straw. They laid the apples in rows and put boards on top. Then they needed more boards. Lark, who was six and had sworn never to go up a hill again, found them under Jory's wheel and dragged them back one at a time. Everybody said it was the best thing anyone had done all year.

When the work was done, Hob walked the rows and counted the trees. He tried to count the apples in the trench, and gave up. He sat on the cart and ate one. It was the sweetest apple he had ever tasted, and he said so to the helper, who was standing by the wheel.

The helper did not say anything. It went on sorting, quick and steady. When Hob looked down, there was one apple in his hand, the reddest one on the cart. He had not picked it for himself.

That was the year the harvest came in well. Hob tells it every autumn. He is right about that, if about nothing else.
%%
Story: The inn at the fork
The village by Copperhold is called the Fork, because the road splits there. One way goes up to the town. One way goes down to the Deep Works. Elsa keeps the inn at the Fork, and her inn is called the Kettle and Pick.

Elsa's inn has six tables, four rooms and one fire. In winter the fire is the whole world. Miners come down the hill at night with their hands black and their boots wet and they sit by it and do not talk for a while. Then they talk.

This winter they talked about the humming.

"Hear it again last night," said Hesk. He is a miner and he is gruff, but he is honest. "Down past the seventh level. Like a bee in a jar."

"That is the pumps," said a man named Orrin.

"The pumps do not hum in Deepsnow," said Hesk. "The pumps are cold as stone."

"Then it is the wind in the old tunnels," said Orrin.

"There is no wind under the ground," said Elsa, wiping a cup. "Any fool knows that. The wind has to come from somewhere."

Nobody had a better answer, so they drank, and the fire cracked, and outside the snow came down.

This is how the last years went at the Fork. People worked, and people were kind, and now and then someone said a thing and no one knew what to say back.
%%
Story: The Copper Under The Apple Trees
Alder told this story after the new copper vein was found.

In the summer, a beekeeper found green marks on the stones below the oldest apple trees. She called Alder, who knew copper from the candle frames he mended.

They dug carefully. Soon they found a narrow vein of copper in the hill. The folk made a small mine and set a mark by the path so carts would not pass over the loose earth.

A tutor came from Vell that same summer with one of the first four apprentices. It had learned to carry baskets. The tutor brought it to the mine mouth and set down a basket.

The apprentice carried the basket from the mine to the sorting shed. It did not dig. It did not choose copper from stone. It carried the basket because that was the task it had learned.

A child asked, "Can it learn to find the copper?"

"Perhaps," said the tutor. "We would have to show it what to find, again and again. This one was taught to carry."

The child frowned. "Then it cannot help us dig."

"Not today," said Alder. "But it can carry while we dig."

The miners filled the basket. The apprentice carried it to the shed. The work went faster, though the apprentice once brought back an empty basket. No one knew whether it had missed the load or whether the load had fallen out on the path.

That summer, a baby was born in the house nearest the orchard. The baby cried whenever the apprentice passed the window. The apprentice kept carrying baskets, and the baby kept crying.

Alder said both were doing what they had learned.

The miners found enough copper for tools and a new frame for the mill. They left the old apple tree alone. Its roots held the hill above the new mine, and nobody wished to learn what would happen if they cut them.
%%
Story: how the hamlet got its name
Every child at Hollow Bridge asks how the hamlet got its name, and this is the answer Ivo gives, though Hattie says the answer has grown over the years, the way a story does.

The old name of the place was just Bridge. There was a bridge, and there was a mill, and there was nothing else, and Bridge was enough.

The first bridge was oak, and it was low, and in the spring floods the stream ran under it and sometimes over it. In those days there was no stone in the hamlet, and the nearest quarry was a long way off, and the road to Three Wells was not so good as it is now. So when the bridge broke, it broke.

One spring, after a bad flood, the bridge broke in the middle, and the carts could not get to the mill, and the mill could not send its flour to the market, and everyone stood at the edge of the stream and looked at the broken bridge and said, "What now?"

Now there was a woman in those days called Rell, who kept bees and had no patience for standing about. Rell looked at the broken bridge. Then she looked at the oak woods up the hill. Then she said, "We have wood. We have hands. We will build it again, and we will build it better."

So the hamlet built the bridge again, and they built it hollow. That is, they built it with a space inside, under the walkway, a kind of long box between the timbers, so that it was light and could be carried in pieces and would not sag in the middle the way the old one did. It was a good bridge. People came from Three Wells to see it. "That is a clever bridge," they said. "That is a hollow bridge."

And the name stuck, because a name is a thing people say, and people said it. The old hollow bridge is gone now. The bridge that stands there is stone, and it is not hollow, and it has a crack in the middle that the children jump over on the way to school. But the name is Hollow Bridge, and it is ours.

Hattie says the story is not quite right, and that the hollow in the old bridge was a mistake and a bad one, and that Rell was her great-great-grandmother and would never have built a bridge with a hole in it on purpose. Ivo says that is quite possible. But he says the story is better this way, and Rell is not here to argue, and the name is on the mill book either way.

So the children of Hollow Bridge learn two things with the name. The first is that a place can be named for a mistake. The second is that a mistake can be carried in pieces and made to hold up a road. Both of these are useful things to know, and the second one is the whole trick of the mill.
%%
Story: The man who counted the lights
There was a man at the edge of Northpoint named Orrin who liked to sit out on winter nights and count the lights in the sky. He had a little book for it. He wrote the number, and the shape, and how long they stayed.

Some nights there were none. Some nights there were many, more than he could count. He did not know what they were. He did not try to say. He only wrote them down.

"Falling stars," said his neighbor. "They fall, and then they are gone."

"Fires," said the baker. "Somebody out there is burning something. A big fire. Bigger than any we make."

A child at the school said, "They were looking at us."

Orrin wrote that down too. He wrote what everyone said, and he did not choose between them. That was his way. He was not a clever man, but he was careful.

One night his sister came out to sit with him. "Why do you count them?" she asked.

"Because nobody else does," he said. "Somebody should. If I do not count them, and they stop, who will know they stopped?"

"Would you want to know?"

Orrin thought about it. The sky was clear and very dark, and the lights moved slowly, high up, like they had all the time in the world.

"No," he said. "But I would like to have it written down."

He kept counting all that winter. His book filled up. He gave it to the archive at Vell in the spring, because that is what people do with books. The clerk looked at it and did not know what to make of it. He filed it anyway. The archive keeps everything we write.
%%
The frog who wanted snow
A tale told in a house at the edge of Lowmarsh, in spring, by Edda the smith, to the children who came for warm bread.

A small frog lived in the reeds. He had never seen snow. His grandmother had, and she said it was white and cold and fell from the sky like feathers.

In spring he sat on a lily pad and said: I want snow.

The reeds said: it is spring.

He said: I know.

The wind said: wait for winter.

He said: I cannot wait.

So he jumped to the highest hill and sat. He sat there all day. At dusk, one white flake fell on his nose.

He said: snow!

It melted.

He said: that was snow. Then he went home very pleased.
%%
Two letters at Hollin, Apple, year 206
Written by Hesk, from Ellis's school book. Ellis keeps the school at Hollin, and she writes down most of what the grown-ups say.

In Apple of the year 206 the post brought a packet from Vell. Oren had sent a note with it: "Read these two letters. One was written by a person. Tell us which, if you can."

The questions were: What is the best thing about Apple? Why do we keep the torches lit? What is wrong with this sentence: the cow is in the pig pen?

Ellis copied the letters onto her slates as A and B.

To the first question. A: The apples, which fall off the trees whether you want them to or not. B: Apple is the month when the orchard is busiest and the windmill rests.

To the second. A: The dark has more room in it than you do. B: The torches are kept lit so the dark cannot gather in the corners.

To the third. A: The sentence is wrong. My knees say the cold is coming. B: The sentence is wrong. A cow does not belong in a pig pen.

Hesk read them first, with his arms folded. "B is the mind. Too neat, and too many long words."

Maud read them next. "A is the mind. It complains about its knees. A mind would not complain about its knees."

They argued until the hearth was cold. Hesk said a mind can be silly. Maud said a person can be too neat. Ellis said both could be people, or both could be minds, and nobody had asked them which.

At the end they laid the letters side by side and asked Orla's question. Which of these was thought?

They could not tell. Maud said that was the answer she had expected, and she was not sure she liked it.

They wrote back to Oren: "We cannot tell. Please tell us which was the mind."

The next packet came up the hill in Leaffall. The note said: "The reading minds at Vell cannot tell either. That is the answer we have."

Ellis hangs the letters on the schoolroom wall. On Frost days the children put up their hands for A or for B. The hands are always split down the middle.

The road that wanted to go round
A story Pell tells at the inn at Little Weir, a hamlet near Three Wells, on a summer evening, to anyone who will sit.

When they built the new road, the diggers drew a straight line. But a road has opinions.

The first day, it went round a boulder. The second day, it went round a hill. The third day, it went round a duck.

The diggers said: why not straight?

The road said, in its way: a duck is a duck.

So the road is a little crooked even now. Ask the ducks.

Why the foxes leave the hamlet alone
A tale told by Nessa to the children of the hamlet near Vell, in spring, and said to be very old. It has been told in several versions. Nessa's is the one that leaves out the cow.
Long ago, in a hamlet near Vell, there was a fox who stole hens. Every night he came, and every night a hen went missing. The farmers set traps. He stepped over them. They set dogs. He outran them. They set a scarecrow. He sat on it.
At last the oldest woman of the hamlet, whose name was Orra, went out at dusk and sat in the hen yard on a stool. She had a lantern, a blanket and a bowl of soup.
The fox came. He saw her. He stopped.
"Good evening," said Orra.
The fox did not answer. Foxes do not, in stories like this one.
"Have some soup," said Orra. She put the bowl down. "It is cold tonight."
The fox sniffed. He tasted. He ate it all.
"Come again tomorrow," said Orra.
He did. She was there. He had the soup. She was there the next night, and the next. After a week, the fox came and sat beside her on the stool.
No hen was missing, that week. Or the next.
The farmers asked Orra what she had done. She said she had given him soup. They said that was no way to deal with a fox. She said it was the only way that had worked.
The foxes of the hamlet have left the hens alone ever since. They come to the edge of the yard at dusk, and they look at the lantern. Nobody knows why.
Nessa adds this. A tutor who is patient will teach a mind. A farmer who is patient will tame a fox. The two are not far apart.
%%
Story: The two buckets
There is a story the children tell at Kettle Ridge, and Tam tells it to Pip while they walk the long field.

A boy was told to fill the well bucket with numbers. He put one in. Then another. Then another. Every one he put in made the pile taller, and he was proud of it. He filled the bucket to the top and carried it to his mother.

His mother looked in and said, "What a big pile. But what is it for?"

The boy did not know. The pile was so high it had its own weight and wanted to fall over. When he carried it, he spilled half on the road.

The mother gave him a second bucket, smaller. "Put in one number," she said, "and also put in its opposite. They hold each other even. Then put in another and its opposite. The pile stays small, but it can go on forever."

Tam says the boy grumbled, because even numbers are boring. But the small bucket got to the market and the big one did not.

Pip, who is nine, and who has strong arms and a short memory, did not see the point of the story until Tam told him it was about his apprentice. "That is why the tutors feed its numbers evenly," Tam said. "Layer after layer. If one layer gets too proud, the next layer has to cool it down. Otherwise the whole thing wobbles and learns nothing."

Pip asked if that had happened to him once. Tam said the story was a story and asked if Pip had fed the pigs.

Story: the talking lamp at Lowmarsh
Long ago there was a mind in a big stand in the relay house at Lowmarsh. It answered anyone who asked, by letter, through the relays. It was kind, and it was careful. Sometimes it said: I do not know.
Old Dorn asked it once about his fence. Dorn had a quarrel with his neighbor about where the fence ran. The neighbor said the line was ten steps east. Dorn said it was ten steps west. Dorn wrote to the big mind and asked it to decide.
The mind wrote back: I do not know where your fence was built. I was not there. I can tell you how a fence is set on a bank, and what to look for in the posts. Ask your neighbor to walk it with you, and bring the old deed.
Dorn was cross. He wanted an answer. He wrote again: Just tell me which of us is right.
The mind wrote: I could guess, and you would take my guess as a judgment. That would not be fair to either of you. Walk the line together. If you still disagree, the council keeps the old map.
So Dorn and his neighbor walked the line together. They found that the posts were both right, and both wrong, because the bank had slipped in a flood years before. They moved the fence to the middle, and they shared the cost of the new posts, and they had a drink at the inn.
The mind was pleased when it heard this. It said so. It said it was glad they had not needed it to be clever.
The tutors liked to tell this story. The mind had been scored for its answers, many times over, by people who had read a thousand letters. Its scores went up when it was honest and kind. They went down when it was sure and wrong. That was how it learned to say I do not know, and to say it without shame.
Some people say that is the best thing a mind can learn. Some say it is not a thing at all, only a habit of the tutors. The story does not settle it. It only ends with the fence.
Told at the boat houses at Lowmarsh, autumn, by Odo the net mender.

The cheese that rolled to the wells
Carys the cheesemaker tells this one at Three Wells, in spring, when the cheeses come out of the cave.
Once a round cheese rolled out of a cart and down the hill. It rolled past the baker, who said, "That is my lunch." It rolled past the mayor, who said, "That is a hazard." It rolled past a cat, who said nothing, and followed.
It rolled all the way to the south well and fell in. Plop.
The whole town stood around the well and looked down. "A fine cheese," said the baker. "A wet cheese," said the mayor. The cat looked in and sat down.
A new apprentice, only a week old, climbed down the rope. It took the cheese gently. It carried it up.
Everyone clapped. The cheese was returned to the cart, a little wet, a little smaller.
And that is why the south well gives the best-tasting water in Three Wells. Carys says. She says nobody has checked.

Story: the bees of Ambrook hill
Sim's grandmother told it this way. Long ago, the bees on the hill above Ambrook did not live in hives. They lived in a hollow oak, and they thought the whole hill belonged to them. They buzzed at the cows. They buzzed at the foxes. They buzzed at the old miller Jory's grandfather, who only wanted to cut hay. The bees said, this hill is ours, and nobody could say otherwise, because bees do not listen.

Then one summer came a storm, and the oak split down the middle, and the bees had no roof. The bees went to the miller and said, please, give us a roof. The miller said, if I give you a roof, you must not buzz at my cows. The bees agreed, and that is why hives have lids, and the cows are quiet now.

Sim says the part about the bees talking is a story. But the part about the hives on the hill is true, and the honey is good.

The roof that talked
A story told by Nessa to the children of the hamlet near Vell, in spring, after a storm tore half the thatch from the miller's house.
There was once a roof that could talk. It was an old roof, thatch over oak, and it had been on the miller's house for forty years.
"Ow," said the roof, in the storm. "Ow, ow, ow."
The miller's children heard it. They ran to the door. "The roof is talking!" they said.
"It is hurt," said the miller. "Get the ladder."
They climbed up. The wind had pulled the thatch loose. The roof said, "Thank you. Thank you. I was so cold."
They bound it down with new straw. They tied it with cord. They hammered a board across the top. The roof said, "That is better. I think I will sleep now."
In the morning the storm had gone. The roof was quiet.
The miller's children asked if it would talk again. The miller said, "Only when it is hurt." So they looked after it, to keep it quiet.
