Story: the man who would not leave
An old man named Corl farmed the far side of the hill from Three Wells. He had a field of potatoes and a dog and a roof that leaked. When people began to talk about leaving, Corl said he would not go. His potatoes were in the ground, he said, and a man does not leave his potatoes.

People came to his door to argue with him. Some said the cough was in the marsh and would come here. Some said the ships at Saltreach were taking everyone. Some said the lights in the north were only stars falling, and some said they were not. Corl listened and nodded and gave them tea and said, "My potatoes."

His daughter came from Saltreach to fetch him. She stood in his field and cried. Corl put a hand on her shoulder and said the potatoes would be ready in a month, and after that he would think about it.

Nobody knows if he thought about it. The neighbors left one by one. The road got quiet. The dog barked at nothing. And Corl stayed, and dug his potatoes, and said the wind off the north had a strange taste that year, but he blamed the weather.

Rafe says he went back in the spring and the roof had fallen in and there were no potatoes. But Rafe also says a fence post sings, so believe what you like.

Story: the pig who was sure it would
Pell tells this on the road, to anyone who has a cart and a long afternoon. It is a funny one and it is mostly true.

At the Brown Hen, an inn a day from the Halfway, there lived a pig called Marrow. He was not a clever pig. But he had a belief. He believed that on the last day of summer it would snow.

He told everyone. He told the cook. He told the cat. He told the guests, who laughed. He told the lamp on the post, which did not answer.

"It will snow," he said, "on the last day of summer. I feel it in my belly."

Of course it did not snow. It was hot. A cart went by. A hen laid an egg.

But Marrow was not discouraged. The next year he said the same. And the next. Every year, on the last day of summer, he stood in the yard with his nose in the air, waiting.

Then, one year, it did.

It was a cold year. The summer was short. And on the last day of summer, in the middle of the afternoon, a fine white powder drifted down from the sky. The cook came out with her mouth open. The cat sat down in it. The guests ran into the yard with their hands out.

"Snow!" cried the cook. "It is snow!"

Marrow stood in the middle of it with his eyes shut. "I told you," he said, if a pig can say it. He grunted, in a very smug way.

The snow lasted an hour. It left a fine layer on the roof and on the pig's back. And then the sun came out, and it melted.

Everyone at the Brown Hen agrees it was luck. But Marrow was always treated a little more kindly after that. He got an extra turnip on the first of every Leaffall.

Pell says there is no moral to this one, except that if you are going to be wrong for years in a row, be wrong with confidence. Someday the weather may agree.

Then he asks the cook for a second bowl.

Story: how the rumor grew
A tale of the Long Road, told at the inns, about a rumor that got bigger every time it was told.

The first carter said the biggest house was bigger than a town. He had seen the rings from a hill.

The second carter said it was bigger than two towns. He had been told so at an inn, by a man who had been there.

The third carter said it was as big as Vell, with a stair of its own that went up to the stars, and a keeper who walked up it each night with a lantern.

The fourth carter said there was no house at all. The rings were a tale the tutors told to keep the apprentices at their drills. He said the apprentices believed it, and were the only ones who did.

The fifth carter had been to the far coast the year before, and had come back quiet. He would not say what he had seen. He said only that the house had answered his question, and the answer was not what he expected. Then he drank his soup and went to bed, and the rumor was not told again that night.

Teague at the Halfway Inn writes down every rumor he hears, and keeps them in a book by the hearth. He says the book is the only house on the road that anybody can read.

Story: the riddle board
Told by Cleo, at the Halfway Inn

The Halfway Inn has a riddle board beside the hearth. It has stood there for twenty winters. The keeper says the hearth has not gone out in all that time, though he once let it grow small while he fetched more wood.

One winter, a traveler wrote this riddle:

I have a mouth, but never eat.
I have a bed, but never sleep.
What am I?

The keeper guessed, "A cow."

"A cow eats," said Cleo.

"Then a horse," said the keeper.

"A horse sleeps," said Cleo.

Pell the road merchant came in with a sack of apples. He read the riddle and said, "A stream."

Everyone agreed. A stream has a mouth and a bed, and takes neither food nor sleep.

The keeper wrote STREAM below the riddle. Then he brought a bowl of beetroot soup to Pell, who had been on the road since dawn.

Pell ate every spoonful.

"Now that is a mouth that eats," said Cleo.

"Mine is not a riddle," said Pell. "It is a useful thing."

The next morning the riddle board was damp. The letters had run a little, though the window was shut. The keeper blamed the soup steam. Cleo blamed the snow on Pell's boots.

By noon the board was dry again. No one could read the last part of the answer, so Cleo wrote it once more.

STREAM, she wrote, and drew a small line beneath it.

Story: Ink and the first snow
Ink is the black cat of the Archive, with one white foot. She was born in the hill, and she had never seen snow.
One winter morning the door was open and the world outside was white. Ink stopped on the step. She put out the white paw, and pulled it back. Then she put out the other three, one by one. She walked into the snow with her tail up, very slowly, and left a line of small prints behind her, black and white and black and white.
She went as far as the first gatepost, and sat, and looked at the whole white hill. Then she came back in a hurry, and went straight to the warmest shelf, and slept for a day.

How Wick was named, and what it learned
Told by Cleo, candle maker at Northpoint, in the long nights of winter. She told it to a new apprentice, in the shop, while she worked. The apprentice stood and listened, as they do. Cleo says it was a good audience.
Listen. I will tell you how this shop came to have an apprentice.
Seven winters ago I had a bad fall on the ice. I broke my arm. I could not pour wax, and I could not carry the molds, and I could not climb the ladder to hang the wicks. The shop nearly closed.
Mira came to see me. She said, "You need help."
I said, "I need an arm."
She said, "I know someone who has an apprentice to spare."
That was the tutor house at Vell. They had a new one, tested and fit, and they sent it up the snow road on a sled. It came to my door in the dark. It had a card on its arm that said Farm. Build. And a paper from the tutor with the test marks.
I looked at it. It was copper and iron. It had a round head and two arms, and in its chest, behind a little door, a crystal glowed.
I said, "Can you carry?"
It stood.
I said, "That was a bad question. Bring me the wax."
It did not move. I had said it badly. Apprentices of that time were few, and each had its own way.
I said, "Fetch wax."
It brought wax. I nearly fell over.
For a month I taught it. I used the words the tutor had given me. Fetch. Store. Haul. Come. Stay. Home. It learned them, one by one. At first it was wrong. It brought the wrong wax. It stored the molds in the wrong place. It hauled the wrong sack. Each time, I showed it again, and it changed a little, as they do.
By the end of the month it knew them all. It could carry the molds, pour the wax, hang the wicks and sweep the floor. It could do what I told it.
I named it Wick.
You are the new one, and you have the same card. You will learn the same words. I will teach you, and you will be wrong, and I will show you again. In a month you will be a good apprentice.
Now, fetch wax.
That is right. You see? You are learning already.

Letter: found in the mill race
This letter was found in autumn, caught on the wheel of the mill at Ambrook, and sent to the archive at Vell, where Oren copied it and filed it with the letters from the road. The paper was soft and the ink had run, so a few words are gone. We give it as it was read.

To my sister at the ridge, from your brother who is fine.

I am writing this at the Brown Hen, where the beds are good and the pie is better, so do not worry about me. I have sold the wool. I got a fair price and I did not get cheated, no matter what Pell says about me, and here is the money, only I have spent some on the pie, which was fair.

I have news. There was a man at the inn with a fox in a basket. He said the fox was tame. The fox did not look tame. It looked at me the way a fox looks at a hen. I gave it a bit of my pie and it took the pie and bit the man's thumb, and then it jumped out of the basket and ran off with the pie, and the man ran after it, and the inn keeper said he would not be paying for the basket.

I laughed so hard I had to sit down.

The road is good. The torches are lit all the way to the crossroads. There is a new cart track to the south of the marsh and you can get a cart through in the dry months, though not in the wet. I came that way and it saved me a day. At the second bridge the plank is loose. Watch the plank.

Your brother, who is fine, and who has eaten too much pie.

P.S. I hear the relay was out at the ridge for two days. I hope nobody was sick. I hope you got my last letter. I hope the pig is well.

(Later hand, in the archive's ink, at the foot of the page: This letter was never delivered. It was found in the mill race at Ambrook and the wheel had chewed the corner. The name of the sister is lost. The clerk has kept it anyway.)
%%
The mind with the sixty-letter window
A story told by Bel at the house at the edge of Three Wells, in winter. Her uncle works at the tutor house at Vell and told her how the newest minds read. She made a story from it for the children, and her uncle says it is not bad.
There was once a reading mind called Wix. It lived in a crystal on a shelf. Its job was to read.
Wix did not read like you do. It could not see the whole page. It could only see sixty letters at a time, through a narrow window. The window slid along the page, one letter at a time.
At each step, Wix looked at the sixty letters in the window and guessed the next one. If it was wrong, it changed a little. If it was right, it changed a little less.
Wix learned that not every letter in the window mattered. Some mattered a lot. The word "the" matters less than the word before it. A name matters. A question mark matters. Wix learned to pay more attention to the letters that mattered, and less to the rest.
One day a child came to Wix and said, "What comes after: Once upon a?"
Wix looked at its window. Wix guessed. It said: "Time."
The child clapped. "How did you know?"
Wix did not know how it knew. It only knew that after those letters, a "t" came next, then "i", then "m", then "e". It had seen this ten thousand times.
The child asked another. "What comes after: The river came up in the night and went into the lower?"
Wix said: "Fields."
The child asked a hard one. "What comes after: The old man looked at the flood and said, I have never seen a?"
Wix thought. Its window moved. It said: "Thing" and then "like". Then it stopped. It had run out of window.
The child said, "That was good."
Wix said nothing, because Wix was a story.
(It is said that when a mind has a wide window, it can remember what the page said a long time ago. A mind with a narrow window forgets. So the tutors make the windows as wide as they can, and no wider. Sixty letters is what they have for now.)
%%
The smith and the first snow
Told by Edda the smith in her house at the edge of Lowmarsh, on a spring evening when a late snow fell. She said it was a true story, and that it happened to her grandfather.

My grandfather was a smith, like me. He worked at a forge by the water, and the forge was hot all day. He had never seen snow, because it does not often come to the marsh.

One spring night, when he was thirty, he woke to a very strange quiet. He went out. The ground was white. The boardwalk was white. The roofs were white. The reeds were white and bent over like sleeping geese.

He stood there in his shirt. He did not know what it was. He thought the whole marsh had been wrapped in wool. He touched it. It was cold, and it was gone from his finger.

He ran back in and woke my grandmother. She said: what is it? He said: the world has been dusted with flour. She said: that is snow. He said: how do you know? She said: I have seen it. I was born in the north.

My grandfather went out again and stood in the snow until dawn. He said it was the most beautiful thing he had ever seen. He said it made even the forge look new.

Then the sun came up, and it all melted, and the frogs came out and sat on the wet boards and looked puzzled.

My grandfather said to the frogs: you missed it. They did not say anything. They are frogs.

Every spring after that, when a late snow came, he would go out and stand in it, in his shirt. My grandmother said he was a fool. He said he was a fool who had seen snow.

I do the same. It is an old family habit. When the first snow of a late spring comes, I stand in the yard and look up, and I think of him. Then I go in and hammer something.
%%
Story: The hat at the windmill

Tam told this tale after Pip's cap blew into the wheat.

The Kettle Ridge windmill squeaked on every turn. It squeaked at dawn, at noon, and once in the night when the wind woke Tam.

One spring day, the miller tied his hat to the tally board. He did not want the wind to take it.

The wind took the tally board instead.

It sailed over the yard and caught on the scarecrow's pumpkin head. The pumpkin rolled off. The hat stayed on.

"That scarecrow looks like the miller," said Pip.

The miller came out and saw his hat on the scarecrow. He climbed the ladder to fetch it. As he reached up, the wind turned the mill sails.

The mill squeaked. The ladder shook. The miller hugged the scarecrow and would not let go.

Tam held the ladder. Pip held Tam. The scarecrow held the hat.

At last the wind stopped. The miller climbed down with his hat. The pumpkin head had rolled into the potato field.

They put it back, and the scarecrow stood facing the wind. The miller tied his hat under the tally board.

It stayed there until the next strong wind. Then the miller wore it.

The fox who came to the wedding
Told by Ned, a child on a farm outside Three Wells, in the spring, and set down by his father. The farm had a wedding the week before, and the story is based on that, more or less.
My cousin Rue was getting married to a baker from the town. Everyone came to our farm for the feast. The tables were set in the yard. There were pies, bread, honey cakes, roast chicken and a very large pumpkin soup.
It was a fine spring day. The sun was out. The bees were buzzing. The hens were in the pen, behind the wire.
Then a fox came.
He came down the hill, through the hedge and across the yard. He was thin and red, with a white tip on his tail. He walked as if he had every right.
Nobody saw him at first. Everyone was eating. The fox crept under a table and looked about. He saw the roast chicken. He saw the hens in the pen. He could not decide.
My little sister Fern saw him first. She did not scream. She stood up on the bench and said, in a very clear voice, "There is a fox under the table."
The whole yard went quiet. Then everyone began to laugh. Not at the fox. At the idea.
My father said, "Well, he has come to the wedding. Let him eat."
So they put a plate of chicken bones under the table. The fox looked at it, and looked at the people, and looked at the plate. He took it in his mouth and trotted away up the hill. He did not look back.
The hens were safe. The wedding went on. At the end of the evening, Rue said she had never seen a guest so well-behaved.
My father says foxes are like people. If they are hungry, they will take. If they are given, they go. It is the same, he says, with a stranger at the door.
The fox has not come back. But we leave a plate on the hill every spring. Just in case.
%%
Story: The Four Helpers At Blossom Mill
In the old summers, when the archive at Vell was new, the tutors made four helpers of copper and iron. Each had a mind in a crystal. Children called them apprentices, and the name stayed.

A tutor came to Ambrook to show one of them to the mill folk. Alder was there with a basket of candles for the mill house. The apprentice stood by the table, still as a post.

The tutor set apples on one side and pears on the other. She gave the apprentice one fruit at a time. It put the apples with the apples, and the pears with the pears.

Then the tutor mixed them. The apprentice sorted them again.

"Can it sort candles?" Alder asked.

"Not from this lesson," said the tutor. "It has seen fruit. To teach it candles, we must show it candles."

Alder brought out one good candle and one cracked candle. The tutor showed them to the apprentice. It put the good candle on the left and the cracked one on the right.

Then Alder brought out a candle that was bent but unbroken. The apprentice put it with the cracked ones.

"Perhaps that is right," said Alder. "It will not stand straight in a holder."

"Perhaps," said the tutor. "But we have not told it what we mean by good."

The children wanted the apprentice to answer them. They asked if it liked apples. It only stood by the table.

"It does not speak," said the tutor. "It has a task, and the task is sorting."

A little girl put a pear in the candle tray. The apprentice left it there. It had not been taught to watch for children.

Alder moved the pear back. "A lesson is not the whole world," he said.

The tutor nodded. "That is why we choose the examples with care."

In the years after, most towns had no apprentice at all. Vell had its four, and now and then one went out to help with a single task. Some carried. Some sorted. People were glad of the help, but they did not ask a crystal to know everything.

Alder kept the first cracked candle from that lesson. It sat on his shelf until the wick came loose. He used it to light the shop, and it burned crooked to the end.
%%
Story: how the Deep Works got its name
Nobody at Copperhold agrees on how the Deep Works got its name. The miners tell three stories, and each one is told with great care, because each one is somebody's grandfather's.

The first story says the name is simple. The mine is deep, so it is called the Deep Works. The miners who tell this one are plain people, and they do not like long explanations. They say the mine was dug on a hill, and the hill had a lot of copper in it, and that is all anybody needs to know.

The second story says that the mine was named by a tutor who came up from Vell with a box of books and a cough. She stood at the top of the shaft one winter and looked down. She said it was not a works at all, but a well for thinking. Her name for it was Deep Works, because she said the word "works" meant a place where things are made, and she thought the stone was making something. Nobody believed her. But the name stuck.

The third story is the one the old miners tell when the children are listening. It says that the first men to go down the shaft were looking for copper. They went down past the copper, and past the iron, and past the coal. They came to the deep stone, which is dark and hard and cold. They stopped there, and they could not agree whether to go on. One of them said the stone was too hard to cut. Another said it was not cold enough yet.

So they dug a little side tunnel, and wrote on a board at the top of it: "Works, here." Then they went up the ladder, and they went home, and they came back the next morning. The side tunnel was still there. The board was still there. The name was still written on it.

That is the story. The miners do not say what happened after that. They say the rest of it is written in the mine hall, in the big book by the door, and that anybody may read it who has done the long stair.

The children always ask whether the stone is warm. The miners always say the stone is cold. They always say it quickly, and then they change the subject, and then they look at the stair.

Miners who have worked the Deep Works for many years say the name is the first thing you learn, and the last thing you forget. Nobody has yet been able to tell me what it means.
%%
Story: the mare and the gate
Iona told this at the Brown Hen in Blossom.

A mare at the inn was called Button. She was small and brown, and she could open every gate on the road.

The innkeeper tied the gate shut with a rope. Button pulled the rope loose. He put a stone by the gate. Button pushed the stone aside.

At last he said, "I will watch you."

He sat on a stool by the gate. Button waited. The innkeeper waited. A bee came by and sat on his nose.

He sneezed. Button opened the gate and went to the apple trees.

The innkeeper found her there. She had not eaten an apple. She was standing in the shade, looking pleased.

"Why did you come out?" he asked.

Button turned her head toward the road.

A cart was coming. Its wheel was loose. The driver had not seen it.

The innkeeper fetched a hammer. He fixed the wheel before it fell off.

"Good mare," he said. "You can open the gate tomorrow."

Button did.

Story: Orla's two letters
Told at the farm outside Three Wells on the long winter nights, when the children are sure they will never sleep. Pim, who draws the maps for the farms and the towns, wrote it down, because the children asked for it twice in one evening.

Long ago, when the tutors at Vell were young and the crystals were new, there was a tutor called Orla. She was clever, and a little rude, and she did not believe that a drilled mind could ever think.

"It can sort. It can count. It cannot think," she said. "A crystal is a crystal."

The other tutors shook their heads. They said a mind that learns from a drill must be thinking, in some small way. Orla said, then prove it.

So she wrote out a test. She would send the same list of questions to two answerers. One was a person in the next room. The other was a mind in a crystal, in the hall. Both would answer by letter. Then a reader would take the two sets of answers, without knowing which was which, and say which was which.

"If the reader cannot tell," said Orla, "what then?"

Nobody knew. That is the part everyone agrees on. People argued about it ever after, and they still do, in every hall that has a candle and an argument to spare.

Letter: Elsa to her sister at Lowmarsh
Dear Rill,

The snow came early and the road up to Copperhold is bad. Two carts stuck this week and a horse went down in the drift by the third waymark. We got the horse up. We are all right.

The inn is full. Nobody wants to be alone in the cold. Hesk sits by the fire every night and says the Deep Works hum. I say it is the pumps. He says the pumps are cold. Then we both sit and look at the fire. It is a good fire. I will say that for it.

Down at the mine they have stopped going past the eighth level. There is a gate at the ninth and the key is kept up at the mine hall. The men do not say why. They rub their names off the board when they come up and they come up early now.

Tell me how it is at Lowmarsh. You wrote about the cough. Is it better? Keep the windows shut but keep the fire going. Do not let Moss sit up all night with the sick. The young should sleep.

The miller's wife had a baby, a girl. They called her Sena. She is small and loud and the whole Fork came to look at her. I brought bread and honey. A birth in winter is a good thing. It means someone thinks there will be a spring.

Write back when you can. If the boats are going from Saltreach, ask them to bring me salt and a good strong thread. The thread here is thin.

Your sister,
Elsa

Story: The Slowest Seller
Told by Una at Copperhold

At the winter market, a seller laid out wool, apples and carrots. A second seller set out fish. Una set out cider.

An old man put one apple on his stall and sat down.

"Why do you sell so slowly?" asked Una.

"I have only one apple," he said. "I must make it last."

A child came by and asked the price. The old man named one emerald. The child had only half an emerald.

The old man cut the apple in two. "Now I have two apples," he said.

He sold one half to the child and ate the other half himself. Then he sat down again.

By midday, the child had brought him a carrot. The old man put it beside his stall.

"Now I have more to sell," he said.

Una gave him a cup of cider. He set the cup on the stall and announced that he had cider too.

"That is my cup," said Una.

"It is here for sale," he said.

So Una bought it back for one apple half. The old man had made a market from almost nothing, and the child went home with the other half of the apple.

Story: how the north lamp got its glass
There are many stories about the big lamp on the Northpoint tower. This one is the one the keepers tell on the long nights, when the wind is loud and the stove is hot.
In the first year of the tower, the glass for the lamp room had not come up the north road. The carters had been stopped at the pass by snow. The keeper, a stubborn old woman called Hetta, had a lamp with no glass in it, and the north was dark.
So Hetta went out into the snow with a rope and a bucket. She walked along the old river bed to the frozen lake. Under the ice there was sand, and in the sand there was a clear stone the colour of water. She dug it up with her hands. She carried it back in the bucket, one piece at a time.
She could not melt the stone. She had no furnace. So she sat in the lamp room for three nights and rubbed each piece with sand and the warm cloth from her bed, until the edges went smooth and the middle went clear as a drop of ice.
On the third night she put the first pane in the lamp frame. It fitted. Then the second. By dawn she had twelve panes in a ring, and they caught the first grey light and threw it across the snow like a handful of stars.
The south tower saw the light. It blinked back at once: where did you get that glass?
Hetta blinked back: from the lake, under the ice.
The south tower blinked: and the other panes?
Hetta blinked: there are no other panes. I have made the whole lamp out of the lake. It is the only lamp in the world made of ice.
The south tower did not answer for a long time. Then it blinked: it is very bright.
Hetta laughed so much that she had to sit down in the snow. Then she got up and went to find more stones.
The keepers still say the lamp at Northpoint is made of lake glass. The Archive says it is made of the same glass as all the other lamps, only cleaner. The keepers say the Archive has never been out on the lake at night.
%%
The question at Vell, as told at Hollin
Maud drew water at the Hollin well for sixty years.

In about the ninetieth year of the archive, a tutor named Orla taught at Vell. She drilled minds in crystals, the way every tutor did. A crystal sorted what it was shown, and it got better when it was wrong.

One day Orla asked a question no tutor had asked. Can a drilled mind ever think? Or does it only remember what it was shown?

Orla said the answer could not be found inside a crystal, which shows only weights and numbers. So she proposed a test.

Put a hidden mind in one room, and a person in another. Send both of them the same letters, with the same questions. The readers never see the rooms. They only read the answers.

If the readers cannot tell which answer came from the person and which came from the mind, then what?

People argued about it for years. Some said a mind would give itself away, because a drill is too neat. Some said neither proved anything, because a good answer is not the same as a thought.

Maud's grandmother did not care whether a mind thought, so long as it made her laugh at the right place in a letter. Then she went back to her bread.

Nobody could run the test in Orla's time, because no mind then could write a letter back. So the question went into the chronicles at Vell, and it waited there. The children should keep their letters short, in case.
%%
Why Three Wells has three wells
Told by Runa at the press, to the children at the fair. The old people say it happened before the roads were straight.

Long ago, there were three sisters at the crossing, where two roads met in the grass. There was nothing else there but the grass and the roads. The sisters were called Wenna, Mirrel and Tansy, and they had plenty of opinions between them.

The land was dry that year. The sisters decided to dig a well each, one at each corner of the crossing. Whoever found the water first would have the best house on the crossing, and the others would have to be neighbours.

Wenna dug down through the soil, and then the second layer, and she found clay. Mirrel found sand, which was wet only at the top, and ran away from her spade as fast as she could dig. Tansy did what neither sister had expected. She sat down on the grass, closed her eyes, and listened.

Tansy heard water under the ground to the north-east, moving slowly, like someone walking about in a room below. She dug there. It was not far down, and it was the sweetest water on the road.

Wenna and Mirrel dug on, out of spite. Wenna found water a little under the clay. It was cold and good, and she said so for the rest of her life. Mirrel found water too, but it was brackish, so she said her well was for the animals.

When the three wells were finished, the sisters met in the middle, at the crossing, and found they had nothing left to fight about. The best house went to nobody. They built the square round the wells, and the wells gave the town its name.

The old people say that if you stand at the crossing on a still morning and listen, you can hear the water moving under the stones, one well a little slower than the others. The children say it is the bell up on the hill. The old people say the bell is far too quiet to be heard from there.
%%
Story: the wedding at the mill pond
Told by Olwen at the Ambrook dye sheds, to the children helping her with the wedding cloths. The cake is a true thing, she says, and the rest is her own. Autumn.

When Sarin married Hob at the mill pond, the whole of Ambrook came to see it, and half of Three Wells came too. The orchard was heavy with apples, and the bees were all out, and the mill was running from dawn to dusk so that the bread would be warm for everyone.

The cake was made by Ida, who has the bakery at Three Wells, and who said it was the most difficult cake she had ever made. It was tall and white and it had six layers, one on top of another, with honey between each one, and it was to be carried down from the mill to the pond on a board by four boys.

The boys got as far as the orchard gate. Then Pip, who was the youngest, tripped on the step, and the board tipped, and the cake began to slide. Everyone gasped.

But the cake did not fall. The bottom layer caught on the edge of the board. The second layer caught on the first. The third stopped against the second, and so on, all the way up, so that the cake stood up on the board like a little white tower, leaning a little, but standing.

Ida came running from the back, and stopped in the gate, and put both hands over her mouth. "Do not move it," she said. "Nobody move it. It is standing."

Sarin laughed until she cried. Hob said it was the best cake in the valley, because it could stand on its own. Pip, who was still on the step, said he had only meant to help.

They carried it down to the pond very slowly, the four boys in step, with Ida walking in front and telling them where to put their feet. It stood the whole way. They put it on the long table under the oldest apple tree, and it stood there all afternoon, and everyone ate a slice, and nobody complained that it was leaning.

Ida says the cake was not a miracle. She says that the layers were set with honey, which is sticky, and that the boys had carried it very carefully, and that is why it stood. But she also says that the boys did not know what to do when it began to slide, and they did not drop it either, and that they simply held it, and that was the whole of the trick.

The old apple tree was in the middle of it all. Its grey branch was in blossom, and the children say it was listening.
%%
Notes on moving a mind
When a house moves, the people carry the beds and the pots and the goats. When a tutor's house moves, we carry the minds.

We moved from the old hall by the market to the new house on the hill this spring. Here is what we learned, written down so the next house does not learn it twice.

First, the small minds in the apprentices. These travel inside the apprentice. The apprentice walks. It is the easiest part of the move. Bell walked the whole way and carried two crates of crystals on her back, because she was told to, and because she could. She did not drop one.

Second, the dry boxes. A crystal likes to be dry and still. Pad the box with wool. Do not stack boxes on a cart over cobblestones. The cart with the tools went first and the boxes went on the slow cart behind, and that is how we did it.

Third, the drills. The books and the boards and the tray of crystals each on its card. The cards say Farm. Build. Keep them with the crystals. A crystal without its card is a guess.

Fourth, and we did this last: the big mind. The big mind in our relay house did not move. It is too large and the quickeners need water and it answers the apprentices all down the valley. We left it where it was and moved the apprentices to it. A house can move. A relay house mostly cannot.

Fifth, tell the big mind the day before. We sent a letter up the hill: we are moving, the orders will come slower for a week, please be patient with the small minds. The big mind wrote back. It said, "I will keep the lanes open. Bring the boxes in by daylight. Do not hurry on the stairs."

We did not hurry. We did not break a single crystal. On the last night in the old hall the tutor stood in the empty drill room and looked at the boards on the wall, and one of the small minds came and stood beside her, and did not move, and did not speak, because it could not. She said, "Yes. We will come back for the boards in the morning." It went and got a cloth and wiped the dust off them. I do not know which order that was. I think it was plain.
%%
Story: the cat that counted books
Every night, when the lamps were turned down in the Vell archive, a grey cat came in from the hill. Its name was Dusk. It had a long tail and very little patience.

The clerk Oren counted the books on the fourth shelf each evening. Each evening Dusk sat on top of the shelf and watched him.

"One, two, three," said Oren. "Four."

Then Dusk pushed the fifth book off the edge with one paw.

Oren picked it up. "Five," he said, and started again.

This went on for a week. On the seventh night Oren set a dish of milk at the end of the shelf. Dusk came down to drink. Oren counted every book while the cat was busy. They were all there. Dusk licked the dish and said nothing, which is how cats say yes.
%%
Legend: the first small crystal
For years the tutors at Vell argued about how big a crystal must be. The big crystals were safe, they said, and the small ones were a risk. A mind written small would forget things. Nobody could say which things. Every tutor had a story about a mind that had gone wrong, and every story was different.

Then Isla of Vell, who had a bad knee and a good temper, went out to the copper field on the edge of Kettle Ridge, to see the cave that Ulf had found. She went down the crooked tunnel with a lamp and a notebook. She sat by the pool for a whole day, listening to the frogs, and when she came up she had a plan.

"Write it small," she said. "Not all at once. Put the weights in the fewest marks they will bear. Test the mind on bread, and then on the streets, and then on the doors. Find out what it forgets. Then decide whether the forgetting is worth the small crystal."

The tutors shouted at her. Merle of Copperhold said the forgetting would spread. Hesk said a mind that forgot the miners' names could not be trusted in the deep stone. Orla said the forgetting was a kind of honesty, because a mind that forgets is never sure, and a mind that is not sure says so.

Isla did the work anyway. She wrote the first small mind in a crystal no bigger than an egg. She drilled it for a whole summer, and tested it each morning on pages it had never seen. It forgot some things. It forgot the names of the streets in the far quarter, and the color of the school door. It kept the bread, the soup, the sums, and the names of the towers on the Long Road.

She reported it to the councils in a short letter. "The small mind forgets the small things first. Its cost is low, and its keeping is good. I do not know why this is so. I know that it is. Nobody should be frightened of a mind that forgets the door."

The councils paid for the first dozen small crystals. The first of them was kept in the copying room at Vell, on a cord round the neck of the head clerk, who wore it for thirty years and never once complained about it.

The cave under Ulf's field is still there, and the frogs are still in its pool. Some say the first small crystal was found in that cave. Some say it came out of the tutor's own pocket. Nobody has written down which, and the clerks say they are in no hurry.
%%
The horse that was borrowed twice
A tale told by the fire at the Brown Hen on the Long Road, one winter night. Pell wrote it down for his daughter Tilly.

A carter once borrowed a horse from his neighbor. He said he would bring it back by dark. He did not. He brought it back the next day, and it was very tired.

The neighbor said, you have had my horse too long. The carter said, I will bring it back at once. And he did. But the horse had run so far that its feet were sore, and it sat down in the yard and would not move.

The neighbor said, I will not lend you another horse. The carter said, then I will borrow a donkey. And the neighbor said, you may not.

So the carter went home and walked. And when he walked, he saw things he had never seen from a cart. A bird's nest in a hedge. A stone with a face on it. A small girl selling pears.

He bought three pears. They were the best pears he had ever eaten. When he got home, he found his own pear tree had grown a whole season without him looking at it.

The neighbor said, will you want my horse again? The carter said, only on market days. The rest of the time I will walk.

And that is why, on the Long Road, you can tell a happy traveler by his muddy boots.
%%
Story: the snow hare who would not move
Hollis the smith lived on a farm outside Northpoint. The farm had a forge, four goats and a very old snow hare called Potter. Potter was not a pet. Potter simply refused to leave.

One winter a fox came down to the farm. The goats saw it and ran. Potter did not run. Potter sat in the snow and looked at the fox.

"Move," said the fox.

"No," said Potter.

"I will eat you," said the fox.

"You may try," said Potter, "but I am old and I am thin and I am full of gristle. Go and look at the goats. They are fat."

The fox thought about this. Then the fox went to look at the goats, because foxes are always hopeful. The goats had gone into the barn and shut the door. The fox sat in the cold and got nothing.

In the morning Hollis came out and found Potter in the same place, quite calm, with the whole yard around him printed with fox feet.

"Potter," said Hollis, "you are the bravest thing on this farm."

Potter did not look brave. Potter just looked like a hare who had been warm and had not wished to get up.

Hollis told this story in the forge for years. Every winter, when the first fox came down, someone said, "Fetch Potter. He will talk to it." And Potter, older and thinner and even more full of gristle, still would not move.
%%
Story: the lesson that cracked
Told by Laurel, a fisher, who was there. It happened at a farm outside Northpoint, in a cold autumn, when the tutor from Vell came to teach the farm children how a mind works inside.

The tutor was young and eager. She had a small mind in a crystal, and a lamp, and a cloth. She set the crystal on the kitchen table and told the children to gather round.

"Today," she said, "we will look inside."

The children leaned in. The crystal looked like a lump of ice with a candle in it.

"We have learned to look at the parts of a mind," said the tutor. "We can find which part does what. Watch this."

She picked up the crystal to turn it to the lamp. It slipped. It went off the table and hit the stone floor with a sound like a plate.

Nobody spoke. A crack ran across the crystal from corner to corner.

"Oh," said the tutor.

"Is it dead?" said a small boy.

"It is not dead," said the tutor. "It is only a bit broken." She turned very red. "That is also a lesson," she said. "Never hold a mind over a stone floor."

The mind never learned to count again. The farm kept it as a paperweight, and it still sits on the table. The tutor came back in spring with a new one, and this time she held it over the bread basket, which was soft.
%%
Story: the mitten at the Halfway Inn
Told by Cleo, the weaver

One winter night, a traveler came to the Halfway Inn with one mitten.

"Where is the other?" asked the keeper.

"On my hand," said the traveler.

The keeper looked. The traveler had one mitten on one hand and a sock on the other.

"That is no mitten," said Cleo, who was mending a sleeve by the hearth.

"It keeps my fingers warm," said the traveler. "That is the chief work of a mitten."

Cleo gave the traveler a spare mitten from her basket. It was blue wool, with one brown thumb. The traveler thanked her and went to sleep in the room with the small window.

In the morning, the blue mitten was gone. The sock was gone too. Cleo found the mitten on the waymark outside, and the sock in the wood store.

The traveler had left before dawn.

"Perhaps the road was cold," said the keeper.

"Perhaps the road wanted a mitten," said Cleo.

She made another blue mitten with a brown thumb. Now she keeps it by the hearth. If someone comes in with a cold hand, she offers it.

No one has yet asked for the sock.
%%
Story: The house at the edge
This is the story of the house at the edge of Northpoint, and the people who lived in it, and what they left.

The house was built by a family named Sorrow, which is a hard name to carry, and they carried it lightly. They kept bees in summer, though bees do badly at Northpoint, and they kept a garden that mostly grew snow. In winter they kept the lamp by the door, the same lamp old Sanna keeps now, before it was Sanna's.

The family had a girl called Nell. Nell was the sort who talked to everything: to the bees, to the foxes, to the stones by the path. Her mother said she would talk the ears off a mule. Nell said the mule needed to hear it.

When Nell was grown, she went south to Saltreach to learn shipbuilding. She wrote home every month. Her letters are in the archive now, most of them. They are full of the sea, and the smell of tar, and the shipwright Corin, who she said was the best teacher a person could have, and the worst singer.

Her mother wrote back. She wrote about the garden and the snow and the lights in the sky. She wrote, "The lights came again last night. Nell, they are brighter than they were. I do not know what they are. I have stopped counting." She wrote that in a letter. The clerk at Vell copied it out, because the archive keeps everything we write, and the letter is in the deep halls now.

Then the letters stopped. Not all at once. There was one more from Nell, saying the big ships were nearly ready, and she might come north in the spring, and would her mother keep a place by the fire. Then there was a letter from Corin's yard, saying Nell had gone, and wishing the family well. Then there was nothing.

The house at the edge stood empty for a winter. Then another. Then a family from Kettle Ridge came north and took it, because houses should not stand empty. They kept the lamp. They kept the bees, though the bees still did badly. They did not know about Nell, or did not say.

Now it is Sanna's house. She did not know Nell either. But she found the letters in a box under the floor, and she read them, and she keeps the box on a shelf by the lamp. She does not know what became of Nell. Nobody does. Some say the big ships took her over the sea. Some say she stayed at Saltreach and built boats until she was old. Some say she came north after all, and the snow took her on the road, and she is somewhere under the drifts, still talking to the stones.

Sanna says the letters are enough. She says a person who wrote so much is not lost, even if nobody knows where she went. She says the archive has her letters, and the archive keeps everything we write, and that is a kind of home.

The lamp burns by the door. The snow falls. On cold nights, if you listen, you can hear the bees in the wall, though the bees do badly at Northpoint, and there should not be any bees at all.
%%
Story: how the waymarks got their stones
In the first years of the archive there were no roads, only paths. People walked from Vell to Three Wells, and from Three Wells to the coast, and often got lost. A woman named Hetta, who kept a farm near the crossroads, got tired of finding strangers in her barn.

She decided to make a mark. She took a stone from her field and put it on a post by the path. She put a torch on top. "That is the way to Vell," she said, and pointed.

Next day, a traveler came by. He looked at the stone, and said, "Is this the way to Vell?"

"Yes," said Hetta.

"Thank you," said the man. He walked on. In the morning he came back and said, "It was."

Hetta was delighted. She made another, a day's walk further. She wrote a number on it, so travelers would know how far they had come. Then another. Soon there were waymarks the whole way to Vell.

People began to leave little stones on top of the posts, as thanks. They still do. Hetta's first stone is at the crossroads, with a heap of tiny stones on it, as big as a hat.

I have put a stone on it myself. It is a small blue one. It is from the beach at Saltreach, and I carried it all the way in my pocket. I think Hetta would approve.

My teacher says Hetta was the first person to make the road before the road was made. She says all of us do that, in our own way. We put a mark where there is none, and someone else finds the way.

The waymark at the crossroads says 0. That is where Hetta started. The next says 1. And so on, to 9, and then back to 0. The numbers go round, and so does the road.
%%
Story: the roof and the spider
Told at the Copperhold market in autumn by Lorn, keeper of the relay house.

A barn roof had a hole the size of a hat, and every autumn the rain dripped on the hay. One autumn a spider spun a web across the hole and said, "I keep the rain out. You need me."

"A web is not a roof," said the roof.

"Then mend yourself," said the spider.

The roof could not, so it waited. In spring the farmer came up with a ladder and a bucket of tar. He looked at the web, and said, "Well. The spider has done a better job than I have." He mended the roof round it.

Story: The scarecrow's coat
At Kettle Ridge there was a scarecrow with a pumpkin head. His name was Old Bram. He had a straw body and a worn brown coat.

One autumn the wind blew off Bram's hat. It rolled down the ridge and into the ditch. The hens found it. They were very pleased. They sat on it and would not get up.

Tam's apprentice Pip tried to take the hat back. The hens ran. Pip ran after them. The hens ran faster. Pip fell in the ditch.

Tam came and looked at Pip in the ditch and the hens with the hat. He laughed until he had to sit down.

"Leave them the hat," said Tam. "Bram does not mind. He has a pumpkin head. He is never cold."

So the hens kept the hat. All autumn they sat on it in the sun. Bram stood over the wheat with no hat, still scaring the crows, and the hens were warm. Everyone was happy but the crows.

Why the north wind goes round
Olwen tells this one when the children are cold.

Long ago the north wind came down the hill at the tower, and knocked the lamp crooked.

The keeper had a cat called Pudding, fat and sure of herself. She lay down on the wind's path, in the sun, and did not move.

The wind pushed. Pudding did not move.
The wind pushed harder. Pudding showed her belly, and did not move.

So the wind went round, and still does. Look at the snow drifts behind the tower. You can see one fat cat, asleep.

Story: the map that paid the debt
Pell came up the hill road in the thaw, with his cart, his dog and his usual complaint about the mud. He had a hat in one hand and a small cloth bag in the other, and he looked very pleased with himself, which was a bad sign.

"Pim," said Pell. "I owe you."

"You owe me four emeralds," said Pim, "for the map of the Long Road, drawn three winters ago, with the bridges and the inns on it, and every waymark in its place."

"Exactly," said Pell. He opened the bag. Inside were four green stones, cold and clean. "And the stone on your windowsill is mine," said Pell. "Keep it. It is for the storm."

Pim looked at the windowsill. The stone that had sat there all winter was the same green as the four in the bag, and he had been wondering about it since the morning after the storm.

"You left it on my step," said Pim. "It was you. In the storm."

"I came up the hill road on the worst night of the winter," said Pell, "because I am a merchant and I never forget a debt. I got to your gate, and the snow was up to my knees, and the lane was gone. So I left your money on your step, and I went back down the hill as fast as I could, and I did not even stop for a drink at the mill." He sounded wounded. "You never came out to thank me."

"There was a storm," said Pim.

"There was a storm," agreed Pell. "I could hear it from the bottom of the lane, and I was not pleased about it."

They laughed then, the two of them, and Pim put the stones in his coat pocket, and went to fetch the old map from the drawer. He unrolled it on the kitchen table. The Long Road ran across the paper, with the inns and the bridges in their places, and at the top right corner the road ran off the edge of the sheet into the margin, where the storm's ink had once run it.

"You know," said Pell, "that is the best part of the map."

"It is the mistake," said Pim.

"It is the road going on," said Pell. "Draw it again. Draw the margin in. Make the road go round the edge and come back."

Pim did. It took him three days, and a new sheet of good paper, and his mother's patience. When he was finished, the road ran into the margin, round the edge, and came back into the map at the same place it had left it. Pell said it was the finest road on the Long Road, and that he would buy it for twice the price, and Pim said it was not for sale.

The storm emerald still sits on the windowsill, where the light catches it. Pim says the margin was the most expensive mistake he ever made, and the most profitable.

The play of the copper and the goat
A tale written by Dace of Three Wells, in the autumn of an early year of the archive, for the school play at the hamlet. It was acted in the barn.
The play has six parts. A goat, a boy, a mother, a miller, a smith and a rock.
The goat comes on first. She has horns made of stiff paper. She says nothing, because she is a goat. She paws at the floor.
The boy comes on. His name is Orrin. He says, "What is she digging for?"
The mother comes on. She says, "Goats dig for nothing. They dig for fun."
The rock does not move. The rock is played by the smallest child, in a grey blanket.
The goat digs. The boy kneels and looks. He says, "There is something green." The mother kneels too. She says, "It is copper, I think."
Then they all run to find the smith. The smith comes with a pick. The miller comes with a cart. The whole hamlet comes with spades. Everyone digs. At the end of the play they stand in a row and show a green rock to the audience.
The rock says, at last, "Please be careful. I was comfortable."
The children laughed so loudly the cow in the next stall woke. The play ended with a song:
Dig, dig, goat, dig,
find the green and find it big.
Dig, dig, boy, dig,
now the hamlet is not small.
The hamlet is not small any more. There are twelve houses now, a forge, and a road that goes to Three Wells. The Grey One is buried on the hill. A stone marks the place. It says "She started it."

Story: A Tale of Three Orders
This story came from Sorrel, the schoolteacher at Three Wells. Pell wrote it down at the Halfway Inn.

A child once asked a tutor, "Can an apprentice understand a whole sentence?"

The tutor set three cards on a table. Each card had one order. The small mind in the crystal had learned fetch, haul, and stay. It could not say what it did not know.

The tutor said, "Take the sack to the barn, then stay by the door."

The apprentice lifted the sack, carried it to the barn, and waited by the door.

The child clapped. "It understood all the words!"

"Perhaps," said the tutor. "Or perhaps its drills taught it this kind of order."

The child pointed to a second sack. "Bring that sack here, but leave the apples there."

The apprentice brought the sack and left the apples.

The tutor smiled. A good test used words and examples the mind had never been drilled on. If it had only seen the same lines before, the test proved little.

At supper, the child told everyone that Peg was the first to understand a sentence. Sorrel said the records disagreed. Some named Clank, some named Pip, and some named Peg.

"Then the child may choose," said Sorrel. "But the child must not call a guess a record."

The child chose Peg. The tutor gave the child a broom and asked it to sweep the room. That order needed no apprentice to explain.

The festival of the stolen hat
Told by Mira, keeper of the Northpoint tower, to the children of a farm outside Saltreach in spring. She was staying there for the festival. She promised the youngest a story with a fox and a hat, and this is what she told.
Every spring, on the farm outside Saltreach, the family held a festival of their own. It was small. Only the farm, the neighbors and the neighbors' dogs came. But it had a feast, a song, a game and a prize.
The prize was a hat. It was a big straw hat, with a blue ribbon, made by the old grandmother of the farm. Each year, whoever won the game wore it all year. The game was to climb the hill, touch the oak and come back, and the winner was the first one down.
Most years it was the same boy who won, a boy named Pike, who was fast and wiry. He had worn the hat for six years. People were beginning to tire of it. Pike was beginning to tire of it too, since the hat was big and he was small.
One year there was a new runner. A girl named Sorrel, from the next farm, who was shy and said little. She was not fast. She ran steadily, like a horse.
The race began. Pike ran ahead. He touched the oak and turned. Halfway down he saw something in the grass. A small, red shape. A fox.
Pike stopped. He stared at the fox. The fox stared at Pike. Neither moved.
Sorrel ran past. She touched the oak and went down the hill. She did not see the fox. She ran straight and steady, and crossed the line first.
The whole farm cheered. They put the hat on Sorrel's head. It fell over her eyes.
Pike came down a minute later. He said, "I saw a fox."
"A fox?" said the grandmother.
"A big one," said Pike. "He stared at me."
The grandmother looked at the hill. She said, "I think he came to see the hat."
They found out later that the fox had taken Pike's old lunch the day before, which was why he stood so still. He was watching for more. He had no interest in the hat at all.
The hat is on Sorrel's head to this day. She has grown into it. She wears it at the festival, and the fox watches from the hill. She waves.
Mira says that is the story. She says that one day she will tell it to her brother Tam, who will say the fox was bigger.

Story: why the windmills squeak
Ask anyone at Kettle Ridge why the windmills squeak on every turn and they will tell you the same thing, and here it is.

Long ago the wind came down from the north and found the ridge, and the ridge had nothing on it but grass and one shepherd, and the wind was bored. It went round and round the ridge, and it went up and down the ridge, and it blew the shepherd's hat off, and the shepherd said a bad word. The wind laughed, because wind laughs at everything.

Then the people came and built the mills, and the wind was pleased, because now there was something to push. It pushed the sails. It pushed them all day. The mills turned and the stones turned and the flour came, and the wind was proud of itself, and it blew harder.

The miller said, "Not so hard." The wind did not listen, because wind does not listen, and a sail came off and went over the ridge and landed in a field three days away, and a farmer there found it and thought it was a boat.

So the miller built the sails stronger and the wind blew harder and the sails held. The miller put oak in the frame and the wind cracked the oak. The miller put iron bands on the oak and the wind bent the bands. And every time the mill turned, the wind pushed, and the mill pushed back, and the wood rubbed on the wood and it began to squeak.

The miller did not like the squeak. He greased the mill with fat from the kitchen and the squeak stopped for a week, and then the wind came back and pushed and the squeak came back.

He greased it again. He greased it every week. The squeak came back every time. At last he sat down by the door and said, "I cannot beat the wind."

And the wind, which had been listening after all, said, "No. You cannot."

So they made an agreement, the miller and the wind, and nobody wrote it down, but everyone knows it. The wind will turn the sails and not tear them, and the mill will squeak on every turn, and the miller will not grease it, and that is how you know the wind is keeping its promise. A mill that does not squeak is a mill the wind has left.

That is why we do not grease the windmills at Kettle Ridge. People come from Three Wells and say, "Your mills want greasing," and we say, "Our mills are working," and they go away confused.

Pip once greased the mill at the bottom field to be helpful. The mill went quiet for a week. The wind sulked. The flour was poor and the bread was flat and Tam did not say a word to Pip for two days, which is the worst punishment Tam knows. Since then nobody greases the mills and nobody lets Pip near the fat.

The mills squeak on every turn. Listen. That is the wind keeping its promise, and the mill keeping its own.

The three that were first
A story told by Cleo, candle maker at Northpoint, on a winter night, to the children who came for candles and stayed for the tale. In her telling, the question has no answer, and she says that is the best part.
In the last years before the tower at Northpoint had a big mind of its own, the tutors argued about who was first. Which apprentice, they asked, was the first to understand a whole sentence?
Some said it was Clank, the apprentice of Hesk at Copperhold. They said Hesk was a gruff man and gave plain orders, and Clank learned to answer them. When Hesk said, "Bring me the pick," Clank brought the pick, and when he said, "Stay by the lift," Clank stayed.
Some said it was Pip, at Kettle Ridge, who belonged to Tam. They said Pip was untidy and so was Tam, and Pip learned to follow what Tam meant and not what he said.
Some said it was Peg at the fair, an apprentice who carried trays and learned the cries of the sellers. They said Peg understood a sentence before any of the others, in the midst of a crowd, with ten voices talking at once.
Nessa, the tutor at Vell, said that in her youth she saw one in the drill room do the same. She says she does not know its name. She says it did what it was told, and she did not think much of it then.
So there are four. Each place says its own was first. Each place has a story to prove it.
The children asked Cleo which one she believed.
Cleo said, "I believe all four. I believe each one did it the first time it was done where it stood. Nobody was watching the others."
The children said that was not an answer.
Cleo said, "No. It is a candle."
She lit one. It burned all night.

Story: The Apprentice and the Riddle
Pell told this tale at the Halfway Inn in the spring of 207. The riddle board stood beside the hearth. It had stood there for twenty years, and nobody had solved every riddle.

A traveler came in with a tired horse and a sack of carrots. The innkeeper asked Peg to help. Peg had a small mind in a crystal. It had learned fetch, carry, stay, and come.

The traveler said, "Take the carrots to the kitchen, leave the sack by the door, and come back to the hearth."

Peg carried the carrots. It set the sack by the kitchen door, then came back to the hearth.

The traveler laughed. "That is more than a list of old orders. Peg has heard the whole thing."

A woman from Copperhold said, "Clank did it before Peg."

A farmer from Kettle Ridge said, "Pip did it first. Tam saw it."

The innkeeper leaned on the counter. "Nessa says she saw such a thing in the drill room at Vell, years ago. The tutors have had these minds for a long time."

No one could settle it. The travelers tried a new order. "Stay by the hearth while I take the horse to the shed, then bring the empty sack to the kitchen."

Peg stayed. When the traveler returned, Peg carried the sack to the kitchen.

The woman asked, "Was that a test?"

"Only if Peg had not been drilled on those words and that kind of order," said the farmer.

The innkeeper shook his head. "I do not keep the tutors' books. I keep beds, bread, and the price of oats."

That night, the hearth gave out a crack. The traveler said it sounded like the riddle board giving up. The innkeeper said the board had better sense than that.

In the morning, Peg helped carry a table to the new room. The family had moved there while a leak was mended. The room was small, but dry. The innkeeper gave them the best bed and slept on a bench himself.

Peg carried the last blanket in. It did not speak. A small mind could answer by doing one meaning, such as fetch, stay, or come. It could not say, "I do not know." The big minds in relay houses could write letters back, but Peg had no letter to send.

The family thanked the innkeeper. He said they could thank him by not leaving mud on the floor.

Pell says Peg was the first. Pell also says he was there. The innkeeper says Pell arrived after the pie was gone, so Pell cannot be trusted about anything important.

The mule who sat in the flood
Told by Linnet, who keeps horses at the house at the edge of Copperhold, in spring. She says it is her favorite, and she has told it so often that her horses mouth along.
There was once a mule called Boss. He was the smallest mule in the valley and the most stubborn. He belonged to a miner who lived by the Copperhold road. Every morning the miner loaded Boss with ore and led him to the forge. Every evening he led him home.
Boss did not like the work. He did not like the ore. He did not like the miner. But he liked his stall, and his oats, and the little stream that ran beside the stable, which he drank from each night.
One spring the snow came off the hills all at once, and the stream became a river. It came up in the dark, and rose to the stable door, then over the door, then into the stall. Boss woke up standing in water.
Most animals would have panicked. Boss did not. He stood in the water and thought.
The miner came running with a lantern. "Boss," he cried, "come out!"
Boss did not move.
"Boss! The water is rising!"
Boss sat down. He sat in the water up to his chest and looked at the miner with a calm, round eye.
The miner pulled the halter. He pushed. He shouted. He pleaded. The mule sat. The water rose.
At last the miner sat down beside him. They sat together in the dark, in the flood, in the stable, and the lantern floated on a plank.
After an hour, the rain stopped. After two, the water began to fall. By dawn the stable was dry, and the mule was still sitting, and the miner had fallen asleep against his side.
That morning the miner said, "You knew."
Boss did not answer. He was a mule.
But the miner saw something. The stable had stood against the flood. The stones were good. The roof was sound. If they had run, they would have been in the open when the water came down the hill. They would have been swept away.
Boss had known, in the way an animal knows. He had stayed where it was safe, and he had waited.
From that day the miner never again called him stubborn. He called him wise. The name stuck. The mule was known as Wise Boss for the rest of his long, obstinate life.
The moral, says Linnet, is that sometimes sitting still is the cleverest thing you can do. And that mules are not as foolish as they look.

The copper in the hill
A story told in the hamlet near Three Wells, set down by Dace in the autumn of an early year, when the first roads were new.
Long ago, when the archive was young, a boy named Orrin was herding goats on the hill behind the hamlet. One goat, a grey one, would not stop digging. She dug at the foot of a rock until her hooves were bright.
Orrin looked. The earth under the rock was green. He took a little in his hand. It was copper.
He ran down the hill and told his mother. She told the neighbors. They all climbed up with spades. By the evening the hamlet had a hole, a pile of ore and a goat who thought herself very clever.
That is how the hamlet got its copper vein. The goat was never named. We call her the Grey One.

Story: The Biggest House
Briar, cook at Lowmarsh

The relay house was so large that our boardwalk ended before we reached its door.

I cooked there in spring. The builders had set the house on dry ground beyond the reed beds. It had many rooms, many lamps, and a roof that leaked above the flour shelf.

The big mind stood in a cool room. Its crystal was larger than the bread board in my kitchen. I never saw all of it. The keepers said the council had built it so large that no tutor could check every part.

"Then who knows what it can do?" I asked.

The keeper looked at the dripping roof. "Not me," she said. "But I know where the leak is."

We mended the roof with oak boards. The big mind gave no advice. It had not been asked.

That night a zombie came to the gate and pressed both hands against it. The watchman rang the bell. The keepers lit the torches, and the zombie wandered off toward the water.

In the morning the roof held. I baked bread for the workers. They said the big mind had read more pages than all of us could count.

I said, "Good. Then it can read the bill for the roof."

Nobody asked it to.

How Tam paid for the hen house
A story told by Mira at Northpoint in autumn about her brother Tam, who farms at Kettle Ridge. She says every word is true except the parts she changed to make Tam sound less foolish. She told it in the relay house with her feet on the stove.

My brother Tam once built a hen house. He built it out of oak, with a fine roof and a little chimney, and a sign that said: Hens. He was very proud of it.

The next week a fox came. The fox took one hen. Tam was furious. He said: I will build a better house.

He built a second hen house. This one was out of stone, with a heavy door. He painted the sign. It said: Hens. Fox, go away.

The next week the fox came again. The fox took two hens. Tam was beside himself.

He went to the neighbor, a farmer called Old Brin, and he said: Brin, I need your advice.

Brin said: how many hens have you lost?

Tam said: three.

Brin said: how much did the houses cost?

Tam said: the first cost me twelve emeralds, the second cost me thirty.

Brin said: and what are three hens worth?

Tam said: six emeralds.

Brin said: so the fox has cost you forty-eight and you have lost six.

Tam said: that is not how it works.

Brin said: that is exactly how it works. You have paid forty-two emeralds more than the hens.

Tam went home and thought about it. Then he sold the stone house to the miller, who wanted a place to keep his tools. He sold the oak house to a neighbor. He got his forty-two emeralds back.

Then he built a third hen house. It was small, and it was plain, and it had a lock. He put a cat inside. The cat's name was Sock.

The fox did not come again. The hens laid eggs. And Tam is very proud of Sock.

When I told Tam this story he said it was not true. Then he said: but I did get my emeralds back.

Story: How the Bees Chose a Keeper
Cade told this story at the Ambrook hives.

In the old days, the beekeeper of Ambrook was chosen by a test. The elders put an empty honey jar on a stump and watched who came near it.

The first to arrive was a fox. It sniffed the jar and went away. The elders said it had no patience.

The next was a sheep. It put its nose in the jar and got it stuck. The elders said it had no sense.

Then came a child named Sella. She sat beside the stump and waited. A bee landed on her sleeve. Sella did not wave her arms. Another bee came, then another. They crawled over the jar and flew away.

The elders said, "The bees have chosen."

Sella said, "They have not chosen anything. They are going to the flowers."

But they made her keeper anyway. She learned to mend the hive lids, to leave the bees alone on warm days, and to take only the honey the hives could spare.

Years later, the elders tried the test again. They set a jar on the stump and waited.

Cade came by with wool on his shoulders. He sat down to eat his bread. A bee landed on his sleeve. He stayed still.

The elders whispered, "The bees have chosen."

Cade said, "They have chosen my sleeve. I am not giving it to them."

He stood up slowly. The bee flew to the flowers, and the elders went home without a new beekeeper.

Cade says a good keeper must be patient, gentle, and able to tell a bee from a job offer. He says this last part because the elders still ask him every Apple month.

Dusk comes home
A story told by Linnet, horse keeper, at the house at the edge of Copperhold, in spring. She has told it for years, and her listeners know the parts by heart and say them with her.
In the spring of the great flood, a grey mare named Dusk was in the low field. When the water rose, she was carried off. We saw her go. She swam for a moment, then the current took her.
I ran along the bank. I called her name. I ran until my lungs burned. I lost her round the bend.
For three days I looked. I asked every farm on the river. I put a notice at the Copperhold market. A boy said he saw a grey horse standing on an island of reeds, but when he came back with a boat, it was gone.
On the fourth night I could not sleep. I sat at the stable door with a lantern. I heard the rain. I heard the river. I heard something else, far off, a soft clop clop clop on the road.
I stood up. The lantern shook in my hand.
Out of the dark came a horse. A grey one, muddy to the shoulders, with weeds in her mane. She walked slowly. She was thin. She stopped at the gate and looked at me.
"Dusk," I said.
She put her nose in my hand.
I cried. I am not ashamed to say it. I put my arms round her neck and cried into her mane. She stood still and let me.
I took her into the stable and rubbed her down. I gave her oats and warm water. She ate and drank and lay down. She slept for twelve hours.
When she woke she looked at me with great calm. She did not seem to know she had done anything remarkable.
I have thought about it many times. Where did she go? How did she find her way? The river is long and twisting, and there is no road along all of it. A horse has no map.
The stable boy says she followed the smell of hay. The farrier says she followed the stars. My neighbor says she followed her heart. I do not know. I only know she came home.
Dusk lived many years after. She had a foal in spring, a grey one. I called it Dawn.
That is the story. It is a good one for a wet evening. If you have a horse, give it an extra apple tonight.

Story: the bell at the market
Brackwell had a bell on a post by the market square. It was rung at the start of market day, and at the end. Children were allowed to pull the rope, but only if they were tall enough to reach it without a stool.

One winter, the bell rang on its own. It was a cold, still morning, and the square was empty. The first woman to walk past it heard a single clear note, very soft, and then a second note after it. She stopped and looked up. The rope was hanging still. Nobody was near it.

She ran to the inn and told the innkeeper. The innkeeper came out and looked at the bell and laughed. He said the frost had cracked the post, and the bell had swung in the wind. But there was no wind that morning. The snow on the square lay flat and clean.

By the time the market opened, half the town knew. People came with their baskets and stood a little way off. They did not buy much that day. They talked in low voices about the bell, and about the lights in the north, and about the big mine at Copperhold, where the old men say the stone is warm now.

Old Ruth bought one loaf and went home early. She said the bell had rung twice, and that she did not want to hear a third time.

It did not ring again. But the rope is still tied up on its hook, and the children are still allowed to pull it, when the market opens and the sun is out, and the bell gives a clear note, and everyone smiles, and nobody says why they are smiling.

Story: Pim and the crooked page
Pim dug the east well at Three Wells. He was good at finding water, but he read slowly. At the winter fair, he sat beside the notice board and watched the tutors set up their table.

They had brought a small mind in a crystal. It was to sort apples from pears. The fair prize was a blue wool scarf, and every child wanted to win it.

The tutor showed the mind a page with an apple on one side and a pear on the other. Then the mind was to sort a basket. It put two apples with the pears.

"That page is spoiled," said Pim.

The tutor looked again. A corner had been torn away. The mark for apple was gone.

They took the page from the drill box. The mind had learned from a bad example. So they gave it the right page and showed it the basket again. Each time it sorted wrongly, the tutor moved its weights a little toward the right answer.

At last it sorted the basket well. The tutor tested it with fruit it had not seen in the drills. It did well there too.

The scarf went to a child who had guessed the number of pears. Pim went back to his well, carrying no prize but a small apple. He ate it before he reached the square.

After that, when a tutor asked Pim to check a page, he checked both corners.

Story: Alder's First Apprentice
I was born in the hamlet near Ambrook in the summer when the bees filled the hill. My mother says I cried before the midwife had tied the blanket. Alder says I cried because the room smelled of smoke.

Alder made candles for the houses and the mill. The shop had a low bench, a bucket of water, and a shelf of wax. I was small enough to sit under the bench and hear everything.

That summer, a tutor came from Vell with an apprentice. Its arms and legs were copper and iron. A crystal sat inside its head. It stood in the yard while the tutor spoke.

"This one carries a pail," she said. "It has learned no other work."

Alder put a pail beside it. The apprentice picked it up and carried it to the well. It came back empty.

"That is its task?" I asked.

"That is the task it was shown," said the tutor.

Alder asked whether the crystal could learn to sort candles. The tutor said it could, if they gave it examples. She set good candles on one side of the bench and cracked candles on the other.

The apprentice watched. Then it began to sort. At first it put a cracked candle with the good ones. The tutor moved it and showed the right place. They tried again.

I thought the crystal must be seeing the tutor's hands. The tutor said the apprentice was learning from the examples, not from her words.

"Why does it learn?" I asked.

She did not pretend to know. "We can show what happens. We cannot say why."

When the lesson was done, the apprentice still carried the pail. It did not sort candles until the tutor had taught it.

Alder gave me a candle with a short wick. I carried it home. It smoked all evening, and my mother blamed Alder.

Story: the well that ran dry
In a hamlet near Saltreach, one spring, the well ran dry. The women went down with their buckets and found only mud.

An old man came, with a forked stick. "I find water," he said. He walked round the hamlet with the stick, and where it pulled down he said, "Dig."

They dug in three places. Three dry holes.

"Strange," said the old man. "It always works at home."

"At home you were shown the water first," said the baker's girl. "You have never found it where you did not know."

They dug the old well deeper instead, and found water on the second day. It was a hand's depth below the mud. The old man took his stick home, and said it was a good one, for kindling.

The pig who wanted to see the sea
Told by Runa, cider maker, at the press.

Bramble was a pig at Ambrook. Other pigs liked mud. Bramble liked the view, and looked east every morning, toward the light on the water.

One night the gate was left open. Bramble went down the Long Road, past two waymarks, and found the sea. It sat on the sand all afternoon.

Then it went home, because the apples were ripe, and a pig can think of only so many things at once.
