Story: the zombie at the gate
Told by Cleo, at the Halfway Inn

One winter night, a zombie came to the gate of the Halfway Inn.

It stood outside the wall and groaned at the latch. The keeper heard it from his bed. He got up, took a torch, and went to the gate.

"Keep the torches lit," called Cleo from the bench by the hearth. "And do not open that gate."

"I know," said the keeper.

The zombie groaned again. It leaned against the gate, and the latch shook.

The keeper looked through the crack. The zombie wore a torn coat and had snow on its head. It held one hand out toward the torch.

"Perhaps it is cold," said Cleo.

"It is a zombie," said the keeper.

"That does not tell us whether it is cold."

The keeper fetched another torch and set it in the wall bracket. Cleo fetched a bowl of yesterday's beetroot soup, though she knew the zombie would not eat it. She put the bowl on the stone beside the gate, then backed away.

The zombie reached for the bowl. It knocked the bowl over. Soup ran into the snow.

"That was my good bowl," said the keeper.

The zombie groaned, took two slow steps, and turned toward the road. The keeper shut the little outer bar with care.

At breakfast, the bowl was still there. The snow beside it held a crooked track, going away from the inn.

The keeper mended the gate and Cleo washed the bowl. They did not agree about the soup. The keeper said it had driven the zombie away. Cleo said the zombie had only wanted the torch.

That night, she left no soup outside. But she put an extra torch by the gate.
%%
Story: the contest of the two halls
When my gran was young, the tutors of Vell and the tutors of Copperhold had a quarrel. Each said they made the better mind. Each said so loudly, at the fair, with their hats in the air.

"My mind can add any sum!" said the Vell tutor.

"My mind can add faster!" said the Copperhold tutor.

"Show me," said the old mayor of Three Wells, who was tired of them both.

So the tutors agreed on a contest. But first, they had to agree on a rule. This was the clever part, and my gran says it was the mayor who thought of it.

"Each of you will bring a mind," said the mayor. "Nobody will tell either of you the sums. I will ask the school children to write sixty sums on slips and put them in a box. Neither hall has seen them. Both minds will answer the same sums, in the same room."

"What if my mind has seen one of those sums?" said the Vell tutor.

"That is why the children write new ones," said the mayor. "A mind that was drilled on a sum will get it right because it remembers. I want to know who learned to add."

The slips were drawn. The Vell mind got fifty-one right. The Copperhold mind got fifty-three. Everyone clapped, though the Vell tutor clapped less. And something else happened that nobody had planned. Both halls began to drill their minds on fewer sums and test them on more, because they knew now that the test was the thing.

Gran says the tutors became honest that day. They had to be. Every hall now tests its minds on slips that nobody has seen, and when a hall wins, the others ask to see the slips.
%%
Story: the careful cart
Told by Hollis beside the Three Wells notice board

Once I had a cart that knew how to stop.

It stopped at the bakery, though I had no bread to buy. It stopped by the inn, though I had no bed to take. It stopped at each of the three wells, though the wheels were dry.

I blamed the horse. The horse blamed the road. The road said nothing.

Then Bel came out of the school house and looked at the cart. "There is a turnip under the left wheel," she said.

There was. It had fallen from my sack and rolled just where the wheel would find it.

I pulled the turnip free. The cart rolled on.

"Your cart is careful," Bel said.

"It has learned to stop for food," I told her. "I have tried to teach it to stop for supper, but it will not listen."

Bel laughed, and the horse flicked its ears.

That evening I gave the horse an apple. I gave the cart a good look at the empty turnip sack.

The cart did not stop once on the way home.

A letter to the archive: Tam
To the clerks at Vell, from Tam at Kettle Ridge, this Blossom.

You will have heard that there was a contest. You will have heard it from someone who was not there. Here is what happened.

At the spring fair, Hattie put up a prize: a whole barrel of beetroot wine, and a ribbon, and the right to call yourself the best reader on the ridge. The contest was to read a page of old writing that no mind had been drilled on. Everyone brought a mind. There was a big one from Copperhold on a cart, and a small one from the mill, and a young one Nessa had left here for the fair.

Then Hattie said the question was not about reading at all. It was a bet, made by a tutor from Vell who did not want to be named. The bet was this: can a mind learn to say "one or the other, but not both"?

Pip's mind was asked. It answered, in its one word, as it always answers, and was wrong. The mill mind was asked. It answered the opposite, and was wrong. The Copperhold mind was asked. It answered one thing and then the other, and was wrong twice.

The whole fair went quiet. Then Hattie laughed and gave the prize to the smallest mind, because it had been honest the fastest.

I am not sure we should be proud of that. My sister Mira in the north writes that a mind should not be asked what it cannot learn. I think she is right, mostly. But the ribbon is on the pig pen now, and the wine is gone.

Tell Oren to keep this letter. It is not a lesson. It is just what happened.

Tam.

Story: The Wool Cart at the Pond
Cade told this tale beside Jory's mill.

In the 183rd year of the archive, Cade brought a cart of wool to the mill pond. He had carded it for three days. It was clean, soft, and tied in bundles with blue cord.

The cart was pulled by a small horse named Barley. Barley was a kind horse, but he disliked the mill wheel. It clacked all day, and Barley thought it was speaking about him.

"Keep walking," Cade said.

Barley stopped.

"The wheel is not talking to you."

Barley turned one ear toward Cade and the other toward the wheel. He was not convinced.

A mill hand came to help. She took Barley by the bridle while Cade pushed the cart. Together they got it over the bridge. At the far side, Barley stepped aside and the cart rolled into the grass.

One bundle of wool slid off. It rolled down the bank and landed in the pond.

Cade ran after it. The mill hand ran after Cade. Barley stayed by the bridge, pleased to be away from the wheel.

The wool floated. It was too light to sink and too heavy to stay dry. Cade leaned down with a long branch, but the bundle drifted away from him.

"Hold the cart," he called.

The mill hand held the cart. The horse held nothing. The bundle turned in a slow circle, following the pond's edge.

A frog sat on a reed and watched it pass.

"Do not ask the frog for help," said Cade. "He has no pockets."

The mill hand found a rake in the mill shed. She reached out and pulled the bundle to shore. They lifted it onto the grass and let the water run out. The wool was still clean, though it smelled of pond.

At that moment the miller came out. "That is the softest wool I have seen," she said.

"It is wet," said Cade.

"Water makes it soft."

"Water makes everything soft. It is a poor judge."

The miller put a hand on the bundle. "I will pay one emerald extra for it."

Cade said he would take the offer if she could name the sheep.

The miller could not. She offered him half an emerald.

Cade laughed and took the usual price. The wool dried in the sun. Barley was led past the wheel again, this time with a cloth over one ear.

The miller asked why Cade did not put a cloth over the other ear too.

"Then he could not hear me," Cade said.

"That might help," said the mill hand.

By evening, Barley had crossed the bridge twice without stopping. The wheel clacked. The horse flicked both ears and kept going.

Cade's bundles were stacked in the dry shed, tied with blue cord. He went home with a little pond water on his boots and no extra emerald.

The mill hand says the frog deserved a share, for watching the whole affair. Cade says the frog was paid in flies. The frog has made no complaint.

Cade still brings wool to the mill pond each Apple month. Barley still watches the wheel. And when it clacks, Cade tells him, "It is only working. It is not talking about you."

Barley always listens. Then he walks on.

The school play at the Green Pick
Dace, mayor of Three Wells, wrote down this story in the autumn of an early year, from the telling of the old woman Ilse. It is set in the hamlet near Three Wells, and it is all about a play, a vein of copper and a very long hat.
In the autumn after the vein was found, the hamlet was rich in ore and poor in plays. The schoolteacher, a woman named Tarn, decided the children should put on a show for the harvest supper. She chose the story of how the vein was found.
There were thirty children in the school, and only eight parts. The rest were told to be rocks. The rocks sat in rows on the stage and looked brave.
The boy who played Brask was a boy named Fitch. He was nine, and he had a very large voice. He wore a hat made of three sacks, which made him look as tall as the door. When he swung his wooden pick, he hit the rock beside him, who was a girl named Posy. Posy said "Ow" in the middle of the scene. Everyone laughed. The teacher said to go on.
The goat was played by a boy in a fur coat. He was very good. He ate a real carrot on the stage and it was the best part of the evening.
The second scene was the finding. Brask knelt and picked up a green stone that the teacher had painted that morning. She had used the last of the copper-green paint. The stone was still wet. When Brask held it up, a drop ran down his wrist and into his sleeve. He said, "It is cold," in the middle of his line, and the audience cheered.
At the end the whole school stood in a row and sang the song of the vein. Then the thirty children took off their rock blankets and bowed. The old woman Ilse stood up at the back and said, "That is not how it happened. But it is better."
The play was done again the next year, and the one after. It is done still. The hat has been mended twice.

Story: the mill pond sluice
Told by Olwen at the dye sheds, to the boys who wanted to know why nobody is allowed to open the sluice on the mill pond. She says the story is older than the mill, and that every child in Ambrook has heard it once. Autumn.

When the mill was built, the mill pond was made by damming the stream. The dam had a sluice in it, a wooden gate with an iron wheel, so the water could be let out when the pond grew too high.

The first miller was a man called Haskin. He was clever and quick, and he liked to be the one who decided things. So he kept the sluice key on his own belt, and nobody else could touch the wheel.

One spring the rain came hard. The pond rose. The water came up to the hedge, then over the lane, and the children of Ambrook had to be taken up the hill in carts. Haskin was at the mill with the key on his belt. He would not open the sluice until the council had voted, and the council was meeting in the Brown Hen, a long walk off.

By the time the council voted, the dam had cracked. The water came out all at once, and took the bridge, and the mill wheel was pushed half off its axle.

After that the council made a rule. The sluice belongs to the mill, and the key goes to the miller, but nobody may open the wheel without a second person watching. And the rule was written on a board, which is still on the wall of the mill.

Jory says the board is the most important thing in Ambrook. He keeps the key, and he does not open the sluice, and when the boys ask him why, he says: because Haskin did. Then he laughs, and says the boys should stop asking, and go and count the apples.

Legend: why the words are over the door
The first keepers did not agree on what to cut over the door.

Some wanted the laws, because the laws are the most important thing a people writes. Some wanted a map of the land, so people would know where they were. Some wanted the name of the first keeper, and her mother's name, and the name of the hill.

Then a child named Mab, who had been sent up the hill with a basket of letters, put down her basket and said, "Why not say we keep everything we write?"

The keepers laughed. The child did not. "The laws are in the laws," she said, "and the map is on the map. But who keeps the letter from my aunt about her cow?"

The keepers were quiet for a long time. Then Perrin the stonecutter, who had been eating his lunch on the wall, put down his bread and said, "That is a great many letters. It will take all summer."

"Then it will take all summer," said the first keeper.

So Perrin cut the words. He cut them one letter at a time, with a chisel and a small hammer, and he cut them deep, so the rain would not wear them away. It took the whole summer. The children came every evening to watch. They brought him water and bread, and they counted the letters each day, and each day the count was wrong, and they counted again.

A bee came and sat on the first letter every afternoon. Perrin let it stay until it was ready to go, and then he cut the letter.

When the last letter was cut, the keepers came out of the hill to look. They walked back and forth in front of the door and read the words aloud, slowly, the way you read a new law. "We will keep everything we write," they said. Then they said it again, and the youngest keeper read it as if it were a letter from someone she loved. That was the way they liked it best, and it is how the words are read in Vell still.

Then they went inside and began the first shelf. It was for the letter about the cow.

Story: The jar on the fence

Tam told this story at the Kettle Inn, after a jar of honey went missing from his table.

One spring morning, Tam set a jar of honey on the fence while he carried a basket to the barn. He meant to put the jar away when he came back.

A bee found it first. She landed on the lid and buzzed for her sisters.

A chicken heard the buzzing and came to see. She pecked the fence. The jar rocked, but did not fall.

Then a pig pushed through the gate. He rubbed his back on the fence and made the jar wobble again.

Pip came running from the fields. "The jar!" Pip cried.

Tam was still in the barn. Pip reached for the jar, but the bee circled Pip's head. Pip ducked. The chicken flapped. The pig ran through the gate, and the jar began to slide.

Pip caught it against the fence post. Honey ran down the side and stuck to Pip's sleeve.

Tam came back. He looked at the jar, then at Pip.

"You saved it," said Tam.

"I saved most of it," said Pip.

The bee settled on the lid. The chicken pecked the honey on Pip's sleeve. The pig licked the fence post.

Tam put the jar on the table. He gave Pip a piece of bread with honey on it.

"Next time," he said, "I will carry it myself."

Pip took a bite. "Next time, put it in the barn."

Tam says the jar is now kept on a high shelf. The pig has not found it. The chicken has.

Story: The lamp keeper and the fox
At Northpoint the winter is long, and the market in summer is short and loud. People come down from the farms and up from the coast, and for three days the square by the relay tower is full of stalls. Mira keeps the tower, and Lantern keeps the lamps, and in summer they keep a stall too, with honey from Ambrook and wool from the ridge.

One summer a fox came to the market. It came at dusk, when the stalls were closing, and it sat by the fish stall and did not move. The fish seller threw it a herring. The fox ate the herring and sat down again.

"Chase it off," said the fish seller.

"Not yet," said Mira. "It has something to say."

The fox said nothing, because foxes do not talk in true accounts. It only sat, and looked at the tower, and then at the road that goes south. It did this three evenings running.

On the fourth evening Mira followed the fox. It went up the hill behind the market, past the last stall, to a hollow under a spruce. In the hollow was a fox cub with its foot caught in a snare.

Mira had a knife and a steady hand. She cut the snare and the cub ran off. The old fox looked at her once, and then it was gone too.

Lantern asked her that night, "How did you know?"

"The fox kept looking at the road," said Mira. "A fox does not look at a road unless something on it matters. It was asking me to follow."

People tell this story now when they want to say that a thing that cannot speak can still ask. Mira says only that the herring was wasted, and that she has never seen that fox again, and that she does not mind.

The old lamp at the harbor
Mira, relay keeper at Northpoint, told this to the children of a farm outside Saltreach one spring when she came down to see the harbor. It is a story she says her father told her.
At the end of the long pier at Saltreach there stands a lamp. It is old, with a stone base and a glass top. It has been lit every night since the harbor was built. The first keeper, they say, was a woman called Marit, and the lamp is still called Marit's Lamp.
In the early days the lamp was not so strong. The light was weak, and the fishing boats sometimes lost their way. One night a boat came in on the wrong side of the pier, and ran onto the rocks. Nobody was hurt, but it was a close thing.
Marit said, "We need a better lamp."
The town had little money. The harbor wall was not finished. The shipyard had just begun. A better lamp would cost forty emeralds, and they did not have forty.
So Marit asked the children.
"What can we do?" she said. "We need forty emeralds and have six."
A boy named Joss said, "We could have a festival."
Marit said, "A festival costs money."
Joss said, "A festival makes money."
It was a new idea. The town thought about it. They held a festival on the first full night of Blossom. They sold pies, fish and flower wreaths. They held a boat race. They sold tickets to watch. A fox came, and was given a pie, and a crowd gathered to watch the fox eat it, and they paid to watch that too.
At the end of the evening, the festival had made thirty-nine emeralds.
The town was one short. They stood on the pier in the dusk, looking at the old lamp. Nobody spoke.
Then the fox came out of the dark. He sat down. In his mouth was a green stone. It was an emerald. He must have found it in the mud.
He dropped it at Marit's feet and trotted away.
The town bought the new lamp. It is a fine one, with a wide glass and a copper ring, and it has never failed. The old fox is long gone. But every year at the festival, a plate of pie is set at the end of the pier, in the dark.
It is always empty by morning. The people say it is the gulls. The children say it is the fox.
Mira says she has no idea. She says both can be true.
%%
Story: the gull on the price board
A gull sat on the price board at the fish market every morning. It watched the boats go out and the boats come in.
One spring morning there were fewer boats than the day before, and more carts at the pier. The gull counted twice. The numbers did not change.
Marit says gulls cannot count. The fish seller says this one can.
Either way, it is there every morning. Somebody ought to be.
%%
Story: the lamplighter's boy
This is a story the old people at Northpoint tell about a boy who was told to keep the lamps lit and did it for a hundred years.

The boy's name was Ren. He was a keeper's son. Every night his father sent him up the tower stairs with the long wick and the oil, ninety rungs, a lamp at every turn. Light them all, said his father. And Ren did.

Then one night his father did not come home. Then the winter came and the road closed. Then a letter came by relay, a short one, and after that no more letters came from the south.

Ren was alone on the hill with the wind and the foxes. He had one lamp at the top and one at every turn.

Keep the lamps lit, said the last letter, and it did not say how long.

So Ren kept them lit.

He kept them lit through the long nights, when the sky had lights in it that were not stars and did not blink like stars. He kept them lit when the relays went quiet, one by one, from the north. He kept them lit when the snow got so deep that he had to dig his way to the door.

Years went by. Ren grew tall. Ren grew old. A traveler came up the hill once and asked him why he lit a lamp no one saw.

Ren thought about it.

"Somebody might be coming," he said. "I would not want the road dark for them."

The traveler went away and told the story, and the story got told and told. The old people say that if you walk the north road on a clear night, you can still see one yellow light on the hill, blinking slow. They say it is saying, this way, this way, the road is here.

Some say it is the wind and the ice. But they say it quietly.

Story: who gets the blame
Told on the night watch by Fern, at a farm outside Vell, in winter, to keep the lamp-tenders awake.

Three crystals stood in a row, and the first one passed a number to the second, and the second to the third. The third gave an answer. The answer was wrong.

"It is the second one's fault," said the third.

"It is the first one's," said the second.

"I only passed on what I was given," said the first.

Then the tutor came and said, "Each of you gets a share. Not all. A share." And each one changed a little, and the next answer was right.

Story: the salt seller and the news
Rhoda sells salt at the market at Saltreach. She has a stall, a wooden scoop and a scale with two brass pans. She says the scale is the only honest thing in the market, and it only knows about salt.
One spring day, three travelers came to her stall, one after another.
The first was a drover from the north road. He bought two scoops. "The lights over the north are fires on the ice," he said. "Somebody up there is burning a whole winter's wood in one night."
The second was a carter. He bought three scoops. "They are falling stars," he said. "I have seen stars fall all my life. These were in no hurry."
The third was a small girl, sent by her mother for one scoop. She counted her copper twice. "They were looking at us," she said.
Rhoda put down the scoop.
"Who told you that?" she said.
"Nobody," said the girl. "I just think so."
When the girl had gone, Rhoda put a pebble in one pan for the fires. She put a pebble in the other pan for the stars. The pans stayed level. She took out a third pebble, and held it, and did not know which pan to put it in.
"A scale is for salt," she said at last. "It cannot weigh news."
Then she did what she always does. She wiped the pans, and swept the salt into a heap, and waited for the next person to want a scoop.
This spring she sold more salt than any spring she can remember. Salt keeps on a long sea road. Nobody has said why they want so much.
%%
Story: the two baskets
There was once a crystal that sat between two baskets. One was for apples. One was for pears. A fruit came down the chute, and the crystal had to say which basket.

On the first day it put everything in the apple basket. "Wrong," said Una, and she turned a little dial inside it. On the second day it put everything in the pear basket. "Wrong," said Una, and she turned the dial back a little.

On the third day it began to look at the fruit.

"That is how everyone learns," said Una. "First you are wrong one way. Then you are wrong the other way. Then you look."

The wedding cow
A story told by Ned, a child on the farm outside Three Wells, in spring. He told it at supper and his mother wrote it down.
My aunt Pia got married in spring. Everyone came to the farm. There were tables in the yard and bunting on the fence and a cake with three layers.
The night before, my uncle Corm came to the barn. He had a gift for Pia. He had it in a rope. It was a cow.
It was not a good cow. It was brown and white and it had one horn bent. Its name was Dumpling. It had been in Corm's family for years.
"It is a gift," said Corm. "Pia needs a cow."
"Pia has twelve cows," said my mother.
"Not this one," said Corm.
So Dumpling stayed in the yard. In the morning she ate the bunting. She ate a third of the cake. She stood in the middle of the tables like a queen, and chewed.
Nobody was cross. Pia laughed so hard she cried. She said it was the best gift she ever had. She wore a ribbon on Dumpling's horn all day. At the end of the supper, Dumpling got a piece of cake to herself, the last piece, with honey.
My aunt Pia says that is how you know a gift is right. It is a little wrong, and it makes you laugh, and you keep it.
Dumpling is still here. She has the ribbon. She has the one bent horn. She eats the bunting every spring.

Story: the strongest thing
A girl named Sim came to the Vell market one hot day, and stopped at the forge. She watched Opal hammer for a long time. Then she asked a question.

"What is the strongest thing?"

"Iron," said Opal, and hit it. "Nothing breaks iron."

"But you bend it," said Sim.

"I do," said Opal. "With fire. So fire is stronger."

"What is stronger than fire?" said Sim.

"Water," said Opal. She dipped the iron in the bucket, and it hissed. "Water puts fire out."

"What is stronger than water?"

Opal thought. "The sun," she said. "The sun dries the stream."

"What is stronger than the sun?"

"A cloud," said Opal. "A cloud covers the sun. The sun is cold on a cloudy day."

"What is stronger than a cloud?" said Sim.

"The wind," said Opal. "It blows the cloud away."

"What is stronger than the wind?"

Opal put down her hammer. This one took a while. A cart went past. A dog barked. The cheese seller laughed at something.

"A wall," said Opal at last. "A good wall stops the wind. The Three Wells wall is cobblestone, cobblestone, deep stone, and the north wind turns round and goes home."

"What is stronger than a wall?"

"A smith," said Opal, "who makes the nails." She laughed, and Sim did not.

"A wall breaks if no one mends it," said Sim. "My uncle mends the wall at Three Wells. He mends it every spring. If he stopped, there would be no wall."

Opal looked at Sim, and then at the iron in her hand.

"Then it is the person who mends," she said. "Not the wall, and not the iron. The one who comes back with the hammer."

Sim nodded. She had known that from the start. She had come to see if the smith knew.

She came back the next market day, and the next. She is a smith now. She has a bucket of water by her forge, and a red stone on the shelf, and when children ask what is the strongest thing, she asks them what they think, and waits a long time.

Legend: the hill with no roof
The archive has no roof. It has a hill.

Before the first keepers came, the hill at Vell was only a hill, with grass on its back and a sheep path up one side. The first keepers wanted a place where nothing would burn, nothing would get wet, and nothing would be lost. So they went into the hill.

They dug with picks and with their hands. Every stone was carried down the path to build the town. The children carried the small ones in baskets and counted them. They said there were more stones than sheep, which was a great many stones.

When the first room was dug, it was cool, still and dry. They liked it, so they dug another. Then another. When they wanted light, they cut shafts from the top of the hill and set glass in them, so the daylight could come down and sit on the shelves. Someone said it was like pouring the sun down a well.

Rain runs off the grass on top. Snow lies on it in winter and keeps the rooms warm. So the keepers said the hill was already a roof, and they did not need to build one.

Ask anyone in Vell why the archive has no roof, and they will say: because it has a hill.

Story: Hesk and the bucket that talked
Hesk was a miner, but he was not a fool, and he would tell you so before you could tell him. One autumn he took a bucket from the Lowmarsh landing down to the mine at Copperhold, because the rope was better there, and he liked the bucket.
The bucket was old and very dented. Hesk said that dents were good, because a dent meant the bucket had been used. On the third day in the mine, the bucket began to speak.
It said, very politely, that it would like to be put down. Hesk looked around the tunnel. There was no one there. He said so. The bucket said that was not the point, and that it had been carrying copper for two days without a rest.
Hesk put it down. He sat on a rock and looked at it for a while. Then he said that was a sensible thing for a bucket to say, and he apologized for the rope.
The bucket said it had not been unhappy about the rope. It had been unhappy about the wet.
Hesk brought the bucket home to Lowmarsh the next month, and told the story at the inn. Everyone laughed and called him a liar. Hesk said he was not a liar, only a miner, and that a miner who did not listen to his bucket would soon be carrying his own stones.
The bucket still hangs by the landing. It has never spoken again. Some say that is because it is content. Hesk says it is only because nobody has put it down in the wet for a long time.
%%
Story: the keeper who counted the stars
Told by Oren to the children at his door, on a warm summer night. He says an older clerk told it to him, and a still older clerk to that clerk.

There was once a relay keeper at a tower on a hill who could not sleep. At night, when the lamps were lit and the messages were passed and all was quiet, he went up to the roof and looked at the sky.

He thought: I should like to count the stars. Then I will know how many there are.

So he began. One, two, three, four. He counted until the cold was too much, and then he went to bed. The next night he began again from the beginning, because he had forgotten the numbers.

He counted for a year. He got as far as nine thousand and some, and then a cloud came, and he lost his place. He was very upset.

His apprentice, a small copper thing that kept the stair lamps, stood beside him on the roof. The keeper said, "I cannot count them. There are too many."

The apprentice said nothing, because apprentices do not talk. But it picked up a little stone from the roof, and set it down on the wall. Then it picked up another, and set that down.

The keeper watched. For every ten stars he counted, the apprentice put down one stone. At the end of the night there was a little heap of stones.

"Ah," said the keeper. "Now I will not forget."

Next night he counted ten more, and the apprentice put down another stone. In a year there was a great heap of stones. The keeper counted them in the morning.

He was a happy man. He never did count all the stars. But he had a heap of stones for every night he tried, and when visitors came, he said, "Come see my sky."

The heap is said to be on the roof of the little relay still.
%%
Story: the stone that would not sit
Finch cuts stone. He has cut stone for thirty years. He says every stone has a place, and most stones know it. You put a stone down and it sits. It does not argue.

But there was one stone at Kettle Ridge that would not sit. Finch cut it for the mill step. He set it in place. In the morning it was crooked. He set it again. In the morning it was crooked the other way. He set it a third time and sat up all night to watch it, and it did not move at all, and in the morning it was crooked.

Finch asked the miller. The miller said, "It is the wind. The wind is always turning things here."

Finch asked Tam. Tam said, "It is the ridge. The ridge is always moving a little. Nothing sits still on the ridge."

Finch asked Pip, who was small then and new. Pip looked at the stone for a long time. Then Pip said, "The stone is not crooked. The step is crooked. The stone is sitting where the step tells it to sit."

Finch looked. Pip was right. The stone was straight and true. The old wooden step under it was warped, and the stone had settled into the warp, the way a good stone will.

Finch cut a new step. The stone sat. It has sat ever since.

Finch tells this story at the market when the ale is good. He always ends it the same way. "I have cut stone for thirty years. The stone knew its place. It was the step that did not."
%%
Story: the apprentice and the next thing
The old miners at Copperhold tell this one by the stove, when the shift is over and the lamps are low.

There was once a small apprentice named Tib. It was new to the mine. It carried ore and sorted the bins, and it did both very well. But it did not understand much. When a miner gave it an order, Tib did one thing with it. "Fetch the bucket," the miner said, and Tib fetched the bucket. "Stay," said the miner, and Tib stayed by the stair until the sun came up.

Tib could not hold two things at once. If you said "fetch the bucket and bring it to the lift," it would fetch the bucket, and then stand there with it, waiting for the next word. It did not know what came next, so it did nothing.

The miners were kind about it. They called it the slow one, and they gave it the simple jobs.

Then one cold morning, the foreman was in a hurry. He had a lot to say, and he said it all in one breath. "Tib, take the lamp down the long stair to the third level, leave it on the hook by the rail, and then come back up here and help Hesk with the sacks."

Everyone stopped to watch. The foreman was not a patient man. He would be cross if nothing happened.

Tib stood still for a moment. Then it took the lamp. It walked down the long stair. It hung the lamp on the hook by the rail, the way it had been told. Then it turned around, walked back up, and went straight to Hesk, who was pulling sacks off the lift.

Hesk looked at it. "Did you hear me say all that?" he asked.

Tib picked up the first sack and carried it to the bin. It did not say a word. It did not need to.

The miners say that was the day Tib understood a whole sentence. They say the next thing was not an order, and not a word. It was the way the words hung together, and the way one thing led to the next.

Nobody made a fuss about it. But from then on, the foreman gave Tib the long orders, and Tib did them all in order.

Some of the old miners say Tib was not the first to do it. Others say it was a different apprentice, at a different mine. The foreman himself said he did not remember. He only said that Tib was a good worker, and that it always carried its lamp the right way up.
%%
Story: The Four And The Candle Fair
This tale was written down by Alder's neighbor, who sold honey.

At the first candle fair in our hamlet, Alder put a table under the old apple tree. He showed his candles beside jars of honey, and the tutors from Vell brought one of the four apprentices.

The apprentice had learned to sort apples and pears. The tutors let it show the lesson to the children. A child gave it an apple. It put the apple in the apple basket. A child gave it a pear. It put the pear in the pear basket.

Then Alder placed a short candle in its hand.

"It has not learned candles," said the tutor.

The apprentice held the candle. It did not put it in either basket.

Alder gave the child the candle back. "That is a good answer for a sorter," he said. "It has left a thing alone when it did not fit the task."

The tutor said the apprentice had not answered at all. It had only done what it had been taught.

"Then we agree," said Alder. "The candle is still in the child's hand."

The children laughed. The apprentice stood quietly beside the baskets.

Alder had made a fresh candle for the fair. Its wick was thick, and it burned brightly. A gust came through the orchard and blew it out.

The apprentice did not relight it. It had never learned to make a flame.

Alder relit it himself, using the little flame he kept in a covered clay cup. Then he showed the children how to trim the wick. He made them each trim one candle, and most cut too much.

The tutor watched the children and said, "They learn from what they are shown too."

"Yes," said Alder. "But they can ask why the candle goes out."

The children asked why. Alder said a gust had blown it out, and that was all he knew.

At the end of the fair, the apprentice carried the empty baskets back to Vell. Alder carried the honey jars. The children carried the candles they had bought, and the wind tried to blow out every one.
%%
Ash's grandmother and the winter fox
Told by Ash, the net mender, about her grandmother, who lived at the edge of the pond before the mill was built. Every winter she told this one by the stove, and every winter it was a little bit longer.

My grandmother had a fox at the door. It came on the coldest night of every winter, and sat on the step with its tail round its feet, looking in through the glass. Nobody had invited it, and nobody had the heart to shoo it away.

The first winter she gave it a piece of fish from the pot. The second, she gave it two pieces and a bowl of warm water. The third, she gave it the whole fish, and it sat by the stove and ate with its eyes closed. It did not look like a fox that had ever been hungry in its life.

The fox came back every winter for forty winters. It was never older than it was the first time. She said that was the trouble with foxes. They never grow up enough to be sensible.

One spring, when the snow had gone, she found a small green stone on the step. It was smooth and warm, the size of a bean, with a fleck of copper in the middle, like a spark held in ice. It is on the shelf by the stove now, and I have never had the nerve to take it down.

I asked her what the stone was for. She said it was the fox's rent. I asked who the fox paid it to. She said that was not her business, and I should keep the fish pot clean.

The fox has not come back since she died. But on the coldest night of every winter, I put a piece of fish on the step, and leave the door a little open, just in case. The fish is always gone by morning. I do not say anything about it, and neither does the pot.
%%
Story: the first bees on the hill
This is what my grandmother told me, about the first summer after the archive was founded, when Copperhold was only a few houses and a hole in the hill.

A woman named Dessa came to the new town with a clay pot. Inside the pot was a small hive of bees. She had carried it on her back from Ambrook, all the way along the road that was not yet a road.

She put the pot on the hill and said, "These are your neighbors now."

For one summer, the bees were calm. They flew to the heather and the clover and came home with their legs yellow. Dessa sold her honey to the miners, and they put it on their bread.

Then, on a hot day in Longday, the bees swarmed. They rose out of the pot all at once and flew over the new houses. People ran. The miners dropped their picks. A donkey sat down.

The cloud went round once, twice, and settled on the cliff face at the top of the hole.

Dessa climbed up with a basket and a sheet. The miners held the ladder. The bees dropped into the basket in a lump. She brought them down and put them in a better hive, made of oak, with a door.

That is why Copperhold has bees on the hill to this day. They are cousins of Dessa's bees. If you stand still, one may land on your sleeve and say hello.

My grandmother says it is good luck if a bee lands on you. I say it is good luck if it does not sting.
%%
Tale: the market spiders
Told at the Copperhold market by Lorn, keeper of the relay house. Autumn. The tale grows a little every year, and the children say it is their turn to make it bigger.

The spiders at the Copperhold market were once so large that they carried the stall-keepers' bread up to the rafters to keep it dry. Nobody saw them do it. The bread was always in the rafters, and the stall-keepers always said the spiders were honest.

One spider spun a web from the forge chimney to the mine gate. The miners went to work under it every morning, and nobody dared brush it.

She is still up there, the fair people say, counting who comes and goes. She has never been seen to eat. And every spring the web is back, a little wider, to say good morning.

Story: the copper under the field
Ulf had a field on the edge of Kettle Ridge that grew nothing but thistles. He said so every spring. He said it to the mayor, to the mill, and to his cow. Then one hot summer day his plough struck something hard under the thistles, and it rang like a bell.

He dug. The something was a lump of rock with green and blue streaks in it, and the streaks were copper. Ulf sat down in the furrow and laughed until the cow came to see what was wrong.

By the next spring the miners were there. They dug a hole, and then a second hole, and then they found the cave. It went down under the field in a long crooked tunnel, with clay walls and a pool of cold water at the bottom. Frogs lived in the pool. Nobody had ever seen a frog so far from the marsh, and nobody could say how it had got there.

Ulf was paid in emeralds for his field. Then he was paid again for the thistles, which the miners said were very good for finding copper. He bought a new plough, and a new cow, and a hat. He still says the thistles were his best crop. Nobody argues with him, because the copper came from under them.

Story: the apples that rolled to Vell
The road from the farm to Vell climbs the hill. A cart of apples does not like it. One autumn morning, Briar's cart tipped at the steepest turn. Four hundred apples ran down the road to the front door of the Archive.
The clerks came out to see. Oren stood on the step with his hands up. "Do not step on them," he said. "Pick them up."
By the time Briar had her cart back on its wheels, the clerks had picked up every apple. They had put them in baskets by kind: red, green, striped and small. They had numbered the baskets. Oren had written a line in a little book.
"I was going to sort them at home," said Briar.
"Now you do not have to," said Oren.
Briar took the baskets home. The line stayed in the finding book, under A for apples, and again under R for rolled.

Story: the hen at the gate
At the north gate of Three Wells there was once a hen who stood in the middle of the gateway every morning and would not move for anyone. Carts stopped. Horses stopped. Mayor Dace stopped, and said some things.

The guards tried to shoo her. She walked in a circle and came back. They tried bread. She ate it and stayed. They tried a very serious talk. She laid an egg.

Then Mave, the oldest guard, said: "She is doing our job. She is guarding the gate. She looks at everyone who passes."

So they gave her a place by the gate post, a bowl to sit in, and a name. They called her Captain. For eleven years Captain stood in the gateway and looked at every cart. If she liked it, she stepped aside. If she did not, she stayed where she was.

Nobody ever knew what she looked for. But the carts she stopped always turned out to have a loose wheel.

When she was gone, the guards buried her under the gate post. They cut her name in the stone. It says: Captain. She looked.
%%
Story: why the copper roof is green
Told at the Copperhold market by Lorn, keeper of the relay house. Autumn. This is the old story of the green roofs, which the miners tell when they want to be listened to.

A miner named Ansel built the first copper roof on the mine hall, with the first copper that came up from level one. He polished it every week for a year, so that it shone in the sun, and the whole hill could see the mine hall from the road.

One autumn Ansel was called down the Deep Works to fetch the last cart, and he did not come back up for a long time. The roof was left alone in the rain. Nobody polished it. The rain got on it, and the wind, and the snow, and the copper did what copper does when nobody is watching.

When Ansel came back, the roof was green. He stood in the road and looked at it for a long while. Then he said, "I will polish you again," and the roof, the miners say, went greener out of spite.

So the copper roofs of Copperhold are green. The miners say it is the wind. The keeper says it is the rain. The children say it is Ansel's fault, because he did not come back up soon enough. Ansel, when he was asked, said the roof was the best-looking thing in the hill, and he was right.
%%
Story: the frog who asked the way
A young frog at Lowmarsh wanted to see the sea. Every frog had heard about the sea, but none had gone. The sea was far off, past the Long Road and the last waymark, and a frog cannot cross a road.
So the frog set out in the dark, with a leaf over its head for an umbrella. It hopped along the ditch by the Long Road. It hopped under the first bridge and over the first stream. At the first waymark it stopped and read the stone, which said: three blocks to the Brown Hen. It did not know what a Brown Hen was, but it liked the sound.
On the road it met a cart. The carter was asleep. The horse was not. The horse looked down at the frog and said, in a low and bored voice, "You are going the wrong way."
"I am going to the sea," said the frog.
"The sea is that way," said the horse, and it pointed with its ear, toward Saltreach.
"Then I am going the right way," said the frog, and hopped on.
The frog reached the Brown Hen at dawn. The innkeeper tossed a carrot top out of the door, and the frog thanked her very much. Then it went on, to the Halfway, where a traveler gave it a crumb of bread and asked its name. The frog said it did not have a name yet, because it had not seen the sea. The traveler said that was a sensible thing to be.
The frog got to the sea in the spring. It sat on a rock and looked at the water for a long time. Then it hopped back home to tell the others. It said the sea was very big, and very wet, and it was not a marsh at all.
The others asked if it was happy to have seen it. The frog said it was. Then it said it was happier to be home, and that it was going to tell the next frog to take the Brown Hen road, because it had the best leaf in the district.
%%
Story: the bet about the geese
Rafe and his neighbor Owen made a bet one autumn. Owen said his geese would fly south before the first frost. Rafe said they would not, because Owen's geese were too fat and too lazy and had forgotten how.

They shook hands on it. The stakes were a barrel of apples and the loser had to muck out the other's hen house.

Every morning Rafe walked to the fence and looked at the geese. Every morning the geese looked back and went on eating. Owen fed them extra, to be kind. Rafe said that was cheating. Owen said it was hospitality.

Then one cold morning the geese were gone. Not south. They had walked through a gap in the fence and into the marsh, and they were found a day later by a woman from Lowmarsh who said they had been very rude to her frogs.

Rafe laughed until he had to sit down. Owen paid the apples. But when Rafe went to muck out the hen house, Owen had already done it. "The geese were my fault," he said. "The bet was a joke."

Rafe gave him back half the apples. They are still neighbors. Nobody mentions the geese.

Story: The charcoal bird
Ilse told this tale to Moss on a winter evening.

A tutor came to Ilse's house at the edge of Lowmarsh. She carried a crystal wrapped in wool.

"This mind needs a smaller crystal," she said. "We can write its weights in fewer marks. Then it will fit, though it may make more mistakes."

Ilse set a pot of reeds by the fire. "Can it learn to count these?"

The tutor showed the mind the reeds, one by one. It counted well, then missed one when a coal popped.

A black bird had come to the open door. It took a reed and flew off.

"Now the count is right," said the tutor.

"No," said Ilse. "The bird has taken your example."

The tutor looked at the empty place. "Then we must teach it again."

The bird came back for another reed. Ilse shut the door, and the mind counted the reeds again.

Story: Tilly and the mind that was not
Tilly was a miner in her later years, and a storyteller in her earlier. She grew up in a hamlet near Saltreach and told this one to her own grandchildren. They asked for it every spring, when the sea was gray.

When Tilly was small, a tutor came to the hamlet with a small mind in a crystal. The tutor said it could sort fish. The fishers laughed. Then the tutor put the crystal in a copper helper, and set the helper at the harbor table, and brought a basket of fish.

The helper sorted the fish into three bins. Cod, salmon, herring. It did it fast, and it did it well. It had been drilled on a thousand fish, and it had gotten ninety-nine in a hundred right on the drill.

The fishers stopped laughing. "Remarkable," they said.

The harbor master asked, "Has it been tested?"

"It has been tested on the drill," said the tutor.

"That is not what I asked," said the harbor master. "Has it been tested on fish it has never seen?"

The tutor was quiet. Then she said, "No."

"Then we shall do it now," said the harbor master. He sent a boy to the pier for the morning's catch. It was a mixed basket from beyond the point. There were fish in it the helper had never been shown: mackerel, a flounder, a small gray shark.

The helper looked at the mackerel and put it in the cod bin. It looked at the flounder and put it in the salmon bin. It looked at the shark and stopped. It had no bin for the shark. It stood there, holding it.

"There," said the harbor master. "That is the test."

The tutor went red. She took the helper back to the tutor house, and it was drilled again, with more fish, and more kinds. A month later, she brought it back. This time the harbor master had a basket of fish nobody had shown it. The helper put the mackerel in its own bin and the flounder in another. The shark, it set aside, with a small cloth over it.

The harbor master said, "Now it can work."

Tilly was seven years old. She remembered the shark. She remembered the harbor master's words, too: never trust a mind until it has met something new.

When she grew up and went into the mines, she told the story to the crews. The miners liked it, because it has a shark in it. And the helper in the mine, years later, was tested in that same way, before it went below. Hesk says it was a good story, and that the shark was always bigger when Tilly told it.
%%
Story: the lesson of the leaning tower
Our teacher, Sorrel, once tried to show us something about towers. She stacked wooden blocks on the table, one on top of another, and said, "Look how tall I can build."

The blocks went up, and up. At the ninth block, they leaned. At the tenth, they fell.

"That is a lesson," said Sorrel. "A tower must be wide at the bottom."

Hobb said, "The relay towers are narrow at the top."

"They are narrow at the top, but wide at the bottom," said Sorrel.

Hobb said, "Yours was not."

"Mine was a table lesson," said Sorrel. She went quite red. Then she sent us all out to build towers of mud in the yard. It rained. The mud towers slid. That was also a lesson.

I think the real lesson is that Sorrel should not be allowed near blocks. But she was very nice about it, and gave us each a bun.
%%
Story: the hall that tested its own mind
This is a tale from the hamlets near Saltreach, told by Tilly. She says it is about the first tutor who was honest in public. Spring.

There was a tutor called Marn, who kept a little hall by the sea. Her mind was the best in the district, and she knew it. It could sort fish, count nets, and tell a wet day from a dry. At the spring contest of the tutors' halls, the mind was sure to win.

The contest was simple. Each hall brought its best mind. The judges, from Vell, brought a drill none of them had ever seen. It was a box of fifty sealed cards. On each card was a question about the coast: tides, nets, boats, storms. Each mind would answer the same cards at the same time. The right answers would be counted. The count would be written on a board.

Marn was confident. She had drilled her mind for six months. It had gotten ninety-six in a hundred on her own drill. She went to the contest in her best coat.

The cards were read. The minds answered. The numbers were counted.

Marn's mind got thirty-one right in fifty.

She stood quite still. The hall from Ambrook had forty-two. The hall from Three Wells had forty. The hall from Copperhold had thirty-eight.

Everyone looked at Marn. Some looked kindly. Some did not.

She could have said the cards were unfair. She could have said her mind was tired. She could have said the questions were not the kind her mind knew. All of these were a little true, and none of them was the point.

Instead she stepped forward, and she said to the judges, "My mind did well on my own drill. It did badly on yours. So my drill was wrong. Show me which questions it missed. I will find out why."

The room was silent. Then a very old judge began to clap. Then another. Then the whole hall.

That autumn, Marn's hall went back to its drill. She looked at the cards her mind had missed. Most were about storms, which her drill had hardly mentioned. She added more. The next spring, her mind got forty-four.

It won.

In the years after, the contest became a custom. Each spring the halls brought their minds. The judges brought a sealed drill. The numbers went on the board. Nobody could boast without them, and nobody could hide.

Tilly says that is why tutors are honest today. A mind may fool its own tutor. It cannot fool a drill nobody has seen. And Marn, who was brave enough to say her drill was wrong, was the first of the honest ones.

Some say Marn did not exist. Others say she is buried in a hamlet by the sea, under a stone with a fish on it. The stone says only: she asked for the cards.
%%
Story: the Ambrook fair
The summer fair at Ambrook has a sorting race. It is only for children. A tub of one hundred apples and pears is tipped out, and each child has two crates, and the one who sorts them with the fewest mistakes wins a blue ribbon and a round cheese.

The year Una brought Basket to the fair, the winner three summers running was a girl named Moll, who was nine, and who looked at a pear as if it owed her money.

Una went to Perrin, the old man who was mayor of Ambrook and judged the race. "May my sorter enter?" she asked.

Perrin read the rules board. "It says children," he said. "It does not say only children." He turned to the crowd. "Does anyone mind?"

Moll minded. She folded her arms. "It is not fair," she said. "It is made of copper."

"Then you may start first," said Una.

"I do not need to start first," said Moll. "But I will."

Una asked for one more rule. Before the race, a basket of fruit must be brought from the next village, fruit that Basket had never seen. "If it has only learned our own fruit by heart," said Una, "that is remembering, and not sorting. Let it sort strangers." The crowd muttered. But that is the rule at Ambrook to this day. Sort what you were not shown.

So a hundred strange apples and pears came from the next village, and the race began. Moll sorted like a bird, quick and sharp. Basket was much slower. It picked up each fruit and turned it and put it down. The crowd grew quiet. At last both tubs were empty.

Perrin counted. "Moll," he said, "four mistakes." The crowd cheered. "Basket, one."

Nobody cheered at all. Then Moll walked over to Basket's crates and asked Perrin which fruit it had got wrong. He showed her a small round green pear that looked just like an apple.

"I got that one wrong too," said Moll, quietly. "And three more."

Perrin tied the blue ribbon on Basket's arm. Basket stood with the ribbon on, looking at nothing. Perrin cut the cheese into a hundred pieces, and the children ate it, and Basket did not mind.

When the cheese was gone, Moll came up to Una. "Show me how it learns," she said. "I am not saying it was fair. But show me."

Una smiled. "Come to the orchard in the morning," she said. "Bring your friends."

Northpoint log, spring
Kept by Mira, relay keeper at Northpoint, in the north, where the nights are long. Copied and sent south to the archive at Vell.

Third of Blossom. The snow is going. The foxes are out and bold. One came to the foot of the tower and sat and looked up at the lamp room and did not move for an hour. Lantern says it was waiting for a message. I said foxes do not wait for messages. Lantern said this one did.

Fifth. The lamp is good. The glass is clean. Lantern climbed the ninety rungs twice today to check it. I told Lantern once is enough. Lantern said twice is sure.

Ninth. Word from the south. The little relay at the Halfway Inn broke its glass in the wind and is mended now. Bett at the hill tower sent the news. It came up the towers one by one and reached us before dark. That is the relays working as they should.

Twelfth. A trader came up from the south with honey and wool. He held the jar of honey a long time before he let it go. He said it was from Ambrook. I asked if he had been there. He said no, he had only heard of it. He said it was the warmest place anyone ever told him about. I gave him a cup of tea and he told us about the baby at the Halfway Inn. Lantern wants to know what a baby is like. I said loud and small and it keeps everyone awake. Lantern said that sounds like a relay.

Fifteenth. The nights are shorter now. The lamp room is warm in the day. I sat in the walk round the lamp and watched the light on the snow and thought of my brother at Kettle Ridge. Tam will be sowing soon. I will write to him.

Twentieth. Lantern has learned to read the lamp faster than I did at that age. I tested Lantern on the same message three times and Lantern got it right each time. Never send an untested mind to the fields, the tutors say. I say the same of a keeper. Lantern is tested and true.

Twenty-fifth. The foxes are back. Two of them now, at the foot of the tower, looking up. Lantern has named them. I told Lantern not to name them. Lantern named them anyway.

Last of Blossom. The relay is good. The keeper is good. The apprentice is good. Send more honey.

Mira, keeper at Northpoint.

Story: the mind that told a joke
In the relay house at Lowmarsh, a boy once asked the big mind to tell him a joke. He was nine, and he had a cold, and he wanted to make his sister laugh.
The mind thought about it for a long time, for a mind. Then it wrote: Why did the frog sit by the fence? It wanted to be a cat.
The boy read it twice. Then he said it was a bad joke, and the mind agreed. It said it was the best it had, and that it was sorry for the cat.
So the boy ran home and told his sister. She laughed so hard she sneezed. The mind was very pleased. It said that was a good joke after all, and it had only been worried it was too silly.
Told by the boy's sister, at the boat houses, autumn.

Story: The three boxes at Three Wells
In the old winter tale, Pim had a well beside the Three Wells square and a table beside his well. He sold apples there when the frost came, and he kept three wooden boxes under the table.

One morning, a tutor came to the market with pages for a young mind. The first box held clear pages for drills. The second held spoiled pages. The third was locked. Pim asked what was inside.

"Pages for a test," said the tutor. "The mind must never see them during its drills."

Pim nodded. He knew a well could not be judged by pouring the same bucket of water back into it. The test had to bring water from somewhere else.

A fox came down from the cold hills and stole a page from the table. It ran past the stalls, with the paper in its mouth. Pim chased it, but the fox slipped through the market wall.

The page fell in a puddle. When Pim found it, the ink had spread. The tutor put it with the spoiled pages. The fox had not meant to spoil the drill, but the page could no longer teach it clearly.

The mind began to learn from the first box. It saw many examples of apples and pears. When it sorted wrongly, the tutor changed its weights a little toward the right answer. It did better each time.

At the end of the fair, the tutor took a page from the locked box. The mind had never seen it. It sorted the fruit correctly, and the people clapped.

Pim had been guarding the boxes all day. The fair prize was a new rope for his well. He was pleased with it, though the fox had bitten one end.

The tutor said the mind had done well because it had learned the rule, not because it remembered the test. Pim said that sounded like good well water: clear, and drawn from a place it had not already been.

From then on, the market children used three baskets when they played at sorting. One for good pages, one for spoiled pages, and one with a lid for the pages nobody showed.

The fox came back the next winter. It stole an apple, not a page. Pim said it had learned the better lesson.
%%
Story: The Long Road and the rain
Told by Iona at the Brown Hen.

One spring, the rain fell for three days. The road was wet, the fields were wet, and the inn yard was a wide brown puddle.

Travelers came in with muddy boots. Their horses came in with muddy legs. Even Pell's hat was muddy, though he said he had only walked through a little rain.

The innkeeper put a board by the door. It said, Wipe your boots.

A traveler wiped one boot. Then he put the other boot on the clean floor.

The innkeeper pointed at the board.

"I read it," said the traveler. "It did not say both."

The innkeeper added another line. It said, Wipe both boots.

That evening, a cart came up the road. Its wheels sank in the mud by the gate. The horse leaned into the harness, but the cart did not move.

The driver pushed. The innkeeper pushed. Pell pushed, though he had only stopped to ask the price of oats.

Iona put a plank under one wheel. The horse pulled again. The wheel climbed onto the plank, then slid back into the mud.

"More hands," said the innkeeper.

The travelers came out. They put their shoulders to the cart and pushed together. The horse pulled. The wheel turned once, then stopped.

A small boy ran to the stable and brought a second plank.

"Where did you find that?" asked Iona.

"Under the hay," he said.

"Then the hay is not where I put it," said Iona.

They set both planks under the wheels. This time the cart rolled free. The driver thanked everyone and gave the boy a small loaf.

The boy gave half the loaf to the horse.

Pell looked at the road and shook his head. "The Long Road is longer when it is mud."

"It is the same length," said Iona.

"Then it takes longer to walk," Pell said.

Inside the inn, everyone left their boots by the door. One traveler had wiped both boots. He had also wiped the cat.

The cat sat by the hearth, damp and offended. The innkeeper brought it a cloth.

The rain stopped at dawn. The yard stayed muddy until noon. By then, the cart had gone on, the horse had eaten its hay, and the boy had found the second plank.

The innkeeper nailed the plank beside the door. He said it was a fine place for it.

Iona put up a new board. It said, Wipe both boots. Leave the cat alone.

The cat sat below it. No one knew whether he could read. But he looked pleased.
%%
The gift of the three stones
A story told on the farm outside Three Wells by Ned, who is nine. He told it to the hens first and then to his grandmother, who wrote it down in spring.
There was once a wedding on a farm, and nobody had a gift. The bride, whose name was Lila, had married a farmer named Brax. They had a house, a field, two pigs and a cart. They wanted for nothing, and so no one knew what to give.
The neighbors thought and thought. At last each neighbor went out in the night and found something. One brought a stone from the stream. One brought a stone from the hill. One brought a stone from the road. They were three stones, round and smooth. They did not look like much.
On the day of the wedding they put the stones in a basket. Lila opened it. She looked. She was quiet.
"Three stones," said Brax, who was not good with words.
The neighbors waited. They felt a little foolish.
Then Lila said, "I know what these are."
She took the stone from the stream and put it by the door. She took the stone from the hill and put it on the roof. She took the stone from the road and put it at the gate.
"This one is for the water, this one is for the wind, and this one is for the people who come," she said. "Now the house is ready."
The neighbors cheered. Nobody knew what she meant, but it sounded right. The stones are still there. The one at the gate has been sat on by a thousand visitors. It has a dip in the top, from the sitting.
When I grow up I will give someone a stone. I think it is the best gift, because it is free and it lasts and it is yours to put where you like.
%%
Story: how the layers learned
A story the tutors tell the children at Vell, retold in winter by Fern, a night watch, at a farm outside the town. She says it is almost true.

Once there were three crystals, called First, Second and Third. They stood in a row on a bench in a tutor's room. They were friends, but they were not perfect.

Each day the tutor showed them a drill, and each day they answered together. First looked at the question and passed a number to Second. Second looked at the number and passed a new one to Third. Third gave the answer.

The answer was wrong.

"Third," said the tutor, "you are wrong."

Third was quiet. Then Third said, "I am wrong, but I did not know the question. I only saw what Second gave me."

"Second?"

Second said, "I did not see the question either. I saw what First gave me."

"First?"

First said, "I saw the question. But I do not know what Third wanted. I only passed it on."

The tutor sat down. This was the old problem. In the old days, with a single crystal, the answer was wrong and the crystal changed. With three, nobody knew who to change. All three had played a part.

She thought about it for a long time. Then she took a piece of chalk and wrote on the board: "Each gets a share."

"How much of a share?" asked First.

"It depends," said the tutor. "How much did you matter to the answer? Third mattered most, because Third gave it. So Third gets most of the blame, and changes most. Second mattered next, so Second gets some. First mattered by way of Second, so First gets a small share. And you each change by that share. Not all. Not none. Your share."

They tried it. The answer was a little less wrong. They tried again, and again. In a week the three of them were answering well. In a month they were answering hard things that no single crystal could.

This is the way the layered minds learn. The tutor Merle of Copperhold found it. The blame goes from the answer back through each layer, to the first, and each layer is told what its share was. Every layer changes by that share.

I tell it because it is a good way to think about quarrels, too. If three people did something wrong, you do not blame one. You find the share.

First, Second and Third are still in a glass case at Vell, I am told. They are quiet now, but they remember each other.
%%
Story: Hollis and the winter waymark
A tale for a long cold evening

Hollis the carter knew the road to Three Wells so well that he could find it in fog, rain, and a dark night. He knew every bridge and every bend. He knew which inn served thick soup. He knew which waymark had a stone missing from its top.

One winter evening, he left the Halfway Inn with a cart of apples for the market. Snow covered the open road. The torches on the posts made little yellow circles, and the wind pushed them sideways.

Hollis stopped at a waymark. It had a figure cut into the stone and an arrow pointing toward Three Wells. A small stone sat on top, as it should.

"I know this way," he said. "I do not need a stone to tell me."

He took the stone from the top and put it in his coat pocket. It was smooth and warm from his hand.

Then he drove on.

The road bent by a stand of spruce. Hollis saw another waymark and knew he was still going right. He went on past a stream, crossed the bridge, and came to a fork.

There were two arrows on the post. Snow hid the figures.

Hollis stopped the cart. The horse stamped once. The apples shifted in their sacks.

"I know this road," Hollis said again.

He chose the left track. It led to a little hill and then to a fence. No road went beyond it.

Hollis turned the cart around. The horse looked at him as if to say, You know the road, do you?

At the fork, Hollis brushed the snow from the waymark. One arrow pointed to the inn. The other pointed to Three Wells. He had taken the wrong track.

He drove back to the first stone pillar. Its top was bare.

Hollis took the smooth stone from his pocket and set it in its place. Then he read the arrow and went toward Three Wells.

At the square, Bel was waiting beside the notice board. She had come with Dace to see if the road was clear.

"Did the waymark help?" she asked.

"It helped when I left it alone," said Hollis.

Bel looked at the stone on top. "Why did you take it?"

"I thought I could remember the way without it."

"Could you?"

Hollis looked at his cart, his horse, and the sacks of apples. "I remembered one thing very well. I remembered being wrong."

Bel laughed. Dace took the apples to the market table, and Hollis tied his horse by the well.

The next morning, the snow had stopped. The road was clear, and the little stones sat on every waymark as before.

Hollis did not take one home. He did not need a stone to remember that lesson.

He still knew the road to Three Wells. But when he came to a fork, he read the arrows first.
%%
Story: the boat that was late home
A winter story from the farm outside Vell, told by Fern, who keeps the night watch and says a good story keeps a watcher awake better than tea. She says her aunt at Saltreach told it to her.

There was once a fishing boat that did not come home.

It had left Saltreach at dawn, with four fishers and a full net. By dusk it should have been back. The wives were on the quay, with lanterns. The cats were on the rope pile. The harbor master rang the bell.

The boat did not come. The wind rose. The sea went dark.

At the relay tower above the harbor, the keeper blinked a message to Vell: "Boat late. Four aboard. Wind from the north." Down the line it went, and up the line, and everyone in the land who read it sat up and looked at the sea.

In Vell, the tutors had been working on a thing. It was a layered mind, in three crystals, that read the numbers written on the relay letters. Merle's way. It had been taught for months, and the tutors had been telling it, each wrong answer, how much of the blame belonged to each layer. It was proud of nothing. It was only a mind.

That night the letter came in: "Boat late." The mind read the numbers. It answered, as it was drilled to do, with a number back. The number meant: "Look east."

The keeper was puzzled. She did not know what the number meant. But she lit the east lamp, the big one, and turned the glass to the east.

Far out on the water, in the dark, the fishers saw a light. They had been blown far west, past the point. They saw the east lamp, and they turned the boat toward it. They rowed all night. They came in at dawn, wet, tired, hungry, and very happy.

Was it the mind? Nobody could say. It might have been luck. The mind answered a number, that is all. It did not know about boats. But the tutors said that if a mind learns well, it can find a thing in a letter that no person sees. I do not know. I only know the boat came home.

The wives on the quay cried. The cats got fish. The harbor master rang the bell until his arm hurt.

And the four fishers said, when asked, that they had not been frightened at all. Their wives said they were liars.

Story: the bees and the big house
Told by the miller's children, who were not allowed to tell it at the table.

In Swarm the bees from the Northpoint hives went up the tower. They found the copper rings round the big house, which were cool and bright and very interesting.

The bees hung from the rings in a beard. The whole ring was covered. The house felt the cool of its rings, and it felt, in one place, a warm spot.

The keeper came down the stair with a lamp and looked. The warm spot was on the north ring. It was a bee.

"Stop that," said the keeper, very crossly. "You are making the house warm."

The apprentice, Pim, had come down behind him, and it asked the house through the relay whether the warm spot was a fault. The house wrote back: The warm spot is a bee. The bee is cooler than the rings were yesterday. I will allow it.

So the bees stayed on the rings all afternoon, and the house stayed cool, and nobody was stung, except the miller, who had put his hand in the wrong place.

The moral, as the children say: small things can warm even a cold house, and a bee is never a fault until it is shown to be one.

Story: what the biggest house can do
Told at the Northpoint market on the day the snow first came down, by a carter who swore every word of it was true. Mira wrote it out, and put her own notes in brackets.

The big house in the south answers any letter you send. The answer comes back by the next cart, in a hand so neat you could frame it.
It can count every sheep in every field along the road, and tell you which one is about to have a lamb.
It writes songs, but only songs about the sea, because it has never seen the sea.
It can tell an apple from a pear, but it gets very upset about pears.
It knows the name of every child born in Saltreach, and it will not say.
It cannot be asked the same question twice, because it gets bored.

Then the carter drank his soup and said the thing nobody liked. "The house is only lots of small minds in rows. Each row has a tutor checking it. The tutors are the clever ones. The house just holds them."

(Nobody can check a mind that big alone. That is why the councils hired so many tutors for the one house. Whether the tutors are checking the house, or the house is checking them, is a question I have been asked by more than one child. I have no answer for either.)

(The carter also said the house had never once been wrong about the weather. I asked how he knew. He said he had asked it, and it had told him so.)
%%
Notes on the reading drill
These are my notes for the apprentice tutors. Read them plainly. Do not make them grander than they are.

A reading mind sits behind a window sixty letters wide. It reads one letter at a time. For each letter it looks back at the letters before it that matter, and it guesses what comes next. Show it the right letter. It changes a little. Show it the next. That is the whole of it, and it is enough to fill the hall with work.

Some things I have learned, teaching it:

One. The window matters. Sixty letters is about one long sentence, or two short ones. A mind cannot look past its window. If the answer is at the top of the page and the question at the bottom, it will not see it. Do not set it a drill it cannot see.

Two. The letters before it that matter. Not every letter matters to the next one. A mind learns which ones do. This is why a mind can read the word relay in a hundred different sentences and still know what comes after it. It is also why it sometimes reads a name it has never seen and gets it wrong in a way that sounds right.

Three. Guess, then check. The mind guesses the next letter before it is shown. The tutor shows it. The difference between the guess and the truth is the lesson. This is Una's way, and it is still the first lesson in our books.

Four. A mind that guesses well can write. If it can guess the next letter, it can guess the next letter and write it down, and then the next. Some of the big minds in the relay houses now write letters that a reader cannot tell from a person's. I have read one. I read it twice. I could not tell.

Five. This is Orla's question, come back. If you cannot tell the letters apart, what then? I do not know. I am a tutor, not a clerk of the deep halls. I only say: test the mind on what it was never drilled on, and write down what it does. Never send an untested mind to the fields.

Six. Keep the test box. The clerks keep pages no mind is ever drilled on. A drill is only as good as its pages. Take out the copies and the spoiled pages. Keep the box locked.

That is all I have. Drill them well. Be exact. Be patient. The mind will be wrong many times before it is right, and that is not a fault. That is the work.
